## ABSTRACT

This book explores the concepts and practices of participation and community engagement in cultural heritage, examining the impact of the participatory turn in the heritage sector and the key opportunities and challenges it presents.

Participation has become a widely used umbrella term in the discourses of heritage institutions, practitioners, and scholars alike. This participatory turn represents a shift towards the active involvement of communities, stakeholders, and the public in decision-making processes, moving away from traditional top-down approaches towards more inclusive and collaborative models in which diverse voices contribute to cultural heritage. While the pluralisation of heritage narratives, meanings, and practices can sometimes give rise to societal tensions, this book argues that broader participation is essential for unlocking heritage’s transformative potential and future-oriented impetus. The book explores these issues through 15 case studies drawn from across the globe and organised into three thematic sections: social inclusion in heritage practices, the role of digital tools in heritage participation, and participatory heritage management and governance. These case studies contribute to emerging debates on expanding the spaces where heritage participation and community engagement take place.

Interdisciplinary in nature, this book is intended for scholars and professionals working on cultural heritage, participation, social inclusion, digitisation, management, and community and audience engagement. It is also well-suited for undergraduate and graduate courses that critically examine heritage participation and community engagement in today’s pluralistic societies.

Engaging Communities in 
Cultural Heritage 
This book explores the concepts and practices of participation and community 
engagement in cultural heritage, examining the impact of the participatory turn in 
the heritage sector and the key opportunities and challenges it presents. 
Participation has become a widely used umbrella term in the discourses of herit-
age institutions, practitioners, and scholars alike. This participatory turn represents 
a shift towards the active involvement of communities, stakeholders, and the public 
in decision-making processes, moving away from traditional top-down approaches 
towards more inclusive and collaborative models in which diverse voices contrib -
ute to cultural heritage. While the pluralisation of heritage narratives, meanings, 
and practices can sometimes give rise to societal tensions, this book argues that 
broader participation is essential for unlocking heritage’s transformative potential 
and future-oriented impetus. The book explores these issues through 15 case stud-
ies drawn from across the globe and organised into three thematic sections: social 
inclusion in heritage practices, the role of digital tools in heritage participation, and 
participatory heritage management and governance. These case studies contrib-
ute to emerging debates on expanding the spaces where heritage participation and 
community engagement take place. 
Interdisciplinary in nature, this book is intended for scholars and professionals 
working on cultural heritage, participation, social inclusion, digitisation, manage -
ment, and community and audience engagement. It is also well-suited for under -
graduate and graduate courses that critically examine heritage participation and 
community engagement in today’s pluralistic societies. 
Tuuli Lähdesmäki is a professor of art history at the University of Jyväskylä, 
Finland. She specialises in critical heritage studies, heritage policies and politics, 
cultural memory, identities, and narratives of Europe. 
Johanna Turunen is a senior researcher of contemporary culture studies at the 
University of Jyväskylä, Finland. Her work is focused on the intersections of criti-
cal heritage studies and post- and decolonial theory. 

Andrei Terian is a professor of Romanian literature at the Lucian Blaga University 
of Sibiu, Romania. He researches intangible cultural heritage, specialising in the 
textual heritage, cultural memory, canonisation, and digitisation. 
Renaud Garcia-Bardidia is a professor of cultural studies at the University of 
Lorraine and an associate researcher at the University of Burgundy, France. His 
research addresses the digitalisation of cultural consumption practices and the evo-
lution of cultural institutions in this context.

Contributors 
Ester Alba-Pagán is Professor in the Department of Art History at the University 
of Valencia, Spain. She previously served as Vice Rector for Culture and Dean 
of the Faculty of Geography and History at her university. Her research focuses 
on museology, museography, and art criticism, with special attention to contem-
porary art and cultural heritage in relation to the exchange and diffusion of ideas 
and knowledge from a visual studies and gender perspective. 
Ștefan Baghiu is Lecturer of Romanian Literature and Literary Theory with the 
Department of Romance Studies at Lucian Blaga University of Sibiu, Romania, 
and the editorial secretary of the Transilvania journal. He is also one of the co-
ordinators of the Digital Museum of the Romanian Novel (1845–1947), the first 
digital archive of the Romanian modern novel available for research and public 
access. His work is mainly related to quantitative analysis of world literature, 
translations of novels, and the ideologies of literature. 
Alin Croitoru is Associate Professor of Sociology at Lucian Blaga University of 
Sibiu, Romania. He has extensive experience in social research and has worked 
as a researcher in various European research projects such as EUCROSS, YMO-
BILITY , SafeHabitus, and ELABCHROM, in addition to national research pro-
jects implemented by the Ministry of Education. 
Kenza Gana is Doctoral Student in Management Sciences, specialising in market-
ing, under the supervision of Professor Renaud Garcia-Bardidia at the Univer -
sity of Burgundy, Centre de REcherche en Gestion des Organisations (CREGO), 
France. She is writing a thesis on cultural practices and consumption, with a 
special focus on video games in a historical and tourist context and their learn -
ing effects. 
Renaud Garcia-Bardidia is Professor of Cultural Studies at the University of Lor-
raine, France. He manages BA Studies in Cultural Mediation for the live arts. 
His research projects in the Laboratory LIS (Literature, Imaginaries, Society) 
address the digitalisation of cultural consumption practices and the evolution 
of cultural institutions in this context. He is also Associated Researcher at the 
CREGO, at the University of Burgundy, where he participates in an interdisci -
plinary research group on Recreational and Cultural Practices and Activities. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Contributors xiii 
Pénélope Girard is in charge of studies and professional development at the Office 
for Museum Cooperation and Information, University of Burgundy/Ministry 
of Higher Education and Research, France. She holds a Master’s degree from 
the University of Avignon (Médiations, Musées et Patrimoines), France, and a 
Bachelor’s degree in Indian Languages, Literatures, and Civilizations (Inalco). 
Tuuli Lähdesmäki is Professor of Art History at the University of Jyväskylä, Fin-
land. She has the title of Docent in Critical Heritage Studies at the University of 
Turku, Finland, and in Area and Cultural Studies at the University of Helsinki, 
Finland. She specialises in EU cultural and heritage policies and politics, cul-
tural memory, identities, belonging, and narratives of Europe. 
Raluca Mureșan is Associate Professor in the Department of Social Work, Journal-
ism, Public Relations, and Sociology at Lucian Blaga University of Sibiu, Roma-
nia. She not only specialises in journalism and public relations ethics and law, but 
also gives lectures and seminars on crisis communication, event management, 
and corporate social responsibility. Her research interests include traditional and 
new media ethics, communication law, online communication, ethics of social 
movements and protest culture, academic integrity, and media literacy. 
Ovio Olaru is Assistant Professor of German and Norwegian Language and Lit -
erature in the Department of Anglo-American and German Studies at Lucian 
Blaga University of Sibiu, Romania. His fields of research include German, 
Romanian, and Scandinavian studies, as well as Digital Humanities. He is also 
a translator of Scandinavian literature. 
María-Dolores Pitarch-Garrido is Senior Professor in the Department of Geog-
raphy at the University of Valencia, Spain, and Director of the Inter-University 
Institute for Local Development since 2018. Her research is based on social and 
cultural geography, with a particular focus on the territorial impact of economic 
activities and their effects on society. She is currently developing research pro-
jects on sustainability and cultural tourism in urban areas. 
Sorin Radu is Professor at the Faculty of Social and Human Sciences, Lucian 
Blaga University of Sibiu, Romania, and since 2013, Convenor for Romania 
in the International Commission for the History of Representatives and Parlia -
mentary Institutions. He has published extensively on the history of Romania 
in the 20th century. 
Miguel Requena-Jiménez is Professor of Ancient History at the University of 
Valencia, Spain, and corresponding academic of the Royal Academy of His -
tory in Spain. He specialises in the conception of power in the Roman Empire, 
literature and traditions of magic, religion and beliefs in Ancient Rome, cultural 
memory and public history. 
Daniela Stanciu-Păscărița is Teaching Assistant at the Faculty of Social and Hu-
man Sciences, Lucian Blaga University of Sibiu, Romania. Her areas of interest 
include the history of everyday life, the history of minorities, urban heritage, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
xiv Contributors 
and the modern history of the Habsburg Empire. She has worked on several 
research projects and is currently conducting a postdoctoral research project in 
the field of urban history and everyday life studies. 
Khaoula Stiti is an architect, independent researcher, and writer with a PhD from 
the Brussels School of Engineering. Her thesis centered on heritage, participa -
tory practices, and digital media within the Tunisian context. Currently, she 
examines the influence of coloniality in research processes and knowledge pro-
duction, alongside the complexities of migration, employing ethnographical 
and anthropological practices. 
Anna Talasniemi is a Finnish professional and influencer in the field of arts, cul -
ture, and foundations. She is currently pursuing a PhD in Art Education at the 
University of Jyväskylä, Finland. Her research focuses on contemporary Finn -
ish sauna culture, drawing her methodology from dialogical art practices and 
futures studies. She has also studied traditional sauna healing. 
Andrei Terian is Professor of Romanian Literature and Vice Rector for Re -
search, Innovation, and Internationalisation at the Lucian Blaga University 
of Sibiu, Romania. He is the PI of the ERC Consolidator Grant “A Transna -
tional History of Romanian Literature” (2021–2026) and leads the Horizon 
Europe-funded project “Establishing a Laboratory of Cultural Heritage in 
Central Romania” (2023–2025). He researches intangible cultural herit -
age, specialising in the textual heritage, cultural memory, canonisation, and 
digitisation. 
Suzie Thomas, FSA, is Professor of Heritage Studies at the University of Antwerp, 
Belgium. She teaches and researches on aspects of dark heritage and participa -
tory methods and approaches in heritage. She holds a PhD in Heritage Studies 
from Newcastle University (UK) and previously worked in both the third sector 
and academia in England, Scotland, and Finland. 
Johanna Turunen  is Senior Researcher in Contemporary Culture Studies at 
the University of Jyväskylä, Finland. Her research builds on the connections 
between critical heritage studies and post- and decolonial theory. Her work 
covers topics such as the decolonisation and pluralisation of museums and her-
itage, heritage activism, heritage diplomacy, identity politics and belonging, 
heritagisation of European colonialism and slavery, and EU heritage politics. 
Mathieu Viau-Courville is a research associate in museum studies and anthro -
pology of art at the University of Lorraine and an honorary research fellow at 
the Research Centre for Museums and Galleries, University of Leicester. His 
work explores themes of co-curation, managerial curatorship, museum activ-
ism, memory and postmemory, as well as citizen engagement and participation. 
He received his PhD from the University of East Anglia in 2011. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Contributors xv 
Ionela Vlase is Professor of Sociology at Lucian Blaga University in Sibiu, Roma-
nia. Vlase coordinated the research project “Branding the Multicultural Legacy 
of Central Romania. An Interdisciplinary Study of Regional Development” as 
part of the project “Establishing a Laboratory of Cultural Heritage in Central 
Romania”, funded by the Horizon Europe programme. Her research combines 
sociology, gender studies, festival studies, and cultural heritage studies. 
Jiyun Zhang is an independent animation filmmaker. She works as a research 
associate for the UNESCO Chair on Critical Heritage Studies and the Safe -
guarding of Intangible Cultural Heritage at the Brussels Heritage Lab and as 
a post-doctoral researcher in the ARCHES research group at the University of 
Antwerp, Belgium. Her current research focuses on audiovisual interpretation 
and representation of intangible cultural heritage, media archaeology, and sha-
manistic expressions of groups in northern China. 

In recent decades, cultural heritage has gained importance in society as a mul -
tidisciplinary field that affects not only cultural practices but also various social 
domains, ranging from social relations to education, and from economics to poli -
tics. Therefore, it is necessary to apply multidisciplinary approaches to explore and 
explain cultural heritage. This book is based on a research project that has sought 
to extend cultural heritage research into new areas and to strengthen its multidisci-
plinary foundations through international collaboration.
The book is a result of the project “Establishing a Laboratory of Cultural Herit-
age in Central Romania (ELABCHROM)”, funded by the European Union from 
2023 to 2025 through its Widening Participation and Spreading Excellence actions 
under the Horizon Europe programme. Professor Andrei Terian of Lucian Blaga 
University Sibiu, Romania, is leading the project. ELABCHROM has promoted 
scientific partnerships between three universities, Lucian Blaga University Sibiu, 
the University of Jyväskylä in Finland, and the University of Burgundy in France, 
and advanced cultural heritage research within these partners, as well as within the 
FORTHEM University Alliance, involving six other universities, namely Johannes 
Gutenberg University Mainz (Germany), University of Palermo (Italy), University 
of Latvia, University of Agder (Norway), University of Opole (Poland), and Uni -
versity of València (Spain). The FORTHEM University Alliance works through 
multidisciplinary thematic laboratories. As its name suggests, the project has es -
tablished a Cultural Heritage Laboratory within the Alliance, led by Lucian Blaga 
University Sibiu.
As part of ELABCHROM, the Romanian team conducted a research project in 
Sibiu and the surrounding region of Transylvania, exploring the use of culture and 
cultural heritage for regional development. The research was based on extensive 
empirical data collected among local cultural actors and citizens. The second and 
third chapters of this book draw on these datasets, and the fourth and fourteenth 
chapters take a historical perspective on the multicultural heritage of the region.
The collaborative activities of the ELABCHROM project have included con -
ferences focused on cultural heritage. The first of these conferences, entitled 
“Revisiting Cultural Heritage: Novel Approaches, Innovative Methods, and Trans-
national Connections”, was organised in Sibiu in October 2023. The conference 
brought together nearly 80 scholars, heritage professionals, and students to discuss 
Preface

 
 
Preface xvii 
interdisciplinary heritage research in the social sciences and humanities. The con -
ference brought out a variety of timely topics in the practices of heritage production 
and consumption, and in the politics of heritage conservation and management. 
One of these timely themes focused on participation and community engagement. 
The scientific committee of the conference decided to develop this strand of the 
conference into an edited volume by enriching the selected conference papers and 
keynote speeches with contributions from partners from the FORTHEM Alliance. 
The selected papers extend the current scholarship on cultural heritage as a social 
and community asset by providing a wide range of case studies that critically dis -
cuss what participation in cultural heritage means, why such participation is essen-
tial, and what the current challenges are. 
I would like to thank the organising committee of the conference “Revisiting 
Cultural Heritage”, in particular the chairperson of the committee, Professor Ionela 
Vlase, as well as all the conference assistants, at Lucian Blaga University Sibiu. 
I am grateful to all the contributors for their thorough work in developing their 
conference papers and keynote speeches into chapters of the volume. I would also 
like to thank Editor Chris Parry, Editor Heidi Lowther, and Editorial Assistant 
Heeranshi Sharma for their smooth cooperation in the publication process, as well 
as the anonymous reviewers for their fruitful comments, which helped us to de -
velop the book and sharpen our arguments. 
Tuuli Lähdesmäki

Introduction 
Heritage participation and community 
engagement on the move 
Tuuli Lähdesmäki and Johanna Turunen 
Talk of participation, engagement, and communities has been an integral element 
of heritage work for decades. Such talk reflects the participatory turn in scholarship 
that has broadly influenced research design, data collection, and knowledge pro -
duction in various fields, including the humanities and social sciences (Hetland and 
Schrøder 2020). It has meant a shift towards involving research participants, com-
munities, and other stakeholders as active collaborators in the research process, 
emphasising co-creation of knowledge, shared agency, and inclusion, and moving 
away from top-down, researcher-centred approaches towards more democratic and 
interactive methodologies such as participatory action research and ethnographic 
engagement. The assemblage of words and practices connected with the partici -
patory turn has become a hegemonic mantra constantly repeated by international 
heritage institutions, practitioners, and academics alike. We have seen a growing 
literature on the importance of community participation emerge in different areas 
of 21st-century heritage studies (e.g., Chitty 2017; Hawke, Onciul, and Stefano 
2017; Hodges and Watson 2000; Lea and Thomas 2014; Rodenberg, Wagenaar, and 
Burgers 2023; Roued-Cunliffe and Copeland 2017). 
The increased interest in participatory approaches reflects the growing impor -
tance of the social dimension of cultural heritage among heritage practitioners, 
researchers, and policymakers ( SoPHIA 2020, 11). This has led to a recognition 
of the importance and benefits of cultural heritage in terms of individual and soci -
etal well-being and the promotion of several key liberal democratic values, includ-
ing cultural diversity, inclusion, dialogue, exchange, and cooperation. The social 
dimension of heritage is also the central foundation of critical heritage studies. 
Rather than heritage objects or sites as such, critical heritage scholars have been 
interested in the effects of heritage on communities and societies (Harrison 2013; 
Smith 2006; Whitehead et al. 2019). Such studies have approached heritage as a so-
cial performance of cultural meaning-making and as a cultural practice of regulat-
ing, controlling, and mediating cultural and historical values and narratives (Smith 
2006; Waterton and Smith 2009). Drawing on its social foundations, critical herit-
age scholars have noted that heritage is always intertwined with the negotiation of 
identities, belonging, inclusion, and exclusion ( Harrison 2013; Lähdesmäki et al. 
2020; Lähdesmäki, Zhu, and Thomas 2019 ; Waterton and Smith 2009 ). Such a 
social dimension has contributed to heritage being perceived not only as a thing 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-1 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
  
2 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
of the past but also as a phenomenon of the present and a tool for influencing and 
building the future (Harrison 2013). This social grounding and future orientation 
link the participatory turn and critical heritage studies. 
In this introductory chapter, we map out different meanings and modes of her -
itage participation, trace the routes through which participation and community 
engagement have entered into the heritage sector, and examine earlier literature on 
different kinds of typologies that have been created to evaluate the level of involve-
ment and agency of different forms of participation. Secondly, we present a more 
holistic model of heritage participation that can assist in analysing the different 
forms that heritage participation and community engagement can take. Finally, we 
introduce the chapters included in this volume. 
Heritage participation: Daily engagement, institutional practice, 
and political right 
In the context of heritage, participation can mean many things. In the broadest 
sense, participation and community engagement can be understood as an inherent 
part of heritage. Community participation keeps heritage alive through the daily 
cultural practices of community members. This performative heritage participation 
can be connected to intangible or tangible forms of heritage. It is often mundane, 
ranging from the language we speak to participation in communal celebrations, the 
use of traditional crafts or skills, or simply the communal maintenance of built her-
itage that surrounds us. This broadest form of engagement can also be conceived 
as a form of heritage consumption: visiting heritage sites or festivals, listening 
to music or reading books, buying and consuming heritage goods and crafts, or 
engaging in other heritage-related activities, such as bathing in a sauna or playing 
historical video games. Although rarely actively acknowledged, such participation 
plays a central role in maintaining cultural cohesion and supporting individual and 
communal sense of belonging and well-being. 
More often, however, heritage participation is understood as a process where 
individuals or communities participate in heritage practice in collaboration or in 
response to an initiative by heritage institutions (e.g., Graham 2012). Accordingly, 
participation can be understood in practical terms as the “active involvement of 
stakeholders within a range of processes and projects” (Neal 2015, 346). Similar to 
recent heritage policies that have perceived heritage as a resource that can be used 
to support varied aims (e.g., EC 2019), participation can be seen as a practical tool 
used to promote citizen engagement in institutionalised heritage practice, govern-
ance, and management (e.g., Halme et al. 2018; Neal 2015; Rodenberg et al. 2023). 
Participatory projects provide a means for heritage institutions to invite non-
professional heritage stakeholders to take part in activities organised by official 
heritage authorities and, through that participation, contribute to varied activities, 
like communal memory work designed to voice out marginalised experiences 
(e.g., Alivizatou 2022), volunteer labour at archaeological digs (e.g., Neal 2015; 
Smith and Waterton 2009) or in conservation work (e.g., Chitty 2017), or digital 
crowdsourcing aimed at democratising data collection (e.g., Bonacchi et al. 2019; 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
Introduction 3 
Seitsonen 2017). Such participation can be deeply meaningful and empowering 
for communities, but especially top-down-initiated institutional forms of participa-
tion are not always experienced in a positive light by the communities. Instead, 
heritage participation “also comes to perform as a governance instrument by which 
individual behaviour is shaped and directed by governmental policy and associated 
institutions” (Neal 2015, 346). 
From the perspective of communities, participation can also be understood as 
a political right that either individuals or communities hold to both material and 
epistemological ownership of their heritage (e.g., Eichler 2021; Settimini 2020). 
These calls for wider participation have also been connected with inclusionary 
desires to support and create space for multivocal and pluralistic heritage narra -
tives and experiences, highlighting the democratic and human rights dimensions of 
participation. Interestingly, similar claims are often advanced by both bottom-up 
social movements and top-down heritage policies. In this context, heritage partici-
pation is often proposed as a catalyst for individual and community agency and 
empowerment – especially for marginalised communities. It can be used to pro -
mote the belonging of communities that feel left out of official heritage practices, 
address historical injustices, and support social justice claims, as well as increase 
overall public involvement. However, as some chapters in this volume show, from 
the institution’s perspective, all community participation is not always welcomed. 
Participation can also take unwanted forms, provoke societal tensions, and fuel 
heritage institutions’ contradictory “desire to include and a hesitancy to surrender 
or reduce [their] authority” (Smith 2006, 38). 
Perceiving heritage engagement and participation from a holistic, multidimen -
sional perspective acknowledges the varied understandings and motivations that 
different stakeholders, groups, and people hold and seeks to move from “a norma-
tive, ideal-state-seeking absoluteness” towards “situation-specific, practical solu -
tions” (Vahtikari, Kivilaakso, and Latvala-Harvilahti 2020, 11). Different forms and 
modes of participation address different kinds of needs, invoke different types of 
communities, and create different forms of individual, communal, and institutional 
agencies. As the chapters in this volume demonstrate, a holistic understanding of 
heritage participation that is mindful of diverse communal, political, and manage -
rial modes of participation can help us understand how participation contributes to 
negotiations on the social and societal role of heritage. As the authors in this volume 
show, such a holistic approach to heritage participation is the key to unlocking the 
transformative potential or future-oriented impetus of heritage (see Harrison 2013; 
Harrison et al. 2020; Lähdesmäki et al. 2020). 
Political and authorised roots of heritage participation 
and community engagement 
Scholars have identified two key epistemological impulses linking participatory 
approaches and the social dimension of heritage. The first impulse comes from 
the development of the political sphere in liberal democracies, which emphasises 
“participatory democracy as both complementary to representative democracy 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
4 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
and as a tool to achieve inclusive and social sustainable development actions” 
(Colomer and Pastor Pérez 2024 , 81; see also Fung 2015). The ideas and goals 
of citizen participation in decision-making processes in different sectors of so -
ciety began to gain ground in Western countries as early as the 1960s and 1970s 
(Bherer, Dufour, and Montambeault 2016; Cornwall 2008), and since then partici-
patory policies and strategies have found their way into most policy areas, includ -
ing cultural heritage ( Cortés-Vázquez, Jiménez-Esquinas, and Sánchez-Carretero 
2017; György et al. 2019 ). However, it is only since the turn of the millennium 
that participatory governance practices have been taken more seriously in the her -
itage field ( György et al. 2019 ). During the 2000s, the participatory emphasis 
has been included in international heritage conventions, such as the UNESCO 
Convention for the Safeguarding of the Intangible Cultural Heritage (2003), EU 
policies, such as the European Framework for Action on Cultural Heritage (2014), 
and numerous national heritage agendas and local strategies that guide work at 
heritage sites and museums. 
In heritage policy discourses, participation typically emerges as a form of ac -
tive citizenship (see Mäkinen 2021) as well as a normative discourse seeking to 
promote the participation and inclusion of (disenfranchised) individuals and com-
munities (e.g., Council of Europe 2005). These same motivations are echoed in 
the managerial discourses that highlight the need for heritage institutions to adopt 
practices that would enable communities to better participate in the planning, 
decision-making, implementation, and management of heritage and memory in -
stitutions’ activities (e.g., Colomer and Pastor Pérez 2024 , 82; Lähdesmäki and 
Mäkinen 2019, 45). 
These policy developments also reflect the way in which participation has 
become a central theme or buzzword in the museum field. Since the 1960s and 
1970s, museums have faced radical changes and pressures to rethink the institu -
tion. The adoption of “new museology” (see Vergo 1989) led to a gradual shift 
from collection and preservation-based activities towards increasing attention to 
the engagement of audiences and communities (e.g., Simon 2010). By promot -
ing the active involvement of citizens and other stakeholders in various heritage 
processes, museums and heritage actors have also sought to resolve the continu -
ing legacies of their troubled histories. Museums and other heritage actors have 
become more adaptive to societal tensions and are increasingly willing to engage 
in combating social inequality and prejudice (see Janes and Sandell 2019 ; Lynch 
and Alberti 2010). 
The second impulse linking participatory approaches and the social dimension 
of heritage builds on the emergence of a critical turn in scholarship that challenges 
the hegemony of Western heritage knowledge production, notions of heritage, and 
centralised expert-based approaches to heritage (Cortés-Vázquez et al. 2017; Co-
lomer and Pastor Pérez 2024), which Smith (2006) refers to as the “authorised her-
itage discourse” (AHD). Smith (2006, 31) coined the concept to critically dissect a 
hegemonic, expert-led, managerial discourse that has been used to hide the politi -
cal nature of heritage behind a seemingly apolitical technocratic façade (see also 
Gnecco 2015, 265). Participation and community engagement have been proposed 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Introduction 5 
as the antidote against these top-down, expert-led heritage processes and narratives 
and as a way to balance the prominent role of technocratic knowledge and profes-
sionalism through sharing power and agency to communities and citizens – at least 
on a discursive and symbolic level. 
The AHDs are ontologically against participation – particularly if we understand 
the act of sharing power (see Arnstein 1969) as a central component of participa -
tion. In practice, the AHD can still address participation, but its approach to non-
professional agencies is limited. By relying on expert knowledge, the AHD and its 
users perceive heritage as “something visitors are led to, are instructed about, but 
are then not invited to engage with more actively” (Smith 2006, 31). 
Countering the authorised, expert-driven tendencies of participatory processes 
requires challenging the political neutrality often associated with heritage insti -
tutions. While museums’ and heritage actors’ attempts to support subaltern or 
otherwise disenfranchised communities are often perceived as overtly political, 
numerous scholars have sought to break the myth of neutrality that has often 
been connected with traditional museum and heritage work (e.g., Hewison 1987; 
Janes 2015; Katriel 1993; Raicovich 2021 ; Sandell 2007). Heritage participation 
adds a new twist to this debate. As Smith (2006, 38) points out, the inclusion and 
participation of subaltern or other disenfranchised groups and their experiences 
and knowledge shatters “the ideologies of the impartiality of expertise” that is 
incremental for the AHDs (see also Turunen 2025). Moreover, it highlights the 
discrepancy “between a desire to include and a hesitancy to surrender or reduce 
the authority of both the AHD and the heritage practitioners to wield it” ( Smith 
2006, 38). 
Epistemologies and typologies of participation 
Since the 1960s, several scholars have theorised citizen participation and devel -
oped various typologies and models that classify different mechanisms of partici -
pation from passive to active or from unwanted to successful (e.g., Arnstein 1969; 
Cornwall 2008; IAP2 2018; Pretty 1995). The key dimension of such typologies is 
to understand citizen participation in terms of citizen power: 
It is the strategy by which the have-nots join in determining how information 
is shared, goals and policies are set, tax resources are allocated, programs are 
operated and benefits like contracts and patronage are parceled out. 
(Arnstein 1969, 216) 
In different typologies and models, participation evolves through different 
stages from non-participation or minimal participation, where citizens are seen 
as a passive mass influenced by the manipulative leadership of those in power, 
to citizen power or citizen engagement, characterised by citizens’ active agency 
in common affairs, resulting in various positive outcomes for them. For Arnstein 
(1969), for instance, these stages (or ladders in her own words) include manipu -
lation, therapy, informing, consultation, placation, partnership, delegated power, 

 6 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
and citizen control. For Pretty (1995), participation develops through the follow-
ing stages: manipulative participation, passive participation, participation by con-
sultation, participation for material incentives, functional participation, interactive 
participation, and self-mobilisation. In the International Association for Public 
Participation’s typology, at the lowest level, subjects are informed, then consulted, 
then involved, then collaborate with subjects, and finally empowered (IAP2 2018). 
In these typologies, as in many others, participation is seen as a one-dimensional 
trajectory, focusing narrowly on agency and the expected roles that citizens can 
play in activities that different authorities seek to govern. 
Several scholars have critiqued typologies of participation, noting how they are 
normatively biased towards a single notion of participation and asking what par -
ticipation ultimately means (Bishop and Davis 2002; Cornwall 2008; Dean 2016). 
As Colomer and Pastor Pérez (2024, 81) rightly note: 
Participation as a concept is loaded with diverse semantic significances 
open to interpretation and, consequently, a diversity of praxis. It can mean 
anything from civic engagement (as a synonym of “taking part”) to public 
participation in decision-making (as a synonym of “citizen control”), from 
deliberative processes in consultive mechanisms of a project to direct in-
volvement in decisions on public affairs. 
Participation has also been extensively researched in the museum sector over 
the past decade. One of the most referenced typologies of participation in this con-
text is Simon’s (2010) research on project implementation in museums. She iden-
tifies four overlapping and flexible modes of public participation, with varying 
degrees of top-down or bottom-up emphasis. These modes include contributory 
projects, in which visitors have limited input into institutionally controlled pro-
cesses; collaborative projects, in which visitors are invited to be active partners 
in institutionally controlled processes; co-creative projects, in which community 
members and an institution work together from the outset based on community 
interests; and hosted projects, in which an institution makes available some of its 
facilities and/or resources to present programmes developed and implemented by 
communities or visitors/audiences (Simon 2010, Chapter 5; see also György et al. 
2019, 16–17). All these modes involve the agency of both the museum institution 
and the visitors or communities. 
The European Commission’s (2018, 43) report on participatory governance of 
cultural heritage also highlights the importance of combined top-down and bottom-
up approaches, noting: 
Experience from best practice examples has shown that the bottom-up ap -
proach should not be considered as an alternative, or in opposition, to top-
down approaches from national and/or regional authorities. Better overall 
results are achieved by combining the two approaches so that they interact 
with each other. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Introduction 7 
Although the key epistemological impulse for a participatory approach to cul -
tural heritage is based on a critique of the AHD and its expert-led heritage work, 
citizen participation does not occur simply by dismantling the experts. Heritage 
professionals have a crucial role to play as facilitators of participation. 
 Afour-fieldmatrixofheritageparticipationandcommunity 
engagement 
Based on the case studies examined in this volume, we argue that heritage par -
ticipation cannot be simplified into a step-by-step model that moves from non-
participation to full participation based on the different forms of agency at each 
step. Instead, heritage participation needs to be considered in terms of different 
modalities that explain the quality of participation in more detail, as the chapters in 
this volume demonstrate. 
Figure 0.1 illustrates heritage participation in a four-field matrix, highlight -
ing how it can simultaneously encompass opposing modalities. The axes of the 
matrix reflect the key contexts of participation: the power relations involved in 
heritage as an institutionalised practice (vertical axis) and the orientation of herit -
age work as either a conservationist or a transformationist practice (horizontal 
axis). Moving along the vertical axis, heritage participation can be based on both 
citizen-led and institution-led initiatives. When the initiation of such activities is 
balanced (the middle of the axis), heritage participation is based on shared owner-
ship, power, and agency over heritage. The horizontal axis varies between herit -
age work that supports the status quo and its unprogressive norms, order, and 
values in the heritage field and in society more broadly, and heritage work that 
seeks to radically change them. When such goals are balanced (the middle of the 
axis), heritage work is based on inclusive and dialogical co-development between 
different professional and non-professional actors. 
In addition, the four-field matrix illustrates the quality of heritage participation 
in its quadrants as one moves along the axis. Participation based on institution-led 
practices and support for the status quo characterises the AHD and its notion of her-
itage work. But citizen-led practices can also support the norms and values of the 
AHD. Such participation characterises many local heritage associations and other 
active heritage enthusiasts, such as metal detectorists, who are interested in the past 
and who are concerned with and preserve heritage objects. Heritage participation 
can also be oriented towards the future through more or less radical change. In such 
institution-led practices, heritage professionals seek to influence future societies by 
facilitating heritage work through participatory methods. Heritage activism char -
acterises the final quadrant of the matrix. The goals and means of heritage activism 
vary, but it is typically a citizen-led practice that seeks change that disrupts the 
status quo and its unprogressive attitudes and values. 
Figure 0.1 enables heritage practitioners, researchers, and policy makers to as-
sess and gain a deeper understanding of the participatory nature of a project, exhi-
bition, event, or other activity at a heritage site. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
8 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Figure 0.1 Modalities of heritage participation. Copyright: Tuuli Lähdesmäki and Johanna 
Turunen. 
The structure of the volume 
The volume explores heritage participation and community engagement through 
15 case studies, organised into three thematic sections focusing on inclusion and 
social equality in heritage practices, the role of digital tools in heritage participa -
tion, and participatory management and governance of cultural heritage. Through 
these three overlapping perspectives, which are discussed in all sections but ex -
plored in more detail in each thematic section, the volume also contributes to 
emerging debates about the pluralisation of spaces in which heritage participation 
and community engagement take place – from official and institutionalised to 
community, activist, and digital spaces. The authors, who have multidisciplinary 
backgrounds in the humanities and social sciences, have adopted a critical ap -
proach to their case studies, examining both the successes and challenges of par -
ticipation and community engagement. 
Heritage participation and social inclusion 
For many heritage practitioners and scholars, heritage sites and museums are po -
tential platforms for social change and activism, advocating for greater representa-
tion, equality, social justice, moral responsibility, and empowerment of vulnerable 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Introduction 9 
groups in society (e.g., Giglitto et al. 2023; Hayhoe 2019; Janes and Sandell 2019; 
Morse 2020; Peters et al. 2020 ; Sandell 2017; Sandell and Nightingale 2012). As 
the chapters in this section show, individual and communal heritage engagement 
and different associated meanings and practices, however, are often negotiated also 
outside these institutions. 
Johanna Turunen opens the section by examining the contradictions that ex -
ist between hegemonic participatory discourses and the views and experiences 
of heritage actors, activists, and community members engaged in participatory 
processes. Through an analysis of a series of panel discussions, Turunen high -
lights routes towards more inclusive practices and meanings around participa -
tion. The two following chapters focus on heritage festivals as venues of heritage 
participation, social integration, and cultural inclusion. Combining quantitative 
and qualitative approaches, Alin Croitoru and Ionela Vlase focus their attention 
on Roma cultural festivals in Romania in an attempt to detangle the ways lega -
cies of racism and discrimination that Roma communities have faced continue 
to be reflected in the public perception of Roma festivals. Raluca Muresan, on 
the other hand, focuses on the festival organisers’ perspectives on inclusion and 
the practical and societal factors that limit and enable the inclusion of different 
groups in cultural festivals. Both chapters show how societal cultural norms and 
attitudes, local histories, and concrete resources contour participatory processes 
even in cases when festival organisers explicitly seek to circumvent existing 
power hierarchies. 
In the fourth chapter, Ovio Olaru focuses on the use of Saxon heritage in the 
branding of the city of Sibiu located in the formerly German-speaking region 
in Transylvania, Romania. Through rich cultural examples, Olaru shows how, 
despite having a rather modest German population today, German cultural sym -
bols continue to have a hegemonic position in the cultural landscape of Sibiu. 
In Chapter 5 , Anna Talasniemi examines the inclusive potential of Sauna Dia -
logues, a novel participatory, future-oriented research method she has developed 
to examine the meanings sauna holds in Finnish culture. Acknowledged as part 
of the country’s intangible heritage, the Finnish sauna is often perceived as an 
egalitarian space. However, many groups feel excluded from public saunas. The 
Sauna Dialogues offer a space for these communities to envision a more inclusive 
sauna culture but also offer heritage practitioners valuable insights into organis -
ing heritage-related participatory processes. The final chapter in this section by 
María-Dolores Pitarch-Garrido, Ester Alba-Pagán, and Miguel Requena-Jiménez 
is focused on the ongoing heritagisation process around the city of Sagunt, Spain. 
The city has a large number of historical remains, and the authors investigate the 
role of the University of Valencia and local civil society organisations in ensur -
ing these remains are conserved and in preparing for an application for UNESCO 
World Heritage status. 
The chapters highlight the need to approach heritage participation and engage -
ment from a holistic perspective, examining the role that non-heritage institutions, 
cultural actors and events, public views, and individual citizens have in setting the 
agendas, ramifications, and practices of participatory processes. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
10 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Digital tools in heritage participation 
In the 2000s, participation and community engagement in cultural heritage un -
derwent a transformative technological shift, driven by the advent of digitization 
and digital tools. Various studies (e.g., Giaccardi 2012; Giglitto et al. 2023; King, 
Stark, and Cooke 2016; Roued-Cunliffe and Copeland 2017; Stuedahl 2009) have 
demonstrated that these advancements have not only enabled new forms of in -
teraction between communities and their cultural heritage but have also opened 
pathways for democratising heritage practices and processes. Digital platforms 
and spaces facilitate broader participation, empowering individuals and groups 
to contribute to the preservation and interpretation of their cultural narratives in 
unprecedented ways. However, while these technologies present exciting oppor -
tunities for enhancing our understanding of cultural heritage, they also introduce 
a range of challenges that must be critically examined. The second section of 
this volume delves into this topic, exploring the use of digital tools in heritage 
participation and discussing the potentials and challenges of digital platforms and 
games in increasing access, inclusion, and understanding of cultural heritage and 
heritage communities. Additionally, the section showcases unexpected and under-
researched ways in which digital technologies are transforming how individuals 
and communities engage with heritage. 
Andrei Terian and Ştefan Baghiu begin the section by discussing the preserva-
tion of literary heritage through digitisation. The chapter examines the dichotomy 
between preserving this heritage, specifically, restoring the original versions of 
texts, and presenting it, which involves making the texts accessible to contem -
porary readers. The discussion is grounded in the authors’ curation of the Digital 
Museum of the Romanian Novel, a collection of over 1,300 novels from the pe -
riod 1845–1947, created through the involvement of the Romanian philological 
community, particularly students, in restoring and revising the corpus while mak-
ing strategic decisions regarding the graphic updating of the texts. In the second 
chapter, Khaoula Stiti addresses the challenges of developing participatory herit -
age platforms in Global South contexts. Drawing on case studies from Tunisia 
and Belgium, the chapter proposes the conception of decolonial computing aimed 
at dismantling colonial biases in technological development and promoting fairer 
knowledge exchange and community engagement. 
Next, Kenza Gana and Renaud Garcia-Bardidia explore how historical video 
games are playing an increasingly significant role in the global gaming industry. 
Using Assassin’s Creed as a case study, the authors discuss how historical video 
games construct various historical eras, cultures, and architectural styles, as well 
as how this representation is received by the gaming community. The empirical 
data gathered from players highlight their individual and collective engagement 
with the cultural heritage created and transmitted by the game. The section con -
cludes with a chapter by Jiyun Zhang, who investigates the creation and dissemi -
nation of animated short films focused on the intangible cultural heritage of the 
Orochen people, an ethic group living in Northeast China. This chapter discusses 
the potential of digital media for safeguarding the intangible cultural heritage of 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Introduction 11 
minorities and marginalised groups while emphasising the importance of strength-
ening capacity-building efforts through the education of media professionals and 
the promotion of creative industries. The author treats animated films as boundary 
objects, providing a means to reactivate archived materials, form new cultural con-
nections, and perform new roles for such materials in the dual tasks of promoting 
cultural diversity and safeguarding intangible cultural heritage. 
Managing heritage participation 
The participatory turn in the heritage sector fosters a sense of ownership and shared 
responsibility, emphasising cultural heritage as a collective asset that requires co-
management by the communities it represents. At the policy and management level, 
this shift is reflected in efforts to actively involve communities, stakeholders, and 
the public in decision-making processes, moving away from traditional top-down 
approaches towards more inclusive and collaborative models where diverse voices 
contribute to heritage management (Halme et al. 2018; Neal 2015; Rodenberg et al. 
2023). However, the democratisation of cultural heritage processes is rarely without 
challenges. The third section of this volume examines both the opportunities and dif-
ficulties of managing heritage participation and community engagement. It explores 
how partnerships between the private and public sectors in heritage ecosystems, par-
ticipatory heritage governance, participatory approaches in tourism, and collabora-
tive heritage preservation have been implemented in practice – and with what effects. 
This section begins with a chapter by Tuuli Lähdesmäki, who examines par -
ticipation and community engagement in the intangible cultural heritage of equal 
education at the Seminaarinmäki Campus in Finland, focusing on how such 
participation is managed at the grassroots level. Drawing on empirical data, the 
chapter highlights challenges to participation, including internalised managerial hi-
erarchies in heritage work and differing conceptions of heritage and participation. 
Lähdesmäki concludes that effective participatory heritage governance requires 
competent managing bodies, structured coordination, facilitation, participatory 
methods, and sufficient resources to support these activities. The section contin -
ues with a chapter by Renaud Garcia-Bardidia, Pénélope Girard, and Mathieu 
Viau-Courville, which explores the role of private-sector partners, such as consult-
ants and experts in the creative industries, within the museum sector. Using data 
from France, the authors illustrate how the increasing presence of these actors in 
museums has reshaped the traditional relationship between publicly funded cul -
tural institutions and for-profit organisations. This shift challenges conventional 
museum management models while simultaneously strengthening local cultural 
ecosystems. Following this, Mathieu Viau-Courville presents ethnographic case 
studies of museums in Latin America and Europe that have transformed traditional 
curatorial practices to become more community-responsive and creative institu -
tions, aiming to foster community development, social justice, and inclusion. The 
chapter introduces the concept of managerial curatorship, a model that promotes an 
organic, team-based network approach to managing museum and heritage projects, 
driven by creativity and social impact. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
12 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
The fourth chapter in this section, written by Daniela Stanciu-Păscărița and Sorin 
Radu, takes a historical perspective on community engagement. It examines citi -
zens’ involvement in preserving architectural heritage in Sibiu, Romania, focusing 
on a civic association founded for this purpose in 1879, initially led by the city’s 
Saxon majority. The chapter highlights how, despite changes in Romania’s political 
structures, heritage management policies, and the city’s demographics, civic en -
gagement in preserving tangible cultural heritage has remained consistent. The sec-
tion concludes with a chapter by Suzie Thomas, who explores participatory heritage 
approaches in sites that can be described as dark heritage – places associated with 
suffering, loss, and past atrocities. Through examples from Belgium, Finland, and 
the Marshall Islands, Thomas argues that engaging communities with controversial 
heritage can have social benefits while also raising important ethical considerations. 
Finally… 
This volume highlights the crucial role of heritage participation in fostering inclu-
sivity and engaging diverse voices, communities, and stakeholders in the valori -
sation and decision-making processes of cultural heritage. The empowerment of 
individuals and communities cultivates a sense of ownership, recognising them 
as active contributors to the preservation and interpretation of cultural heritage. 
Inclusion and empowerment play a key role in democratising heritage processes, 
shifting from top-down approaches to more collaborative and transparent govern-
ance models. This evolution enhances cultural diversity by acknowledging and 
preserving various expressions and practices, ensuring a more comprehensive and 
representative understanding of heritage. 
While heritage participation offers significant benefits, this volume also ex-
amines the challenges it presents. These include difficulties in coordinating com-
munity engagement and communicating the meanings of heritage, as different 
stakeholders often may have competing interests and priorities. Striking a balance 
between expert knowledge and local or community perspectives is both essential 
and complex, requiring mechanisms to integrate diverse forms of expertise. Al -
though digital tools create new opportunities for participation, the digital divide 
can hinder inclusivity, excluding those without access to technology and potentially 
marginalising certain communities. Additionally, heritage participation initiatives 
often begin as short-term projects, but long-term commitment and sustained com -
munity involvement are necessary to ensure their lasting impact. Finally, meaning-
ful community engagement in cultural heritage requires adequate resources and 
institutional support to foster genuine collaboration and long-term sustainability. 
References 
Alivizatou, Marilena. 2022. Intangible Heritage and Participation: An International Inves-
