Section I 
Heritage participation and 
social inclusion 



 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
1 Looking beyond recited truths 
of heritage participation 
Inclusive perspectives on institutions, 
epistemologies, and representation 
Johanna Turunen 
Participation has been one of the most repeated buzzwords across different policy 
sectors for the past decades. While participatory practices have raised some critique 
(e.g. Neal 2015; Colomer and Pastor Pérez 2024 ), the notion that participation it -
self – if done correctly – is a desirable outcome continues to contour much of the 
debate. As a result, participatory approaches have been integrated rather uncritically 
into practically all dimensions of heritage practice, at least on the level of normative 
discourses. 
This has led to a peculiar situation. Although participation was initially proposed 
as an antidote against the expert-driven, top-down managerial heritage complex, 
the widespread policy pressure to integrate participatory activities has resulted in 
the wide adoption of participatory discourses within heritage management. This has 
resulted in different, previously disenfranchised individuals, communities, organi-
sations, and other non-professional heritage stakeholders having more access to of-
ficial heritage processes; however, these authorised participatory processes often  
result in a tokenistic process of participation that fails to grant the individuals and 
communities it seeks to engage any actual power or agency (cf. Arnstein 1969). 
As such, there has been an emergence of an authorised understanding of heritage 
participation (Pastor Pérez et al. 2021; see also Smith 2006) that hides the “contra-
diction between the implementation of participation and its associated ideal state” 
(Vahtikari, Kivilaakso, and Latvala-Harvilahti 2020, 11). 
This chapter is an attempt to look beyond the “recited truths” (see Lentin and Ti-
tley 2011; see also De Certeau 1984) of heritage participation through an empirical 
analysis of a series of panel discussions organised as part of the Whose Heritage? 
seminar in Helsinki, Finland. By revealing the contrasts that manifest behind the 
authorised forms of heritage participation, the chapter attempts to identify avenues 
towards more inclusive discourses and practices of heritage participation. 
Whose Heritage? Empirical data and background 
This chapter is empirically grounded in analysing a series of panel discussions 
held at the Whose Heritage?  seminar organised in November 2021 at the Na -
tional Museum of Finland. The two-day seminar was co-organised by the Finnish 
Heritage Agency, the Culture for All Service, and the HERIDI1 research project – 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-3 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
20 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
where I worked at the time. The core organisation group responsible for the 
seminar consisted of Satu Kähkönen, a senior advisor from the Finnish Heritage 
Agency; Rita Paqvalén, the executive director of the Culture for All Service; Tuuli 
Lähdesmäki, an associate professor from the University of Jyväskylä and leader 
of the HERIDI project; and me, a postdoctoral researcher working in the same 
project. The aim of the seminar was to engage heritage actors from varied back -
grounds and perspectives in dialogue around topics such as alternative meanings 
and narratives, contestations over ownership and power, and potentials of partici -
pation and dialogue. The event was successful in many senses. It attracted over 
400 participants and resulted in collaborations that resulted, for example, in the 
publication of an edited volume based on the seminar ( Lähdesmäki et al. 2024). 
The panel discussions analysed in this chapter were organised on the second 
day of the seminar. The three panels were organised under three guiding ques-
tions: what is heritage, who owns heritage, and how do participation and inclusion 
around heritage take place? The three panels included a total of 14 professionals, 
practitioners, managers, and experts from cultural and heritage sectors, as well as 
academic researchers from the field of heritage and memory studies. 
While I do not wish to label or typecast the panellists into any pre-determined 
role, giving some background to the panellists and their “locus of enunciation” 
(Mignolo 2007; see also Harding 1993) is needed. Locus of enunciation refers to 
the difference between “epistemic location” and “social location”. However, as 
Grosfoguel (2007, 213) explains: 
The fact that one is socially located on the oppressed side of power relations, 
does not automatically mean that he/she is epistemically thinking from a 
subaltern epistemic location. 
I believe the same applies vice versa – a privileged social location does not auto-
matically mean one is epistemically speaking and thinking from a privileged posi-
tion. Many of the panellists also slip between different categories. When selecting 
panellists, we paid attention to both institutional diversity (including also panellists 
with no institutional connections to heritage) and cultural and social diversity. Many 
panellists come from different kinds of minority backgrounds. As such, some panel-
lists are bending or blurring the boundaries of easy categorisations. While they may 
work in privileged positions, they come from marginalised backgrounds and are 
epistemologically positioned in support of subaltern voices. 
The first panel was chaired by Eeva-Kristiina Harlin, a project manager at the Sámi
Museum Siida, and included three panellists: Áile Aikio, a Sámi museum scholar and 
practitioner based at the University of Lapland; Riikka Taavetti, senior lecturer spe-
cialised on queer history based at the University of Turku (at the time University of 
Helsinki) and an active member of Friends of Queer History Association; and Cátia 
Suomalainen Pedrosa, the director of Cultural Centre Caisa, an international cultural 
space located in Helsinki. Suomalainen Pedrosa was born in Portugal but has lived 
her adult life in Finland. The second panel was chaired by Susanna Pettersson, (at 
the time) the director general of Nationalmuseum in Sweden, with three panellists: 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Looking beyond Recited Truths of Heritage Participation 21 
Julian Owusu, a Finnish-Ghanaian hip-hop dancer and teacher; Elina Anttila, the 
director general of the National Museum of Finland; and Marko Stenroos, a Roma 
scholar and advocate working as the senior planning officer at the Finnish Institute 
for Health and Welfare. The third panel was chaired by Martina Marti, a diversity 
specialist, theatre director, producer, and artist with a Swiss background. The panel-
lists were Elisa Sarpo, the community manager at the National Museum of Finland; 
Sari Salovaara, a senior expert at the Culture for All, specialised in the cultural rights 
of disabled communities; and Kari Huuskonen, museum curator at the Museum of 
Visual Impairment. Both Salovaara and Huuskonen are disabled. 
The empirical data consists of a total of 5:12:44 hours of video recordings of 
panel discussions that are available on the National Museum of Finland’s YouTube 
channel. I analyse these verbal texts through directed conceptual analysis (see Hsieh 
and Shannon 2005). I have translated all panel quotations used in this chapter from 
Finnish to English. I use the concept of “recited truths” (see Lentin and Titley 2011; 
see also De Certeau 1984) and “inclusive heritage discourses” (Kisić 2016, 2018) 
as guiding analytical tools. Recited truths are hegemonic phrases and discourses – 
often promoted through policy discourses – that hide “the gap between their implied 
content […] and empirical realities of many of those who are affected by it” ( Tu-
runen 2023, 400). By revealing this contrast that manifests in the authorised forms 
of heritage participation, I seek to identify more inclusive discourses of heritage 
participation. Here, I draw on Višnja Kisić (2016, 2018), who has proposed that 
inclusive heritage discourse is not about inclusion in the usual sense of the 
word. It does not imply access by diverse groups to the unchallenged ways of 
doing heritage, nor does it imply that different groups could be unified under 
one hegemonic heritage umbrella. On the contrary, it is inclusive because 
it recognises diverse notions of heritage and accepts the diversity of under -
standings, visions, interpretations and uses of heritage(s) by diverse actors. 
(2018, 134) 
In the following empirical analysis, I focus particularly on emerging forms of 
meaning-making that are created around participation and inclusion behind overt 
participatory discourses. The analysis is structured around three themes: (1) the 
emotional politics involved in heritage participation especially in the context of 
heritage institutions, (2) the knowledge and skills that participation requires, and 
finally, (3) the potential that the production of more inclusive narratives and repre-
sentations can have both inside museums and in the society at large. 
Emotional politics in institutionalised modes of participation 
Unsurprisingly, a big portion of the discussion on participation and inclusion was 
focused on the difficulties and possibilities that are linked with institutions. I will 
start with a quote from Sari Salovaara, a senior expert at Culture for All, that en-
capsulates a lot of the frustrations and emotions that emerge from the authorised 
tendencies that many participatory processes contain. Salovaara has participated in 

 
 
22 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
various participatory processes around the heritage of disabled communities – both 
as a disabled person and as an equity and accessibility expert. 
There are many kinds of participation, and also many frustrating things are 
done in the name of participation. We may talk about participation, but in 
reality, there is no real opportunity to influence anything. Perhaps the greatest 
feelings of frustration emerge when a process is dressed up as participation, 
but you are invited as an expert into a table that is already set. […] Your 
inclusion is just a way to tick a box. […] That is where the feeling of dispos-
session emerges, that burning feeling that is the opposite of what was aimed 
at. That creates the anger that we hear and see a lot in this society. 
The kind of participatory projects described by Salovaara exemplify processes 
that draw on participatory discourses – in line with current national and interna -
tional policy frameworks – but fail to create genuine possibilities for invited com-
munities or experts to influence the process (cf. Arnstein 1969). Salovaara’s words 
exemplify the discrepancy “between a desire to include and a hesitancy to sur -
render or reduce the authority of both the [authorised heritage discourse] and the 
heritage practitioners to wield it” (Smith 2006, 38). To some extent, the problem 
is structural and rooted in the very policies and funding systems that have been 
set up to resolve it. As long as participation is perceived as a project – temporary, 
sporadic, and often externally funded – it will lack the necessary power to change 
the underlying structures of the system. At the same time, each project lays the 
foundation for the next one, keeping the system moving. Helen Graham (2012) 
has identified this cyclical counter-hegemonic process that takes place in muse-
ums and heritage institutions already over a decade ago. As she explains, museums 
and other heritage actors have become more adaptive to societal tensions and are 
increasingly willing to engage in combating social inequality and prejudice (see 
also Janes and Sandell 2019; Lynch and Alberti 2010). They engage in participa -
tory projects, which is “followed by the optimism for an always-yet-to-be-realised 
community co-production practice” which would resolve the structural problems 
related to the ways museums engage different communities and stakeholders (Gra-
ham 2012, 566). When museums cannot scale up the changes achieved in (often 
project-based) micro-level dynamics and fail to transform broader power dynamics 
in the museums or in society at large, the cycle starts again (Graham 2012, 568). 
Cátia Suomalainen Pedrosa pointed out in the panel that the cultural sector 
needs to be more self-critical and move from superficial “quick wins” towards 
deeper transformations that would also address more difficult questions around 
privileges and power. As the director of Cultural Centre Caisa, a Helsinki-based 
international meeting space that seeks to promote diversity, community, and soli -
darity through art and culture, she works to overcome institutionalised challenges 
around inclusion on a daily basis. As she explains, 
We have opened our eyes and reached a point where there is a lot of discus-
sion about these issues. But we only seem to resolve these issues or search 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Looking beyond Recited Truths of Heritage Participation 23 
for “quick wins” to check a box and say we have done this. I don’t think we 
are ready to examine our own privileges, our structures and our weaknesses 
so that we can genuinely start to build something good and better for all, 
something more equal. 
One way to create spaces that would be more welcoming to alternative per -
spectives has been to create new institutions where different communities get to 
decide on practices from their own perspective. Suomalainen Pedrosa commented 
on this desire to simultaneously critique institutions and create a museum or cul -
tural centre of one’s own from a more critical perspective. As she expands, such 
processes of “building new walls” fail to address the initial problem: the fact that 
we have institutions that are inaccessible or unwelcoming to a variety of different 
groups. As she continues, attempting to fix this problem by splitting people into 
more boxes “only serves the majority population: by putting [those who do not 
fit the ideal definition of a Finn] into separate boxes, we are somehow easier to 
understand and handle”. 
While separate institutions may be empowering for the communities involved 
and therefore seemingly boost participation in society, as Suomalainen Pedrosa 
points out, they do not remove the initial problem – the larger institutions that hold 
power remain exclusive, and the participation that is achieved does not amount to 
actual sharing of power. Also Riikka Taavetti contemplated this problem. Taavetti 
is a memory scholar who works on queer history both in an academic capacity and 
as an active member of the Friends of Queer History Association. 
Attempting to build new institutions is always problematic because the 
power of representation does not transfer to them. If we formed a museum 
for rainbow history, its ability to talk about Finnish history would be com -
pletely different from that of the National Museum of Finland. And it would 
always be defined somehow as an alternative, oppositional, or marginal 
institution. 
A central concept that is often invoked in connection to attempts to transform 
institutions to be more inclusive is the idea of a safe space where difficult and 
painful aspects of history could also be collectively encountered (e.g. Gurian 
2010). This idea was also discussed in the Whose Heritage? panels on several 
occasions. Claiming safe spaces has become more commonplace and more as -
sertive among minorities. However, as Marko Stenroos, senior planning officer 
at the Finnish Institute for Health and Welfare and a Roma scholar and advocate, 
explains, claiming spaces is often very difficult and emotionally demanding for 
minorities. 
Lately, we have discussed claiming space, not apologetically but openly, by 
saying, “I want a safe space where we can discuss these issues.” (…) And 
when you start taking this idea to broader debates, it automatically faces 
opposition, and we need to be aware that claiming that space will not be 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
24 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
a painless process. So, if we [the Roma] want to claim our own space in 
Finnish society, we have to be ready for the emotional demands it might 
entail. 
Building safe spaces for heritage institutions requires handling a wide spectrum 
of emotions that might come from many directions. On the one hand, museums 
need to be ready to handle the emotional tensions that erupt when a community that 
is used to being invisible is finally given an opportunity to be seen. Elisa Sarpo, the 
community manager at the National Museum of Finland, explains, 
If you have been invisible – if we talk, for example, of the Roma, who have 
also been excluded from our society through legislation. You have not had 
the chance to participate, and when all of a sudden, you are given an op -
portunity to participate, that moment is so charged with so many emotions, 
contradictions, and all sorts of things. Receiving those emotions can also be 
very demanding and difficult for museum staff. But I feel it is my duty to 
receive those emotions and also try to understand why they exist, what are 
the structural reasons behind them and also try to understand what being 
marginalised – or whatever term you want to use, they all have their prob -
lems – but the traces it leaves in people. 
On the other hand, this process can also be uncomfortable and painful for the 
privileged. Julian Owusu, a Finnish-Ghanaian hip-hop dancer and teacher, discussed 
in the panel how having one’s privileges confronted can “cause immense pain” if 
“you have lived your whole life in a world where there is one truth that everything 
has been modelled around”. This pain, if not adequately addressed, often erupts in 
polarisation and even violence. This was the case, for example, in a chain of events 
that resulted in a right-wing youth group stealing and burning the Pride flag from the 
National Museum of Finland. Thinking back on these events, Sarpo also pondered 
on the difficulties of bravely standing up for minorities but doing so in a way that 
would not intentionally provoke polarisation. 
When we are creating space for identities that have been previously hidden, 
it can cause others – eh, again, others – to react. We have to be aware of this 
possibility, but we should not be afraid. [We have to think] how we could 
create some common ground, a place where we can all meet on some shared 
humane level. We [as an institution] should not cause situations that force 
people into oppositional positions of us and them. 
Elina Anttila, the director general of the National Museum of Finland, agrees 
and highlights the importance of being able to handle difficult emotions. 
I agree that it is not a pain-free process. But it is so important that different 
experiences or perspectives about the same things gain visibility and space 
and that we have to face how different they are. And there are undoubtedly 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
Looking beyond Recited Truths of Heritage Participation 25 
sentiments that will cause pain or provoke others in one way or another, 
which is why safe spaces are so important. However, the idea of a safe space 
must also contain the idea that difficult and even frightening things might 
take place there. The key is to engage them in a safe manner. 
While we can demand that National Museums be more welcoming to differ -
ent minorities and create more participation opportunities for people from all 
backgrounds, not all institutions can or should face the same demands. It is im -
portant to question who different kinds of institutions should serve and what 
inclusion means in that specific context. As Áile Aikio, a Sámi museum scholar 
and practitioner, explains, the Sámi Museum Siida’s mission is to serve the Sámi 
communities, the only acknowledged Indigenous community in Europe. While 
the institution could discuss a variety of difficult histories that could serve a va -
riety of audiences – for example, the silenced history of the mistreatment of the 
Sámi by the Finns – the needs of the Sámi outweigh the needs of the majority 
population. 
Alternative epistemologies, discourses, and skills of participation 
The second proposition that emerged from data concerns our notions of knowl -
edge – both as an epistemological challenge and a set of skills that are required for 
participation. Looking at participation from an etymological perspective offers an 
avenue to challenge some of the epistemological and ontological challenges con -
nected with the relationship between heritage, knowledge, and underlying value 
regimes. Gabriella Giannachi (2022, 14) argues that instead of the Latin morpheme 
part (“take part”), the contestation of the concept of participation relies on the cip 
which is a weakened form derived from capere meaning “capture, seize, and take”, 
“take on”, or “take in and understand”. As she explains, 
what is contested in the term “participation” is not so much the fact that 
the term implies taking part in something but rather that it somehow sug -
gests the gaining of a capacity or, perhaps even more importantly, the 
act of taking something in. To participate, therefore, means to take part 
in something but also to understand, to gain knowledge and possibly, to 
become aware of this process. It is precisely this act of understanding, 
of becoming aware, that makes it possible to differentiate among various 
types of participatory experiences and that makes participation an episte -
mological concern. 
(Giannachi 2022, 14) 
Epistemologies are often discussed, especially in connection to Indigenous her-
itage and knowledge. In the panel, Aikio explained how the Sámi tradition of yoik 
is approached as an intangible heritage, and as such, its preservation is perceived 
as a matter of recording and archiving different yoiks. However, as Aikio shows, 
the recording loses much of its worth – and political power – if the associated 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
26 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
traditions and forms of knowledge that keep it alive cease to exist. Keeping the 
tradition alive becomes quickly entangled in politics. Aikio explains: 
First, we have to enable reindeer herding. Because if we do not have reindeer 
herding, we lose the knowledge that is connected with reindeer’s ear mark -
ings (that are mentioned in the yoik). Without this knowledge, we cannot 
understand what the yoik is about. (…) This leads us to the question that in 
order for us to preserve this heritage as living heritage, we have to secure 
land rights for the Sámi. We have to stop the construction of the wind power 
field on Rastegaisa (a sacred Sámi mountain). So, in the end, the heritage 
connected with the yoik is about power and self-determination. 
While Emma Waterton and Laurajane Smith (2010) have contended that the in-
troduction of participatory measures has sometimes resulted in an incentive to favour 
community knowledge over heritage professionals, different expert voices continue 
to garner disproportionate attention when defining heritage narratives and discourses 
(see also Smith 2006). This has had powerful effects, not only on how we talk about 
heritage but also on what we consider to be heritage. Aikio’s story exemplifies how a 
seemingly apolitical heritage – such as a single yoik that, from a heritage specialist’s 
perspective, can be easily recorded and added to an archive – is enmeshed in deeply 
political questions about the right to practice Sámi culture. The story provokes us 
to ask uncomfortable questions about the nature of things we should be preserving. 
There is a disconnection between the desire to preserve things and the importance of 
keeping the practices, knowledge, and values that give things meaning alive. This is 
a central concern for the heritage sector, especially as it comes to intangible heritage. 
As exemplified by Julian Owusu in the panel: 
The sauna (building) is not the point. The point is what we do there, how we 
spend time in a sauna, the whole culture that men can only cry in a sauna, you 
can talk of your emotions only in a sauna, that we give birth in a sauna and so 
on. These are the things that make the sauna important. It is not that we have 
a wooden shed where you can throw water on hot stones, but that wooden 
shed is the thing we can archive. 
Taking such meanings in and allowing them to participate in defining what di-
mensions of heritage should be preserved and how is not a simple task. The act of 
gaining knowledge and taking something in also has another side. It can also mani-
fest as a requirement for specific skills and as a prerequisite for participation. While 
there are varied reasons that contribute to exclusive tendencies in participatory 
initiatives (see Pastor Pérez and Colomer 2024, 229), it is widely acknowledged 
that participation requires skills, capacities, and social capital that all groups and 
individuals may not possess (see also Lähdesmäki chapter 11 in this volume). 
The notion that some groups are more accessible for the museums to engage 
with was also evident in my data. Marko Stenroos contemplated how working with 
museums and heritage actors has been possible for him as a Roma only because 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Looking beyond Recited Truths of Heritage Participation 27 
of his academic education and ability to speak the same language. Talking the 
same language does not mean the ability to talk, in the case of my data, Finnish – 
although that might also be an issue in collaborations with, for example, migrant 
communities. The same language refers here to the ability to talk and understand 
a professional discourse and understand the kind of things that are possible in that 
professional context. As a result, “despite the intentions of practitioners, work 
relating to social inclusion often becomes assimilatory in nature through a pro -
cess of education and participation within the dominant paradigm” ( Neal 2015, 
349). Such hidden requirements construct participatory initiatives as “privileged 
spaces for civic learning and redistributing political capital” ( Colomer and Pastor 
Pérez 2024 , 81), where only those who are privileged enough to possess those 
skills already can participate in a manner that also enables the use of power. It is, 
therefore, important to differentiate between “having a voice in heritage issues 
under debate” and “having any power in decision-making processes” ( Colomer 
and Pastor Pérez 2024, 82). 
On the other hand, the inclusion of groups can also be hindered by an inability 
to match the hopes and expectations of different sides. Sari Salovaara, an advo -
cate for disability issues and accessibility, discussed the difficulties of building 
the first exhibition on the history of disabilities in Finland. Although initiated 
with good intentions and engaging a broad spectrum of different stakeholders, 
the collaboration contained numerous misplaced hopes, frustrations, and disap -
pointing compromises for the participating disabled communities. A part of the 
problem was that the disabled communities’ expectations surpassed what was ac -
tually possible within the frame of one single exhibition. The expectations surged 
because of the possibility of finally being seen, but many felt the process ended 
up being empty and frustrating for the ones who were left (again) powerless (see 
also Arnstein 1969). 
Participation as a form of inclusive representation 
Participation entered heritage debates, especially through the drafting and rati -
fication of the 2003 UNESCO Convention for the Safeguarding of the Intan -
gible Heritage, which popularised the notion of “heritage communities” whose 
involvement was incremental in the preservation of intangible cultural heritage. 
While the convention highlights the role that participation of (often minority) 
communities have in keeping heritages alive , the more traditional connection 
between heritage and communities is linked to the construction of national iden -
tities (e.g. Crang and Tolia-Kelly 2010 ; Graham and Howard 2008 ; Hall 1999). 
In this latter context, participation is often conceived as the inclusion of other -
than-national communities and their experiences in hegemonic national herit -
age canons. This desire to include more voices in our historical narratives has 
been supported by political interests and pressures that have emerged in response 
to increased migration, diversification, and feminist, post-colonial, and Indig -
enous critique – the same developments that have contributed to debates around 
participation. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
28 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
In the panel, Elina Anttila pondered on the history of Finnish museums and the 
building of Finnish national identity. 
We have a long record of Finnish history that is missing so many people 
and identities. […] If we think about the creation of the Finnish museum 
institution, it was one of the key institutions through which the difference 
to non-Finnishness has been created. For the message to be clear, the image 
of Finnishness has to be clearer, and then that image must be very uniform. 
Today, we, of course, all understand that this image has drowned out a mas-
sive amount of diversity that never got its voice heard. But now, there is an 
opportunity to bring these voices forward. 
Anttila discusses the many silences in the Finnish national narrative in the quote. 
Such silences and amnesia are not particularly rare (see Mason and Sayner 2019; 
Trouillot 2015). All silences are also not a matter of absence. Many silences are 
what Vinitzky-Seroussi and Teeger (2010) have characterised as covert silences: 
they “are not about the complete absence of talk, ritual or practice. Rather, they are 
about the absence of content” (2010, 1104). One example of covert silence is the 
ways Roma’s history has been recorded in Finland. Marko Stenroos, senior planning 
officer at the Finnish Institute for Health and Welfare and a Roma scholar and advo-
cate, noted in the panel how the early accounts of Roma history are narrated almost 
exclusively from the perspective of Finnish officials. Historical records and national 
Finnish narratives are not empty of Roma content. There is no difficulty finding 
historical records of the Roma, a community whose existence in Finland outdates 
the Finnish nation-state by roughly 500 years. As Stenroos noted in the panel, the 
problem is that these records contain only “stories constructed by Finnish officials 
and their ideas of what being a Roma is like”. 
Similar problems were raised by Kari Huuskonen, museum curator at the Mu -
seum of Visual Impairment and Sari Salovaara, who both highlighted how the his-
tory of disabilities has often been recorded only from a medical perspective. Such 
stories are very problematic for attempting to build an empowering identity – in 
other words, they are affectively poor. Modlin, Alderman, and Gentry use the con-
cept of “affective inequality” (2011) to describe how different groups’ experiences 
are narrated with varying levels of affective intensity. While some groups, most 
often the dominant ones, are described via emotive stories of personal experiences 
that enable visitors to engage emotionally with their stories, other stories and expe-
riences are excluded or told with less emotional engagement. Affective inequality 
is not only about words and discourses, rather, it is about “understanding how our 
bodies and minds react to different narratives” (Turunen 2021, 33). Instead of sim-
ply aiming to add stories, it is, therefore, more important to be mindful of the kind 
of stories we want to add and the affective opportunities they create for feelings of 
empathy, solidarity, or even similarity. 
Increased pluralisation has altered the societal atmosphere in which museums 
and heritage actors are embedded. Writing already in 1995 in a museum studies 
classic Birth of the Museum, Tony Bennett noted how the desire for representational 

 
 
Looking beyond Recited Truths of Heritage Participation 29 
adequacy has created an “unachievable” and “insatiable” pressure for museums 
to engage in representational politics ( Bennett 1995, 103). The desire to pluralise 
should not be approached as an attempt to create an encyclopaedic collection of 
representations. Instead, as Elina Anttila also explained in the panel, while it may 
be too difficult to include representations of all possible variations, museums need 
to create an atmosphere where “all identities feel that they have an opportunity to 
be heard”. Therefore, participation as a representation of diversity is never a sin -
gle act. Instead, it should be considered “a dynamic, politically fluid and socially 
responsive process” (Neal 2015, 348) where, as Anttila also highlighted, we must 
keep giving voice and visibility to different groups again and again. Through this 
processual multiplicity, we can create representations that would allow a broader 
range of the public to find something to connect with. Integrating different perspec-
tives does not always require new exhibitions or collections. As Riikka Taavetti 
points out 
attempts to create alternative readings of existing exhibitions are also very 
valuable. Nothing in the objects changes, nor in the way they are exhibited, 
but we look at them from a different perspective. 
While nearly all panellists highlighted the importance of adding more stories 
into our collective memory, they also highlighted that the act of representation 
cannot and should not be reduced to filling gaps in the historical narratives. Di-
versifying narratives should be about the future – about creating representations 
that normalise diversity both in the museum and in the society at large. Starting 
from the museums, plurality should be ingrained in the people who are visible in 
the museum, most importantly the staff. Although ideally, this would take place 
naturally by having a diverse group of trained museum and heritage experts read -
ily available, at least in Finland, we are not there yet. As Sari Salovaara explains, 
even when you emphasise and encourage diversity in recruitment policies, there is 
a very limited pool of trained experts that would differ from the White, able-bodied 
norm. Enticing people from different kinds of minority backgrounds into the mu -
seum needs to start by making a career in heritage seems like a valid career option. 
As Salovaara concludes, “We need to make diversity more visible in museums […] 
so that more people would experience that ‘I was welcome in that museum’”. 
The same need for diverse representations applies to the wider society. As Áile 
Aikio, a Sámi museum scholar and practitioner, explains, “It is important that all 
minority youth get a chance to imagine all kinds of futures […] It is very important 
that they have examples of their own reference group that show ‘this is possible’”. 
Cátia Suomalainen Pedrosa, the director of Cultural Centre Caisa, expands: 
…it is about the example that is given to others. If we talk, for example, about 
deaf children or children with immigrant backgrounds who never see adults 
who would mirror their own families (…) as teachers or in other positions 
of power. (…) We cannot forget children from majority backgrounds either. 
We do not work only for the minorities. We want to also help the majority 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
30 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
population realise that a teacher, artist, or performer can be something else 
than a member of the majority. 
Suomalainen Pedrosa aptly highlights the other side of the coin: the adjustments 
that the majority populations need to make in their own thinking patterns. These 
changes are often described as a process of unlearning ( Tlostanova and Mignolo 
2012; Turunen 2021). Also, Elisa Sarpo pondered the topic and the often genera -
tional time span unlearning requires. 
We have to remember that I have grown up in a society that has fed me these 
attitudes [about minorities] and given me knowledge of the world. And this 
is what I have been operating with – of course, trying to challenge things 
to the best of my knowledge and seeking more information. (…) But when 
I think about the youth who grow up into this new atmosphere with new 
concepts and a more diverse outlook on life. There is a lot of hope there, at 
least for me. 
This hope is descriptive of a concept of positive utopias that was initially 
brought into the debates by Marko Stenroos but referred back to repeatedly in later 
discussions. In thinking out loud about what a Roma museum should be or do, he 
concluded that the aim of the museum is not just to look into the past but to imag -
ine what Roma’s position in Finnish society could be in the future: “Even though 
our history is what it is, our future does not have to get stuck in that history”. The 
ultimate aim of participation should also be conceived through the things it may 
enable for the future. 
Concluding thoughts on inclusive modes of participation 
As was discussed in the introduction, heritage participation “appears to have been 
accepted, largely uncritically, as a ‘good thing’” ( Neal 2015, 349) in many policy 
discourses. Talk of participation has become a politically motivated discourse that 
allows a wide variety of societal actors – museums and heritage actors included – 
to re-legitimate their societal relevance both to their funders and a growing number 
of communities and stakeholders who have felt left out or excluded. As I have 
shown throughout this chapter, the daily experiences and practices of participation 
paint a picture that departs these often-recited positive sentiments. In this conclud-
ing part, I want to briefly summarise a few key points from the analysis and ponder 
what adopting more inclusive perspectives to participation might require from mu-
seum and heritage professionals. 
The problem at the heart of authorised institutionally driven heritage participa -
tion is access to power – a typical problem with participatory initiatives identified 
by Sherry Arnstein already in 1969. The frustrations and difficulties that many of 
the panellists brought up highlight the discrepancy “between a desire to include 
and a hesitancy to surrender or reduce the authority of both the [authorised herit -
age discourse] and the heritage practitioners [who] wield it” ( Smith 2006, 38). 

 
 
 
Looking beyond Recited Truths of Heritage Participation 31 
All attempts to reform authorised heritage processes towards more inclusionary 
modes require restructuring the hierarchical power positions that contour institu -
tionalised heritage work. Communities need more agency and ownership in setting 
out the limits and aims of their institutional commitments. An inclusive idea of 
participation must ensure community members access to spaces that are physically, 
epistemologically, and emotionally safe and accessible and enable communities to 
work from a community-driven perspective. At times, this might also mean that 
communities – or members of the public who react to actions taken by museums 
or heritage institutions – might choose to participate in ways that might contrast or 
oppose institutionally provided authorised modes of participation or exercise their 
right to decline from participation. While this is something museums and heritage 
actors should be aware of and prepared for, it cannot prevent museums from work-
ing towards more inclusive practices. 
Questions of power are also entangled with epistemological questions: the ex -
tent to which alternative knowledge systems can participate in defining what counts 
as heritage and the hidden epistemological norms and requirements that can limit 
specific communities’ ability to participate. As the panellists actively articulated in 
the discussions, a certain kind of assimilation and adoption of typical authorised 
discourses often function as unacknowledged prerequisites for participation – or at 
least for claiming a position that allows you to have any power in the process. The 
genuine inclusion and involvement of subaltern or other disenfranchised groups, 
including their experiences and knowledge, shatters “the ideologies of the impar -
tiality of expertise” (Smith 2006, 38) that has historically been incremental for the 
museum and heritage work (see also Coffee 2023; Katriel 1993; Sandell 2007). 
Such epistemological inclusions are tightly connected with the heritage institutions’ 
need to unlearn some authorised practices they are accustomed to. Transforming 
heritage participation towards more inclusive practices requires critical analysis 
and dismantling hidden epistemological norms blocking genuine participation. 
This epistemic work can also take the form of pluralising heritage narratives to 
better reflect contemporary and past diversity. There is, however, a need to normal-
ise diversity in museums beyond this narrative dimension. Finding ways to allow 
people with various backgrounds and skill sets to be included in museums in posi-
tions of power needs to be ensured on a systemic level. Breaking structural power 
hierarchies impacting participatory processes negatively requires normalising the 
diversity of narratives, experiences, and people at all levels – from museum visitors 
to museum leadership. 
Finally, the importance of emotional labour was also emphasised, and this emo-
tional dimension is an area that will need further investigation. If participation 
is approached from the perspective of radical inclusivity, there are bound to be 
conflicts, tensions, and hurt feelings. Learning to let go of the power that herit -
age institutions are accustomed to requires a lot of internal institutional reflection 
and conscious unlearning. At the same time, claiming, gaining, and using space 
can also be emotionally demanding for the communities. As such, both the act of 
granting and claiming space are hinged on the ability to handle negative or difficult 
feelings that inclusive, participatory processes might stir up in the actors who are 

 
  
 
 
 
 
32 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
involved in the participatory process as well as in the general public. A key chal -
lenge for the future is to ensure that heritage institutions – especially those with a 
lot of institutionalised power – have the knowledge, skills, and bravery to engage 
in this emotional work.

2 Examining public perceptions of 
Roma representation in cultural 
festivals in Sibiu 
Alin Croitoru1 and Ionela Vlase 
Festivals enable inhabitants of multicultural sites to better understand each other 
and to express themselves without fear of being stigmatised or othered. According 
to Jackson (2020, 650), festivals “are public events where ethnic groups gather to 
revel in carefully selected aspects of their culture, to invite others temporarily to 
celebrate their identity with them, and to demonstrate to the general community 
their continuing existence”. Participants belonging to minority groups feel safe in 
displaying their differences and celebrating their cultural traditions during these 
festivals that can represent a “counterspace” enabling the temporary dissolution of 
the hierarchical power relations and the resistance to marginalisation within time-
space delineated frame suspending the community routine life ( Hassanli, Walters, 
and Friedmann 2020; Jackson 2020; Savinovic, Kim, and Long 2012; V orobjovas-
Pinta and Hardy 2021 ). In such communities, the literature on participation in 
ethnic festivals provides evidence of the formation of a sense of community be -
longing that is fuelled by positive feelings elicited through shared values within 
public events (Hassanli, Walters, and Williamson 2020). Taking the case of Roma 
representation in cultural festivals in Sibiu, our chapter builds on the discussion of 
the problematic term of “community” as discussed in heritage studies. Waterton 
and Smith (2010, 9) rightly argue that the use of this concept, although conveying 
an image of homogeneity, cohesion, and peaceful cohabitation of members of such 
communities, it often conceals different power relations based on class, ethnic, and 
other hierarchies leading to the misrecognition of cultural distinctiveness of some 
groups and to their underrepresentation in engaging with heritage: 
the heritage sector is dominated by a particular notion of community, one that 
overlooks the fact that representations of reality can have powerful effects on 
any group under construction. It can lead to misrecognition, discrimination, 
lowered self-esteem and lack of parity in any engagement with heritage. 
Research conducted on two different multicultural festivals organised by the 
Municipality of Copenhagen shows that festivals create various opportunities for 
1 Authors have contributed equally, listed in alphabetical order. Alin Croitoru is the corresponding 
author, Email: alin.croitoru@ulbsibiu.ro 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-4 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
36 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
embodied encounters carrying emotional and playful exchanges among partici -
pants, while being also charged with identity politics because festivals “are about 
the making and unmaking of borders, and they can produce anxiety, fear and re -
sentment, as well as curiosity, fascination and conviviality” ( Koefoed and Neer -
gaard 2020, 709). Festivals’ interactions can take various meanings for participants 
ranging from liberation to domination as they act as “social and material media-
tors” in the negotiation of pleasure, belonging, and identity, as shown by the above-
mentioned authors. 
With very few exceptions, the literature on cultural heritage does not address the 
subjective experiences or impact of Roma festivals in Central and Eastern European 
(CEE) countries, despite the fact that this population makes up the most numerous 
ethnic minorities in Europe. Such an exception is the chapter by Piotrowska (2023) 
that describes the Romani music festival (Roma Nowa Huta) organised in Krakow 
(Poland) since 2012 as a celebration of the International Day of Roma Culture. This 
festival takes place in the familiar surroundings of their neighbourhood, enabling 
the Roma community to stage their authentic living as well as their music and dance 
performances, traditional crafts, and mysterious occupations such as fortune-telling 
in front of non-Roma community members and tourists. As emphasised by local 
organisers of this Romani music festival in Krakow, the event is seen as a cultural 
bridge between Roma and non-Roma community members because it enables the 
careful selection and presentation of the positive aspects of Roma culture, creating 
socio-cultural bonds through “various forms of social connectivity which occur at 
a macro level, simultaneously transforming the festival into a social space enabling 
reciprocal gazes of the Roma and the non-Roma” (Piotrowska 2023, 116). 
In the context where the Roma minority in Romania still faces significant struc-
tural challenges and ethnic segregation remains present ( World Bank 2018), this 
chapter aims to explore how the local stakeholders and the general population 
perceive festivals dedicated to the Roma minority in Sibiu, a Romanian county 
characterised by ethnic diversity, a longstanding presence of Roma minority, and a 
vibrant cultural life. By holding the status of European Capital of Culture in 2007, 
Sibiu has strengthened its symbolic standing regarding cultural life. The Roma 
festivals in this region, consequently, have the potential to attract broader national 
and international interest, serving as a tool for gaining bargaining power to restore 
social justice and equity in participation to cultural heritage as a catalyst for the 
social integration of Roma. 
The chapter proceeds as follows. We first outline a conceptual and national his-
torical framework, and we provide key defining elements for the local context by 
referencing festivals dedicated to the Roma community. The methodological de -
tails are presented in a dedicated section, followed by a set of analyses. On the one 
hand, the empirical part aims to provide a perspective on the factors influencing 
the presence of negative attitudes towards Roma minority festivals based on data 
collected through an opinion survey conducted in 2023 in Sibiu (N > 900). Quan-
titative analyses shed light on significant predictors shaping the negative attitudes 
towards festivals dedicated to the Roma minority. On the other hand, based on a 
dataset of 35 interviews with stakeholders, we explore how organisers of cultural 

 
 
Public Perceptions of Roma Representation in Cultural Festivals 37 
festivals and cultural activities relate to the issue of cultural events dedicated to the 
Roma minority from the standpoint of social inclusion. The qualitative data add 
depth to how the cultural reality is structured at the local level and illustrate the per-
spectives of various stakeholders on Roma minority festivals. The chapter provides 
insights into the ways of fostering more inclusive communities through the socially 
sustainable mechanisms of festivals dedicated to Roma minorities. 
Situating Roma minority in the cultural landscape 
Roma citizens make up the largest minority group in Europe but the recognition 
and promotion of its cultural heritage is still a contentious issue, even if start -
ing with 1980s, the question of heritagisation of otherness became prominent with 
the decolonial turn frame that questioned the dominant politics of nation-building 
across many Eastern European countries ( György 2019). In most CEE countries 
such as Hungary and Romania, Roma communities were subject to strict assimi -
lation policies during socialism that included proletarianisation and forced sed -
entarisation of their (semi)nomadic lifestyle and adverse attitudes towards their 
cultural traditions and social norms and behaviours considered “backward” and 
“uncivilised” by countries’ mainstream population that was considered to repre-
sent the “nation”. The cultural activities of Roma minority started to receive some 
attention and publicity only during 1980s, as noted by György (2019, 211) whose 
analysis focus on the birth of Rom Som (“I am Roma”) Club in Budapest promot -
ing the “Gypsy folk music” and who notes that by the 1980s, “the prohibition of 
the ethnic self-organization had slowly started to ease, at least at a local level and 
primarily in those cultural forms (music and dance) that had been – to a certain 
extent – accepted by the state power”. This decade marked the beginning of a 
process of canonisation of minority cultural heritage within the state socialism in 
which the “Gypsy cultural clubs”, especially those dedicated to youth, played an 
emancipatory role, despite their marginal symbolic and geographic position, as 
pointed out by the Hungarian historian quoted above. As a result of this progressive 
democratisation of culture in the socialist bloc, the ethnic minorities’ marginalised 
culture found a way to express themselves in the public space enabling Roma art-
ists to assert their “Gypsiness” and to contribute to the promotion of a positive eth-
nic identity (György 2019). However, the inclusion of Roma cultural heritage into 
the mainstream heritage needs to be done with careful attention to the tendency of 
representing this minority as the exotic or racialised “other”. This caution rests on 
the acknowledgement of the fact that present-day cultural politics carry, to a certain 
extent, the legacies of historical processes of marginalisation of this ethnic group. 
Although Roma people live immersed in and (mainly) dispersed among the 
mainstream population of CEE countries, they manage to preserve their unique -
ness and a sense of community even in the absence of a shared religion or po -
litical leadership, while the relationship between Roma and non-Roma sharing the 
same space is often characterised by mutual misrecognition. Stewart (2013, 423) 
pointed out how scholarly efforts to convey a generic Roma identity can be mis-
leading. Roma do not constitute a homogenous population but a differentiated one 

 
 
 
38 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
by specific traditions and crafts inherited from past generations within different lin-
eages, as well as by individualised rituals and the aesthetic code of their clothing. 
In Romania, current social attitudes towards the Roma population and their cul-
tural distinctiveness can be traced back to the failed politics of assimilation dur -
ing communism. In his sociological work documenting the socialist biopolitics of 
power, Anghel (2024) examines the techniques to govern the “deviant” lifestyles of 
Roma by the Romanian authorities whose main goal was to shape the “new Soviet 
man” and a new ideal society oriented towards moral progress, social modernity, 
and economic productivity. Building on an analysis of interwoven processes of the 
decentralised biopolitics encompassing two technologies of power (i.e. proletari -
anisation and sedentarisation) and the everyday acts of resistance by various Roma 
groups, Anghel (2024) shows that the heterogeneous Roma population in Romania 
had different socio-economic outcomes. The above-mentioned author draws on 
Foucault’s (1997) distinction between biological racism (i.e. the evolutionist belief 
rooted in the biological determinism according to which humans’ physical traits 
are the basis of judgements regarding their superiority or inferiority) and ethnic 
racism (i.e. the socio-cultural rather than physical differences between groups play 
a central role in establishing a hierarchy of people’s worth based on asymmetrical 
power relations between groups). According to Anghel (2024), biological rather 
than ethnic racism has underpinned the governmentality of Roma during com -
munism, and although Roma did not constitute the traditional class enemies (i.e. 
bourgeois) within socialism, they have been portrayed as lumpenproletariat given 
their nomadic or semi-nomadic lifestyle and a lack of class consciousness guiding 
their contribution to the societal progress. Therefore, the socialist biopolitics aimed 
at disciplining and improving their work ethic and transforming these unruly and 
backward citizens into good proletarians. Unlike Roma from other CEE countries 
who were already sedentary by the time of the establishment of the communist 
regime, a significant share (about 30%) of Roma in Romania still deployed an 
itinerant lifestyle given their lack of properties because of their long history of 
enslavement that came to an end in the mid-19th century. In addition, their unlikely 
integration into the main productive system encompassing industry and agricul -
ture was complicated by their traditional occupations that did not include such 
preoccupations, while they even manifested some adversity against such work, as 
noted by Ion Chelcea (1944) in his ethnographic documentation of Roma from 
Romania. These peculiarities represented some of the challenges to be dealt with 
by the socialist governmental programmes that had to devise targeted measures 
and underlying knowledge to justify the effective assimilationist interventions in 
educational, health, and work domains of the Roma population. Studying archival 
documents of the State Undersecretary for National Minorities issued in 1952, An-
ghel (2024) describes the sedentarisation technique of the Romanian communist 
regime as a brutal alteration of Roma traditional ways of life which was legitimised 
by the civilising goal of modernisation theories: 
modernization-cum-assimilation program which included the prohibition 
of movement on the territory of the country “for carts without registered 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Public Perceptions of Roma Representation in Cultural Festivals 39 
licenses”, the allocation of housing or plots of land for the Roma to build 
(with the help of the state) their own housing, their incorporation into a stable 
form of employment, the enrolment adults in literacy courses and children in 
the state education system and the provision of health education. 
(2024, 109) 
Despite state bodies enforcing these policies, the bargaining power of some 
Roma local elites and entrepreneurs within different communities helped them 
maintain their traditional crafts they have continued to trade on the informal mar -
ket. Their counter-conduct reveals a disbelief in the socialist ideology of labour, 
and even if Romani political movement has not been successful in its endeavours 
of obtaining the “cohabiting nationality” status for Roma in Romania, these eco -
nomic and cultural acts of contestation represent forms of everyday resistance 
against the assimilationist state policies, as shown by the above-mentioned author. 
Out of the 12 million of Roma currently living in Europe, Romania hosts the 
largest share of this minority which is estimated up to 2 million people (Fundación 
Secretariado Gitano 2024). Other estimates suggest smaller numbers, but most of 
the existent research acknowledge that the actual Roma population in Romania is 
underestimated by the census records, where the figure is 569,477 (National Insti-
tute of Statistics 2021). The Roma Survey (2021) conducted by European Union 
Agency for Fundamental Rights on a sample of 8,461 respondents self-identified as 
Roma from ten European countries (i.e. Croatia, Czechia, Greece, Hungary, Italy, 
North Macedonia, Portugal, Romania, Serbia, and Spain) shows that a relatively 
high proportion of Roma in Romania still face discrimination in some core areas 
of life such as work (26%) and education (14%) because of their skin colour or 
because of their ethnic identity or religious beliefs. On the other hand, the Special 
Eurobarometer 493 (2019) indicates that 60% of Romanian respondents consid-
ered the discrimination of Roma people as a widespread phenomenon. Except for 
these large population surveys inquiring about general public’s opinion regarding 
the prevalence of discriminatory attitudes against Roma, no study has been con -
ducted so far on the views of different towns or regions in Romania documenting 
more specific aspects of Roma identity and heritage. 
Local context 
Sibiu is one of the most ethnically diverse counties in Romania and it is character-
ised by a vivid cultural realm. Data of the latest population census (National Insti-
tute of Statistics 2021) shows that Sibiu’s population is ethnically heterogeneous. 
Romanians make up the majority (80.6% of the total county’s population) followed 
by 12,808 individuals self-declared as Roma (3.3%), Hungarians (1.6%), and Ger-
mans (0.7%), while 13.6% have not reported their ethnicity. The size of the Roma 
population is probably larger but because of their perceived discrimination, some 
members of this population usually do not self-declare as Roma (Cernat 2021). His-
torically, Germans have been much more represented (about 20% of the Sibiu’s 
population according to the 1977 census) and their cultural heritage is still very 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
40 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
present in the multicultural legacy of Sibiu (see Olaru’s chapter in this volume). Re-
gardless of their actual numbers, some ethnic groups are more likely than others to 
be celebrated in festivals dedicated to their culture (e.g. language, gastronomy, and 
crafts). Currently, there are cultural activities and festivals dedicated to the Roma 
minority in Sibiu. The most well-known is “The International Festival of Roma Cul-
ture” which reached its ninth edition in 2023. In general, it includes musical activi-
ties, dance performances, presentations of traditional costumes, film screenings, and 
other activities. Also, there is an artisan fair of Roma craftsmen selling traditional 
products and gastronomies. These events are organised by representatives of the 
local Roma community in partnership with local and county public authorities and 
take place in highly visible locations throughout the city, being reflected in the local 
press (Mesagerul 2023; Ora 2022), social media, and national media ( Observator 
2015; Prima News 2023). These events represent an important symbolic moment 
within the community, providing visibility to the Roma minority and offering the 
opportunity for its representatives to communicate on cultural, social, and political 
issues. However, even though such festivals exist, very little is known about how 
the local community perceives the idea of festivals dedicated to the Roma minority 
and, furthermore, what factors contribute to explaining the harbouring of negative 
attitudes towards them by a non-negligible share of the general population. 
Data and method 
The data for this chapter stems from the Horizon project “Establishing a Labora-
tory of Cultural Heritage in Central Romania (ELABCHROM)”. The methodologi-
cal design of the research comprised both a survey on local population attitudes 
towards different types of festivals and qualitative data gathered through inter -
views with local stakeholders (e.g. cultural experts, festival organisers, and artists). 
The survey was conducted among people living in Sibiu and the data collec -
tion took place in 2023 between April and June. The self-administered question-
naire was designed for this specific research project and included sections with 
questions related to cultural heritage and local identity, interest in festivals and 
cultural events, representation of various ethnic categories in local festivals, com -
modification of heritage through cultural events, as well as socio-economic and 
demographic information about the respondents. The questionnaire was dissemi-
nated across various social media platforms, email lists, and calls for respondents 
on local news platforms, among other channels. Informed consent was required by 
respondents who, in order to fill in the questionnaire, had to confirm their willing-
ness to anonymously participate in the survey by answering a dedicated question. 
The total sample resulting from the survey included over 900 individuals. 
The qualitative data used in this study were collected through 35 semi-structured 
interviews conducted with key stakeholders in the cultural domain including fes -
tival organisers, representatives of municipalities in the field of cultural agenda, 
and artists such as actors, musicians, and writers. The transcripts of the interviews 
were coded with the help of MAXQDA software which enables the identification 
and analysis of text segments dealing with attitudes regarding the organisation of 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Public Perceptions of Roma Representation in Cultural Festivals 41 
festivals dedicated to different ethnic minorities in Sibiu. Specific codes relating to 
social in/equity in participation to cultural festivals and representation of minorities 
such as Roma, Saxons, and Hungarians have been consistently used throughout the 
full verbatim transcript of interview data. 
The main research objective of this chapter is to provide empirical-based 
knowledge in relation to the following research questions: (1) What individual de-
mographic, socio-economic, and attitudinal variables correlate with the negative 
attitudes towards Roma festivals? and (2) How cultural stakeholders in Sibiu nar -
rate the ethnic diversity and the equity in participation into the different kinds of 
festivals? These two questions are giving the structure of the following section of 
analyses and results. 
Analyses and results 
Quantitative analysis of factors correlated with negative attitudes towards 
Roma festivals in Sibiu 
In this section, we aim to provide an overview of the attitudes of the population in 
Sibiu towards Roma festivals. Following the logic of quantitative analyses, we will 
first describe the dependent variable of the study, namely the negative attitudes to-
wards Roma festivals. Subsequently, we will outline the independent variables that 
have the potential to explain the variation in the population’s attitudes within this 
context. After this, a logistic regression model is employed to test the relationships 
between these variables and to understand how attitudes towards Roma festivals 
vary among different categories of the population. 
The dependent variable is tapped into by a survey question referring to attitudes 
towards Roma festivals. It was asked through the following sentences: 
In many cities around the world, festivals dedicated to minorities are organ -
ised nowadays. Some people consider this to be wrong, while others see it 
as a good thing. Considering the reality in Sibiu, what is your opinion on 
organising festivals dedicated to the Roma minorities? 
The answers were recorded on the following scale from (1) to (5): (1) It is very 
wrong; (2) it is wrong; (3) it is neither a wrong thing nor a good thing; (4) it is good; 
and (5) it is very good. This original variable has been recoded into a binary vari -
able grouping all the answers taking values of 1 and 2 on this scale under the label 
of “negative attitudes towards Roma festivals” that stand in contrast to those who 
registered neutral or positive attitudes towards Roma festivals (values of 3, 4, and 
5). The descriptive analyses of this variable are included in Table 2.1. The mean 
value of 0,10 indicates a non-negligible share of the population holding the negative 
attitudes towards Roma festivals (10% of the sample). 
The set of independent variables in our analytical model includes demographics, 
socio-economic information, interest in cultural activities, opinions about the com-
munity’s cultural life, and respondents’ evaluations of the representation of ethnic 

 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
42 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
minorities in local festivals which enables to assess people’s views on the social 
justice and the politics of recognition in participation to heritage (Smith 2010). 
The data in Table 2.1 indicate that within the sample of 907 respondents with 
valid answers to the questions included in the analysis, 38% are men, the average 
age is 35.51 years, 46% have completed tertiary education, the average income is 
self-assessed at 3.54 on a scale from 1 to 5, and the average level of religiosity is 
3.47 on a scale from 1 to 5. In comparison to the structure of the general popula -
tion, our sample has a higher level of education, a slight overrepresentation of 
women, and a lower average age. Both income and religiosity levels are above the 
value of 3, which represents the theoretical midpoint of the scale used.
