Regarding respondents’ interest in cultural activities, the average value of 3.85 
indicates a high level of declared interest within the sample. Furthermore, there is 
a relatively positive evaluation of the diversity of existing festivals (mean value of 
3.55) and strong consensus that festivals taking place express the identity of the 
local community (mean value of 4.16). 
The last pair of independent variables allows us to examine how the represen -
tation of ethnic groups is perceived in the existing festivals. On the one hand, we 
can observe that there is a value above the theoretical average of 3 regarding the 
representation of the Romanian ethnic group (with an average value of 3.52). On 
the other hand, at the sample level, there is an average value of 2.46 concerning the 
representation of other ethnic groups in the existing festivals (other than Romani -
ans and Roma). Based on the data, we can note that the evaluation indicates that 
the Romanian ethnic group is considered to be relatively overrepresented in the 
existing festivals, while other ethnic groups are underrepresented. 
The logistic regression model presented in Table 2.2 allows us to test the rela -
tionships between the variables previously mentioned and to identify statistically 
significant predictors for “negative attitudes towards organising festivals dedicated 
to the Roma minority” (DV). It provides a way to interpret the relative impact of 
independent variables on the odds of the dependent variable. Given the exploratory 

 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
  
 
 
44 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
nature of our study and the scarcity of studies on this topic, the Nagelkerke 
R-squared of 0.11 suggests that the logistic regression model is adequate for our 
data. However, there are other significant factors that were not measured in our 
study, which could contribute to a better understanding of negative attitudes to -
wards Roma festivals. 
Within the logistic regression model, the first relationship indicates that men in 
the sample have higher odds ratio of holding negative attitudes towards Roma fes-
tivals (p < 0.01). The negative relationship between age and the DV suggests that 
younger individuals are more likely to exhibit negative attitudes towards festivals 
dedicated to the Roma minority (p < 0.01). While completing tertiary education 
does not significantly influence the odds of holding negative attitudes towards 
Roma festivals, household income level is in a statistically significant negative 
relationship with the DV (p < 0.05), meaning that as declared incomes decrease, 
the odds ratio of having negative attitudes towards Roma festivals increases. De -
clared religiosity also plays a role in shaping negative attitudes towards Roma 
festivals, with those considering themselves more religious having a higher odds 
ratio of falling into the category of people with unwelcoming attitudes towards 
Roma festivals (p < 0.05). 
Regarding the indicators for the relationship between the cultural profile of the 
respondents and their attitudes, the following observations can be expressed. The 
odds ratio of holding negative attitudes towards festivals dedicated to the Roma 
minority decreases as individuals show more interest in cultural activities taking 
place in the community (p < 0.01). A weaker but similar relationship is observed 
in the evaluation of the diversity of existing festivals and the greater the perceived 
diversity, the lower the likelihood of having negative attitudes towards Roma fes-
tivals (p < 0.1). Controlling for all other factors in the model, agreement with the 
idea that festivals define the identity of the local community has no bearing on the 
DV since the corresponding coefficient is not statistically significant. 
While the evaluation of the adequate representation of the Romanian ethnic 
group is not in statistically significant relationship with the DV , evaluation regard-
ing the representation of other ethnic minorities in existing festivals show a statisti-
cally significant positive relationship (p < 0.1). The more individuals believe that 
other ethnic groups are overrepresented in local festivals, the higher the likelihood 
of displaying negative attitudes towards festivals dedicated to the Roma minority. 
Qualitative insights: Stakeholders’ views on the Roma festivals in Sibiu 
This section aims to complement the knowledge on the drivers of unwelcoming 
attitudes by the general public on the Roma festivals with local experts’ views on 
the parity of representation of Roma minority in cultural festivals. Based on the 
analysis of interviews with organisers of cultural events in Sibiu as well as artists 
and municipality representatives of the cultural institutions (see also Mureșan’s 
chapter in this volume), we can draw a mixed picture of the level of representa -
tion and participation of Roma in local festivals dedicated to their culture. Most 
interview excerpts referring to Roma in the multicultural landscape of Sibiu tend to 

 
 
Public Perceptions of Roma Representation in Cultural Festivals 45 
portray this community as rather well represented in the cultural events dedicated 
to their heritage. They suggest, however, that although Roma have a specific place 
in the local cultural agenda in Sibiu, they do not receive enough practical assistance 
in terms of technical aspects of the organisation of their dedicated cultural events. 
One of the interviewees with significant experience in organising cultural events 
for a local museum refers to the ostensible lack of managerial skills of this com -
munity that is stereotypically reproduced with regard to the cultural competence 
of Roma: 
Look, for instance, in the museum I work in, this year we have several events 
dedicated to ethnic minorities like Hungarian minority that has two or three 
events or the Roma minority… On the local cultural agenda we see that Roma 
have three festivals per year, I mean, they are, there is… a good intention but 
the discussion is a bit complicated and I would like to refer here to those 
festivals dedicated to Roma, because, you see, there are good intentions, they 
receive funds from the local municipality, their festivals are hosted in the Big 
Square of Sibiu let’s say, but they are not helped with real matters, I mean if 
you let them on their own they show deficiencies of managing their events. 
They always have issues with ensuring adequate sound that is very bad, or 
whatever…. 
(ELAB_17, man, 39 years old) 
In other instances, cultural stakeholders reinforce the negative image of Roma 
as a poor community seeking by all means to obtain material benefits from the 
mainstream population and to reduce their interest in cultural participation only to 
financial gains, contrary to other ethnic groups in Sibiu. This stereotypical view is 
echoed in the interview with a middle-aged woman working as a museum curator 
in Sibiu for 20 years: 
Concerning Roma, things are a bit slower because they are not that open. 
When there is openness, all they understand through participation or allow -
ing for their child’s participation in an event, is only financial: If you pay me, 
I come. 
(ELAB_04, woman, 44 years old) 
The “politics of misrecognition” ( Fraser, 1995a, 2000) are transparent in these 
interview excerpts with local experts who contest Roma people’s genuine inter -
est in cultural values and their proper management skills. Other views find that 
Roma culture, as well as other ethnic cultures in Sibiu, is too much oriented to the 
past and to old traditions and this lack of “modernisation” could deter non-Roma 
participants from attending events dedicated to activities that are stuck in outdated 
schemes: “Roma culture is not enough modernised, like Saxons’ culture, is too 
much oriented to the past, to the traditional clothing and folk music. Well, there are 
some aspects that are missing however because cultures must be livelier, modern -
ised” (ELAB_17, man, 39 years old). 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
46 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Likewise, some interviewees raise criticism regarding the content of Roma 
events and the commodification of their otherness, echoing findings from literature 
of ethnic festivals from other European cities such as Copenhagen (orientalism, 
exoticization and “eating the other”, see Koefoed and Neergaard 2020). The fol-
lowing excerpt from an interview with a manager of local cultural events points 
to the need to genuinely create bridges between Roma and the local mainstream 
population through Roma festivals that can serve as events where traditional crafts 
are integrated into the market and become cultural markers of this ethnic group: 
[Local minorities] we have them represented. Even for the Roma there are 
all kinds of programs: “Ethnic Minorities Path”. For the Saxons, the same. It 
seems to me that in the town, instead, events are focused on the Roma ethnicity 
which have nothing to do with Roma culture. Too much festivity and too much 
bluster, so to speak. Pigs on roster and so on… From my point of view, this is 
not what should be done in a festival aimed at the Roma ethnic group, where 
we have many quality craftsmen who deserve to be present and communicate 
with, otherwise the money is being lost, spend with all these festivities. Better 
support those Roma with a shop or something to sell their fabulous products – 
they are very well made, both wood and brass – or jewellery and so on. 
(ELAB_07, man, 40 years old) 
Aside from their traditional crafts, Roma have been also remarked for their 
music interpretation. More than being an act of creation, their music is mostly 
oriented for the commercial purpose in response to the aesthetic and recreational 
demands of the mainstream population. To better satisfy the tastes of local popula-
tions, Roma musicians use the instruments and themes that are common among 
those populations (Fraser 1995b[1992]). The stigmatisation of Roma music and 
dance led to the ambivalent attitude of authorities and society that oscillated be -
tween tolerance and prejudice, as observed in Roma communities from neigh -
bouring countries like Hungary: “while the authorities supported the dance house 
movement, they remained initially antipathetic toward Roma folk music” (György 
2019, 222). In the aftermath of the communism’s fall, Romania witnessed a moral 
panic in relation to the emergence of the extremely popular music genre called 
“manele” which is associated with Roma aesthetics condemned by Romanian 
elites for the allegedly inferior cultural value of those songs inviting to irrational 
spending, unconventional sexual relationships with multiple concurrent partners, 
and other immoral behaviours (Sendroiu and Mogosanu, 2019). The latter authors 
argue that the stigmatisation of manele, stereotypically constructed as a Roma cul-
tural byproduct, acts as a tool of boundary-making between Roma and non-Roma, 
regardless of the ethnic composition of producers and consumers of this music. As 
a result of this stigmatisation, the Romanian mainstream media banned this music, 
although it continues to be highly consumed on online streaming platforms. One 
organiser of an intellectual film festival conveys the struggles of unmaking these 
boundaries, that cultural stakeholders can successfully undertake owing to their 
position in the complex web of power relations between intellectual elites, mass 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Public Perceptions of Roma Representation in Cultural Festivals 47 
media, Roma representatives, and general public. She recalls the ambivalence 
among the public when faced for the first time with the participation of a popular 
Roma manele singer invited into a Q&A session that followed the screening of a 
Roma film in a symphonic concert hall in Sibiu: 
… the Romanian hipsters and intellectuals were dismayed: “What’s going 
on?” and (laughter) in every way. It was in all the bubbles; it was live and 
streamed and it wasn’t a consensus Q&A… (laughs) So how do I say it? I 
made this jam and – how can I say it? – the clash and opening of a thing 
that can be done at a cultural event, where, for a moment, somehow, semi-
controlled, but still, more freely, you can facilitate the meeting of people who 
never practically meet. 
(ELAB_11, woman, 53 years old) 
This quote from the interview with an experienced film festival organiser show-
cases the potential of tactful and well-planned cross-cultural encounters in spaces 
perceived as belonging to highbrow culture to reduce the barriers among Roma 
and non-Roma population. The role of festival organisers is therefore crucial in 
de-centring the consecrated cultural elites so as to make cultural festivals more 
inclusive and challenge the stigma associated with Roma. 
Conclusions 
The chapter builds on the idea that festivals can represent powerful tools for social 
integration and heritage participation for various groups of population. Several con-
textual elements are important for our study. On the one hand, the Roma minority 
occupies a critical position in Romania and other CEE countries in terms of size 
and cultural heritage. This minority has a long history of racism and continues to 
face major challenges concerning discrimination, underrepresentation in public po-
sitions of power, and social recognition of its distinct character. The assimilation 
policies during the communist period contributed to the stigmatisation of this ethnic 
minority, and the post-communist transition period has seen very slow progress in 
addressing important social issues for this minority. On the other hand, there is a gap 
in research regarding how the general population and cultural stakeholders perceive 
Roma festivals. Employing a mixed methods research framework and empirical 
data from Sibiu, a vibrant cultural county in the central part of Romania, we aimed 
to contribute to a better understanding of the factors influencing the population’s at-
titudes towards Roma festivals and to explore how these festivals are seen by those 
who contribute to the cultural life of this region. An important contribution of our 
study resides in the mixed methods approach of data gathered through both a survey 
on population attitudes and interviews with diverse actors such as the festival organ-
isers, artists, and municipalities’ representatives in the cultural domain. 
The quantitative approach allowed us to use survey data to show that only a small 
percentage of the general population holds negative attitudes towards Roma festi -
vals (about 10%). However, the logistic regression model reveals a distinct profile 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
48 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
of those who are more likely to fall into the category with such attitudes. These at-
titudes are more frequent among men, younger individuals, people living in house-
holds with lower income levels, and those who declare higher religiosity. A greater 
interest in cultural activities and the perception that the existing festivals are diverse 
are associated with a lower probability of negative attitudes towards Roma festi -
vals. Lastly, those who believe that other ethnic groups (excluding Romanians and 
Roma) are overrepresented in current festivals have a higher odds ratio for nega -
tive attitudes towards Roma festivals. While the demographic and socio-economic 
characteristics introduced in this model indicate the need for communication about 
Roma festivals to better target specific audience segments, the aspects related to 
cultural interest and adequate representation of ethnic minorities in existing festivals 
suggest the need for transparency regarding their inclusion in the cultural agenda of 
the region and the appreciation of the added value that such festivals can bring to the 
entire population and not exclusively to the Roma minority. 
Interviews with stakeholders provide complementary insights and indicate a 
general perception of good representation of the Roma minority in the cultural life 
of the region. They suggest that Roma festivals are included in cultural agendas 
funded by public sources and their presence in public spaces with visibility and 
symbolic value for the city’s life is acknowledged. However, interestingly, in the 
discourse, it can be observed that those organising Roma festivals are considered 
to have some deficiencies in organisation, a lack of creativity in the type of activi-
ties, and a tendency to stay rooted in the past. These observations highlight a need 
for more substantial support from established cultural institutions and the utility 
of developing partnerships between cultural institutions and representatives of the 
Roma minority interested in organising such festivals. 
The chapter introduces a new perspective on heritage participation research re -
garding ethnic festivals dedicated to the Roma minority and highlights the need for 
in-depth research to understand how the general population participates in these 
festivals and, more importantly, what social effects they produce. The limit of the 
present study lies in the fact that the data capture a snapshot rather than a longitu -
dinal picture that would allow us to analyse trends and effects, which could be an 
important topic for future research.

3 Local cultural festivals as spaces 
of inclusion 
The festival organisers’ perspective 
Raluca Muresan 
Festivals are of special interest from the perspective of the social role they can 
fulfil in the community. In addition to their contribution to the economic develop -
ment of areas and the attraction of tourists, festivals also bring important social 
benefits to the communities that  host them. The social goals that can be pursued 
by festival organisers include social inclusion and engagement, enhancing the feel-
ings of belonging to the community, increasing tolerance, and accepting diversity. 
Festivals “bring people together” and can be used by local authorities as a means 
of celebrating cultural diversity, facilitating connections and relationships between 
participants, but also the interaction between people of different ethnicities, ages, 
or belonging to different social categories. Social inclusion may be the stated aim 
of a festival and could be an outcome of festival involvement and participation, but 
assessing this success is not straightforward (Laing and Mair 2015, 255). 
In recent years, there has been a growing interest in event research, but most  
studies have focused on the economic impact or role of attracting tourists, with the 
social impact of events being less investigated. Event management, an emerging  
field, has been developing in recent years not only in the direction of event planning 
but also in that of the impact and roles of events on society in general. However,  
within studies of the social impact of events, the topic of their role in promoting  
inclusion, diversity, and tolerance is peripheral or niche (Hassanli, Walters, and Wil-
liamson 2020), and topics related to under-representation, marginalisation, and in-
tolerance have also received limited attention (Finkel, Sharp, and Sweeney 2018, 1). 
Little work, however, has been done to examine whether festival organisers 
are truly aiming to make their festivals spaces of inclusivity, attracting a wide and 
diverse audience and staff, as well as achieving a diversity of participation in their 
management and staging (Laing and Mair 2015, 252–253). 
The aim of this chapter is to contribute to a better understanding of the local cul-
tural festival sector in terms of accessibility, diversity, and inclusion. The novelty 
of this study is that it approaches this topic from the perspective of festival organis-
ers. For this purpose, we collected and analysed the opinions of 20 organisers of 
cultural festivals in the Sibiu area in order to identify the extent to which they aim 
to pursue certain  social objectives through the events they organise and whether 
they have adopted provisions or measures to ensure social inclusion, looking in 
particular at issues such as equal access to culture regardless of ethnicity, religion, 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-5 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
52 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
gender, or income, and whether they are aware of the recreational needs of people 
with disabilities. With over 100 cultural events and festivals held every year, Sibiu 
ranks among the top cities in Romania in terms of the local public’s interest in the 
consumption of cultural products and the consistent participation of its inhabitants 
in the cultural life of the city. By investigating current practices, we will try to de-
termine the extent to which these cultural events contribute to community develop-
ment, creating stronger and more inclusive communities. 
Local cultural festivals as spaces of inclusion 
Events and festivals have a significant long-standing cross-cultural value (Jones 
2012, 112). They have always been an intrinsic part of people’s lives, marking im-
portant milestones and achievements being used to celebrate and engage communi-
ties (Pernecky and Lück 2013). We can learn a lot about the cultural architecture of 
a society from how people gather and celebrate (Dashper and Finkel 2021). 
There are many definitions of the term festival, articulated at different times 
and in different academic disciplines, one of the most cited being that of Falassi 
(1987, 2): 
periodically recurrent, social occasion in which, through a multiplicity of 
forms and a series of coordinated events, participate directly or indirectly 
and to various degrees, all members of a whole community, united by ethnic, 
linguistic, religious, historical bonds, and sharing a worldview. 
Noting that there are numerous definitions of the festivals, whether formulated 
in dictionaries or encyclopaedias, in scientific literature, or in documents of inter -
national organisations, W. Cudny (2014, 643) reviewed them and proposed a short 
and unambiguous functional definition of the term, which sums up all the basic 
characteristics of festivals. According to Cudny (2014, 643), 
a festival is an organised socio-spatial phenomenon that is taking place at a 
designated time – outside the everyday routine – increasing the overall vol -
ume of social capital and celebrating selected elements of tangible and intan-
gible culture. 
Being primarily social phenomena, festivals offer, in addition to the undeniable 
economic benefits that they bring to the communities that host them, a variety of 
social benefits, predominantly positive ( Arcodia and Whitford 2007 , 15). Thus, 
we observe a polarisation of previous research on the role of festivals in achiev -
ing social goals, some of which praise the role of festivals in promoting social 
integration, and others accusing festivals of exacerbating existing inequalities and 
providing only a temporary illusion of equality and social cohesion ( Noh, Cole-
man, and Kim 2024, 2). 
Festivals are organised by communities for various reasons, such as presenting 
cultural heritage, promoting a positive image of the city, attracting more tourists, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Local Cultural Festivals as Spaces of Inclusion 53 
and providing cultural entertainment and recreational opportunities for residents. 
In addition to providing cultural and recreational opportunities, festivals foster a 
sense of community pride, help local communities develop their own identity and 
can improve the understanding between community members of different ethnici -
ties and cultural backgrounds. 
The consumption and production of festivals are two closely related domains, 
and the relationships between stakeholders are clearly shaped and influenced by 
power dynamics (Quinn 2009, 31), involving the level of interest in the event, 
financial resources, and influence in decision-making (V orobjovas-Pinta, Pearce, 
and Hardy 2024). Creating, organising, and hosting festivals and events that wel-
come everyone is essential to promote social inclusion. Through festivals, the par-
ticipation of minorities in cultural life can be encouraged in a way that limits the 
loss of their cultural identity and prevents them from remaining isolated from the 
majority community. A festival is often a recurring social occasion for members of 
a community to come together and share a worldview through ethnic, linguistic, 
religious, and historical bonds (Arcodia and Whitford 2007, 12). 
While there is a general expectation that events and festivals should be inclu-
sive and to foster diversity, tensions can arise about the goals and benefits for the 
community or between different subgroups of the host community. Festivals can 
be undemocratic platforms when organisers express their cultural stance by select-
ing events that celebrate their own cultural practices and engage with a nondiverse 
audience. Authors such as Duffy and Mair (2018) argued that while festivals, espe-
cially community festivals, can be very successful in creating a sense of belonging 
for a dominant population or group, other groups, such as migrants or the diaspora, 
may feel excluded, and such exclusion is not limited to cultural or ethnic identity. 
Thus, festivals can highlight or even amplify the differences between certain social 
groups. 
Moreover, critics accuse multicultural festivals of the commodification of “Oth-
erness” in which minority cultures are consumed by the dominant culture, which 
contributes to social divergence rather than inclusion by portraying other cultures 
as “the Other” (Duffy, Mair, and Waitt 2019). The exoticisation of “the Other”, a 
component of Western hegemony, transforms festivals into sites of cultural con-
sumption when the focus falls on the domination and commodification of “Other -
ness”. Cultural differences, ethnicity, and “otherness” become a kind of “spice” 
that can invigorate the culture of the majority community when, in a multicultural 
setting, the consumer position emphasises differences (Koefoed, de Neergaard, and 
Simonsen 2020). 
Accessibility, equity, diversity, and inclusion in the context of events 
According to Finkel and Dashper (2020, 475), accessibility is the first step towards 
an inclusive society, in which all people can play an active and meaningful role and 
must be ensured not only at the physical level but also socially, culturally, economi-
cally, and politically. In the context of festivals, accessibility refers to measures 
taken by event organisers to facilitate the participation of people with impairments, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
54 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
both permanent and temporary, both physical and mental, including perceived class 
and cultural barriers (Finkel et al. 2018, 2) and represents a growing concern in 
event design (Barrera-Fernández and Hernández-Escampa 2020, 6). Although ini-
tially the concept of accessibility focused on the difficulties faced by people with 
permanent reduced mobility, its scope has been extended to other conditions, such 
as visual or hearing impairments, but also to transitory conditions, such as people 
who carry heavy objects or who are accompanied by children (Barrera-Fernández 
and Hernández-Escampa 2020, 5). However, there are other barriers to equal ac-
cess, which are not necessarily physical and may relate to financial costs, align-
ing the content of the event with personal cultural values, or how welcome some 
people feel or how comfortable they feel sharing the space with other participants 
(Finkel and Dashper 2020, 478). 
Therefore, we refer to the event accessibility as a concept that covers a wide 
range of factors related not only to the physical venue but also to the content of 
the event, atmosphere, cultural values, ticket prices, and management (Finkel and 
Dashper 2020, 479). Accessibility is an essential part of inclusion and diversity 
(Milligan, Nieuwenhuijsen, and Grawi 2014, 106), and the diversity of active and 
receptive participants plays an important role in transforming accessibility into 
inclusion. Although they are often used together to “describe the processes and 
policies that promote representative diversity, enable the creation of inclusive en-
vironments, and promote fair treatment” (Noh et al. 2024, 7), the concepts of diver-
sity, equity, and inclusion are defined distinctly in the academic literature. 
Diversity refers to everything that differentiates one individual from another, 
including the full spectrum of human demographic differences, as well as different 
ideas, backgrounds, and opinions (Boden 2020). Key areas of diversity include 
cultural and ethnic identities, gender, disability, generation, LGBTQIA+, and reli-
gious affiliation (Duffy and Mair 2021). 
Equity is defined as fair treatment for all while seeking to identify and eliminate 
inequalities and barriers (Boden 2020). Equity differs from inclusion in that it re-
quires the correction of systemic or organisational injustices rather than achieving 
group or individual outcomes (Bernstein et al. 2020, 396). 
Inclusion involves a sense of cultural and environmental belonging and a sense 
of uniqueness (Boden 2020). According to Finkel, Sharp, and Sweeney, “inclusion 
differs from justice in that the former requires people to participate in society as 
it is constructed, and the latter requires intervention with a redistributive agenda 
to achieve equality” ( 2018, 2). Inclusion differs from diversity in being focused 
on incorporating each person into the organisational processes and culture, not 
just the compositional mix/blend of people (Bernstein et al. 2020, 396). Moreover, 
Nishii (2013) observed that a diverse representation is limited to producing assimi-
lation into the dominant culture, while inclusion is a characteristic of multicultural 
organisations. 
Since inclusion or exclusion can be favoured by certain practices ( Bernstein 
et al. 2020 , 397), by investigating the practices adopted by festival organisers, 
we can identify solutions to counteract the phenomenon of exclusion. Inclusion 
and exclusion are usually described as binary opposites, in which, to achieve the 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Local Cultural Festivals as Spaces of Inclusion 55 
former, the latter must be overcome or, in other words, inclusion defeats exclusion. 
However, as Edwards, Armstrong, and Miller (2001, 425) point out, 
all practices involve forms of inclusion/exclusion (…) and to talk of inclu-
sion as a binary opposite of exclusion, the one overcoming the other, is to be 
positioned in ways that deny the very acts of exclusion entailed by forms of 
inclusion. 
Therefore, we should look at inclusion/exclusion criteria, practices, and their effects 
rather than considering inclusion as a goal. Festivals reproduce certain inequalities 
that exist in society, and by investigating performance and participation in events, 
we can identify who and what are considered important (and unimportant) in a 
community, who has the authority to make decisions, and whose interests are being 
overlooked (Dashper and Finkel 2021). Because for every inclusion, there is also 
exclusion, festivals and events can build communities, but they can also disrupt re-
lationships between individuals who share certain experiences. Thus, festivals can 
highlight and exacerbate differences, they can divide and exclude, even if the inten-
tion of the organisers is to promote unity and inclusion (Smith and Ertem 2023, 3). 
The spaces in which events take place can be considered places “where inequalities 
are performed, re/negotiated and reiterated” (Dashper and Finkel 2021). 
Data and methods 
For this study we used an interpretive and qualitative approach. Data were col -
lected through semi-structured interviews with local festival organisers from 
Sibiu, Romania. In the framework of the project Establishing a Laboratory of 
Cultural Heritage in Central Romania (ELABCHROM), coordinated by the Lu -
cian Blaga University of Sibiu, six researchers from this university conducted 35 
semi-structured interviews with different stakeholders, such as representatives of 
the municipalities in charge of policies and activities related to cultural branding, 
NGOs with cultural profiles, directors of festivals, and administrators of forti -
fied fortresses in Saxon villages. From these, 20 interviews with organisers of 
festivals and cultural events in Sibiu were selected and subsequently analysed to 
identify the extent to which they aim to pursue certain social objectives through 
the festivals and cultural events they organise. The interviews were conducted in 
Romanian, and the author translated the texts included in the results section. As 
shown in Table 3.1, the festivals and events organised by the interviewees are in 
the fields of Music (5), Performing Arts (4), Heritage (3), Film/Photo/Multimedia 
(2), Interdisciplinary (2), Literature (2), and Visual Arts (2). 
The responses were subjected to a thematic analysis in order to identify a series 
of categories deductively derived from the four questions included in the interview 
structure: 
• Do you believe that all people have equal access/participate in culture, regard-
less of ethnicity, religion, gender, or income? 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
56 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage

• In general, would you say that people with disabilities have access to cultural 
events organised by your institution? Are there any provisions or measures in 
this respect? 
• How much are women present in the public space through cultural events? Can 
you give an example that you know of? 
• Are ethnic minorities sufficiently represented in local cultural events or cultural 
heritage? How would you describe the representation of the following ethnic 
groups in local cultural events: the Saxons, the Romanians, and the Roma? 
Using the deductive thematic analysis, we identified four key themes of social 
inclusion: (1) barriers to equitable access; (2) access for people with disabilities; 
(3) representation of women; and (4) representation of ethnic minorities. 
Socioeconomic status and education as barriers to cultural access 
When asked about equitable access to local cultural festivals, organisers identified 
certain barriers preventing everyone from accessing events, the most frequently 
mentioned being limited financial resources, geographical differences, and educa -
tion. Many local festivals are free, thus providing greater access to cultural activities 
for social groups with less financial resources (Arcodia and Whitford 2007; Carlsen, 
Ali-Knight, and Robertson 2007). Some organisers believe that because there are so 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
Local Cultural Festivals as Spaces of Inclusion 57 
many events where entry is free, access to culture is unlimited because all people 
can participate regardless of income level, as the following quote illustrates: 
With so many free events being organised, I don’t think a family, even with 
a low income, who wants to attend an event can’t because there are many 
events, for all ages, for all tastes, that are free. 
(I01) 
Every year in Sibiu, over 100 cultural events and festivals are organised with 
financial support from Sibiu City Hall. Because they are funded by public money, 
the access must be free if they are held outdoors in public spaces. For example, in 
2023, the projects accepted for funding were in the field of Music (42), Interdisci -
plinary (20), Performing Arts (13), Film/Photo/Multimedia (12), and Heritage (10). 
The fewest events were held in the fields of Visual Arts (6), Gastronomic Culture 
(5), and Literature (3). 
A wider access to culture can also be facilitated by the informality of an outdoor 
setting, as opposed to the much more formal setting imposed by theatre or opera 
houses, as shown by Carlsen et al. (2007). Moreover, Smith and Ertem (2023, 5) 
argue that when they are organised in parks, streets, or squares, festivals enhance 
the public character of the spaces in which they are held by encouraging differ -
ent groups of people to use them and providing opportunities for these groups to 
interact. 
In the case of festivals or events to which access is conditional on the purchase 
of a ticket, even if some cultural institutions, such as museums, offer certain facili-
ties, the degree of accessibility is very limited for people with low incomes. Access 
to some festivals is very expensive, making them inaccessible to many people, 
contributing to the deepening of differences between social groups, as the organiser 
of a documentary film festival quoted: 
It’s not normal, but unfortunately, this is the situation and not everyone 
has access. Everyone has access to street events, but for example, for some 
of the performances of the International Theatre Festival, prices are pro -
hibitive for many. This is the truth! And then no… I cannot say they have 
access. 
(I11) 
As Duffy and Mair (2018) show, there are various ways in which event or 
festival organisers can improve inclusion and access, even if there are high costs 
or access is conditional on the purchase of a ticket, and here we can give the 
example of the Sibiu International Theatre Festival, which has free programmes 
in public spaces accessible to a wider audience, and also indoor ticketed perfor -
mances attended by a smaller number of spectators who are willing to purchase 
tickets. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
58 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Another barrier that prevents equal access to culture is the location: people who 
live in rural area or in the suburbs of cities have less access to festivals or other 
cultural events. 
Romania’s rural population suffers a great deal from access to culture. For 
a child from a village in Sibiu County – ethnicity doesn’t even matter – 
access is limited. For that child to go to a museum, to a library, to a con -
cert, his parents have to buy him a ticket and take him usually to Sibiu or 
perhaps to an urban area closer to them (…) Unfortunately, I believe that at 
the moment, the biggest problem in Romania is not gender, nor income, but 
territoriality, and Romania has a big problem in creating cultural activities 
in rural areas. 
(I02) 
This opinion is also supported by the data of a study conducted in 2021 in Ro-
mania, which showed that people living in rural areas participated in cultural ac-
tivities less often than those living in urban areas, and cultural consumption in rural 
areas was significantly lower (Grigoras et al. 2021, 303). The organiser of a theatre 
festival talks precisely about his attempt to bring performances to areas on the pe-
riphery of the city or in rural areas, based on the observation that most events take 
place in the central area of the city: 
We went to audiences that don’t come to the theatre very often because they 
don’t have the opportunity. We specifically targeted rural areas. 
(I10) 
The impact of intersectional identities on barriers to access to culture was rec -
ognised by some respondents who noted that discrimination was amplified in these 
cases: 
There are communities where discrimination is both ethnic and financial. 
The main inequity regarding access to culture is for those with low incomes. 
And here is also an aggravation of the situation in that low income communi-
ties are often the Roma communities. 
(I16) 
According to a 2018 report on Roma inclusion in rural Romania, rural poverty 
often overlaps with areas with a strong representation of the Roma community 
(Toderita, Damian, and Ganea 2018). In the 2011 census, almost two-thirds of the 
number of people who declared themselves as Roma lived in rural areas, and 50% 
of marginalised rural sectors are Roma communities. The marginalisation of these 
communities is related not so much to the fact that they live in rural areas but to the 
precarious conditions in which they live: in overcrowded, isolated housing without 
utilities such as water, sewage, or electricity networks, without access to health 
services or other facilities such as schools or jobs (Toderita et al. 2018). 

 
  
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Local Cultural Festivals as Spaces of Inclusion 59 
Although in Romania, cultural consumption increases significantly with the 
level of education (Grigoras et al. 2021, 233), the lack of education as a barrier to 
access to culture is rarely mentioned in the organisers’ responses, with the discus-
sions in the interviews focusing mainly on the disadvantages caused by poverty 
and the area in which they live. As most local festivals are held in public spaces, 
are free of charge, and are usually aimed at a wide audience, this is not surprising. 
As Frey (2000) pointed out, many people attend festivals because they offer a more 
welcoming atmosphere and represent a more accessible environment for a diverse 
range of people. Perhaps this is why the lack of education is mentioned in the con-
text of literary festivals, where not everyone participates equally, because “first of 
all, it is something that educated people have access to, from the beginning” (I17). 
This barrier is more visible in the case of “high” cultural events, where people with 
poor education feel intimidated and inferior due to differences in status and cultural 
values. 
Access for people with disabilities 
According to the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Dis -
abilities, ratified by Romania in 2010, people with disabilities have the right to 
participate in cultural life, including festivals and cultural events. Recognising 
the rights of people with disabilities to participate in the production and con -
sumption of culture and ensuring their access in an equitable way, both physically 
and socially, are essential conditions for these individuals to be fully involved in 
community life. However, according to a 2021 study, people with disabilities in 
Romania participated as spectators in cultural activities in percentages and with 
a lower frequency than those without disabilities ( Grigoras et al. 2021 , 303). Re-
garding the active participation of people with disabilities as creators of cultural 
activities, the data are insufficient to provide a comprehensive picture of the cur -
rent situation in Romania, but the existing information indicates that it is probably 
low (Grigoras et al. 2021 , 302). The answers of festival organisers asked about 
access to culture for people with disabilities refer to both perspectives: receptive 
and active participation. 
Milligan et al. (2014) asserted that many physical barriers prevent people with 
disabilities from having full access to event venues. The consumption of cultural 
products depends on the existence of essential facilities for the accessibility of 
people with disabilities, such as ramps, access ways, or elevators for people in 
wheelchairs, places specially intended for these people, adapted sanitary groups, or 
the presence of sign language interpreters. 
The adoption of measures or practices that facilitate or even allow the access 
of people with disabilities in the spaces of some festivals can be considered a tool 
of social inclusion that the organisers should be concerned about, but according 
to the previously mentioned study ( Grigoras et al. 2021, 305) only in large cul -
tural centres, such as Sibiu, Cluj-Napoca, or Bucharest, event organisers take into 
account the presence of people with disabilities at cultural activities and also in 
these cities the infrastructure is more accessible. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
60 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
When asked about access for people with disabilities, most organisers referred 
exclusively to access for people with reduced physical mobility, which demon-
strates a very narrow perspective on understanding the needs of different types 
of disabilities. As the organiser of a street art festival pointed out, problems arise 
especially with indoor events, as some of the spaces allocated to cultural events do 
not have facilities for wheelchair access: “The infrastructure for them is precarious. 
Access is very difficult all over the country if you ask me. (…) I think indoor events 
are difficult, quite difficult really, for disabled people” (I15). In the case of outdoor 
events, access for disabled people is easier, as illustrated by the following quotes: 
“In open spaces, public access is quite easy” (I11) or “in the outdoor spaces, access 
is easy and allows access for people with disabilities” (I01). 
Almost all organisers reported that they were concerned about providing physi-
cal access to event venues, but the current state of accessibility requires significant 
improvement. Most festivals and cultural events are held in venues or buildings 
owned by local public authorities; therefore, it is the responsibility of these au-
thorities to invest in and make these venues accessible for people with disabilities. 
There is currently a low-level of accessibility for most essential facilities for the 
access of people with disabilities, as revealed by a 2020 nationwide inspection of 
127 venues and 199 museums and libraries in urban areas. The most frequently 
reported accessibility problems are the lack of adapted toilets and dedicated seating 
(Grigoras et al. 2021, 305). 
On the other hand, we observe that for many festivals that address the local 
community, organisers have a reactive approach: when “they asked us for access 
and we adapted and facilitated their access to events” (I12) or “whenever we are 
asked to provide additional support, we do it” (I06) or “there were requests for 
wheelchair access and we tried to solve it” (I07). Few organisers have a proac -
tive approach considering the special needs of people with various disabilities 
when designing and planning an event, and usually this is the case of the organ -
isers of major international festivals, such as the International Theatre Festival, 
who are concerned about these issues as they are linked to the expectations of 
an international audience. Even here, there is still much to be done to ensure 
equal access: 
We are very careful about all our spaces, to make access possible for people 
with disabilities. At the same time, we have developed the digital component 
of the festival because this aspect of accessibility is increasingly important. 
Of course, it is clear that we still have a lot of work to do, but we are always 
very careful. 
(I19) 
We notice that most of the answers of the interviewed organisers refer to the 
identification of some physical/architectural barriers, which prevent people with 
locomotor disabilities from accessing indoor spaces as consumers of cultural 
experiences, only to a very small extent considering the special needs of people 
with other types of disabilities, such as visual, hearing, learning, or related to a 

 
 
 
 
Local Cultural Festivals as Spaces of Inclusion 61 
long-term inability to carry out normal daily activities. Only the organiser of an 
educational festival talks about adapting the spaces that host cultural events to the 
needs of people with other types of disabilities: 
Not only our institution but absolutely every institution in Romania has a 
real problem with other disabilities: hearing or visual. (…) We are even in a 
strategy to open a special space dedicated to these people – a small space just 
for them – but we don’t even have objects with Braille letters. It’s not just our 
problem. It’s a national problem. 
(I02) 
When referring to the access of people with disabilities, organisers should be 
concerned not only with the inclusion of them in the audience of a festival, as re-
ceptive participants, but also with their active participation, by engaging them in 
the creation of cultural products. 
Some festivals have incorporated activities or programmes that address people with 
disabilities as cultural producers. The organiser of a literature festival commented: 
We have literary events, in addition to the festival, also organised for such 
communities, for example, the blind community. It is about broadening and 
including of the widest possible categories of the public in our events. Cul-
ture is not only about literature, but about caring for the other. 
(I16) 
Another positive example came from the organiser of a theatre festival, who 
talking about his eight-year-long experience of collaborating with a drama thera-
pist who works with adults and young people with disabilities, referred to it “one of 
the most emotional events in the whole festival” (I10), to emphasise the rewarding 
value of these inclusive experiences, which should be encouraged for the benefit 
of all participants. The inclusive nature of culture is not only given by the degree 
of accessibility but also by ensuring content that reflects a wide range of human 
experiences, including the experiences lived by people with disabilities, as pointed 
out an organiser: 
Immediately, I thought of some exercises that would make children without 
disabilities understand the effort and courage of those who cannot use their 
legs. (…) And that should be the role of culture, right? To lead us to more 
empathetic, inclusive, and polyphonic societies. To allow everyone’s voices 
to be heard. 
(I09) 
Previous research has shown that events can generate valuable and positive out-
comes for people with disabilities if they offer them the opportunity to try some-
thing new, develop new skills, and assert their identity, as well as drive change in 
service provision (Walters 2023). 

 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
62 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Gender equality 
Authors such as Coyle and Platt (2018) have shown that festivals have the potential 
to provide “spaces for women to gather as a collective”, and to provide a platform 
for female artists. However, although women constitute the majority of the creative 
workforce, they are underrepresented or ignored (Coyle and Platt 2018). In Roma-
nia, the percentage of women engaged in entertainment, cultural, and recreational 
activities was 76.3%, according to a 2021 study (Guga and Sindrestean 2021, 14). 
Adopting gender-aware frameworks in events analysis can provide insights into 
the importance of gender and how it shapes practice, sometimes in very subtle 
ways. When asked how present women are in the public space through cultural 
events, the opinions of festival organisers go in two directions. On the one hand, 
some believe that women are underrepresented and that although things are mov-
ing in the right direction, it will be some time before we have gender equality in 
this field. On the other hand, some believe that belonging to a certain gender is not 
a relevant criterion for festival organisers. 
Thus, several organisers of cultural events in literature noted the lack of rec-
ognition of the cultural value of women in the Romanian public space. We have 
selected the opinions of festival organisers from Sibiu: 
In literature, it’s not just about under-representation. It’s about zero represen-
tation, if I can call it that, of women writers. (…) There is a lot to build not 
only in Romania. Throughout the European space. This ignorance of half of 
humanity has been going on for a long time. Obviously, it takes a long time to 
change it. The struggle for the presence of women in the public space began 
around the time of World War II. I think it will be won, but it’s… It’s an effort. 
(I16) 
In other fields, in literature … it seems that the ratio tilts in favour of men. 
There are female authors, indeed, but overwhelmingly men. 
(I08) 
The opinions of the organisers of literary festivals in Sibiu converge with the 
results of other studies dedicated to the representation of women in literary festi-
vals held in other parts of the world. Thus, according to Dane (2020, 91), although 
at first glance, there seems to be a relatively strong representation of women in the 
programmes of Australia’s most prestigious literary festivals, on closer examina -
tion, it is found that white men continue to dominate, occupying more space and 
having more opportunities to participate in public discussions that define the field, 
frequently gaining access to positions that denote prestige and power. 
Organisers who believe that women are insufficiently represented in public 
spaces consider correcting this inequity in their events: 
And then, obviously, at all the events we organise, we aim to maintain gender 
parity, if not even – if possible – a dominance of female writers, because it’s a 

 
 
 
 
Local Cultural Festivals as Spaces of Inclusion 63 
statement. And that is the situation on the ground. I think that there are better 
female writers in Romania than male writers today. And then you must show it. 
(I16) 
According to Dane (2020, 121), a greater number of women involved in cultural 
events does not necessarily imply a strong representation, and equality is not en-
sured by an increase in the representation of a certain group if this increase means 
that the previously underrepresented group is tasked only with low-level work. 
Even if we notice that the organisers of literary festivals are especially aware of 
this problem and are looking for solutions to eliminate this inequality, this is not the 
only area where gender discrimination is manifest. Organisers of festivals in other 
fields, such as performance arts (dance and theatre), also talk about the fact that 
“Sibiu is patriarchal at the cultural level” (I09) or that the positions women hold in 
the organisation of cultural events are usually inferior, as assistant managers. 
Those who consider gender an irrelevant criterion when deciding the content 
of a festival or cultural event fall into two typologies: those who consider women 
to be well represented and those for whom such issues are invisible. Those in the 
first category organise festivals in areas close to the traditional role of women in 
the household, such as traditional crafts (sewing, weaving, folk dances, and songs) 
or fashion, and claim that: 
It has never been a criterion for either exclusion or inclusion. There was 
never a difference between women and men or between adults and children. 
(I04) 
In this field, we cannot truly conceive of any discrimination. Absolutely no 
discrimination. 
(I20) 
Those for whom gender is an invisible issue either deny it altogether: “I just 
don’t think there is such a topic” (I12) or consider it unimportant in the cultural 
field, as the organiser of a theatre festival pointed out: 
in culture it is very difficult to respect this 50-50 measure, to schedule… Be-
cause the cultural product is the most important, and if it is made by a woman 
or a man, it is for us – at least for me – on a secondary level. 
(I19) 
The participation of ethnic minorities in local cultural festivals 
When asked how well ethnic minorities are represented in the festivals and other 
cultural events organised in Sibiu, the organisers considered that “ethnic groups 
are now consistently involved in the cultural phenomenon” (I06) and that there is 
“a balance, yet being a multicultural and multi-ethnic city” (I14). Allowing ethnic 

 
 
  
 
64 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
minority groups to express and assert their differences from the majority ethnic 
group through festivals and other cultural events creates a framework for a more 
inclusive sense of belonging to the community, which can contribute to greater 
community solidarity and unity (Hassanli et al. 2020). 
However, going into more detail on the subject of the representation of ethnic 
minorities at festivals, it is interesting to note that many organisers notice differ -
ences between how Roma ethnicity is represented, compared to other cohabiting 
ethnicities, such as the Saxons and the Hungarians. Thus, regarding the Saxons, the 
opinion shared by several event organisers is that this minority is “fairly well repre-
sented” (I05), “obviously, the Saxons from Sibiu have all kinds of events” (I11), or 
“the German minority is represented” (I16). Similarly, for the Hungarian minority, 
the organisers believe that the festivals organised by members of this community 
have visibility: “there are at least two or three events organised by Hungarian com-
munities” (I14), “Hungarikum Days” and “Ars Hungarica” being two of the festi-
vals given as examples by the interviewed organisers. 
Organisers voiced their opinions that there is a different perception of the Roma 
minority compared to the other ethnic minorities, and in particular to the Saxon 
minority, as it results from these quotes: “the Saxon minority receives enough at -
tention (….), but compared to the Roma people, I think that somehow they are seen 
differently” (I17); “as far as the Saxons are concerned, we even have a museum unit 
(…) and a consistent collection on Saxon ethnography, (…) on the Roma area, we 
have a person assigned to deal with that area” (I06). Regarding the reasons behind 
this lack of representation, the organisers have different explanations, which have 
to do either with the reluctance of people belonging to the Roma minority, or with 
the prejudices of the majority community. Thus, according to one organiser: “It is a 
group with which, in my opinion, it is quite difficult to interact with” (I05), and the 
organiser of a documentary film festival narrates an episode in which a commercial 
space in Sibiu refused to host a multimedia exhibition about Roma culture, which 
accompanied a documentary film screened within the festival, although they were 
open to such a collaboration before the subject of the exhibition was known (I07). 
One potential reason for this different treatment may be related to social accept-
ance of these ethnic minorities. According to a study conducted in 2020, which 
aimed to measure the level of acceptance of ethnic minorities, social tolerance is 
stronger for the Saxons, who are accepted as neighbours, while for Roma, Jews, 
as well as Hungarians, the median social acceptance is on a lower rung – living in 
the same city – and immigrants are on the lowest rung, being accepted to live in 
Romania (IRES, 2020). 
Another explanation for this may be that efforts to represent diversity often 
ignore how intergroup interactions actually occur. Representation offers the pos-
sibility of inclusion, but as shown by Bernstein et al. (2020, 398), it is an aggregate 
concept, usually measured at the level of a geographical unit, and when it is disag-
gregated into smaller organisational units, the interaction between groups is found 
to be superficial and therefore not inclusive. 
A better representation of the Roma minority in cultural events could help 
this ethnic group mitigate the negative effects of marginalisation by challenging 

 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
   
 
Local Cultural Festivals as Spaces of Inclusion 65 
stereotypical representations (Hassanli et al. 2020). But when a person belonging 
to an ethnic minority has a low socioeconomic status, the marginalisation is fur -
ther intensified. And if we also consider rural life or geographically remote living, 
we see how the intersectional nature of these factors exacerbates the problem of 
marginalisation (Walters and Shaheer 2021). Experiences of marginalisation are 
unique to specific configurations of identity and are not equivalent to the sum of an 
individual’s oppressed identities (McConnell et al. 2016), but in this case, Roma 
ethnicity, poverty, and living in precarious conditions in rural areas are factors that 
contribute to the complete exclusion of these individuals from cultural events. To 
understand how access to culture can be facilitated for this vulnerable, marginal-
ised group there needs to be an intersectional approach that considers all the factors 
that contribute to exclusion or “invisibility”. 
Conclusions 
This chapter analyses of the opinions of the organisers of local cultural festivals in 
Sibiu, Romania, on the role of festivals in increasing social inclusion and improv-
ing opportunities for vulnerable social groups to participate in cultural activities. 
The results of this research can lead to a better understanding of the practices of 
festival organisers and influence how festivals and cultural events can be used to 
promote social inclusion goals. However, further research is needed to investigate 
the local cultural festival sector in relation to other areas of diversity not covered in 
this research, such as generation, LGBTQIA+, and religious affiliation. 
The main limitation of this study is related to the fact that the data obtained 
refer to a small area, a city in the centre of Romania, and a sample of 20 organisers 
of cultural festivals; therefore, the results cannot be generalised on a wider scale. 
Nevertheless, the study reveals interesting results regarding the way in which fes-
tival organisers in Sibiu – a city recognised for the diversity of cultural events it 
hosts especially since 2007, the year in which it was designated European Capital 
of Culture – try to encourage the participation of some vulnerable social groups and 
the preservation of cultural practices of ethnic minorities. However, improvements 
are needed in terms of what festival organisers can do to deliver better inclusion 
outcomes, particularly with regard to marginalised and vulnerable communities, 
the special needs of people with disabilities other than locomotor disabilities and 
gender equality.
