4 Branding Germanness in 
Transylvania 
Combined and Uneven Heritagisation 
Ovio Olaru 
Seen by many as a beacon of civilisation during the tumultuous 1990s, the myth 
surrounding the German ethnic group in Transylvania has lingered in the literal/ 
physical absence of Germans from the Romanian provinces, with the Saxon popu-
lation in Romania not exceeding approximately 30,000, according to the 2021 
census (Institutul Național de Statistică 2021 ). However, the cultural symbols as -
sociated with Germanness are currently instrumental in Transylvanian regional 
branding. Drawing on the well-documented history of German presence in Tran -
sylvania, the present chapter will pursue the German integration and brand crea -
tion in the Sibiu region, referring to its cultural and historical implications. The 
cultural level refers first and foremost to the continuity of German culture in  
Transylvania – manifest in language, Saxon architecture, way of life, literature 
and theatre, and so on – enjoying the status of a class marker and aspiration to this 
day. Which also explains why the city is fondly referred to by its German name, 
Hermannstadt. Second, the historical analysis will focus on the German ethnic 
component in Transylvania and discuss the paradoxes and contradictions of “good 
imperialism” in inter-imperial Transylvania ( Boatcă and Pârvulescu 2022 ) while 
drawing on the relationship between the Romanian population and their former, 
more privileged co-nationals. These contradictions would explain the protest ef -
fected by the erection of Samuel von Brukenthal’s statue in the centre of Sibiu. 
Lastly, I will focus on the different patterns of participation in heritage: for Roma-
nians, acknowledging the German regional brand entails access to the higher fora 
of Transylvanian culture, whereas foreigners, especially Germans, experience the 
amazement of discovering a German-speaking, seemingly Europeanised island in 
the “Balkan” periphery. 
Combined and Uneven Heritagisation 
In his 2019 book, Romania and the Quest for European Identity: Philo-Germanism 
without Germans, Cristian Cercel argues for the existence of a very strong Roma -
nian pro-German sentiment despite the virtual disappearance of German ethnics 
from Romania during the 1980s and especially following the 1989 Romanian revo-
lution. It has very far-reaching roots, finding its sources in the so-called Ostsied-
lung, the German colonisation of the (then) far-eastern reaches of Europe. It started 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
Branding Germanness in Transylvania 69 
in the 12th century, with the Holy Roman Empire sending German citizens to the 
territories of modern-day Romania, Hungary, the Baltic states, and so on, in order 
to conduct agriculture, defend the empire against invading troops, and virtually 
colonise fertile, albeit less densely populated areas. On account of its German- and 
Hungarian-born financial, social, and cultural elites (Manz 2014, 21) during its 
history, Transylvania was under Hungarian and Austrian rule and joined the Ro -
manian state only after the dissolution of the Austro-Hungarian Empire in 1918. 
Between 1867 and 1918, Transylvania was in an “interimperial” chokehold, to 
borrow Boatcă and Pârvulescu’s (2022) take on Laura Doyle’s concept of “inter -
imperiality” (2020). In their book, Pârvulescu and Boatcă analyse the canonical 
1920 novel Ion by Liviu Rebreanu in showing the different layers of oppression 
and underprivilege suffered by a generic Romanian imperial peasant subject – and 
the types of conflict these layers generate. In the postcommunist period, however, 
this type of predicament became an inverted “imperialist nostalgia,” to borrow 
Renato Rosaldo’s (1993) concept. Within this narrative of “good imperialism,” it 
is not necessarily so “that agents of colonialism long for the very forms of life 
they intentionally altered or destroyed” (Rosaldo 1993, 69) but that the colonised 
themselves long for a form of colonial paternalism; hence its inverted nature. How-
ever, the dichotomy between the past – wherein the Germans had both the power 
and the claims of superior culture – and the present – wherein their culture is cel -
ebrated in their absence, thus ignoring former power relations, leads to its success-
ful commodification. 
In regard to its cultural heritage, Transylvania has a rich multicultural history, 
whereby four main cultures developed somewhat in unison, albeit being strongly 
hierarchised: the Saxons, the Hungarians, the Romanians, and the Roma. Nowa -
days, of course, any discussion about the region refers mainly to a joint cultural 
heritage, as one of Transylvania’s selling points – especially as portrayed by ethnic 
German literature, of which Eginald Schlattler’s The Beheaded Rooster  is most 
telling – is the denomination of Vielvölkerstaat , a state where numerous ethnic 
backgrounds presumably coexisted peacefully in a sort of utopian ahistoricism, 
safe from class divides, economic disparities, racism, discrimination, and so on. A 
very commodifiable trait, especially since the implicit undertone is that custodians 
of this presumably homogenous, internally equal, single space were the Transyl-
vanian Saxons. 
At the beginning of the 20th century, approximately 750,000 Germans were 
living on the territories of modern-day Romania, of which 250,000 were Saxons 
(Koranyi and Wittlinger 2013, 98). Following the war, their number understand-
ably decreased massively since many of them joined the Waffen-SS and lost their 
lives on the front or became POWs. If they did return, they were deported to So-
viet labour camps in January 1945, their properties were nationalised, and they 
faced massive backlash in the immediate postwar period. In 1966, around 383,000 
Germans still lived on Romanian territory (Motzan 1999, 139). During the later 
communist period, there were significant attempts at cultivating their presence in 
Romanian cultural life by establishing German-language cultural journals and pub-
lishing houses, albeit the community was still under careful scrutiny. The 1959 

 
 
 
 
70 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
collective show trial against several German ethnic writers accused of having con-
spired against state order is indicative of the growing suspicion towards them dur-
ing that time. During the 1980s, many emigrated to West Germany on account 
of the growing worsening of the Romanian standard of living – in exchange for 
German financial assistance. Later on, according to Koranyi and Wittlinger (2013), 
around 200,000 Germans left the country between 1989 and 1991, mostly to the 
Federal Republic. 
However, on account of the privileges enjoyed by the Hungarians, the Szeklers, 
and the Saxons, historically recognised as nationes within Transylvania and as 
defenders of Christianity, the remaining Saxons internalised a form of ethnic su -
periority which came to inform the articulation of their collective identity in the 
postcommunist period: 
Experience and memory of privilege—enjoyed not just in the 20th century 
under Hitler but also previously as colonisers—but, more importantly, expe-
rience and narratives of suffering towards the end of the Second World War 
and during the Cold War thus constituted key pillars on which the collective 
identity of these ethnic German groups was based and that provided the foun-
dation for their relationship with the “homeland.” 
(Koranyi and Wittlinger 2013, 98) 
Ultimately, this led to a discourse whereby ethnic Germans were and still are the 
harbingers of positive change, gatekeepers of European ideals, but where “[e]thno-
cultural preservation [against invading hordes from the East – my emphasis] was 
remoulded into nationalist intention even for a pre-national period” (Manz, 22). 
All of this, however, took place in an inherently uneven Vielvölkerstaat where one 
language/culture had precedence over the others. This helps explain why the first 
attempts made by Saxon Enlightenment intellectuals aspiring to introduce cultural 
reform in Transylvania drew on a preexisting “civilisational” hierarchy in which 
Wallach/Romanians were seen as “still at the lowest stage of civilisation,” with 
“scholarship and the sciences” not even “missed among them” ( Neugeboren 1790 
[Sienerth 1996, 68]). These assertions, far from being justifiable even from the 
standpoint of Enlightenment, with its claims on tolerance, show “the aversion of 
the privileged elite vis-à-vis the underprivileged majority” (Török 2015, 49). This 
aversion was to return during the interwar Erneuerungsbewegung [“rejuvenation 
movement”] when Romania’s German population sided with Nazi Germany, and 
approximately 54,000 ethnic Germans joined the Waffen-SS (Koranyi and Wit-
tlinger 2013, 100). 
In contemporary discourses on Transylvanian cultural heritage, the Saxon com-
ponent is given centre stage, whereas the Romanian, Roma, or Hungarian ones are 
overlooked, what Andrea Corsale and Monica Iorio refer to as “intentional amne -
sia” (Corsale and Iorio 2014, 22) in discussing the case of the famous village of 
Viscri. Their analysis is very interesting since the village itself is an experiment in 
touristification and inadvertently illustrates its flaws. Part of the UNESCO World 
Heritage List, thanks to the Mihai Eminescu Trust, an NGO established in 1999 

 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
Branding Germanness in Transylvania 71 
focusing on the preservation of traditional Saxon villages and architecture, 1 Viscri 
[Weisskirch] is a village situated in Bunești, Brașov County, Transylvania, with 
an impressive fortified Evangelical church built during the 13th century. The vil-
lage is regarded as a typically multiethnic microcosm wherein the class hierarchies 
established throughout its century-long history have been carefully maintained 
and preserved. The remaining Saxon elites are at the top of the village hierarchy, 
while Romanians are numerically dominant yet subservient, and the barely toler -
ated Roma are delegated to the village outskirts (Corsale and Iorio 2014, 22–23). I 
hereby find the concept of “authorised heritage discourse” [AHD] very useful, as it 
focuses attention on aesthetically pleasing material objects, sites, places and/ 
or landscapes that current generations “must” care for, protect and revere so 
that they may be passed to nebulous future generations for their “education”, 
and to forge a sense of common identity based on the past. 
(Smith 2006, 29) 
While focusing on “the proper care of heritage and its associated values” (Smith 
2006, 29), which indeed sounds ideal, AHD raises the question not only of “neb -
ulous future generations” that would benefit from preservation but also that of 
agency. Who decides over the proper care of heritage and its associated values, 
and who authorises the heritage discourse in this regard? Most importantly, whose 
heritage is worthy of being preserved and whose is dismissible? The issue raised 
by Corsale and Iorio in regard to Viscri was that this discourse and the associated 
values were not articulated collectively but were the result of an expert consensus 
from outside the community. 
[C]ommunity’ and “heritage” are comfortably self-evident, defined by place 
and shared histories, and often ethnicity and nationality, and redolent of shared 
values and their celebration. However, communities take many forms, are of-
ten riven by dissent, and bear the burden of uncomfortable histories. They are 
often defined by the articulate and the privileged, who are readily recognised 
by policymakers and professionals, leaving others to some peripheral status. 
(Smith and Waterton 2013, 12) 
This expert consensus entailed that, even after the Saxon population had virtu -
ally disappeared after 1989, the Romanian and Roma in the village were appointed 
with becoming the docile custodians of Saxon heritage ( Corsale and Iorio 2014 , 
27), being made morally responsible for the wellbeing of the community and for 
its financial success via touristification. Failure to do so entailed a stigma regarding 
the inability to valorise what is connoted as being an inestimable heritage treasure, 
which in turn has the potential to reinforce negative stereotypes regarding an innate 
Romanian or Roma disinterest in heritage and insufficient participation. Those who 
once had a peripheral social status are now tasked with taking care of a heritage 
they had little role in shaping. This creates the prerequisites for a musealisation of 
inequality – and at the same time, ever-increasing access to and control of heritage 

 
 
72 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
for the privileged – as AHD demands the preservation of values and the intangible 
cultural heritage of which inequality is an integral part, leading to “the replication, 
in different ways, of the historical social pyramid and cultural hierarchy” (Corsale 
and Iorio 2014, 29). 
As a consequence, the Saxon minority occupies a significant place on the Sibiu 
cultural scene and is very visible when compared to other minorities through in -
stitutions such as the German Democratic Forum and the Friedrich Teusch Cul-
tural Centre. This has led to the “commodification of [an] identity” (Corbillé 2017) 
which is not that of its custodians – sometimes themselves belonging to an ethnic 
minority – while at the same time emphasising, in a paradoxical twist, “nature and 
the authenticity of the product [my emphasis; product, here, can designate this very 
identity]” (Harvey 2002, 216). These claims on authenticity are contradicted by the 
very realities of the tourism industry: Roma aides help in the Saxon-themed guest-
houses, Romanian ethnics cook and serve traditional Saxon food while dressed in 
traditional Saxon attire, they learn German and serve as tour guides for foreign 
tourists, Roma coach and carriage drivers are added for local specificity, and so 
on. Whenever German ethnics or even German citizens are involved in these pro -
cesses, they are, as Corsale and Iorio (2014) argue in their article, in positions of 
power: business owners, entrepreneurs, or investors who had seized this opportu -
nity either for profit or from a selfless interest for the preservation of Saxon values. 
However, as David Harvey argued in his book on capitalist geography, 
[e]ven seemingly new avenues for self-expression (multi-culturalism being a 
prime recent example) are captive to the forces of capital accumulation and 
the market (for example, love of nature is made to equal ecotourism, and 
ethnicity is reduced to a matter of restaurants or authentic commodities for 
market). 
(Harvey 2002, 201) 
Even if driven by a sense of ethnic belonging that disregards possible profits, this 
form of “reenactment” naturally becomes “authentic” in the sense of perpetuating 
preexisting hierarchies, while past and present Romanian, Roma, or Hungarian 
heritage in preponderantly Saxon communities is disregarded either as indistin -
guishable background noise or exotic addition. 
This accelerated commodification of foreign tangible and intangible heritage 
has taken place in many Saxon settlements, but is best illustrated by Sibiu becom-
ing the European Capital of Culture in 2007, a case analysed in-depth by Monica 
Stroie (2017), who argues that “displaying multiculturalism,” one of the most 
compelling selling points of Transylvanian specificity, “was reduced to displaying 
Germanness, which then became a local counterpart to multiculturalism and Euro-
peanness” (Stroie 2017, 100). This 2007 moment coincided with Romania’s ascen-
sion to the European Union, which in public opinion was equated with Germany 
since it was one of the most important European economic players and was already, 
alongside Italy and Spain, one of the most desirable destinations for Romanian 
migrant workers. On the other hand, the excessive emphasis laid on Sibiu, Viscri, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Branding Germanness in Transylvania 73 
Brasov, Sighișoara, and a few other former Saxon settlements illustrates the exist-
ence of a “combined and uneven” process of “heritagisation” (Ashworth 2011), 
a play on Trotsky’s “combined and uneven development” ( Trotsky 1932). This 
meant that preservation efforts followed a symbolic capital that had already been 
accrued – through the title of European Capital of Culture, for instance – favouring 
sites which needed less preservation and were readily available for tourism and 
leaving other, less picturesque sites in disrepair.
2 Another step that had prepared 
the 2007 moment was made in 2004, when the Robert Bosch Foundation, in col -
laboration with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Romanian-German Cultural 
Association, opened the Romanian-German Cultural Centre in Sibiu. 
During the antebellum, interwar, and communist periods, there was a very 
clearly felt distinction between the two renditions of Germanness in the Romanian 
cultural sphere, the Transylvanian Saxons and the distant Germans from Germany. 
However, the massive migration of German ethnics in the postcommunist period – 
primarily to Germany, where they became naturalised citizens and taxpayers with 
increasingly looser ties to their Romanian roots – gradually erased that distinction, 
so that the Saxon Germanness and the “German” Germanness became one and the 
same in Romanian public opinion. As a consequence, the Saxon heritage sites were 
lacking custodians after 1989 and new flagbearers had to be found to fill in this ap-
parent representational void. As I argued in a 2021 contribution (Olaru 2021), those 
who felt compelled to continue the civilising mission of the initial German settlers 
were the philo-German, “cultured,” and well-travelled Romanian intelligentsia. 
They drew on antiquated and ethnocentric stereotypes, pushing the agenda that 
Romanian communism had hindered the civilising process, that the presumably 
inferior Balkans could learn from the superior habitus of the Saxon population, 
import their work ethic, and so on. 
However, the unveiling of the statue of Samuel von Brukenthal in September 
2021 in front of the Brukenthal Museum in the centre of Sibiu, facing the Great 
Square, was followed by protests marked by strong nationalistic sentiments but 
which constructed an openly decolonial discourse. Their recurring chant, repro -
duced in the title of one of the press articles covering the story, was “Let us 
forgo statues honouring foreign rulers!” The more than 200 protestors, led by 
the “The People’s Way” Association and the so-called “Avram Iancu” cultural-
patriotic Society, claimed that Brukenthal signed the death sentences of Roma -
nian revolutionaries Horea, Cloșca, and Crișan,
3 that he oppressed Romanians, 
and so on (Digi24 2021). Albeit generally frowned upon as an expression of 
vulgar nationalism, the protest gave voice to a form of implicit resistance both to 
the celebration of Saxon heritage – seen as a threat to national character – and to 
the historical predicament of Romanian subjects in the past. 
Culture and the Arts in Transylvania and Banat 
Another instance of combined and uneven heritagisation is visible in the cultural 
production of or on behalf of German ethnics from Romania, manifest in lan -
guage, Saxon architecture, way of life, literature, theatre, and so on. Regarding the 

 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
74 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
first category, one of the most visible examples was Herta Müller being awarded 
the Nobel Prize for literature in 2009,
4 “who, with the concentration of poetry 
and the frankness of prose, depicts the landscape of the dispossessed,” according 
to the motivation of the Nobel committee (The Nobel Prize 2009 ). Born on 17 
August 1953 in the small village of Nitzkydorf in Banat, Romania, she had been 
residing in Germany for decades prior to receiving the prize and “had hardly been 
a household name in Romania before the Nobel Prize drew worldwide attention to 
her work” (Glajar 2013, 50). Understandably, the prize also drew significant criti-
cism from Romanian intellectuals arguing that she owes her visibility solely to 
circumstances pertaining to Romanian history and that the German ethnic compo-
nent in her works was negligible; on the other hand, she has formulated repeated 
criticism against the intellectual strata which did not resist the communist regime, 
despite their claim at having performed “resistance through culture,” that is, by 
cultivating abstract ideals and keeping a low profile while eschewing politicisa -
tion (Glajar 2013, 53). 
While becoming fundamental for an entire type of anticommunist discourse, 
complemented by a strong lyrical voice, Müller’s rise to fame overshadows what 
was already a so-called “micro-literature” ( Diaconu 2018, 135–156) by shifting 
the focus from ethnic specificity to ideology and exhausting all possible inter -
est for this particular cultural milieu by conferring it a marketable face. This, in 
turn, creates the prerequisites for vicarious cultural tourism, whereby objectively 
documented insights into the recent history of Saxon communities are replaced 
by this alternative “authorised heritage discourse,” believable precisely because 
it was written in the language of Weltliteratur  by a German – hence authentic – 
voice. Furthermore, the “dispossessed” the Nobel committee was referring to are 
the German ethnics themselves – it is implicit that their experience, which was 
elevated to the status of intangible cultural heritage in part through Müller, is spe -
cific to them alone, as if Romanian, Hungarian, and Roma communities did not 
suffer during communism and were not dispossessed. 
Another example of promoting Germanness in a combined and uneven man -
ner in Sibiu is Silviu Purcărete’s rendition of Goethe’s Faust in Ștefan Augustin 
Doinaș’ canonical Romanian translation. The play, which was initially staged on 
the premises of an abandoned industrial hall, was a massive success since it pre -
miered in 2007 and is still sold out at every edition of The Sibiu International 
Theatre Festival, FITS [Festivalul Internațional de Teatru de la Sibiu]. Lasting ten 
full days, usually in June, the festival won numerous awards for acting and scenog-
raphy and is the third largest theatre festival in the world, after the Edinburgh Fes-
tival Fringe and The Avignon Festival, according to its official website.
5 Of course, 
Faust itself cannot cast a shadow over any other play in the festival, but out of the 
entire German theatrical repertoire of Sibiu, it is the most visible. 
This brings us to the question of representativity and heritage participation. Sibiu, 
with its rich Saxon heritage, is compelled to preserve the German-language theatre 
tradition. The German-language theatre department is part of the “Radu Stanca” Na-
tional Theatre – unlike the German Theatre in Timișoara, which is independent from 
its Romanian-language counterpart. Albeit the plays feature English and Romanian 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Branding Germanness in Transylvania 75 
subtitles, they are difficult to follow during the performance and would ideally ad-
dress a German-speaking public, which makes up the brunt of the viewership any-
way. In the case of Sibiu, from which we can extrapolate in making a broader, albeit 
somewhat less nuanced claim about the entire region, participation in Saxon her -
itage in Transylvania entails a class advantage. No wonder the German language 
is perceived as a marker of nobility and belonging to a certain circle of privileged 
“insiders” in the higher fora of Transylvanian culture (for Romanians). Hence the ex-
pectation that German is the lingua franca of cultural dialogue in Sibiu. At the level 
of the city and its surrounding area, the German minority is still very well represented 
through events such as the Holzstock Festival in Hosman – organised by the German 
Forum in Sibiu – and the so-called Großschenker Urzellauf [Fuga Lolelor in Roma-
nian], a sort of festival traditionally taking place during the last Sunday of January, 
in which rural dwellers dress up in grotesque costumes and parade through Agnita’s 
[Agnetheln] main street to scare away Winter and bring back Spring. 
As for heritage participation of foreign tourists – and especially Germans – 
Transylvania effects the amazement of self-recognition, as they encounter a Ger -
man Sprachinsel, a language and architectural island in the periphery. The average 
Germans have a very faint notion of interimperial dynamics in the region and ex -
perience “nostalgia” (the pain of returning home, as its Greek etymology suggests) 
for an island of medieval Germanness. This, again, establishes a form of unequal 
heritage participation, as locals who enjoy Saxon architecture must have the means 
of preserving the buildings and cover the costs of preservation – if the NGOs or 
local institutions are not active in aiding with preservation efforts – as MET did in 
Viscri – whereas the tourists with Transylvanian backgrounds are clearly more in 
the know regarding the sites they are visiting and the events they are participating 
in than other type of tourists. 
In Lieu of Conclusions: Zacken Packen and Infantile 
Philo-Germanism 
A popular Romanian advertisement for paint features a woman taking a picture 
of a white wooden chair she is just done painting with Danke [lit. “thank you”], a 
popular paint brand. The caption says Laikundschären, a silly wordplay hinting at 
“Like and Share” written in pidgin German. Another one has the following prompt: 
“zacken packen is the feeling you get when your family thinks you’re going to paint 
one chair, but the Neamț in you surfaces and you paint them all” (Youtube 2023). 
Usually meaning “Jerry,” but without the undertones acquired by the Jerry used 
by the British to designate German military personnel during WWI and WWII, 
the term “Neamț” used to be mildly derogatory and was used by Romanians in the 
19th century as a moniker for all foreigners, a practice best illustrated throughout 
the novelistic production of that time (Chiorean 2023; Morariu 2020; Olaru 2023). 
In the three Romanian principalities of the 19th century – Wallachia, Moldova, and 
Transylvania – which were under the direct domination of the Ottoman, the Czar -
ist, and the Austro-Hungarian Empire,
6 respectively, foreignness was equated with 
hostility and colonial power dynamics (Drace-Francis 2013, 117). 

 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
76 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Returning to the advertising campaign, it would be hard to imagine the same kind 
of representation of Germans in a Swedish advert, for instance, or a Korean one. It is 
thus clear that it follows a particular pattern of Romanian “self-colonisation” (Kios-
sev 1999) by which Romanian paint is inherently poorer in quality than its German 
counterpart and regards a particular type of technical knack, quality of materials, en-
gineering skills (“V olkswagen. Das Auto”) that Romanians are fond of attributing to 
Germans but hardly attribute to themselves. However, the truly interesting detail is 
the relationship between Danke and Germany. According to the website of Pittsburg 
Plate Glass (PPG), which now owns the brand, the initial company behind Danke, 
Dufa Romania, began as a joint venture between the German company Meffert AG 
Farbwerke (est. 1947) and two Romanian investors in 1995. However, the paint was 
never produced in Germany, but in Bucharest – “in accordance with German qual-
ity standards,” as a very popular claim goes; the company, later called Deutek SA, 
was then purchased by successive investment funds and ultimately by the American 
Pittsburg Plate Glass (est. 1883) in 2017. The Danke. Vopsește nemțește! [lit. “paint 
Germanly!”] brand has existed since 2003, and the campaign containing these short 
clips is from 2024. In other words, Danke is a Romanian brand bought by an Ameri-
can company, disguising itself as German on account of some loose ties with a Ger-
man paint company established after WWII. 
This campaign is another perfect example of “philo-Germanism without Ger -
mans.” Corroborated with a form of admiration for Western advancements in the 
arts, technology, and “civilisation” – understood as a form of collective habitus that 
is both intangible, indifferent, and most of all unintelligible for Romanian sensi-
bilities – this colonisation of the Romanian Self is, in its turn, a pastiche. Zacken 
packen and Laikundschären – alongside numerous other similar wordplays – could 
be construed in different ways. On the one hand, they could be very naively mock-
ing the German language and/or those who paint with Danke and use German 
incorrectly. However, that could hardly be the case, as it is not customary for a 
brand to mock its own clients. On the other hand, a more likely scenario is that 
these amusing marketing gimmicks manifest a form of self-conscious ignorance 
coupled with a self-colonising – but somewhat clueless – admiration for German -
ness. Hence the explicit use of positive stereotypes. But the advertising agency 
responsible for these short clips, gathered under the common denomination of The 
Pocket Dictionary for German Painting [Micul Dicționar de V opsit Nemțește], is 
from the capital city of Bucharest in the South of Romania, a region whose history 
was not intertwined with that of the German-speaking Saxon population in the 
same way as Transylvania. 
Caught between the pressures of branding – in a period when every location 
is prone to pursue the most suitable model for self-promotion and to “sell” its 
specificity – and the genuine concern with heritage preservation, Transylvania sees 
itself compelled to cultivate an image that does not reflect its contemporary cultural 
constituency in order to maintain its position as the up-and-coming European tour-
ist attraction, with all that this effort entails: combined and unequal heritagisation 
and heritage participation, lingering ethnic stereotypes, and the musealisation of 
class divides. 

 
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
 
 
 
 
Branding Germanness in Transylvania 77

5 Sauna Dialogues – the methodological 
potential of sauna as a site for making 
heritage futures 
Anna Talasniemi 
The sauna occupies a central place in Finnish cultural heritage, embodying both 
a historical tradition and a contemporary everyday practice functioning as a site 
for physical and spiritual cleansing. In a Finnish sauna, you usually sit naked on 
benches and pour water on the hot stones of the stove. From time to time, you 
leave the sauna to cool off – and repeat this a few times. Finland – a country of 
5.6 million people – has almost three million saunas (Liikkainen 2017), located, for 
example in private houses, swimming halls, summer houses, apartment buildings 
and individual flats. Probably due to the self-evidence of sauna in Finnish everyday 
life, research on contemporary sauna culture and experience is surprisingly limited 
(Leimu 2002, 72; Laaksonen 2005, 136); recent studies have focused on the field 
of health sciences (Tsonis 2016, 58–60). Sauna is topical because the Finnish sauna 
culture is the first Finnish practice on UNESCO’s Intangible Heritage List since 
2020, new public saunas are being established at a rapid pace, and sauna faces chal-
lenges during the eco-crisis due to its energy intensity and small particle emissions 
of wood heating. Sauna is also of international interest. 
I am taking research into the sauna. In my research, I organise Sauna Dialogues, 
discussion events about sauna that take place in the sauna. I explore the Finnish 
sauna as a corporeal and multi-sensory forum for dialogue, investigating the mean-
ings associated with sauna and the possible changes needed in sauna culture. In 
this interdisciplinary research, I am inspired by creative and participatory methods 
in futures studies, practices of various embodied and creative methodologies, and 
dialogical aesthetics. I am developing a future-oriented and participatory cultural 
heritage methodology and asking: What is the methodological potential of sauna as 
a site for making heritage futures? 
The concept of Sauna Dialogues as a form of communal knowledge produc -
tion for my research is inspired by both critical heritage studies and sauna-related 
ideas. In critical heritage studies, heritage is understood as a dynamic process 
and construction. There has been a call for the democratisation of heritage and 
the adoption of new and imaginative forms of data collection. This call is in 
line with the shift towards an emphasis on people’s participation, moving away  
from the previously dominant expert perspectives (see, e.g., ACHS 2012; Blake 
2019; Smith 2012 ; Wagenaar and Rodenberg 2023 ). Heritage is an activity that  
involves remembering, communicating, transferring knowledge, and expressing 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-7 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
80 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
and reinforcing identity, as well as social and cultural values and meanings (Smith 
2006, 83). This activity is corporeal, intertwining feelings, memories, and other 
forms of cognition (Smith and Campbell 2016 , 453). Sauna Dialogues take place 
within this entanglement. 
While heritage work has often been framed as “the past in the present for the 
future,” (Harrison et al. 2020, 3) there has been a lack of critical examination of 
the heritage practices’ role in shaping futures (Harrison et al. 2020, 3–4; Holtorf 
and Högberg 2021, 1–2). Yet, heritage is a powerful resource for futures and for 
engaging with contemporary ethical and moral issues (Butler 2006, 463). A central 
concept in my study is heritage futures, which is at the intersection of futures and 
heritage studies and can be defined as a mode of intangible heritage that becomes 
a conscious and meaningful shared activity that actively shapes people’s impact on 
nature and society (Siivonen 2020, 16–17). 
According to a survey on sauna and sustainability (Sauna Circle, Karvinen, and 
Talasniemi 2021), the Finnish sauna fosters a connection with both people and na-
ture, and the sauna is considered an equal space for discussion – not to dismiss its 
role as a space for solitude and tranquillity. While numerous respondents see the 
sauna as non-hierarchical and equal, the sauna is also described as excluding many 
people as gender minorities or disabled people (Sauna Circle, Karvinen, and Talas-
niemi 2021; Talasniemi 2022). However, the method aims to build on the shared – 
albeit ambivalent – perception of the sauna as a benevolent and egalitarian space for 
meaningful discussions. Sauna Dialogues also draw on the sauna’s corporeal nature, 
where multi-sensory experiences evoke memories of past sauna visits, forging con-
nections between the past, present, and future ( Seesmeri 2018, 237–44, 257–60). 
Central to my research is the question of how these connections extend into the 
future, and how a heritage setting, dominated by the influence of the past, can sup-
port futures thinking. Can sauna, as intangible heritage, facilitate the critical debates 
required by critical heritage studies? What kind of time machine is a sauna? 
In developing the method, participation is intricately linked to the concept and 
practice of dialogue and to arts-based participatory research: In heritage work, ar -
tistic methods can be used as means of participation (see, e.g., Kisić 2016, 295–96). 
The artistic approaches also leverage the creative futures studies’ methods in fa -
cilitating imaginative processes and shifts in temporal perspectives ( Armanto, 
Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022, 231; Siivonen et al. 2022, 257). Additionally, art 
serves as a reference for understanding the sauna as a multi-sensory space. Art is 
also an inspirational basis derived from the discourse on dialogical aesthetics, a 
concept introduced by art historian Grant H. Kester (2013 [2004]). In conversa -
tions dislocated from their usual contexts, the participants have the opportunity to 
break away from predetermined roles and responsibilities, interacting more freely 
and detached from the routines and structures of everyday life. New meanings 
may emerge in this process. Kester draws upon the concept of dialogical art prac-
tice from literary scholar Mikhail Bakhtin (Kester (2013 [2004], xvii–xviii, 6, 10). 
Bakhtin’s dialogism concerns both interpersonal relationships and our connection 
to the wider world. Meanings are created in these relationships or interconnections 
(Bahtin, Laine, and Nieminen 1991; Gardiner and Bell 1998; Hirschkop 1999; 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Sauna Dialogues 81 
Hynes 2014). Kester also builds on philosopher Jürgen Habermas’ communicative 
theory and concept of public sphere (Kester 2013 [2004], 109–10). 
Based on these considerations, I am developing the Sauna Dialogues method, 
combining my skills both as a traditional sauna healer (in Finnish perinnesaunot-
taja) and a dialogue facilitator. The practice of a traditional sauna healer involves 
sauna rituals, including whisking and singing, which I use to enhance the multi-
sensory nature of the sauna and foster a sense of care. In crafting the Sauna Dia -
logues methodology and using sauna as a laboratory, I seek answers to questions 
about the potential of the sauna: I explore how we create and share meanings and 
envision and shape sauna futures within the sauna. Ultimately, the research aims 
to deepen our understanding of sauna culture, identify and anticipate areas for 
change, and promote a more inclusive sauna culture, participatory heritage futures, 
and equitable heritage practices. I am developing a dialogical research framework 
where tradition is dynamic and evolving, constructed through dialogue. 
In the next section, I describe the development of the Sauna Dialogues method, 
tracing its formation from (heritage) futures workshops and its refinement through 
interaction with experience and theoretical insights. I then focus on the themes of 
dialogue, corporeality and ritual, and the challenges encountered and the solutions 
developed. Finally, I will summarise the main findings and assess the method and 
its possible applications. 
Shaping the Sauna Dialogues method 
What kind of futures are created within cultural heritage and how? How are sauna 
futures built in the sauna? These questions led me to explore participatory, creative 
and collaborative futures-making methods in futures studies. One such method is 
the futures workshop, originally developed by Robert Jungk and Norbert Müllert. 
The primary aim of these workshops, dating back several decades, was to empower 
ordinary people to believe in their capabilities and opportunities to participate in 
shaping their own future ( Armanto, Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022 , 222; Jungk 
and Müllert 1987 ). The heritage futures workshop model, developed in the DY -
NAMO Project (Dynamic Museum and Heritage Futures Workshop as Instruments 
for Ecological Reconstruction), is based on the futures workshop and combines her-
itage and futures studies methodologies (Siivonen et al. 2022, 250, 255). Initially, 
I planned to conduct pilot sessions based on (heritage) futures workshops and to 
proceed with the developed framework. During the process, I found it more mean-
ingful to refine and experiment with the method after each session, informed by my 
experiences, participant feedback and research literature. I integrated elements from 
the futures and heritage futures workshops, incorporating my expertise in dialogue. 
Starting points, location, and participants 
Implementing the heritage futures workshop in the sauna required adjustments. 
Unlike traditional futures workshops spanning even one to two days (Nurmela 
2013, 215–16), these sessions were limited to approximately three hours without 

 
 
 
82 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
several small groups. Discussions were recorded instead of taking notes. The dia -
logues occurred both in the sauna and during cooling off, with the change of space 
pacing the workshop stages. Between September 2023 and October 2024, I con -
ducted six Finnish language Sauna Dialogues with four to seven participants each, 
focusing on the meaning of sauna and imagining its futures. The audio recordings 
and transcriptions are expected to be archived at the Finnish Literature Society. 
The workshop guidelines often refer to the comfort of the workshop space (Ar-
manto, Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022, 223). The potential of unconventional lo-
cations has rarely been considered. However, heritage futures workshops have also 
been held in forests (see, e.g., Paaskoski et al. 2022, 44–46). Professor Richard 
Sandford suggests having futures workshops “in places that are part of participants’ 
heritage” (2019, 18) and initiating discussions about the significance of the herit -
age in their lives. Sandford argues that futures created within the framework of 
cultural heritage can promote participatory approaches and be less instrumentalist, 
“lived futures” (Sandford 2019, 1, 16–18). This is in line with Sauna Dialogues: 
workshops were held in the sauna and discussions revolved around its significance 
and meanings. The events took place in a variety of wood-heated and modern elec-
tric saunas. 
Participants in futures workshops vary according to the purpose, which af-
fects the content and impact created. The Future of Sauna Survey ( Sauna Circle, 
Karvinen, and Talasniemi 2021) on sauna and sustainability revealed forms of un-
derrepresentation in sauna culture, which informed the participant recruitment for 
Sauna Dialogues. Conducted for the Sauna Circle – a network dedicated to promot-
ing and preserving Finnish sauna culture – the survey gathered over 300 responses, 
with approximately half including open-ended answers. According to the survey, 
three key issues contribute to sauna underrepresentation: First, gender diversity 
is insufficiently addressed, sauna culture being largely cis- and heteronormative. 
Second, physical accessibility barriers exclude people with mobility impairments. 
Thirdly, the inherent nudity may deter participation, for example, by people with 
cultural restrictions on nudity (Sauna Circle, Karvinen, and Talasniemi 2021; Ta-
lasniemi 2022). For some of the events, participants were recruited through organi-
sations such as the disability rights association Kynnys. Critical heritage studies’ 
call to democratise heritage and involve marginalised communities is relevant to 
Finnish sauna culture. This aligns with art-based methods, which enhance critical 
awareness and empathy and challenge stereotypes, often including marginalised 
voices (Leavy 2020, 24–26). 
Typically, the group invited to the workshops is diversified using a so-called 
expert matrix (Armanto, Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022 , 228–29). Futures work-
shops have been conducted with vulnerable or marginalised individuals or groups 
(Armanto, Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022; Alminde and Warming 2020), and there 
are examples where workshops have been organised with participants from simi -
lar backgrounds in order to create a sense of trust and encourage open discussion 
(Armanto, Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022 , 232; Rinaudo et al. 2013, 105). This 
practice was followed in Sauna Dialogues especially in the events involving people 
from marginalised groups. 

 
 
 
 
Sauna Dialogues 83 
Participants received information about the event in advance, including the re -
search notification and data privacy statement. Prior to the event, to avoid group 
pressure, I discussed practices, such as participants’ preferences for nudity or cloth-
ing, individually. After the events, I collected feedback. The first event was fol -
lowed by a discussion the next day; in the other dialogues, participants were given 
a short online form after the session to reflect on their experience and give feed-
back. The online form was used by 80% of the participants. Often some feedback 
was given spontaneously during the dialogue sessions themselves. In general, the 
feedback was overtly positive, although in one of the pilot dialogues the last ses -
sion in the sauna was felt to be too long, and in one event two of the participants 
found the singing superficial. 
My role was multifaceted, that of researcher, facilitator and traditional sauna 
healer. In participatory research, knowledge is co-produced, but researcher actively 
shapes the type of participation required (Armanto, Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022, 
222–23). I led the sessions, invited the participants, and exercised various forms of 
researcher authority. I acknowledge the Foucaultian omnipresence of power (Fou-
cault 1977), as it is impossible to detach from power relations in research, and that 
emphasises ethical considerations such as avoiding arrogance and speaking for oth-
ers (Barone and Eisner 2012, 127–28). These concerns are particularly significant 
in participatory research involving people from marginalised groups. It should be 
noted that the interviews and surveys are not neutral; they actively shape knowl -
edge (Fontana and Frey 2000, 647). Sauna Dialogues, akin to group interviews, 
highlight the researcher’s active role in knowledge construction. As a facilitator, 
I guide the discussions, and introducing personal experiences and knowledge can 
sometimes advance the dialogue but it is essential to do so cautiously to avoid over-
shadowing others. The exercise of the facilitator’s power is crucial for maintaining 
equality and aiding understanding in communal knowledge production (Alhanen 
2024, 102–3, 159). To increase my awareness of power and different roles, I docu-
mented my reflections during the process. 
Transforming futures workshops into Sauna Dialogues 
The original futures workshop method comprises (1) a preparation phase, (2) a 
criticism phase addressing present problems, (3) an imagination phase envisioning 
future solutions, and (4) an implementation phase strategising future development 
(Armanto, Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022, 229–330). The heritage futures work -
shop builds on this model (Siivonen et al. 2022, 255–56). The Sauna Dialogues’ 
preparation phase (1) including participant recruitment was presented in the pre -
vious section. The criticism phase (2) was primarily facilitated by the Future of 
Sauna Survey, which highlighted challenges in contemporary sauna culture, par -
ticularly issues related to underrepresentation and ecological concerns. Prior to the 
event, each participant received a summary of the survey results, which I reviewed 
at the session. 
Instead of a criticism phase, the heritage futures workshops start with a section 
to illustrate the impact of past decisions on the present (Paaskoski et al. 2022, 27). 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
84 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
This phase proved challenging, and I ultimately omitted it for three reasons: First, 
my research focuses on the meanings of sauna and envisioning its future, and em -
phasising the past might overly orient participants towards it. Secondly, reflections 
on the past occur naturally in discussions about sauna without a separate focus. 
Thirdly, I faced a practical challenge in finding examples from the sauna context. 
This phase serves as an important illustration how the future is not singular or pre-
determined; rather, we can envision multiple possible futures. In the absence of this 
phase, I presented the idea by means of talk. However, in the first two workshops, 
this phase was conducted with a focus on the participants’ sauna memories. 
A leap into the future (3) is crucial in both futures and heritage futures work -
shops (see, e.g., Armanto, Lauttamäki, and Siivonen 2022 , 230; Paaskoski et al. 
2022, 256). The Sauna Dialogues method is inspired by the sauna’s ability to 
cross different time frames. The integration of futures studies with heritage, 
which traditionally focuses on the past, is intriguing given the human capacity for 
mental time travel and the similarities in brain processes involved in both tem -
poral directions (Suddendorf and Corballis 2007 , 299; Mullally 2014). Although 
we are anticipatory beings, envisioning futures is challenging and constrained 
by present assumptions. We easily think in presentist terms, unable to envision 
futures differing from the current. Heritage researcher Annalisa Bolin addresses 
this challenge, questioning how heritage practices can be directed towards fu -
ture that diverges from the present and embraces uncertainty ( Holtorf and Bolin 
2024). Capabilities for futures thinking have been articulated in concepts such 
as futures consciousness, described by five dimensions of temporal perspective, 
agency, openness to alternatives, systemic thinking, and responsibility ( Ahven-
harju and Pouru-Mikkola 2022 , 390–91). 
The developers of the heritage futures workshop discuss various stimuli for 
awakening imagination, such as experiential activities, embodiment, and multi-
sensory approaches (Siivonen et al. 2022, 255–57). In Sauna Dialogues, I drew on 
those ideas: I included multi-sensory traditional sauna healing practices such as 
singing together and whisking. At the future leap, I guided participants to focus on 
the sounds and scents of the sauna with their eyes closed. A concrete timeframe of 
30 years into the future was selected, and participants were instructed to imagine 
the future world and reflect on it through their own experiences. I also encouraged 
participants to adopt a playful mindset to break away from conventional thinking 
(on play see, e.g., Alhanen 2024, 40–44, 141–45; Dissanayake 1988). 
In the first Sauna Dialogues, the implementation phase (4) occurred in the sauna, 
where participants returned from the future to the present to generate solutions for 
building the future. I guided each participant to take turns sharing reflections fol -
lowed by a concrete and symbolic act of throwing a ladleful of water onto the sauna 
stones. This practice facilitated attentive listening, a key element of dialogue. In 
the final workshop section conducted in the dressing room, participants reflected 
on what actions they could personally take for the future or who else should take 
responsibility. 
Reflecting on the trials, I steered Sauna Dialogues in a more dialogical direction. 
Bakhtinian dialogism underscores the emergence of meanings and understanding 

 
 
Sauna Dialogues 85 
through dialogue where the other person is unique, always more than we can fully 
know. The dialogue is characterised by open-endedness and unfinalisability accom-
modating multiple interpretations. Recognising the centrality of sharing experi-
ences in the practice of dialogue (Alhanen 2024, 27–29), I prioritised the meanings 
of the present and sought to create an open, decision-withholding dialogue space, 
opening more space for sharing the experiences. This was timewise achieved by 
omitting the past-focused phase and minimising the implementation phase. Some 
events started with discussions about what sauna means to each participant, while 
in others we focused on recent sauna experiences. In the final sauna session, par -
ticipants reflected on the most meaningful aspects of the discussion, using a ritual-
istic approach of sharing and water throwing described above. The contemplation 
of personal action and change (the implementation phase) was left to the final, 
relatively short cooling-off period. 
Dialogue facilitates the formation of new meanings and shared frameworks of 
understanding. I see the power of dialogue in fostering interaction, empathy and 
democracy. However, dialogue is not the sphere for decision-making and solutions. 
In their dissertation, designer and researcher Julia Lohmann reflects on the tension 
between the dialogical design and implementation of making change (Lohmann 
2018, 52). This tension is also inherent in the futures workshops: there is a com -
bination of making futures and free imagination, combination of normativity and 
openness (Siivonen et al. 2022, 255). In Sauna Dialogues, open dialogue and imag-
ination were prioritised. While many futures research methods emphasise practical 
solutions, I chose to cultivate sauna as a speculative, imaginative, and dialogical 
environment where practical solutions are not the primary focus. Speculative prac-
tice and imagination may turn into concrete action, into making futures, later. To 
make the future, we must first imagine it. 
In the midst of heritage: Exploring and developing the potential of 
sauna for dialogue and heritage futures 
What potential does sauna have as a site for dialogue and meaning-sharing? What 
is it like to discuss sauna while being within the sauna? How does sauna serve 
as a site for envisioning sauna futures? Are there features that hinder discussion 
and imagination? In this section, I explore these questions from the perspective 
of the development of the method, integrating theoretical analysis with practical 
experience. Many of the questions are related to sauna as a multi-sensory, embod-
ied and ritualistic heritage environment that contains much taken-for-granted, tacit 
and non-discursive knowledge. People often describe their world through cultural 
narratives (Silverman 2000, 823–25), and sauna-related experiences are often de -
scribed through these narratives, as evidenced by recurring expressions and phrases 
such as sauna’s equality. In Sauna Dialogues, the emphasis on corporeal awareness, 
multi-sensory experiences and open dialogue is intended to reach issues and evoke 
ideas that might be obscured by strong narratives. The inherent characteristics of 
sauna merge with the elements I bring in as a method developer, which build on the 
characteristics of sauna and intensify them. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
  
 
86 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Dialogue and meaning making in sauna 
Sauna is generally regarded as a good place for conversation, though none of the 
participants had previously experienced a guided sauna discussion. Many of the Fu-
ture of Sauna Survey respondents and Sauna Dialogue participants describe sauna 
as a non-hierarchical space where roles and ranks are left in the changing room, 
which allows for open dialogue even on difficult and deep issues and even among 
strangers. Sauna can resemble an idealised space, similar to Habermas’s public 
sphere, where everyone has an equal opportunity to speak (see, e.g., Habermas 1974 
[1964], 49). However, critiques of Habermas highlight the model’s focus on argu-
ment over power dynamics ( Bäcklund and Mäntysalo 2009 , 23–24; Fraser 1990, 
59–65; Kester 2013 [2004], 112–14). Not everyone has genuine access to the public 
sphere – as not everyone has access to sauna as a public space. Recognising this, 
Sauna Dialogues emphasise empathy and listening as addressed by Grant Kester 
in his dialogical aesthetics analysis (2013 [2004], 110–14) and pay attention to the 
diversity of participants, as described in the previous section. 
The significance of the sauna’s multi-sensory nature on interaction and thinking 
is evident in the participants’ comments. The sauna’s dim lighting was seen as facil-
itating discussion; one participant mentioned that it allowed for a natural dynamic 
of participation and withdrawal. I find this interesting, as dialogue guidelines often 
stress the importance of seeing the body and facial expressions of interlocutors 
(see, e.g., Alhanen 2024, 57–58). One participant described the multi-sensory as-
pects of sauna as enabling heightened sensitivity. Another participant described the 
experience of how thoughts flow freely in the sauna as fun. In the feedback from 
the first event, the same participant expressed some concern about whether they 
should try to keep their thoughts on the topic as their mind wandered unbound. In 
subsequent discussions, I emphasised that participants could let their ideas wander 
freely; I, as the facilitator, would steer the conversation back on topic if it strayed 
too far. My aim is to support the free movement of participants’ thoughts and to 
foster the emergence of new ideas, highlighting that thoughts do not need to be 
fully formed – germinal ideas or questions are welcome. 
I enhanced the sauna’s ritual and multi-sensory nature in Sauna Dialogues 
through sauna healing practices. The aim was to support the experience of presence 
and embodiment. By focusing on corporeal sensations, the goal was to bring forth 
ideas that may arise from corporeal feelings or memories. The ritualistic nature of 
sauna is both familiar and introduces new elements through guided sauna practices. 
The elements of traditional sauna healing were perceived as facilitating the flow of 
thought and stimulating the conversation. They evoked sensations and images that 
provided space for thoughts and feelings, and were also described to bring a sense 
of sanctity and safeness both to the space and to the experience. One participant 
described how whisking with birch branches brought to mind a sense of temporal-
ity, connecting the past, present, and future as they contemplated the growth of the 
birch trees. Sauna rituals were also said to introduce appropriate pauses, allowing 
for a slight withdrawal, similar to how the dimness of the sauna enables a dynamic 
of participation and retreat. I see these pauses as enabling listening, important in 

 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
Sauna Dialogues 87 
dialogical practice. There was also opposite feedback, as experiencing the singing 
superimposed or considering the sauna healing activities not having an effect on 
the conversation. However, the same participant identified the sauna healer’s ac-
tivities as building trust, maintaining a feeling of care and helping to concentrate on 
relaxing and discussing, and mentioned the aesthetic and sense-pampering experi-
ence to influence the discussion. 
Based on my experience as a traditional sauna healer, sauna healing practices 
often promote a sense of community, a phenomenon that was also present in Dia -
logues. Participants described this as a “shared and magical feeling,” “together -
ness,” and “intimacy.” A sense of community is also seen as an inherent feature 
of sauna as heritage, as sauna has been defined as a ritual characterised by spatial, 
temporal and social liminality, where sauna-goers are seen as liminal beings – 
drawing on the concepts of anthropologists Arnold van Gennep and Victor Turner 
(Edelsward 1991, 83–116). Turner describes the relationship between liminal 
beings as one of complete equality where the liminal group transcends hierar -
chies (Turner 1967, 97–100) and uses the Latin word communitas to describe 
the community of liminal beings ( Turner 2007 [1969], 108–9). Many dialogue 
participants perceived sauna bathing to entail intimacy that fosters connection. As 
a facilitator and sauna practitioner, I consciously strive to create a sense of com-
munity and trust that fosters dialogue. 
Sauna Dialogue also seemed to push participants towards new directions. 
For example, one participant reflected the day after the session: “For some rea -
son Sauna Dialogue made me think of Akseli Gallen-Kallela’s work “Ad Astra.”
1 
Maybe Sauna Dialogue took me to another place, to the stars. The thoughts are still 
quite abstract, a bit elusive and under a strong feeling and atmosphere.” I link this 
to the liminal nature of the sauna, which is present in different ways alongside com-
munitas. Participants describe the sauna as both profane and sacred, embodying 
a mystical ambivalence; it is the “everyday shampoo bottle” and the “time-worn 
ancient walls of the sauna.” In this ambivalence and ambiguity of a liminal state, 
new meanings may more easily emerge. Grant Kester (2013 [2014]) emphasises 
the absence of everyday context as a facilitator of discussion and a generator of 
new meanings. 
The cultural significance and embodied nature of the sauna were highlighted 
in the participants’ feedback. One participant commented that the thoughts they 
had in the sauna would not have occurred to them elsewhere and felt that this 
made the sauna an excellent place to discuss the sauna itself. Another partici -
pant described a similar experience, finding it interesting to discuss the sauna 
experience while immersed in the physical experience. The environment steered 
the discussion towards sharing sauna experiences and allowed participants to 
approach the experience together. Participants were actively immersed in the 
cultural heritage, processing and sharing meanings through remembering, imag -
ining and interacting. The participants’ shared experiences resonate with Richard 
Sandford’s (2019) proposal for using heritage in envisioning “lived futures,” and 
underscore the importance of the community discussing heritage in its authentic 
context. 

 
 
 
88 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Characteristics of the sauna that encourage or block future visioning 
While sauna has great potential for creativity and temporal exploration, includ-
ing futures envisioning, certain elements seem to hinder imagining sauna futures 
in sauna. Reflecting on the first Sauna Dialogues, although the participants’ ideas 
were imaginative and interesting – ranging from a universal sauna card enabling ac-
cess for all, to energy solutions, and nature-inspired public pool saunas – I noticed 
a tendency to lean towards presentism. I observed participants’ linguistic choices: 
instead of describing the future as existing, they used conditional language and 
expressed hopes, indicating a present-oriented view of the future. To counter this, I 
experimented to guide participants to imagine the future from a future perspective 
and to maintain a future-oriented temporal dimension. I hypothesised that describ-
ing the future in the present tense could enhance the realism of the experience. 
This was occasionally achieved by instructing participants to begin their sentences 
with phrases such as “Here in 2054, I see/feel…” and by using the present tense 
myself. One participant found the event too brief for envisioning the future, sug -
gesting that more time and shared discussion would be needed for effective futures 
thinking. They felt that the self-focus and embodied nature of the sauna experience 
attuned them more to the present moment rather than to future-oriented thinking 
and creativity. 
The perception of sauna as unchanging over time made some participants hesi-
tant to envision changes in sauna culture. The feeling that “sauna is sauna and 
has always been like that” prevails. The sensory experiences of the sauna and 
traditional sauna healer’s practices – the sounds, scents, and tactile sensations – 
evoke nostalgic childhood memories, facilitating mental time travel into the past 
and prompting reflection on personal sauna history and experiences. Memories 
included sensory details such as childhood cold toes on the sauna path, the click -
ing of the electric stove, and the scent of birch. Communal responsory singing 
was described as placing the participant in the chain of generations. For most, 
sauna represents safety and permanence, which they wish to preserve. The ritual 
nature of sauna, supported by sauna healing practices, can stimulate renewal and 
new thoughts, but it can also be retentive. Simultaneously, imagining brought up 
thoughts about what one wishes to safeguard. 
Ellen Dissanayake, an anthropologist who writes about art and aesthetics, ex-
plores the intertwining of art, ritual, and play. In the Sauna Dialogues context, I 
recognise Dissanayake’s ideas. Unlike ritual, which can be retentive, play encour-
ages change and novelty, and stimulates spontaneity and freedom ( Dissanayake 
1988, 89). During the last two events, I encouraged participants to adopt a playful 
mindset to break away from conventional thinking (see also Alhanen 2024, 142). 
This was to help the participants envision futures more creatively and freely. Oc -
casionally, playfulness facilitated going beyond the obvious. 
Alongside recurring cultural narratives, sauna embodies a wealth of tacit, non-
verbal knowledge. Many participants found themselves articulating their concep -
tual relationship with sauna for the first time. One participant became aware of the 
nostalgic, deeply embodied, and non-discursive nature of their sauna relationship 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Sauna Dialogues 89 
and noted that nostalgia and lack of reflection could lead to dismissing others’ 
experiences. Recognising their majority position, the participant began to consider 
the challenges others might face: a space that is safe for oneself is not safe for 
everyone and, for example, the often taken-for-granted nudity in the sauna and the 
equality that this implies may prevent people from having sauna. The shared, self-
evident, and tacit knowledge about sauna makes it difficult to challenge the narra-
tives. Behaviour norms are upheld by tradition and material conditions (Alasuutari 
2006), as seen in cisnormative spaces. The sauna’s cultural narrative as an egalitar-
ian space is challenged when it induces fear, marginalises, highlights differences, 
demarcates Finnishness, or idealises ability – experiences noted by some Dialogue 
participants and survey respondents. These experiences make sauna a political is -
sue. Such cases illustrate how tradition can be used to control current cultural ten-
sions (see Chambille 2023, 56, referring to Smith 2006). Sauna can sometimes 
exclude rather than unite, thereby hindering dialogue. 
Simultaneously, participants’ experiences indicate that, despite challenges, the 
sauna’s equitable, non-hierarchical qualities are essential and worth developing. 
Sauna Dialogues heightened participants’ awareness of their relationship with 
sauna and revealed its diverse meanings across individuals, often prompting new 
reflections. Sharing (minority) experiences can disrupt dominant cultural narra-
tives and foster rethinking, future envisioning, and inclusivity. The imagination 
needed for mental time travel into the past and future is also empathic imagination 
cultivated by listening and engaging with others’ experiences. Envisioning sauna 
futures may require moving beyond self-evident narratives and recognising the 
contradictions inherent in sauna culture. The challenges identified in the Haberma-
sian approach resonate with the inherent dissonance of cultural heritage. Agonistic 
pluralism offers a theoretical framework that acknowledges and embraces the con-
tradictions (Chambille 2023). Dialogue does not demand unanimity but encour -
ages openness to other perspectives, forming a basis for meaningful exchange and 
future-oriented thinking. 
One participant reflected, “It’s always easier to criticise or point out flaws. But 
then, when it comes to actually building or somehow improving them, or mak -
ing changes, it’s not so easy anymore.” Although creating concrete actions or be-
havioural changes during a short sauna workshop was challenging, especially as 
I reduced the focus on solutions, the discussions often touched upon the idea of 
change, with some participants even envisioning a “sauna revolution”: challenging 
and changing normative views and practices of sauna culture. Another participant 
described how they had initially been reluctant to think about the future, feeling 
resigned to the idea that “it will be what it will be.” Through Sauna Dialogue, they 
began to contemplate future possibilities, recognising that we all can influence fu-
tures in some way; while defining concrete changes in behaviour remained chal-
lenging, there was a shift in thinking. One participant emphasised the importance 
of collective action and community, both central to the concept of heritage futures, 
when referring to the changes needed: “The most important and meaningful thing 
is that we do this together.” 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
90 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Assessing the potential of sauna and Sauna Dialogues 
In this chapter, I have explored the development of a participatory, future-oriented 
heritage method. Discussions about sauna have taken place within the sauna, mak-
ing it both the subject and setting of dialogue. I conclude by assessing sauna’s 
potential as a site for dialogue and making heritage futures. I reflect on my experi-
ences, analyse the method’s functionality and consider its broader significance and 
applications. 
The novel method was developed experimentally, drawing on the structure of 
futures workshops. I creatively applied my expertise as a dialogue facilitator and 
traditional sauna healer. Interacting with practical experience and relevant literature, 
the method evolved to emphasise open dialogue and imaginative thinking while re-
ducing focus on solutions. I built upon the sauna’s characteristics, its multi-sensory, 
corporeal, conversational and non-hierarchical nature, enhancing elements that 
would foster dialogue – listening, sharing experiences, and building trust. The limi-
nal character of sauna worked to create a space for open dialogue and envisioning 
sauna futures. An understanding of sauna took shape on both a personal and collec-
tive level. Although it was challenging to identify conscious, concrete, and collective 
actions to make a positive impact on nature and society, a wealth of ideas emerged 
about what good sauna futures look like; heritage futures were imagined. 
Discussing sauna within the sauna brought unique benefits and challenges. Dis-
cussing the experience amidst the experience itself was described as engaging and 
meaningful. It strengthened the feeling of community and intimacy. It was felt 
that these insights were unlikely to arise in other settings, underscoring the value 
of discussing a topic within a space intrinsically connected to it. The sauna read -
ily evoked memories that enriched the dialogue. While the perceived permanence 
of sauna made it challenging for some participants to envision its future, it also 
underscored features they felt were essential to preserve. The sauna’s taken-for -
granted nature, along with embedded narratives and tacit knowledge, was found 
to limit openness to alternative perspectives. By addressing underrepresentation 
and involving participants from marginalised groups, new insights into inclusivity 
challenges emerged through sharing of personal experiences. This method facili-
tated dialogue where inherent contradictions and dissonance of heritage became 
catalysts for envisioning futures. Sauna Dialogues align with heritage’s potential to 
foster participation, social equity, and inclusivity while challenging exclusionary 
attitudes. My research aims to incorporate diverse voices into heritage processes, 
deepening understanding of sauna as cultural heritage and to build on and to de-
velop the sauna’s ethos as a space of equity. 
Sauna’s central role in Finnish culture, with its rich cultural layers, undoubt-
edly contributes to the functionality of this method. Similar dialogues may also 
be feasible in contexts where sauna has comparable cultural significance, such as 
in Estonia or Russia (on Russia see Pollock 2019, 7). In my study, the discussions 
in the sauna focused on sauna itself, yet my experience indicates that the sauna 
environment is also well-suited to dialogues on other topics. Interestingly, experi-
ences from sauna events organised outside my research suggest that envisioning 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
Sauna Dialogues 91 
non-sauna-related futures in sauna may be easier than envisioning sauna futures. 
Could the method, however, be applied to other contexts? One factor that may 
influence the environments’ suitability is the conversational and egalitarian quali -
ties connected to it, resembling Habermas’ concept of the public sphere – a setting 
where all participants have an equal opportunity to engage, akin to sociologist Roy 
Oldenburg’s concept of the “third place.” A third place is neither home nor work 
but a social environment where presence is voluntary, egalitarian, and status holds 
no significance. In addition to cafes, bookshops, bars, hairdressers, and other com-
munity meeting places, the concept is applied to institutions like museums and 
libraries (Oldenburg 1999 [1989]; Putnam, Feldstein, and Cohen 2003; Tate 2012). 
What distinguishes the sauna as a place from the third place, in general, is its 
embodied and intimate nature as well as its liminality and ambiguity between 
everyday and transcendent. It is both safe and unordinary. Cultural heritage sites 
often share both the qualities of familiarity and separation of everyday ( Giombini 
2020). They are rich in layered meanings, making them particularly conducive to 
dialogue and visioning futures. Methodological pluralism, as involving diverse 
creative methods, can reveal the affective, embodied, and multi-sensory quali -
ties of heritage, thus expanding avenues for understanding. According to Kester’s 
(2003 [2014]) theory of dialogical aesthetics, non-traditional spaces can dismantle 
everyday hierarchies and roles, fostering a freer, more open conversation. This ap-
proach aligns with Sandford’s (2019) proposal of envisioning futures within herit-
age contexts to create “lived futures.” Heritage settings naturally lend themselves 
to futures thinking due to the presence of futures orientation within them – thus 
far, it has been in heritage field largely latent or dormant, present yet inactive. 
However, in places like sauna, the strong influence of the past can challenge im -
agining of futures that differ from the present. The future-orientation of heritage 
is also intertwined with an ethic of care for society and nature, element strongly 
present in sauna.
