6 History and heritage in Sagunt, Spain 
An opportunity for social innovation 
through university culture 
María-Dolores Pitarch-Garrido, Ester 
Alba-Pagán, and Miguel Requena-Jiménez 
Cultural heritage is a concept that appeared at the end of the 18th century but has 
evolved, changing the perception of its meaning as society has evolved and assimilated 
new values (Chambille 2023). Today, the “New European Agenda for Culture” (Eu-
ropean Commission, 2018) points out that the social dimension is an essential aspect 
in addressing any issue related to culture. Cultural diversity is fundamental to ensure 
well-being and social cohesion, which is achieved through the promotion of cultural 
skills, the mobility of professionals, and the protection of Europe’s cultural heritage. 
The latter should, according to the European Union (EU) proposal, raise awareness of 
common history and values and strengthen the sense of European identity. 
Social participation and culture are related in different ways. For some authors, 
and for the EU itself, cultural participation is an ideal means of overcoming lan -
guage barriers, empowering people, and fostering social cohesion, including refu -
gees and other migrants (Iaione, De Nictolis, and Santagati 2022). Furthermore, 
cultural participation improves people’s health and well-being, promotes urban and 
social regeneration, and fosters the skills needed for the cultural and creative sec -
tors, including digital, entrepreneurial, traditional, and specialised skills. It seems 
to be proven that the enjoyment, in all its fields and for various reasons (physical 
and mental health, integration, knowledge, etc.), of culture is the most direct and 
unquestionable form of social participation. 
Several authors (Bherer, Dufour, and Montambeault 2016; Iaione, Nictolis, and 
Maria-Elena 2022; Zheng and Burgers 2023) have pointed out that social participa-
tion is essential to promote conservation processes and develop policies for the en-
hancement of cultural and heritage elements of all kinds. Promoting and recognising 
heritage as an identifying and differentiating element of the territory is the first step 
towards recovering it, living it, and respecting it. There are many examples of collec-
tive processes and social activism in defence of cultural and heritage elements, whose 
drive also reaches management through the proposal of public policies. In short, social 
participation and shared sovereignty are key in heritage conservation processes (Pas-
tor Pérez et al. 2021). Heritage management must be guided by a more democratic 
perception of social values, integrating the community in conservation strategies. 
The democratisation of culture, considering it as use, attitude, and initiatives, is 
a fundamental qualitative improvement to promote a collective sensitivity towards 
the elements that have been part of the history of a place, as well as for society to 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-8 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
96 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
take ownership of cultural heritage, tangible, and intangible, as a source of well-
being and economic growth. 
In this chapter, we present the case of Sagunt, a municipality in the province of 
Valencia, in the Spanish Mediterranean, with a large number of historical remains, 
the result of a rich and varied past. In this city, there is an important movement of 
citizen participation in defence of a heritage that has been aspiring for years to be 
able to apply for UNESCO World Heritage status. The University of Valencia, the 
most important university in the region, has actively collaborated with local society 
and the municipal government to disseminate the values of this rich historical herit-
age and to ensure its conservation and enhancement. 
Culture, heritage, and social innovation 
Social participation in public management is an essential feature of democratic gov-
ernance in developed countries, driven by access to education, increased cultural 
level, and the demand of citizens to be involved in decision-making, particularly in 
public policies. This approach has given rise to what is known as “participatory gov-
ernance”, a concept that has gained relevance since the beginning of the 21st century, 
especially in the field of cultural heritage (Rodenberg and Wagenaar 2023). 
The concept of citizen participation is not new and has been the subject of 
controversy and analysis since the 1960s, with the multiplication of studies on its 
impact on the improvement of democracy and the methodologies to implement 
it (Arnstein 1969; Bertacchini and Gould 2021). Social participation, variously 
named in recent years, is understood as a counterweight to the traditional top-down 
approach to public policy (Collins, Fairclough, and Turner 2023). 
In 2014, the EU introduced the term “Community-Led Local Development” 
(CLLD) in the context of the Structural and Investment Funds for 2014–2020. This 
strategy seeks to support decentralised management of development projects by 
involving local actors, designing and implementing integrated development strate-
gies in rural, coastal, and urban areas. 
In parallel, social innovation has emerged as part of the innovation-focused de-
velopment plan to combat low productivity and economic stagnation in Europe, 
seeking social and environmental renewal. The European Commission’s “Road -
map for Social Innovation” (2013) defined social innovation as key to address-
ing urgent social challenges and promoting inclusive and sustainable development 
(Choi and Majumdar 2015). In 2014, this concept was included in the Europe 2020 
Strategy as part of a plan for smart, sustainable, and inclusive growth ( European 
Commission 2023). 
Another relevant concept is that of “innovation ecosystems”, referring to territo-
ries where the participation of all social actors has been enhanced, enabling the suc-
cess of policies or initiatives. This term was first included in official EU documents 
under the Horizon 2020 programme, to describe the need to create interconnected 
and efficient environments that support innovation and the growth of small- and 
medium-sized enterprises (González, Kubus, and Mascareñas 2019). The 2022 
“New European Innovation Agenda” consolidated this concept, highlighting its 

 
 
 
 
 
 
History and Heritage in Sagunt, Spain 97 
role in technological development and the response to social and environmental 
challenges. 
“Innovative ecosystems” are also smart, which connect this concept with that 
of “smart territories”. The United Nations defines these territories as those that use 
information and communication technologies (ICTs) to improve quality of life, 
efficiency, and competitiveness, while respecting present and future needs, as well 
as cultural aspects. Social intelligence, linked to the knowledge and culture of the 
inhabitants, is what social innovation seeks to revitalise, especially in territories 
in crisis, physically and socially distant from the most competitive urban centres. 
Since the beginning of this century, concepts such as citizen participation, lo-
cal community development, social innovation, innovative ecosystems, and smart 
territories have been consolidated in public policies. This “participatory turn” in 
cultural and heritage policies has proven to be essential for implementing and un-
derstanding policies in these fields (Saurugger 2010). 
The access of local communities to their heritage generates greater social en -
gagement and active participation in conservation and enhancement processes. 
When policies do not solve society’s problems, society demands its “right to 
culture”, which leads to changes in local development support structures and the 
emergence of new forms of governance, where both social actors and public agen-
cies share responsibilities (Moulaert et al. 2007). These new forms of governance 
give priority to projects that respect the environment and quality of life, based on 
the material and immaterial culture of the territory, which generates identity and 
social commitment (Alivizatou 2022). 
Among the current challenges of cultural heritage are the dissemination, com -
munication, and promotion of cultural values. The aim is to foster identification 
between society and its cultural legacy, promoting the conservation and dissemina-
tion of these values (Alba-Pagán and Pitarch-Garrido 2018). The transmission of 
knowledge should cover a broad social spectrum, adopting an inclusive and non-
elitist perspective. 
In recent years, UNESCO has pointed out the importance of involving the most 
vulnerable groups (children, women, youth, marginalised groups, etc.) in herit-
age decisions, broadening the social spectrum of participation beyond the usual 
cultural associations (Labadi 2013). An effective participatory approach provides 
mutual benefits between cultural heritage and society, with the public university 
being a key partner in achieving this goal. 
Universities have developed, in recent decades, their “third mission”, the transfer 
of knowledge to society, making them key players in the territories. This role in -
cludes collaboration with entities dedicated to citizen welfare and regional develop-
ment (Bellandi, Donati, and Cataneo 2021). Public universities, as territorial agents, 
are able to articulate diverse interests and meet social needs, fostering new social 
relationships and collaborative models to address the main challenges of cultural 
and heritage management (Menter 2024). 
The confluence of these approaches generates a new perspective in the manage-
ment of cultural and local heritage, where universities have the scientific and social 
authority to lead initiatives that favour the participation and valorisation of heritage. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
98 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Sagunt: History and heritage in a strategic setting 
The municipality of Sagunt, located in northern Valencia, Spain, along the Mediter-
ranean coast, has a history dating back to the Iberian culture (6th–1st century BC). 
Its two main centres, Sagunt and the Port of Sagunt, occupied strategic positions that 
facilitated both maritime and land trade, notably controlling the coastal road later 
known as the Via Augusta, which connected to inland mining areas (Ledo-Caballero 
2005; Aranegui 2015). This location influenced Sagunt’s historical development, 
making it one of Europe’s oldest cities with a wealth of cultural heritage. 
Initially called Arse, the Iberian city minted its own currency and engaged in 
Mediterranean trade but gained prominence due to its role in the Second Punic 
War (218–201 BC). The siege of Sagunt by Hannibal in 219 BC, which served as a 
pretext for the war, has been immortalised by classical authors as a symbol of loy-
alty and heroism, forming part of Sagunt’s intangible heritage. Once incorporated 
into the Roman Empire, Saguntum received significant architectural investments, 
gaining monumentality akin to major Roman cities. The city’s Roman heritage in-
cludes the Roman theatre, one of Spain’s first Historical Heritage Monuments, the 
circus, the monumental forum, and various domus, with many artefacts preserved 
in the city’s Archaeological Museum. Modern initiatives like the Domus Baebia 
classical education programme and the ludi saguntini, or Sagunt Classical Culture 
Workshops, have revived interest in this heritage (Aranegui 2015). 
Following the fall of the Roman Empire, Sagunt declined and even lost its an -
cient name until its recovery in 1868. In the 8th century, the city was conquered by 
the Moors and renamed Morbyter (now Murviedro or Morvedre), meaning “Old 
Walls”, symbolising its ancient past. The Islamic period brought agricultural, ce -
ramic, and trade growth, and architectural contributions like the castle and mosque, 
shaping the layout of Sagunt’s historic centre. 
In 1098, Rodrigo Díaz de Vivar (El Cid) briefly conquered Murviedro, an event 
referenced in El Cantar del Mio Cid . In 1239, King James I of Aragon reclaimed 
Sagunt for the Christian kingdoms, initiating the construction of monuments such 
as El Salvador Church and the Church of Santa María. Sagunt’s significant Jewish 
community, especially after the 1391 pogrom, left a historic Jewish quarter, now a 
valued cultural and tourist site symbolising religious coexistence. 
In the Napoleonic era, Sagunt faced a turbulent siege during the Battle of Sagunt 
in 1811, remembered through historical reenactments and the legacy of guerrilla 
fighter José Romeu y Parras. The city later hosted the 1874 pronunciamiento by 
General Martínez Campos, ending Spain’s First Republic and beginning the Bour-
bon Restoration. King Alfonso XII honoured Sagunt for its loyalty by granting it 
city status. 
In 1916, Ramón de la Sota founded the Compañía Siderúrgica del Mediterrá-
neo, establishing an iron and steel industry that transformed the city’s economy un-
til the industry’s decline in the late 1970s (García, Angel, and Morlán 2009). Today, 
Sagunt’s industrial heritage, including preserved blast furnaces and historic work-
ers’ neighbourhoods, commemorates the city’s economic evolution and workers’ 
struggles. Sagunt’s extensive heritage, from ancient to industrial eras, represents 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
History and Heritage in Sagunt, Spain 99 
a significant asset for tourism and economic development, although it has yet to 
receive the full recognition it deserves. 
Enhancing the value of Sagunt’s heritage: Routes and events 
The cultural landscape of Sagunt reflects centuries of evolving social and eco -
nomic structures that shape its presence in multiple prominent cultural routes at 
regional, national, and international levels. Among these is the Phoenicians’ Route, 
a Council of Europe Cultural Itinerary certified in 2003, which highlights the mari-
time routes used by the Phoenicians for trade and cultural exchange throughout 
the Mediterranean. Sagunt played a key role, particularly within the Hannibal Way 
sub-itinerary, which relates to the Roman-Carthaginian conflict of the Second Pu-
nic War (Ruiz 2020). 
Sagunt is also part of the European Route of Jewish Heritage, a Cultural Itiner-
ary of the Council of Europe since 2004, recognised as a Great Cultural Itinerary 
in 2011. Since 2019, Sagunt has been included in the Network of Spanish Jew-
ish Quarters – Paths of Sepharad, promoting cultural, academic, and tourism pro-
jects. This inclusion reflects the preservation of its medieval Jewish quarter, one of 
Spain’s best-preserved Sephardic heritage sites, and supports ongoing archaeologi-
cal and academic research on its Jewish history (Tondre 2007). 
In addition, Sagunt is a member of the European Industrial Heritage Route 
(ERIH), established in 1999 to connect European industrial sites across sectors like 
textiles, iron/steel, and mining. This route underscores industrialisation’s transna-
tional impact on European cities and landscapes. Sagunt’s industrial heritage has 
been revitalised through the Fundació Comunitat València de Patrimoni Industrial 
i Memòria Obrera de Port de Sagunt, promoting international projects and partner-
ships (Fernández and Guzmán 2005; García de Miguel 2002). 
Sagunt participates in several other routes, including the Holy Grail Route, 
which is expanding internationally, and the Iberian Route within the Valencian 
region. Its historical significance as a centre of Romanisation and classical culture 
on the Iberian Peninsula was affirmed when it was named Capital of Romanisa -
tion and Classical Culture. Each summer, the “ Sagunt a Escena” festival, held in 
the Roman theatre, along with historical reenactments, bolsters public interest in 
classical culture and local heritage. Through these efforts, Saguntine society has 
embraced public history initiatives, preserving and promoting its legacy within a 
broader European context ( Chambille 2023). 
Citizen participation, the university, and the enhancement of 
heritage and culture in Sagunt 
Sagunt’s society has a strong tradition of defending its cultural heritage, which 
spans from prehistoric to industrial times, reflecting its role as a historic crossroads. 
Over 60 active associations, from cultural to solidarity groups, form a robust social 
network that promotes civic participation through digital platforms. This network 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
100 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
represents an innovative social ecosystem advocating for Sagunt’s recognition as a 
UNESCO World Heritage site since the early 21st century. 
Sagunt’s territory is considered a cultural landscape and “meta-site” due to its 
extensive archaeological and industrial heritage. This includes Iberian, Roman, and 
medieval cultural artefacts, and, notably, industrial structures preserved from the 
decline of the steel industry in the 20th century. During the 1990s, citizens organ -
ised to defend the city’s blast furnaces, marking a turning point in local heritage 
awareness. This movement shifted heritage conservation from the hands of schol -
ars to a broader community effort, establishing historical preservation as a shared 
identity (Revert 2021). 
While cultural associations led these preservation efforts, public participation 
has not been fully democratic, with associations often focusing on specific heritage 
elements rather than unified protection of Sagunt’s tangible and intangible herit-
age. Among these, the Centre d’Estudis del Camp de Morvedre, founded in 1989, 
has been crucial in researching and disseminating Sagunt’s history, inspiring other 
heritage-focused groups. 
To strengthen public involvement, the Sagunt World Heritage Advisory Council 
was established, including the City Council, Valencian universities, and local asso-
ciations. This council, alongside a Civic Commission, allowed citizens and research-
ers to contribute to heritage management (Cortés-Vázquez, Jiménez-Esquinas, and 
Sánchez-Carretero 2017). Although initial UNESCO application attempts failed, 
recent citizen engagement and collaboration with the University of Valencia have 
renewed interest in preserving and enhancing Sagunt’s cultural heritage. 
The University of Valencia signed, in 2019, a contract with the City Council of 
Sagunt in order to develop a Report of Previous Studies for the Opinion of Sagunt 
World Heritage Site and the execution of a Master Plan involving aspects related 
to the coordination, the formulation of the objectives, and the definition of the role 
developed by the drafting team in the tasks of description, study, definition, draft-
ing, evaluation, and monitoring in the preparation of the dossier. In these university 
approaches, work has been constantly centred on the processes of citizen partici -
pation, especially attending to cultural associations and local collectives, among 
which there are an important number of associations focused on the dissemination 
and defence of their cultural heritage (Pastor Pérez et al. 2021). 
The leading role of the University of Valencia 
Sagunt currently possesses essential territorial branding resources that can underline 
a high cultural and creative density: historical re-enactments, the ludi saguntini, 
the didactic workshops of the Domus Baebia, the cultural routes, and the classical 
theatre festival Sagunt a Escena, held in the remains of its Roman theatre. Sagunt 
can join the new paradigms of heritage-driven, scalable, and replicable urban re -
generation, replacing the object-oriented approach with a spatial approach to herit-
age planning and offering new economic and innovative opportunities (Rausell and 
Sanjuan 2021). This implies a change from a policy oriented to the conservation and 
enhancement of individual heritage elements (e.g., the Castle or the Theatre) to the 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
History and Heritage in Sagunt, Spain 101 
conservation of the whole environment, the territory, which includes these heritage 
elements and others, both tangible and intangible, and which facilitates the reading 
and understanding by the citizens of a complex and rich past. In all these processes, 
the leadership of the University of Valencia, shared with the City Council and local 
cultural associations, has been fundamental and explains the generation of social 
innovation processes that help to generate knowledge and relational social capital. 
Within the framework of this agreement, the University of Valencia initiated a 
process of public participation with the aim of determining the emotional and vindic-
tive link between the citizens and the cultural heritage of Sagunt and the role they 
expected the university to play in this context. The analysis prior to the participatory 
actions had highlighted as a strength the density of the associative network, as a cause 
of the conservation of some ethnological material assets, such as those associated 
with industrial heritage, as well as the struggle for the correct management of the 
heritage of antiquity, especially historically linked to the process of Romanisation. 
The Sagunt society, which is plural and dynamic, has ensured that cultural heritage 
remains alive and articulates feelings of identity and belonging to a community. This 
showed the importance of cultural heritage when it comes to thinking in collective 
and community terms. However, the demands for greater participation in these forms 
of collective sociability have not always been heard in the processes of heritage en-
hancement by the institutions, which have not managed to work in a more collabora-
tive and participatory manner in the processes of cultural management. 
In this sense, the studies for the elaboration of a World Heritage candidacy re -
quired that local associations be given a leading role and be taken into consid -
eration as part of the main heritage actors. One of the main challenges was to 
undertake arduous, in-depth fieldwork to explore the representative elements in the 
collective imagination of today’s society. What is exceptional about Sagunt may 
lie not only in its heritage remains but also in how local society identifies, chooses, 
uses, transforms, adapts, and coexists with this heritage to make the heritage legacy 
it receives a central civic force for human development. 
Communities and local stakeholders 
The future of cultural heritage lies in participation; in a community with the ca -
pacity to be the protagonist of its own cultural and natural landscape. Let us focus 
on society. Organised civil society is one of the keys to safeguarding, since the 
protagonists of the territory are each of the members of the community, and for 
the social group as a whole, it is a symbolic category not only as a geographical 
dimension commensurable on a map, but also as a social and material metaphysical 
dimension that inhabits it, that gives it coherence, that ritualises it, and interprets it 
in a tangle of collective and individual human meanings. 
In recent years, UNESCO stipulates that in planned actions, heritage actors 
must be aware that the level of participation in decision-making and the capacity 
of local actors to make real and possible contributions is often limited, but it is 
therefore necessary to involve the weakest actors (children, women, marginalised 
groups, young people, etc.), seeking to broaden the social spectrum of participation 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
102 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
beyond the necessary and often close collaboration with cultural associations (Lab-
adi 2013). Thus, an effective participatory approach provides reciprocal benefits 
between cultural goods and society, and all this depends on the understanding 
that citizens have and gain about them: who participates in decision-making, as -
sessment, planning, implementation, and evaluation of processes, and how; who 
contributes with experience, knowledge, and skills, and how; who benefits eco-
nomically, socio-culturally, and psychologically, and how; who benefits economi-
cally, socio-culturally, and psychologically, and how. 
Indeed, local associations and collectives play an important role in almost all 
areas of social activity, contributing to job creation, active citizenship, and de -
mocracy, providing a wide range of services, playing a leading role in the cultural 
sphere, representing citizens’ interests before public administrations, assuming a 
very important role in safeguarding human rights, and exercising an essential ac -
tivity in development policies. These groups have a wide presence in Sagunt, and 
more than 300 associations were registered on the Sagunt City Council’s citizen 
participation portal, of which two dozen were classified as “Cultural”. 
These associations defending Sagunt’s cultural heritage are part of the civic 
platforms that made up the Civic Commission in the Sagunt World Heritage 
Council. Its aim is to promote a joint work network and at the same time establish 
different collaboration guidelines. Thus, the fundamental objectives are: 
1 Encourage teamwork and establish channels for dialogue and the programming 
of joint actions between cultural civic associations. 
2 To push the opinion and involve civic platforms in the decisions and work of 
the candidacy process. 
3 Jointly establish a diagnosis of the state of the heritage, its management, conser-
vation, dissemination, and establish the necessary lines of action. 
4 To identify the different points of view and to analyse the divergences and com-
mon opinions or assessments regarding heritage, and specifically those associ-
ated with the World Heritage nomination. 
This process, concreted in the Civic Commission, is led by the Major of the 
city. At the same time, in 2019, the University of Valencia decided to carry out an 
analysis using the integrated participatory methodology, which was attended by: 
• Centre Arqueològic de Sagunt  (Archaeological Center of Sagunt), a historical 
association that publishes a specialised journal, called Arse, and conserves an 
important museum collection; 
• Col.lectiu Patrimoni Saguntí (Saguntino Heritage Collective) which organises 
routes and conferences on local heritage, as well as various competitions associ-
ated with cultural heritage; 
• APIPS. Patrimoni industrial de Sagunt (Industrial heritage of Sagunt), which 
organises an annual cycle of conferences, activities with educational centres, 
and twinning with other towns; 
• AGUICAM, a local association of tourist guides; 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
History and Heritage in Sagunt, Spain 103 
• Centre d’Estudis del Camp de Morvedre, a historical association that publishes 
the magazine Braçal, organises congresses and conferences, and conducts stud-
ies on local history, culture, and popular traditions of the region; 
• Campaners de Morvedre (Bell ringers of Morvedre), an association dedicated to 
recovering traditional bell ringing, and 
• Saguntum civitas, an association specialising in the dissemination of history and 
heritage through historical recreations, especially “Saguntum Invicta”. 
In this first meeting, the main proposals highlighted were: the creation of a 
consortium between public administrations and associations; the creation of the 
Museum of Romanity; and tourist and hotel initiatives related to historical and 
archaeological resources. 
Participation and co-creation 
In this first analysis, the university team noted that the historical inertias of the 
claims of the older associations weighed on the proposals and that a confrontation 
of ideas and debate was necessary as a starting point for an in-depth dialogue. This 
is necessary to cover different issues on the perception of heritage, understanding 
of its historical dimension, educational values associated with heritage, as well as 
to understand the evolution of the concept of heritage and its current relationship 
with the community and society as a whole. 
This process of citizen participation with the collaboration (not guidance) of 
the university is necessary to set in motion processes of co-creation of knowledge, 
of proposals for the enhancement of cultural heritage, the conservation of tangi -
ble and intangible culture, and the communication of this value to the younger 
generations. This co-creation process is essential to involve all people and citizen 
associations interested and involved in the conservation of heritage and culture. 
For this purpose, a semi-structured interview model was used. Following the 
individual interviews, focus groups were organised in which the participants were 
able to give their vision of Sagunt’s cultural heritage, and develop new approaches. 
The following associations took part: Passió per Sagunt; Saguntum Civitas; Sa-
guntum Augusta; Col.lectiu Patrimoni Saguntí; Centre d’Estudios comarcals del 
Camp de Morvedre; Asociación de Voluntarios por el Patrimonio; APIPS; and 
Centre Arqueològic de Sagunt and Campaners de Morvedre. 
The questionnaire was based on a series of open questions, with the aim of 
generating debate and reflection, as well as a consensus on the development of the 
proposal for the elements to be included in the World Heritage nomination: 
1 What do you consider Cultural Heritage to be today? 
2 What developments do you think it has undergone today? 
3 What relationship do you think heritage should have with society? 
4 What degree of involvement and participation do you think there should be 
between the community and its heritage? 
5 How can it be improved? 

 
 
 
 
 
104 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
6 Which of Sagunt’s heritage assets would you highlight? 
7 How would you establish a relationship with concepts such as integral heritage, 
heritage site, and heritage of use? 
8 What elements of cultural management do you consider relevant? 
The aim was also to promote an understanding of cultural heritage as a set of 
tangible and intangible elements, encompassing increasingly diverse typologies 
and not centred on the old traditional view of the heritage, which in the case of 
Sagunt has been historically associated with its most characteristic monuments and 
with a specific period: Roman antiquity. 
From this perspective, it was of interest to gauge the opinion of Sagunt’s civic 
cultural associations on the education in values that the dissemination, teaching, 
and didactics of cultural heritage can offer. Indeed, this transformation led to the 
broadening of the notion of heritage, so that it was understood as applicable to all 
cultural expressions. Thus, the boundaries between cultural heritage and the gen -
eral concept of culture itself are becoming increasingly blurred, introducing new 
conceptions that go beyond the cultural and encompass the intangible, the natural 
heritage, industrial design, etc. 
In this phase, the associations were particularly critical with the administrations’ 
management (local government) due to their lack of coordination (for a similar 
critique, see Lähdesmäki in this volume). At the same time, perceptions evolved 
towards a consensus on the broadening of the concept of heritage, which no longer 
only concerns the monumental aspects of the past, but also includes new manifesta-
tions such as crafts, landscape, and intangible heritage, highlighting as significant 
elements the archaeological remains of the Castle and the Roman sites that have 
identified the heritage of Sagunt for centuries, as well as the industrial heritage. In 
addition to the monumental heritage, there is also the value of the remains from 
other historical periods: Iberian, Jewish-medieval, the War of Independence, the 
Second Republic, and the Spanish Civil War. 
In this sense, the educational value was unanimously considered fundamental 
and generated an intense debate. Dissemination and awareness-raising are con-
sidered to be essential in order to ensure that future generations take up the torch. 
Sagunt’s heritage associations stand out for the development of social dissemina -
tion activities aimed at schools in particular. They seek to promote knowledge of 
history, culture, and heritage, but at the same time to raise awareness (planting a 
seed), and to work not only in schools, but also in the family environment, promot-
ing intergenerational dialogue through collective and social memory. Another issue 
was the constant call for coordination work between the dissemination activities 
carried out by the associations and their link with school curricula, with the need 
for a more integrated and natural teaching with its environment. 
On the other hand, a greater social and institutional commitment was called for, 
understanding heritage as a common good, in which generosity must take prec-
edence over self-interested or personalistic actions. Heritage is part of society and 
should be understood as a way of life. In the case of Sagunt, it would be unthink -
able to think that society can live with its back turned to heritage. In this sense, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
History and Heritage in Sagunt, Spain 105 
there is a perception of Sagunt as an integral heritage site. This social consensus is 
fully in line with the current UNESCO conceptualisation of cultural heritage from 
an integral perspective. 
As a main conclusion, it can be argued that there is a unitary vision of the sym-
bolic and heritage capital of the city of Sagunt, which is perceived as a cultural city 
or a crossroads of cultures, and although specific elements of special relevance are 
highlighted, these belong to different heritage typologies and different historical 
periods, without some elements predominating over others. 
The very process of Sagunt’s candidacy as a UNESCO World Heritage Site was 
itself the catalyst and integrating framework for a local development strategy based 
on culture and creativity. The Sagunt World Heritage Advisory Council established 
the following key objectives: to guarantee the cultural rights of citizens, to redirect 
the productive model towards a knowledge-intensive one, and to use the creative 
and cultural field to pursue a fairer, more inclusive society that respects environ-
mental limits. 
However, this work remained a process of study and analysis carried out by 
the University of Valencia, which did not achieve the goals of strengthening 
awareness of the technical and administrative need for a master plan for heritage 
management in the municipality. The associations were demanding: a project 
office, greater coordination between administrations, a consortium for manage -
ment, and the presence of technical specialists. Despite this, there was a shared 
consensus among the members of the Sagunt Heritage Advisory Council about 
the confidence in the role that the University of Valencia could play in the co-
creative processes of study, training, and revaluation of heritage. 
The role of the University of Valencia as a public institution 
The University of Valencia is the second Spanish university in terms of scientific im-
pact. It carries out extensive teaching and research activity in all areas of knowledge 
(basic sciences and engineering, health sciences, education sciences, humanities, and 
social, economic, and legal sciences). Its most important campuses are located in the 
city of Valencia and its metropolitan area, less than 30 kilometres from Sagunt. 
The institutional culture of the university is based on a series of values that al-
low the principles that govern its actions and constitute the main axes of the differ-
ent areas of social responsibility of the University of Valencia, such as excellence, 
commitment to innovation from intellectual leadership, commitment to promoting 
social and economic progress through the advancement of knowledge, transpar -
ency as a mechanism for management and open dialogue with society, the promo-
tion of justice, promoting equal rights and opportunities for all people, equality, 
solidarity, and cooperation. Culture forms an essential part of the identity of the 
University of Valencia, as an institution strongly linked to its territory and linked 
to the local reality through the promotion of culture and the study of the cultural 
manifestations of its territorial and social environment. 
In 2017, the University of Valencia started a training project in Sagunt. It is a 
highly popular seminar that is organised once a year in collaboration with the city 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
106 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
council, always on a topic associated with Sagunt’s candidacy as a UNESCO World 
Heritage Site. This format is generated as a specialised summer course and takes 
the form of one or two days with lectures on a theme centred on the cultural herit-
age of Sagunt, but also round tables and debates with the participation of cultural 
associations. The first edition began in 2017 under the title “Sagunt, Patrimoni de 
la Humanitat” (Sagunt Human Heritage), and since then it has focused on aspects 
associated with Sagunt as a crossroads of cultures, the keys to heritage manage-
ment, symbolic capital in the collective imaginary, heritage routes, etc. 
In recent editions, the contents have been woven in a collaborative way between 
the university and the communities, especially through the associative fabric, re -
sponding to their concerns and needs. These editions worked on the issues of cul -
tural heritage through “Image and Perception” (2021), the Cultural Heritage of 
Sagunt as social and territorial regeneration (2022), and new ways of approaching 
heritage: Technology, Heritage and Dissemination (2023), while this year (2024), 
the approach will be through the method of Public History, emphasising the role 
of the performing arts as a vehicle for the dissemination of classical culture and 
cultural heritage, specifically classical theatre and its staging in the preserved space 
of the Roman theatre and the Sagunt a Escena Festival project and the didactic and 
pedagogical work carried out by the Domus Baebia. 
From 2023, the university has also launched a training itinerary focused on the 
Cultural Heritage of Sagunt, with a duration of three academic years, aimed at 
anyone interested, with no age or previous training restrictions of any kind. This 
is one of the university’s own lifelong learning activities, which is already being 
developed in other municipalities but which, in the case of Sagunt, focuses on the 
enhancement of its heritage from prehistoric times to the contemporary era. These 
university environments focused on the dissemination and knowledge of Sagunt’s 
cultural heritage aspire to be spaces for analysis, debate, and reflection with the 
aim of revaluing cultural assets and promoting their knowledge among the popula-
tion. This programme is in its second year of implementation in 2024–2025, and 
its potential development has been framed within the new regulatory framework 
of the new Organic Law 2/2023, of 22 March, on the University System (LOSU). 
In particular, Article 19 of the LOSU establishes that the creation and transmission 
of university culture in all its diversity is a fundamental mission of the university. 
To this end, the universities shall ensure that the cultural dimension of all their 
activities is maintained and reinforced, promoting its openness, transmission, and 
dissemination to the social environment with an intercultural perspective, lifelong 
learning, and the democratisation of knowledge. 
The programme developed is integrated within the lifelong learning courses and 
responds to the demands of civic platforms that require the presence of cultural 
heritage in education, especially focused on raising awareness of the cultural herit-
age of Sagunt in its historical, social, and cultural contexts. This programme has 
45 ECTS (The European Credit Transfer and Accumulation System) of core sub -
jects and 30 ECTS of optional and free-choice credits, divided into three academic 
years with a total of 75 ECTS, which include subjects such as: Prehistory, Archaeol-
ogy and Ancient History, Literature, Classical Greek and Latin, Medieval History, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
  
History and Heritage in Sagunt, Spain 107 
Philosophy, and Geography, among others. This educational offer is complemented 
by conferences and cultural activities such as the ludi saguntini, in which more than 
120 students from the University of Valencia take part in workshops focused on the 
dissemination of classical culture for schools and high schools. In addition, every 
year the University of Valencia organised a Summer School focused on Sagunt’s 
candidacy as a World Heritage Site. 
Conclusion: Challenges and opportunities for local development 
in Sagunt 
Sagunt is a city with an enormous wealth of heritage. This in itself is an important 
comparative advantage. However, it is not useful to society and would be at risk of 
disappearing if it were not valued, cared for, and known by the people, both those 
who live in the territory and those who do not, but who may be possible visitors 
in the future. Only on the basis of knowledge is it possible to generate interest, 
identification, and action for the care and maintenance of culture and heritage. For 
this, public-private collaboration is fundamental, normally generated between the 
public administration and citizen associations. However, for this collaboration to 
work in the long term, a leadership process is needed, carried out by a person or 
entity that is capable of bringing together different and variable interests. 
In the case of Sagunt, an important part of this leadership has been developed by 
the University of Valencia. It is a public university that has collaborated in recent 
decades with both the associations and the SaguntSagunt Town Council to develop 
activities and research to enhance the value of heritage and, above all, to educate 
citizens about these values. The university is recognised as a scientific entity and, 
therefore, as a generator of educational content with the rigour and quality de -
manded by the public. This recognition endorses its leadership and explains why, in 
recent years, cultural and educational actions have been developed in the territory 
with great success in terms of participation. 
As indicated in the first part of this chapter, participatory governance is a concept 
that has gained importance in the context of cultural management at the territorial 
level. Cities or towns with a great wealth of heritage have entrusted the manage -
ment of their monuments to the administration, but this is changing. Citizens want 
to participate and to have their voices heard when it comes to making decisions that 
concern everyone. Today’s citizens are more qualified than a few decades ago. and 
are organised in groups or associations with great public and private activity. How-
ever, participation is not easy, and specific guidance and qualification processes are 
necessary, including the co-design of these participation processes. 
All this is included in what we know as social innovation. This approach, to -
gether with the co-creation of a long-term strategy, with the collaboration of all 
social agents, is the basis of the new orientation that the EU wants to promote at lo-
cal and regional levels. When these are complex processes in which multiple public 
and private actors participate, clear leadership is exercised and the actions decided 
by the community are carried out, we speak of social innovation ecosystems. In 
this sense, after what has been explained in this chapter, we can affirm that there 

 
 
  
 
108 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
is an ecosystem of social innovation in Sagunt, in the process of consolidation, 
but solid enough to continue working in a process of participation and bottom-up 
proposals with the specific aim of achieving the declaration of World Heritage Site 
by UNESCO, and with the broader aim of protecting the extensive cultural and 
monumental heritage of this city. It is necessary to set in motion a process of teach-
ing and co-learning in order to achieve an informed civil society capable of making 
decisions about its own future. All these elements come together in Sagunt’s herit-
age reality, in the action of its citizens, who have become fundamental actors of 
change, as well as in the potential of the city’s cultural resources. Therefore, from 
the point of view of heritage, it is possible to apply a social innovation approach to 
sustainable development and the achievement of socially smart territories. 
In this process, the University of Valencia has acquired a leadership role granted 
by all the agents involved and based on trust. The social capital generated over the 
years by the good work of the university in Sagunt explains the existence of this 
trust and the great responsibility acquired by the university professors involved in 
the development of the participatory process in Sagunt. 
In our opinion, the prospects are very positive, and at this moment the Univer -
sity of Valencia is working to consolidate the processes of protection of Sagunt’s 
heritage and the dissemination of its values through research and teaching. The 
third mission of the University of Valencia is evident in the city of Sagunt through 
this long process that looks to the future with hope.
