Section II 
Digital tools in heritage 
participation 



7 Digitising literary heritage 
Some lessons from the Digital 
Museum of the Romanian Novel 
Andrei Terian and Ștefan Baghiu 
This chapter aims to explore on the disciplinary status of literary heritage and the 
consequences of its digitisation, starting from a concrete example, that is, Muzeul 
Digital al Romanului Românesc  (The Digital Museum of the Romanian Novel, 
MDRR), an archive of approximately 1,300 novels developed between 2019 and 
2021 by the Astra National Museum Complex and Lucian Blaga University of 
Sibiu (Baghiu et al. 2019, 2020, 2021). To this end, our study is divided into three 
parts: First, an attempt to systematise current meanings and define the status of 
literary heritage as a form of cultural heritage; second, an analysis of how digitisa-
tion processes have impacted the ways in which literary heritage is preserved and 
studied; and finally, a reflection on how the MDRR experience can provide sugges-
tions on how to preserve and study digital literary heritage. 
In our view, such a reflection is necessary primarily due to the absence of sys -
tematic studies dedicated to this phenomenon in specialised literature. Thus, al -
though the idea behind the term “literary heritage” is more or less synonymous 
with the birth of national literatures ( Casanova 2004; Thiesse 2022), the phrase 
itself is a relatively recent and still rarely used in the field of humanities. It appears 
to have emerged in the early decades of the previous century and gained traction 
especially after World War II, when it was interpreted rather idiosyncratically on 
both sides of the Iron Curtain. For example, in the Soviet Union and former Com-
munist Bloc countries, literary heritage came to refer to those literary works that 
either aligned with or, at the very least, did not contradict Party ideology ( Frank 
2022; Goldiș 2012). In this sense, the Press Department of the Central Committee 
of the All-Union Communist Party of Bolsheviks decided in June 1931 to establish 
the “Literary Heritage” series, which has survived for almost a century ( Kudelin, 
Moskovskaya, and Spivak 2022). In liberal societies, however, the phrase referred 
mainly to works that conveyed certain moral, civic, and national values and could 
thus contribute to better integrating individuals into the communities to which they 
belonged (Bentley 1948; Pollock 1942). A notable example in this regard was the 
Literary Heritage Collection launched in the 1960s by Heron Books, which in -
cluded 52 masterpieces of English literature. In other words, both meanings of the 
phrase “literary heritage” have a common denominator: A corpus of literary texts 
intended to shape readers in the spirit of the values of the society that claims such 
works as “heritage.” 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-10 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
114 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
In recent decades, this initial meaning has started to evolve in three comple-
mentary directions. First, following the model of the older and more established 
“cultural heritage,” literary heritage has come to associate the idea of corpus rep -
resentativity with that of fragility, meaning that, without proper preservation, it 
risks being forgotten of even destroyed (this is the case of the “Recovering the US 
Hispanic Literary Heritage” series, initiated in 1993 – see Castañeda and Lomas 
2019). Second, given the materiality of the objects that generate or represent it, 
categorising literary heritage as intangible heritage has become increasingly prob-
lematic, leading scholars to analyse the connections between literature and spe-
cific locations of symbolic or biographical value (Hendrix 2015; Zemgulys 2008). 
Third, the idea of literary heritage has also been examined as a collection of literary 
responses to an extraliterary event, such as the environmental justice movement 
(Newman 2019). 
What is the heritage in literary heritage? 
Despite the semantic diversity highlighted previously, the study of literature as a 
distinct form of cultural heritage still faces significant resistance from both schol-
ars engaged in cultural heritage studies and those within literary studies. Before 
exploring the reasons for this reluctance, it is important to clarify that we approach 
literature here as a practice of writing through which certain “works” are produced 
as a result of particular uses of a language. Regardless of how theorists understand 
this practice – whether as “imaginative’ writing in the sense of fiction,” as discourse 
that “deviates systematically from everyday speech,” or as “a number of ways in 
which people relate themselves to writing” (Eagleton 2018, 265–68) – what is im-
portant is that literature is regarded and valued primarily as use of language, that 
is, as intangible heritage, and only secondarily in terms of the material medium that 
conveys these works. Moreover, these material media – which in the modern era 
we identify as “books” – are by no means exclusive to literature, as they are equally 
used for communicating other types of discourse (history, religion, science, etc.). 
In this context, a primary issue is that literature is not explicitly recognised 
as a form of intangible cultural heritage, not even in the well-known UNESCO 
Convention for the Safeguarding of the Intangible Cultural Heritage (2003). Even 
though literature appears to largely correspond to the definition of intangible cul-
tural heritage outlined in the document (“the practices, representations, expres-
sions, knowledge, skills – as well as the instruments, objects, artefacts, and cultural 
spaces associated therewith – that communities, groups, and, in some cases, indi -
viduals recognise as part of their cultural heritage”) (UNESCO 2018, 11), it is not 
listed among the domains included in this category. The area most closely related to 
literature among the five examples provided in the document seems to be “oral tra-
ditions and expressions, including language as a vehicle of the intangible cultural 
heritage” (UNESCO 2018, 11), given literature’s linguistic dimension. However, 
the specification “oral traditions and expressions” seems more likely to exclude 
rather than include literature in this category. Indeed, the article in the convention 
that lists the five examples states that they are provided “inter alia,” yet it seems 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digitising Literary Heritage 115 
strange for a practice as rich and prestigious as literature to have no place in the 
list of the five most relevant cases of this phenomenon. Moreover, this exclusion is 
further evidenced by the fact that no proper literary work has been included in the 
UNESCO List of Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity. While certain cultural 
products adjacent to literature, such as The Epic of Manas or Kabuki Theater, do 
appear on the list, the absence of world literary masterpieces such as Oedipus Rex, 
The Divine Comedy, Hamlet, Faust, or In Search of Lost Time remains a conclusive 
indicator of the restrictive nature of the selection. 
One possible answer for this exclusion is that literary works simply do not be -
long there. Artefacts such as “books, manuscripts, newspapers, photographs and 
governmental records, […] films, audio and video tapes and discs” (UNESCO 
2010, 3) are considered by UNESCO not classified as cultural heritage per se, but 
rather as samples of “documentary heritage,” and their safeguarding falls under a 
different list (or register), namely the International Register of the UNESCO Mem-
ory of the World Program. This register includes a number of significant literary 
works (not only from major European cultures but also from smaller ones), such as 
Goethe’s literary estate, the manuscript of the heroic poem Song of the Nibelungs, 
or the manuscript collection of Shota Rustaveli’s poem “Knight in the Panther’s 
Skin.” And yet, the 432 inscriptions currently included in the Memory of the World 
cover only a small fraction of the world’s literary heritage. Notably, the works of 
major writers such as Dante, Cervantes, or Balzac are absent – indeed, there is a 
complete absence of any literary works from Italy, Spain, or France. Perhaps even 
more frustrating is the fact that UNESCO has not yet provided an explanation for 
the exclusion of literature from certain lists or for the category to which it believes 
this art form belongs, nor has it justified the reasoning behind such classification 
decisions. In other words, the Memory of the World further complicates the discus-
sion: If literary works are included in the “documentary heritage” category, then 
they are valued only as social documents, rather than as cultural artefacts as well. 
The distance between these two types of texts is very important, because when we 
define literature as a social document we invest it with a certain degree of truth, 
which overshadows that of invention or cultural fabrication specific to what we 
generally call “fiction.” Certainly, literary sociologists and Marxist critics may wel-
come, to some extent, the prospect of interpreting literary heritage as documentary 
heritage, as their perspectives have consistently looked at literary works as clusters 
of ideologemes (Jameson 1982; Rogozanu 2024) or as instances of text in which 
general social ideologies are reflected. However, such arguments do not claim that 
literature is more of a documentary heritage than a cultural heritage; instead, they 
challenge the very line of demarcation between the two categories is questionable. 
There are, however, distinctions made by contemporary theorists that could explain 
the possible reasons why literature might appear closer to documentary than cultural 
heritage. For example, one of the central distinctions in cultural memory studies is 
the one Aleida Assmann makes between “storage memory” and “functional memory” 
(Assmann 2011, 119–35). The first category includes documents, texts, and records 
that exist in libraries, museums, archives, and databases. Since they are stored in 
specially designated places, they have a rather passive role in everyday life, although 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
116 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
parts of them are periodically activated depending on various circumstances. The 
second category includes commemorations, school curricula, monuments, and rituals 
through which the past is kept alive, being systematically “reactivated” in the atten-
tion of the community. It is evident that this distinction broadly corresponds to that 
between documentary and cultural heritage (both tangible and intangible). However, 
Assmann’s dichotomy is somewhat more nuanced, as according to her classification, 
while most of literature belongs to storage memory, there are also works and authors 
of national and world literatures that remain present in the daily lives of communi-
ties, in that they belong to functional memory. In this regard, Assmann’s distinction 
tends to align, at least in the case of literature, with a common binary opposition in 
contemporary literary studies – namely, that between “canon” and “archive” (Moretti 
2017), between the works of the “great” authors, which remain ever-present in in-
dividual and collective memory, and the “great unread,” which now survive only in 
archives. This confirms that no approach to literary heritage can ignore the analytical 
tools of literary studies. 
But are literary studies themselves interested in such an approach? There are sev-
eral reasons that explain the resistance of this disciplinary cluster to the concept of 
“literary heritage.” First, the term itself seems redundant. The idea of heritage more 
or less implies the assumption that its products should be preserved in some way. 
However, libraries have always done this with literary works. In human history, the 
function of libraries as a means of preserving literary tradition predates by several 
millennia the emergence of the museum as a means of preserving cultural tradition. 
Moreover, the very advent of literary studies as a discipline appears to be linked to 
the act of restoring texts, as demonstrated by Aristarchus’ work on Homeric epics 
(Montana 2020). This suggests that museums and heritage studies had more to learn 
from libraries and philology than the other way around. The second reason concerns 
the ambiguity and insufficient definition of the concept of “literary heritage.” Liter-
ary studies have often operated with much more precise concepts, such as “canon” 
(Bloom 1994), “classicism” (Kermode 1983), or “tradition” ( Leavis 2011). These 
notions are more effective primarily because they provide much clearer answers to 
questions such as: Is the entirety of literature preserved to this day considered “liter-
ary heritage”? And if not, where and by what means should this boundary be drawn? 
Last but not least, the idea of canon suggests that, with minimal exceptions, what 
we consider literary heritage is filtered somewhat naturally through loss of public 
interest. Literary heritage is, according to the rigid mechanism of canonical selec -
tion (Bloom 1994), that is, the well-known “slaughterhouse of literature” (Moretti 
2000a), only that collection of literary works which has remained “alive” through 
reading up to the present day. If something has been forgotten, then it was forgotten 
by the public and critics alike for good reason and is no longer worth our attention – 
this seems to be the prevailing dogma in literary studies. 
In recent decades, this dogma has been increasingly called into question for 
various reasons. On the one hand, not only have numerous voices criticised the 
Western canon as an expression of restrictive – if not outright oppressive – groups 
and value systems (Guillory 1993), but more and more scholars have also pointed 
out that much of our image of literature as a cultural phenomenon is based on an 

 
 
 
 
 
   
 
 
  
 
Digitising Literary Heritage 117 
extremely small number of texts, and that a deeper understanding should include 
a much broader range of works. The leading figure of this orientation is Franco 
Moretti, whose concept of “distant reading” paved the way, over the past quarter 
century, for the rediscovery of the importance of archives and for computational 
analyses of literature (Moretti 2000b). On the other hand, the classification of lit -
erature under the category of intangible cultural heritage has increasingly been 
contested over the past decade. Not only manuscripts – as the most concrete traces 
of literary production – but also other elements of material culture, such as me -
morial houses or objects that belonged to writers, seem to support the idea that 
literary heritage is not limited to its intangible dimension (the text as a sequence 
of letters reproducible in different formats) and includes a strong material compo -
nent (Arcos-Pumarola, Llonch-Molina, and Marzal 2019; Sommer 2024). Yet, this 
perspective, too, can be challenged from multiple angles. For example, a house 
in which a writer once lived may have “cultural” value, but this cultural value is 
certainly of a different nature than that of Casa Milà, designed by Antoni Gaudí 
in Barcelona: In the latter case, the house is a work of art, while in the former, the 
house is merely the setting in which a particular writer produced works of art. 
However, we do not aim to resolve here the complex issue of the boundaries of 
literary heritage. It is enough to recall that, regardless of the angle from which it is 
approached, the core of this concept remains the literary texts themselves – that is, 
the verbal artefacts to which we attribute artistic value. From this perspective, we 
believe that literary heritage can be defined as those corpora of literary texts that 
(a) hold representative value for a given community, (b) are at risk of being de -
stroyed or lost, and (c) are, for various reasons, difficult to access by the community 
for which they are representative. It should, however, be noted that these three con-
ditions are not cumulative. To accommodate materialist perspectives, one might op-
tionally expand the definition to include “the material elements that are, for various 
reasons, associated with these corpora of literary texts”; yet the defining feature of 
the concept of literary heritage remains the texts constituting these corpora. 
Digitising literary heritage: The analogue paradox 
Despite the previous clarifications, the definition we have proposed is far from 
transparent. First and foremost, the non-cumulative nature of its three traits sug -
gests that we are actually dealing with three different phenomena. Thus, texts that 
“hold representative value for a given community” appear to correspond to the tra-
ditional definition of the canon. It is less relevant here whether and to what extent 
the aesthetic value of certain texts is hybridised by non- or para-aesthetic values 
such as nationalism, the size of the communities that claim a particular literary 
heritage is, or whether a literary heritage acknowledged as such by a smaller com-
munity (e.g., “the nation”) is rejected or accepted by a broader one (“the world”). 
What is clear is that all literary texts considered valuable by a community automati-
cally fall into the category of literary heritage. By contrast, the second category is 
less clearly defined. It obviously includes manuscripts – especially those that were 
never printed, either because printing technology did not yet exist or because they 

 
  
 
 
 
 
118 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
were intended exclusively for private use. However, especially for earlier periods 
or for smaller cultures where books were not printed in large quantities, there is a 
real risk that even printed works may be lost. For example, the latest edition of the 
dictionary of the Romanian novel records no fewer than 20 titles of novels printed 
between 1866 and 1914 that can no longer be found today ( Tudurachi 2023) – 
showing that print is by no means a guarantee of a work’s “eternity.” On the other 
hand, works can become inaccessible to a community for other reasons as well, 
which would constitute a third type of literary heritage, mainly comprising texts 
from a literary tradition that contemporary audiences understand only partially or 
not at all. The most evident of such obstacles is the language barrier, which arises 
either because numerous changes have occurred in the linguistic norm between the 
time a work was written and the present – making “older” texts difficult to under -
stand – or because the work in question was simply written in languages no longer 
in common use in that culture (e.g., Latin, Greek, or Old Church Slavonic). 
Several insights emerge from the description of the situations above. First of all, 
what connects all three types of texts included under the phrase “literary heritage” 
is the constant threat that seems to loom over them: The danger of disappearing (in 
the case of texts in the second category), the danger of no longer being understood 
(in the case of the third category), and the danger of being misinterpreted, ideolo -
gised, or marginalised – in the case of texts in the first category, where, despite 
appearances, the threats are by no means fewer than in the other cases. Ultimately, 
this is the difference between canon and literary heritage: The canon is an aesthetic 
concept, largely confined to internal disputes among critics, whereas literary herit-
age is a political notion that affects the entire value system of the community that 
embraces it. This becomes particularly evident when discussions arise about revis-
ing school curricula or challenging the status of “national poets” within a particular 
literature; in such cases, controversies often go beyond the artistic realm and spill 
into the public sphere (as happened with the post-communist reevaluation of Mihai 
Eminescu, the “national poet” of the Romanians) (see Terian 2010). In fact, most of 
the “canonical battles” fought in recent decades have actually been battles over lit-
erary heritage – or, more precisely, battles over the canon as literary heritage. This 
has occurred because the canon of a national literature signals not only its artistic 
value but also its international relevance, labelling it a kind of qualitative liter -
ary heritage. Similarly, any literary work – no matter how weak from an artistic 
standpoint – remains significant for a national literature and can thus be regarded 
as a symptom (and, by extension, a part) of its quantitative literary heritage. As for 
“old” literary texts or those written in other languages, they bear testimony to the 
age – and by implication, the nobility (Casanova 2004) – of a national literature and 
can thus be considered indicators of a historical literary heritage. 
Therefore, in all its three dimensions – qualitative, quantitative, and historical – 
literary heritage serves as a political barometer indicating the value, size, and age 
of a literary system (be it regional, national, or international). For this reason, 
no discourse on literary heritage can ignore the issue of its preservation. Over 
time, the process of preservation has taken various forms, ranging from the mul -
tiplication of manuscripts through copying, printing, and facsimile reproduction 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digitising Literary Heritage 119 
to complex treatments aimed at preventing the deterioration of the material sup -
ports through which literary works were transmitted. In our century, the most 
effective form of preserving texts – both in terms of fidelity to the original and 
accessibility to the public – is digitisation. In this regard, we must dispel from the 
outset a confusion that seems to persist even in the most authoritative works in the 
field (Kirschenbaum 2021 ), and distinguish, within the realm of “archives with -
out dust,” between digital  literary heritage, which refers to literature published 
directly online, and digitised literary heritage, which refers to literature initially 
published in print and only later digitised (and which is the focus of this chapter). 
Yet an even more important distinction concerns the very process of digitisation 
itself, and it reveals to some extent a constitutive paradox of the idea of literary 
heritage. What does it actually mean to “digitise” literary heritage? What exactly 
are we digitising from it? 
Texts, of course. But texts themselves are associations of letters (expressions) 
and meanings, and the passage of time makes it increasingly difficult for contem -
porary readers to connect these two dimensions. As a result, a curator of a digitised 
database faces a choice: They can either preserve the original expression of the 
text, with the risk that for many users of the database the text will become opaque, 
or they can linguistically update the text to make its meaning accessible (again) 
to the majority of readers – but this can only be done at the cost of sacrificing the 
text’s original expression. In other words, in the case of digitised literary heritage, 
we are dealing with a conflict between preservation (of the text) and presentation 
(to the reader). Linguists have already noted this conflict when distinguishing be-
tween an “archive” (faithful to the original text) and a “corpus” (standardised ac -
cording to linguistic norms and open not only to understanding but also to analysis) 
(see Johnston 2010). In the context of literary heritage, it could also be described 
as a confrontation between what we have called quantitative literary heritage and 
historical literary heritage. This conflict becomes even more acute given that the 
two theoretically distinct categories are most often illustrated by the same types of 
texts, since the works at risk of disappearance are usually older – and, for that very 
reason, the hardest for us to understand. For traditional philological editions, the 
choice was simple, since they were conceived as “a mechanism intended to bring 
people texts from out of an archive into a market” (Most 2016, 168). But the tradi-
tional scholars did not necessarily concern themselves with preserving the original 
edition of the text that already existed (sometimes in several versions), otherwise 
the philologists would have had no source on which to base their own editions. By 
contrast, for the reasons outlined above, the curator of a database of digitised texts 
must choose between an analogy with the letter and an analogy with the meaning 
of the digitised texts. Of course, it is possible to respect both types of analogy – 
but not at the same time, that is, not in a single version of the text. Therefore, any 
digitisation process of literary heritage faces what we call the analogue paradox, 
which imposes a set of strategic choices between the preservation and presentation 
of texts. In the following section, we describe our own experience in confronting 
and attempting to resolve this paradox, along with other challenges encountered 
during the digitisation of a corpus of Romanian novels. 

 
 
 
120 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
The case of the MDRR 
In 2019, the digitisation of an archive of Romanian novels began. The idea emerged 
in a context where, although there had been national projects which encouraged 
public libraries to engage in scanning their archives since 2007, the central direc -
tives did not include coordination of the digitised materials. Following a decision 
to digitise the book collections of county and university libraries, most library man-
agers began to scan their collections without consulting other libraries. As a result, 
several libraries digitised, generally based on the canonicity criteria or simply al -
phabetically, nearly everything they had on their shelves and in their storage. This 
led to significant duplication of materials, and failed to produce coherent corpora, 
regardless of the genres considered. Moreover, most rural libraries were unable to 
complete the task due to administrative and bureaucratic shortages. In Romania, 
these institutions struggle to secure structural funding, therefore more than 700 ru-
ral libraries had closed in the 2010s (Udișteanu, Pop, and Muntean 2021). Even in 
2023, out of 41 expected projects for renovations, library computers, and training 
courses, planned to be funded via the National Plan for Recovery and Resilience, 
only 14 were submitted, which led to the postponement of the entire competition 
(Semeniuc 2023). 
In this context, the Digital Museum of the Romanian Novel (MDRR) was born 
primarily out of a desire to create and curate a “smaller,” but “systematic” archive, 
against a background where “larger,” yet “incoherent” archives were chaotically 
emerging. Moreover, the Museum was also envisioned as an open access archive 
of Romanian literature, specifically the Romanian novel, given the lack of access 
to such resources in many smaller cities and villages. Furthermore, MDRR was 
designed to be more than just a textual archive, precisely because it had a signifi-
cant educational and research stake: The Museum intended not only to preserve 
the texts in the formats in which they appeared, but also to maintain the design and 
atmosphere of the pages from the 19th century and the interwar period so that those 
who seek to study the volumes as cultural artefacts can also perceive this visual 
specificity of the modern book market in Romania. 
Since accessing funding for such projects – situated at the boundary between 
research and the preservation of cultural heritage – is challenging in Romania, the 
team including members of the Astra National Museum Complex and Lucian Blaga 
University of Sibiu has decided to apply for cultural funding instead of scientific 
research funding. The funding source also influenced the project’s final form as a 
museum of literature, rather than as a standardised digital corpus. MDRR was thus 
constructed through three different smaller projects of €20,000 each, funded by the 
Administration of the National Cultural Fund (AFCN). Although the team changed 
over the course of the three years, with different undergraduates participating in 
the scanning and metadata extraction process, a core group of scholars was main -
tained through all three projects. Beyond this core, the project aimed to include as 
many students as possible, who, in addition to being paid for a task specific to their 
field, would also learn many essential things about modern Romanian literature as 
well as the digitisation process. From the very beginning, this was the first step in 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digitising Literary Heritage 121 
engaging the community – and especially young people – in building the archive. 
The main goal of MDRR was the digitisation of all modern Romanian novels, but 
an equally important secondary objective was, in fact, the involvement of students 
in the construction of the archive. This served both to teach them specific digitisa-
tion techniques and to familiarise them with the literature of earlier periods. In 
other words, we conceived digitisation as a form of learning and training – a kind 
of internship for academic activity. 
Regarding the actual work of digitisation, all three projects conducted for 
scanning the archive of ca. 1,300 Romanian novels published between 1845 and 
1947 involved, in fact, activities carried out by students from Sibiu, Cluj-Napoca, 
Bucharest, and Timișoara. The initial project from 2019, coordinated by Stefan 
Baghiu and Vlad Pojoga ( Baghiu et al. 2019 ), included not only the digitisation 
of all novels published in volume-length and serials between 1845 and 1900 – and 
conversion to readable PDF files – but also several other related activities: Setting 
up a “digital museum” hosted on the website of the Transilvania journal, organis-
ing exhibitions about Romanian literature in the 19th century, and editing a special 
issue of Transilvania including analyses of the novel production during that period 
and the fictional universe of the Romanian novel in the modern era. Over time, 
the two subsequent projects, which led to MDRR 1901–1932 (Baghiu et al. 2020) 
and MDRR 1933–1947 ( Baghiu et al. 2021), stopped organising exhibitions and 
focused more on archiving and research components. This shift occurred because 
the public impact from the first museum had been quite significant for a literary ar-
chive, and the next projects drew on the audience created by the initial exhibitions 
and the publicity generated in the press and social media with the first museum. 
The initial challenge of the project involved locating the books and charting 
the physical archive of the Romanian novel. Despite most of the books being 
held by the Romanian Academy Library and the National Library of Romania, 
the digitisation programmes at these libraries in Bucharest had not ensured the 
scanning and post-Optical Character Recognition (OCR) processing of a coher -
ent archive or corpus of Romanian novels, while other libraries in the country 
simply lacked complete collections of Romanian novels. Therefore, our first task 
was to define the corpus of novels. This step actually involved the community of 
professors and students, who worked together to accurately locate the novels in 
national libraries. From the outset, digitisation required complex bibliographic 
research operations, both physical and online. Similarly, this step meant engag -
ing a diverse academic community in the process of literary heritage tracing. The 
reference point for this initial activity was Dicţionarul cronologic al romanului 
românesc de la origini până la 1989  (DCRR) (see Istrate 2004 ), which provides 
metadata about all the novels published in volume-length format or as serials in 
Romanian. The novels were initially searched for in Sibiu, to more easily conduct 
the scanning work without travelling across the country. Then, any novels not 
found at the Astra County Library or at Lucian Blaga University Library in Sibiu 
were then sought at the Central University Library Lucian Blaga in Cluj-Napoca. 
Ultimately, the archive was completed, where there were no copies available 
in Sibiu or Cluj, by travelling to Bucharest and conducting the scanning at the 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
122 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Romanian Academy Library or the National Library of Romania. This strategic 
approach allowed for a comprehensive collection of Romanian novels to be dig -
itised while minimising logistical costs. 
However, MDRR still faced significant challenges in terms of scanning docu -
ments and compiling the archive. One of the primary issues was the aged appear -
ance of the pages, which often contained spots and other stains. Here is where the 
digitisation of literary heritage meets one of its largest problems. These imper -
fections translated into a substantial amount of noise in the OCR process, com -
plicating the digitisation and accuracy of the text extraction. Recently, Bogdan 
Vătavu and David Morariu have discussed this early phase impediment and how 
in the last year they have worked on solving it by computational analysis ( Vătavu 
and Morariu 2024). Morariu, a former student involved in the original scanning 
and metadata extraction efforts for MDRR, collaborated with Vătavu to improve 
OCR accuracy and prepare the texts for natural language processing. They used 
Regular Expressions (RegEx) to remove all editorial details, such as page num -
bers, and eliminated headers like author names and titles. They also searched for 
and corrected recurrent OCR errors, such as mistakenly replacing letters with 
numbers. Essentially, they cleaned the text extensively to make it suitable for 
tokenisation. After cleaning, they passed all the tokens through two dictionaries 
to determine which ones were correct and which were inaccuracies caused by 
the OCR process. This meticulous approach aimed to refine the digital text to a 
point where it could be effectively used for further computational analysis and 
applications. 
In fact, the OCR process was also the moment when we encountered the ana-
logue paradox, whose existence we initially discovered intuitively. As mentioned, 
the scanning of documents took place in parallel with their OCR processing. How-
ever, when we tried to extract the resulting data into a Word document, we found 
that the character recognition rate rarely – at least in texts prior to the year 1900 – 
exceeded 95%. Therefore, before even considering how to present the text to the 
public, we were faced with the challenge of preserving it as text (and not merely 
as an image). An important hurdle in this respect was the lack of a Romanian 
standardised alphabet and/or writing norms, particularly problematic for pre-WW 
I texts. Thus, the period ca. 1830–1860 was generally marked by the use of the 
so-called “transitional alphabet” (a combination of Latin and Cyrillic characters), 
making consistent digital transcription challenging. The problem persisted in the 
next phase (ca. 1860–1890), when numerous authors followed a “latinised” alpha-
bet (which suppressed Slavic characters such as “ă,” “ș,” or “ț”), but also in the 
period ca. 1890–1914, given the persistence of the “mute i” (= “ĭ”), a character 
which is no longer in use and therefore is not recognised by modern OCR systems. 
The challenge of the different alphabets used in Romanian over the 19th century 
was initially a very big one. The main issues in setting up a transliteration system 
from the Cyrillic alphabet to the Latin one for “older” Romanian language revolve 
around several difficulties. The most significant one lies in the overlap of letters 
between the two alphabets. One prime example is the letter “c,” which represents 
the Latin “s” in the Cyrillic alphabet. In texts written in a transitional alphabet, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digitising Literary Heritage 123 
when “c” appears, it can sometimes be Cyrillic and other times Latin, and the 
only way to deduce which alphabet it belongs to is through the context of its use. 
Similarly, the letter “p” can represent either “p” or “r,” adding to the confusion in 
transliteration efforts. To address these issues, the only viable approach involves 
reading the text and manually replacing the letters as one goes along – and in 
the phases of getting the novels ready for computational analysis, Snejana Ung 
and Teodora Susarenco have done this manually for several cases. This method 
implies reading in transition – interpreting the text through its Cyrillic and Latin 
contexts – and then writing everything out in Latin, letter by letter. Such an ap -
proach leverages the phonetic nature of the Cyrillic script, where letters are read 
as they are written, which somewhat simplifies the process. However, additional 
complications arise with letters in the transitional alphabet that do not exist in the 
current Cyrillic alphabet used in Russian or Serbian, such as “ă,” “î,” and “șt.” 
These letters have Cyrillic equivalents that are unique to texts from the Romanian 
space (and potentially elsewhere, but not in the modern Cyrillic alphabets of Rus -
sian or Serbian). This uniqueness necessitates specific rules and considerations in 
the transliteration process, requiring a nuanced understanding of both the histori -
cal and linguistic contexts and often manually rendering the digital versions of 
literary heritage. 
Beyond these specific issues, working with the 12 MDRR novels written in the 
transitional alphabet revealed something else as well: The necessity of verifying 
the accuracy of the OCR process for each individual text – an operation that could 
only be achieved by expanding the group involved in the project and crowdsourc -
ing the work. For, despite the progress made by automatic tools for digital corpus 
cleaning, these tools will always have a fundamental limitation: In the “recovery” 
of characters and words, they invariably reduce the text to a standard norm. Even 
if that norm were variable (for instance, if the tool were customised differently for 
each of the 19th-century Romanian alphabets and writing conventions), what an 
automatic cleaning process can never do is recognise and validate deviations from 
the norm as expressions of an individual, idiosyncratic style – and not as authorial 
or typographic errors that must be corrected through standardisation. But this is 
precisely what an archive aims to accomplish: To preserve individual stylistic par-
ticularities through the highest possible fidelity to the original. However, this can-
not be done through automated recognition (since software can only “recognise” 
words included in its standardised repertoire), but only through empirical recogni-
tion of characters – in other words, through human involvement. 
To make this possible, the coordination team decided that, in the post-MDRR 
phase (2021–2025), the project would engage as much of the Romanian philologi-
cal community as possible. In this spirit, the cleaning of the scanned novels was 
undertaken through academic internships involving not only students from Lucian 
Blaga University of Sibiu, but also from other universities in Romania (initially 
Babeș-Bolyai University, Ștefan cel Mare University of Suceava, and West Uni-
versity of Timișoara). Over the course of five years, 100 students from the four 
universities were assigned to manually clean 100 novels per year. Although, in par-
allel with MDRR, other projects also emerged that attempted automatic cleaning 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
124 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
of novels – and in many ways better prepared the texts for computational research 
purposes (e.g., ELTeC-rom 2021; Patraș et al. 2024) – each student involved in 
the MDRR community curated a novel, restoring and correcting its original liter -
ary language. Moreover, transforming MDRR into a community-based project also 
meant extracting metadata from the novels – data that could not be automatically 
generated – and writing extended summaries of the novels, which we hope will 
help form the basis for the first comprehensive encyclopaedia of the Romanian 
novel. 
Now, after nearly six years of work, MDRR does not necessarily seem to 
us like a stereotypical success story, but rather a cautionary tale. On the one 
hand, only a third of the novels in the project’s archive have been cleaned so 
far, and these still need to go through various annotation processes to reach the 
level of a standard linguistic corpus. On the other hand, several sets of MDRR 
metadata have already been the subject of multiple studies (e.g., Chiorean et al. 
2021; Goldiș et al. 2020; Pojoga et al. 2020), which have had a significant im -
pact on Romanian literary research. It is estimated that completing the MDRR 
corpus will require at least five more years. We cannot claim to have solved the 
analogue paradox, but at least we now know the path to satisfying both of its 
demands (archive and corpus): The involvement of an increasingly broad com -
munity, one that becomes convinced of the value of the work as they engage in 
it – and, just as importantly, of the relevance of MDRR as a form of digitised 
preservation of the Romanian literary heritage. For these reasons, even though 
there is still much work ahead, not only have we not abandoned the project, 
but we have in fact expanded it. Starting this year, MDRR has been joined by a 
Digital Museum of Romanian Poetry ( Vancu et al. 2025 ), as well as a corpus of 
Romanian drama (Terian 2025). And we aim to return soon with updates on all 
these projects.
