DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-11 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
8 Challenges in the collaborative 
design of participatory heritage 
platforms between the Global 
North and Global South 
Towards decolonial computing 
Khaoula Stiti 
Since the beginning of the twenty-first century, the intersection of digital technolo-
gies and heritage has been experiencing a transformative shift driven by the increas-
ing convergence of digital tools and cultural preservation practices. This evolving 
landscape brings new opportunities for collaboration, engagement, and represen -
tation of heritage, offering innovative ways for communities and researchers to  
interact with and contribute to the understanding of heritage. As the field moves to-
wards more participatory and inclusive approaches, digital heritage platforms have 
emerged as vital tools for the co-production of knowledge. These platforms not 
only facilitate the digitisation of heritage but also raise more awareness about non-
heritagised sites through the active involvement of users, allowing for a broader,  
more democratic engagement with cultural narratives. 
The growing interest in these platforms has highlighted the necessity to criti -
cally examine their design and development processes. This is particularly urgent 
when these platforms are created through collaborative partnerships between re -
searchers from the Global South and the Global North. Such partnerships, while 
offering vast potential for cross-cultural exchange and knowledge sharing, also 
bring forth challenges. These challenges are often shaped by historical power im -
balances, differing socio-political contexts, and cultural perspectives that influence 
how platforms are built and used. These disparities can affect the functionality, 
accessibility, and inclusivity of the platforms themselves, potentially reinforcing 
existing inequalities or, conversely, providing opportunities to redress these imbal-
ances by integrating more diverse voices and perspectives into the digital heritage 
narrative. 
The central research questions of this chapter are as follows: what are the tech-
nological and cultural challenges in the development of participatory heritage plat-
forms for the Global South in a North-South collaboration, and what potential paths 
can decolonial computing offer to address these challenges and overcome the power 
imbalances and cultural biases inherent in such collaborative projects? The chapter 
starts with a theoretical framework that defines the differences between participa -
tory and consultative heritage platforms, highlighting how participatory platforms  
encourage user interaction while consultative platforms passively deliver content. 
This distinction is important as it will be used later to identify limitations in the 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
  
Challenges in the Development of Participatory Heritage Platforms 129 
design of participatory heritage platforms. The methods section elaborates on the 
research approach, describing the use of a case study and participant observation to 
explore the two different settings. Participant observation was employed as part of 
the P@trimonia (P@ 2.0) 2.0 project, where insights were gathered about partici -
patory processes and challenges encountered in designing a participatory heritage 
platform. 
The results are structured to demonstrate how the influence of the Global North 
approach on the Global South context gives rise to two categories of challenges. 
First, the technological challenges stem from the inadequacy of a one-size-fits-all 
approach. A model designed in the Global North, exemplified by the Open Air 
Museum at the Sart-Tilman university campus in Liège, can fail to account for the 
complexities of local heritage landscapes in the Global South, exemplified through 
the colonial heritage of Tunis. Meanwhile, the cultural challenges manifest through 
mechanisms such as cultural bias, where Eurocentric perspectives dominate the 
shaping of the participatory platform without integrating local contexts; power im-
balances in research collaborations, where decision-making authority and resources 
are concentrated in the Global North; and technological solutionism, which over -
emphasises digital technologies as universal fixes, disregarding the socio-cultural 
intricacies. These challenges underscore the need for context-sensitive approaches 
in participatory heritage platform development and set the stage for the discussion, 
where the complexities of these challenges will be further explored, emphasising 
the need for context-sensitive approaches in the development of participatory herit-
age platforms. 
Building on these findings, the discussion extends to propose a transformative 
solution based on decolonial computing principles, aiming to address the identified 
limitations and biases. This section also emphasises the importance of aligning 
technology development with decolonial perspectives and advocates for continu -
ous self-reflection within the global Human-Computer Interaction (HCI) commu -
nity to challenge colonial legacies and ensure more equitable technology practices. 
Finally, the chapter concludes by summarising the main contributions of the re -
search to the field of decolonising technology. It reiterates the significance of de-
veloping context-aware and culturally sensitive solutions for participatory heritage 
platforms. The conclusion ties together the insights from the study and reflects on 
the broader implications for future research and practice in the field of heritage 
management and technology development. 
 Defining(non-)participatoryheritageplatforms 
Online heritage data can exist in various forms, accessible through platforms that 
support the access, creation, or sharing of activities, services, or content. Defin-
ing participatory platforms in the context of heritage data is important because it 
determines how heritage information is shared and engaged. This distinction, par -
ticipatory or not, can affect how heritage resources are utilised, preserved, and un-
derstood, influencing both the depth of public engagement and the effectiveness of 
heritage conservation efforts. In this chapter, I define a platform as a digital space 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
130 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
where users can engage, interact, or transact within a specific ecosystem. Platforms 
can be categorised as either consultative or participatory, with the former not being 
inherently participatory. 
Non-participatory platforms or consultative platforms, akin to those of the early 
Web 1.0 era, primarily deliver content or information in a unidirectional manner. 
Web 1.0 is characterised by its role as a publishing medium rather than a space for 
interaction (Nath, Dhar, and Basishtha 2014). Users can consume information but 
cannot interact or contribute. Similarly, consultative platforms focus on providing 
access to specific information like news, educational resources, or product details, 
with minimal user engagement. In the heritage context, these platforms are used 
for managing and preserving heritage data through computerised methods ( Meyer 
et al. 2007), and many cultural institutions are digitising their resources for online 
access (V oorbij 2010). 
Conversely, participatory platforms, reflecting Web 2.0 principles, are designed 
to foster active user involvement, collaboration, and contribution (Arora 2015). 
Web 2.0 emphasises interactive and collaborative web use, highlighting social in-
teraction and collective intelligence (Murugesan 2007). These platforms feature 
discussion forums, user-generated content tools, crowdsourcing, and social net-
working elements. In heritage, they enable users to participate in content creation, 
curation, and dissemination, promoting inclusive online communities. However, 
not all participatory platforms engage users at the same level; for instance, some 
platforms primarily focus on data collection, such as citizen science projects where 
participants gather environmental data without much involvement in the analysis 
or decision-making. On the other hand, platforms like participatory design projects 
encourage users to collaborate in the creation or refinement of a product or ser -
vice, involving them in collective problem-solving and reflection. These different 
approaches highlight the varying degrees of engagement and the diverse ways in 
which users contribute to the process. 
As participatory heritage platforms evolve, collaborative efforts in their design 
become increasingly important. These collaborations often involve stakeholders 
from both the Global North and the Global South, which can introduce diverse 
perspectives and methodologies. Given the potential for differing priorities and 
approaches, it is crucial to examine how the influence of the Global North might 
impact the development and functionality of platforms in the Global South. Such 
an examination ensures that collaborative designs are equitable and sensitive to 
the needs and contexts of all participants, ultimately fostering more effective and 
inclusive heritage platforms. 
P@trimonia 2.0 as a case study and participant observation 
From 2019 to 2024, I participated as a doctoral researcher in the P@ 2.0 research 
project (2019–2023), which aimed to develop a participatory platform for man -
aging spatial semantic information related to architectural and urban heritage in 
the Tunisian context. The goals of the project included the democratisation of ac -
cess to architectural and urban heritage, decentralisation of heritage promotion by 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges in the Development of Participatory Heritage Platforms 131 
highlighting lesser-known sites outside traditional museum spaces, and the valorisa-
tion of heritage through systematic data collection, structuring, and dissemination 
(Stiti, Jeunejean, and Ben Rajeb 2022). However, the project design did not include 
elements based on decolonial computing. Additionally, it is essential to emphasise 
that while my doctoral project was a component of the broader P@ 2.0 project, its 
objectives extended beyond merely developing the mobile application. 
P@ 2.0 was designed based on a previous research project, P@ 1.0 (2016– 
2019), which focused on designing the interface of the mobile application. P@ 1.0 
contributed by enabling personalised visits to historical sites via the mobile appli -
cation, offering intellectually and visually enriching experiences (Allani, Kharrat, 
and Leclercq 2020). The P@ 1.0 mobile application emphasised exploring Points 
of Interest (PoIs) rather than linear narratives, allowing users to customise their 
exploration journeys. The PoIs were planned to be dynamically generated based 
on factors such as the user’s location, time, events, and past visits, ensuring each 
experience was unique. This approach encouraged user-driven exploration and in-
teraction with the environment. 
The site selected for the development of P@ 1.0 was the Medina of Tunis, a 
Global South context that has fully undergone the heritagisation process. It is highly 
valued by its residents and has received recognition from national institutions, such 
as the National Heritage Institute, as well as international bodies. Notably, it has 
been a UNESCO World Heritage Site since 1979. The project P@ 1.0 involves sev-
eral key partners with distinct responsibilities, as outlined in the proposal submitted 
for funding from Wallonie-Bruxelles International, a Belgian governmental agency 
that supports research projects as part of its mission. In Belgium, the research lab 
LUCID (Lab for User Cognition & Innovative Design), affiliated with the Univer-
sity of Liège, was responsible for technological and methodological transfers, with 
a senior professor, a doctoral student, and a development engineer involved. In 
Tunisia, the research unit PAE3C (Unité de recherche : Patrimoine, Architecture et 
Environnement, Connaissance Compréhension et Conservation, in English Herit-
age, Architecture and Environment – Knowledge, Understanding and Conserva-
tion), affiliated with the National School of Architecture and Urbanism, which is 
itself affiliated with the University of Carthage, ensures the scientific and historical 
validity of the project data under the guidance of various senior researchers. Ad -
ditionally, the non-governmental heritage organisation Edifices & Mémoires plays 
a critical role in operational actions on-site, led by its vice president. The project 
P@ 1.0 was funded by Wallonie-Bruxelles International from 2016 to 2018, which 
chose LUCID Lab as their primary contact. 
The P@ 2.0 research project aimed to introduce a participatory aspect into the 
existing mobile application developed during the P@ 1.0 project, allowing users 
of all backgrounds to contribute and share data related to the PoIs. The decision 
to introduce a participatory aspect into the existing mobile application developed 
during the P@ 1.0 project was made by the P@ 1.0 partners and a new partner, who 
recognised the need to enhance user engagement and contribute to a more interac-
tive experience. The new research partner was the research unit in Architecture and 
Architectural Engineering (AIA), affiliated with the Brussels Engineering School, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
132 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
which itself is part of the Université Libre de Bruxelles. I conducted my doctoral 
research under the supervision of a senior researcher at this research unit. There -
fore, the P@ 2.0 project team consisted of the existing members from P@ 1.0, 
along with us, the new additions to the team. 
This endeavour posed the challenge of creating a participatory mobile system 
while maintaining the visit experience from P@ 1.0. This was an issue because de-
veloping a participatory mobile system requires balancing active user engagement – 
through contributions – with the preservation of the traditional visitor experience. 
The challenge lay in ensuring that the introduction of participatory elements does 
not disrupt the immersive and educational heritage site visit. In this context, the 
difficulty is to integrate a system that allows for meaningful participation without 
compromising the authenticity and intended experience of the site, thus maintain -
ing its integrity while fostering engagement. The implementation tests focused 
on the colonial heritage of Tunis, aligning with a primary hypothesis that sharing 
heritage-related data can enhance heritage awareness as an initial step in the her -
itagisation process. This hypothesis was supported by the P@ 2.0 project partners 
and included in the project funding proposal. 
The development timeline for the P@ 2.0 mobile application was originally 
planned to span four years, from 2019 to 2023, but it was adjusted to occur be-
tween 2020 and 2024. The development of P@ 2.0 proceeded in two main phases. 
The first phase, conducted in collaboration with LUCID from November 2020 to 
August 2021, was followed by the second phase, led by the private development 
company Eunoia Studio, from August 2023 to March 2024. 
To deepen my understanding of the collaborative process of designing participa-
tory heritage platforms, I engaged in participant observation throughout the P@ 2.0 
research project. Although the focus on collaboration was not part of the original 
goals of the research project or my doctoral research objectives, aimed to examine 
heritage management in post-revolutionary Tunisia, focusing on participatory ac -
tors, digital heritage platforms, and designing a mobile platform based on the P@ 
2.0 project, I recognised its significant value and potential impact. From 2019 to 
2024, as a doctoral researcher, I was actively involved in both the development 
and implementation phases. My observations focused on the collaborative design 
process, particularly the interactions among the research team and between the Tu-
nisian and Belgian partners. By being on the front line of the development of the 
participatory platform, I gained insights into the practical challenges and successes, 
underscoring the chapter’s relevance in exploring how these collaborative dynamics 
influence the effectiveness of participatory heritage platforms. 
Findings: Technological and cultural challenges in the development 
of participatory platforms for heritage for the Global South in a 
North-Southcollaboration 
The findings reveal both technological and cultural challenges encountered in de-
veloping participatory heritage platforms for the Global South within the frame -
work of North-South collaborations. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges in the Development of Participatory Heritage Platforms 133 
The technological challenges: The limitation of one-size-fits-all approach 
for all contexts 
The core argument behind the limitations I propose concerning the development of 
P@ 2.0 is that relying on a one-size-fits-all approach, mainly suitable for the Global 
North context – such as the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman – is inadequate for 
designing participatory platforms in the context of the Global South – such as the 
historical urban landscape of Tunis. Drawing on insights on decolonial computing, 
I contend that participatory platforms should be conceived and developed based on 
two essential factors: the nature of the platform and its specific goals, which will 
determine the experimentation field. By analysing these factors, I aim to demon-
strate why the continuity of P@ 1.0, while beneficial as an existing framework, 
negatively affected the development of P@ 2.0. 
The nature of the heritage platform as a rationale against one-size-fits-all 
platforms 
The P@ 1.0 mobile application was crafted as a consultative platform, extracting 
data from diverse sources on heritage objects and transforming it into digital in -
formation to create engaging cultural content (Allani et al. 2020). P@ 1.0 was first 
developed and tested in the medina of Tunis (2016–2019) as a heritagised context. 
It was later adapted and implemented in the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman, 
Belgium (2020–2022), mainly because of the influence of the COVID-19 pan-
demic. Despite being geographically distant, both sites, the Open Air Museum of 
Sart-Tilman and the Medina of Tunis, share the commonality of being heritagised 
contexts. While the context of the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman was suitable 
to continue the development of a consultative platform, it was also utilised as a set-
ting to experiment with the P@ 2.0 designed to foster active user engagement and 
contribution, primarily focused on the colonial heritage of Tunis. Consequently, 
transitioning from a consultative to a participatory mobile platform is not always 
straightforward due to various factors. 
The shift from a consultative platform to a participatory one requires a funda -
mental change in approach. It involves fostering active user engagement and con -
tribution, which may necessitate a re-evaluation of the underlying architecture of 
the platform, software, functionality, and user interaction pathways. Mere additions 
or minimal adjustments to incorporate participatory features into an existing con -
sultative platform may not fully leverage the potential of a participatory platform 
or adequately cater to the diverse needs and preferences of users. Transforming a 
consultative platform into a participatory one may miss the opportunity to reimagine 
the user experience from the ground up. Designing a participatory platform from 
scratch allows researchers and designers to create features, functionalities, and a user 
interface that seamlessly integrates participatory elements from the outset. This ap-
proach facilitates a more cohesive and intuitive user experience that aligns closely 
with the application’s overarching objectives and the needs of its target audience. 
Moreover, designing a participatory platform from scratch offers the flexibility to 
experiment with innovative interaction paradigms, community-building strategies, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
134 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
and collaborative tools that may not be feasible within the constraints of an existing 
consultative mobile application. 
While it may be tempting to transform a consultative platform into a participa -
tory one, this approach is not always efficient or effective. Designing a partici -
patory mobile application from scratch allows researchers and designers to fully 
leverage participatory design principles and create a more cohesive, intuitive, and 
engaging user experience that aligns closely with the objectives and needs of its us-
ers. Beyond the nature of the platform – whether consultative or participatory – the 
specific objective of the heritage platform, which directly depends on the context, 
justifies rejecting a one-size-fits-all approach in platform design. 
The objective of the heritage platform as a rationale against one-size-fits-all 
platforms 
This argument posits that the design of a heritage platform necessitates a per -
sonalised approach based on the intended goal of the platform. In heritagised 
contexts – where certain cultural or historical elements are designated as heritage 
(Stiti, Jeunejean, and Ben Rajeb 2022 ) – or museum contexts, such as the Open 
Air Museum of Sart-Tilman, there is a heightened emphasis on preserving and 
promoting artefacts. Sart-Tilman, with its focus on art and leisure, presents a 
relatively neutral space for heritage, allowing for a more straightforward preser -
vation approach. In contrast, non-heritagised contexts, such as the colonial herit -
age of Tunis, raise more complex issues ( Stiti 2023). Colonial heritage can be 
potentially troubling or even traumatic to local communities, especially when the 
focus is on preserving European/French heritage. This dynamic forces a deeper 
reflection on how to decolonise such an initiative. Here, the focus might shift 
from merely raising awareness to critically engaging with the historical and so -
cial implications of these sites, exploring ways they could be redefined or rein -
terpreted to address their complex legacies. 
During its development phase, based on the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman as 
an experimental field, the P@ 1.0 platform was designed to meet the expectations of 
users in a museum context that had undergone a complete – at least from the institu-
tional side – heritagisation process. In this regard, the platform contributes to enhanc-
ing the final step of the heritagisation of the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman, which 
is the promotion of the site. Conversely, the P@ 2.0 mobile application was designed 
with the hypothesis that participatory platforms can play a vital role in raising aware-
ness about non-heritagised contexts. Therefore, the platform aims to contribute to the 
initial step of the heritagisation process for the colonial heritage of Tunis. 
The influence of the experimental field as a rationale against one-size-fits-all 
platforms 
The influence of the experimental field in the design process underscores the ra-
tionale against one-size-fits-all platforms, demonstrating the necessity for solu-
tions that address the unique characteristics of each context. In the case of the 

 
 
 
Challenges in the Development of Participatory Heritage Platforms 135 
development of P@ 2.0, the experimental field of the Open Air Museum of Sart-
Tilman impacted how the platform was developed. For example, while P@ 1.0 
effectively utilised materials as motivations for visits at a site such as Sart-Tilman, 
this approach faltered when applied to non-heritagised contexts, where buildings 
lacked recognition and historical significance. This discrepancy highlighted the 
need for a more tailored approach that considers the unique characteristics and 
challenges of each heritage context. Furthermore, Fallot, Bénel, and Di Loreto’s 
(2023) argument regarding the influence of location on platform design resonates 
with the development journey of P@ 2.0. They assert that the functionalities of 
a platform are not only shaped by conceptual approaches but are also contingent 
upon the specific constraints and dynamics of the location. 
Hence, recognising the three elements – the nature of the platform, its objec-
tive, and the chosen terrain for the development process – enables a more nuanced 
understanding of the needs of both the sites and the users within that context. In 
the P@ 2.0 project, the primary focus was on designing the participatory platform, 
which was based on the Sart-Tilman museum context. The nature of the platform, 
its objectives, and the development terrain were not fully considered at the out-
set due to the invisible assumption that what was designed for Sart-Tilman would 
automatically work for the colonial heritage of Tunis. The project ended without 
this approach being addressed, highlighting the importance of reflecting on these 
elements before designing a technological tool like P@ 2.0. Such reflection is es-
sential to ensure a more nuanced and context-specific development process. 
At this stage of the research, I found the explanation of technological limita -
tions alone to be insufficient. While acknowledging these limitations is part of the 
research, it is crucial to understand why they exist in the first place. The limitations 
arose because the development was initially tailored to fit a Global North context 
and then was expected to be adapted to other contexts. To push the analysis fur -
ther, the technological challenges identified in the development of the participatory 
heritage platform can be traced back to underlying cultural issues. These cultural 
issues can significantly impact how technology is implemented and utilised. In the 
following section, I will detail examples of the cultural challenges present in this 
research collaboration. 
The cultural challenges: The Western gaze in technology development 
Western research methodologies bear the enduring legacies of colonialism, shaping 
the definition of legitimate knowledge (Harding 1998). These methodologies per -
ceive knowledge as an objective truth, creating an impersonal and imperial voice 
that discounts the experiences of those under examination. Rooted in positivist 
frameworks, they privilege authoritative representations of the “other” (Swadener 
and Mutua 2008), reflecting the historical underpinnings of colonial thinking as 
a-spatial, a-temporal, and a-relational (Sweet, Sanders, and Peters 2019). Under -
standing the origin and ongoing influence of coloniality in academia necessitates 
recognising the dominance of Eurocentric philosophy – specifically, universalism, 
modernity, and truth (Sweet, Sanders, and Peters 2019). During the collaborative 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
136 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
project of developing the P@ 2.0 platform, there was a possibility that the Western 
gaze could manifest in certain aspects of the research, such as the cultural bias, the 
power imbalance between the different partners, and the technological solutionism. 
The cultural bias 
In the context of developing P@ 2.0, the initial extended parallel efforts between 
Campus Sart-Tilman and the colonial heritage of Tunis have proven to be inefficient, 
primarily due to the potential influence of cultural biases. The framing of the work-
shops by the Belgian partner institutions of the P@ 2.0 research project may have 
unintentionally aligned more closely with the cultural norms of the Global North – 
specifically those of the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman – while overlooking the 
rich cultural context and nuances of the colonial heritage of Tunis. This is illustrated 
by the fact that, while the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman had well-defined in -
stitution and key members, such as the curator of the museum, who attended some 
of the P@ 2.0 meetings, the colonial heritage of Tunis lacked both formal and in -
formal representation in the process. Additionally, the Tunisian partners, such as 
the National School of Architecture members, were implicitly required to present 
themselves both scientifically and within the context of their heritage, which creates 
an imbalance rooted in cultural bias. 
This cultural misalignment is evident in the limited local input during the pro -
longed parallel development. The insufficient engagement with stakeholders in 
Tunis may have led to a project that fails to fully understand the unique cultural 
intricacies and heritage concerns of the area. An overemphasis on Western perspec-
tives in the design and interpretation of P@ 2.0 risks undermining its authenticity in 
reflecting and respecting the Tunisian heritage aspirations. Examples of this over -
emphasis include the prioritisation of material aspects more familiar to the open-air 
museum of Sart-Tilman, such as colours or materials, which may not necessarily be 
useful in the context of the colonial heritage of Tunis, better known for its intangible 
aspects of community memory. Furthermore, the exclusion of residents from the co-
lonial heritage areas of Tunis in the design process of the P@ 2.0 mobile application 
results in a disconnect from the lived experiences and heritage aspirations of those 
directly connected to the area, reinforcing a top-down, Western-centric approach. 
Finally, the app’s design in French, rather than Tunisian or Arabic, marginalises lo-
cal communities by excluding those who may not be familiar with French, limiting 
their ability to engage with the project and reducing its cultural relevance. 
Additionally, the lack of continuous reflexivity and feedback loops between the 
Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman and Tunis during the development of P@ 2.0 
resulted in a static project that failed to address the cultural considerations of the 
colonial heritage of Tunis. This lack was linked to prioritising the Global North 
site, the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman, assuming that what worked there would 
work in the Global South – the colonial heritage of Tunis. In this regard, the ques-
tion of the Western gaze is central because the research project P@ 2.0 partners 
were aware that the Tunisian context was not heritagised. In this case, the emphasis 
on Western perspectives likely shaped the assumptions and approach of the project, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges in the Development of Participatory Heritage Platforms 137 
focusing on frameworks that work within a Global North context without fully 
considering the local dynamics in Tunisia. 
Power imbalances between the Global South and the Global North in research 
The power imbalance between the Global North and the Global South in research 
is a complex and multifaceted issue. Although the coproduction of knowledge is 
encouraged, it can be hindered by power dynamics (Vincent et al. 2020 ). In the 
context of P@ 2.0, the coproduction was a key aim of the project as the goal was to 
create a participatory platform that engaged local communities in the co-creation of 
heritage narratives, especially in the context of colonial heritage. Despite the emer-
gence of new research domains, the North’s predominance in knowledge produc-
tion persists (Connell 2018). This imbalance is perpetuated by a range of factors, 
including hidden power dynamics, the Northern research agenda, and inequalities 
in research funding and infrastructure ( Musamba and V ogel 2020; Okune et al. 
2018). The unequal distribution of resources and the dominance of the North in 
global research networks exacerbate the inequity of Global North-Global South 
research partnerships (Rakotonarivo and Andriamihaja 2023). 
Within the context of the P@ 2.0 research project, a notable power imbalance 
became evident, particularly in the unequal allocation of research funding and dis-
parities in infrastructure. Regarding research funding, despite the collaborative 
ethos, I consider that not all project partners were viewed equally by the funding 
entity. The management of funding rested solely with one Belgian partner, LUCID, 
rather than being distributed among the various partners. This centralised control 
limited flexibility in the planning and execution of research activities and uninten-
tionally created a power imbalance within the project, as some partners had less 
direct influence over resource allocation. Although the funding institution demon-
strated flexibility, particularly concerning COVID-related expense postponement 
and providing additional funding for the technological development of the mobile 
application, it only responded to certain requests if they were introduced by the 
budget-managing partner, LUCID. This introduced unnecessary administrative de-
pendence and led to delays, hindering the efficiency of the project. 
In terms of infrastructure, significant disparities existed among the laboratories of 
the project partners. LUCID and AIA, being more affluent institutions, possessed ad-
vanced and well-equipped labs compared to PAE3C, the Tunisian research unit. This 
disparity influenced the organisation of the mobile application development activities, 
which were primarily conducted in Belgium. Consequently, this created a physical 
distance from the Tunisian context of study, and as a result, it was not integrated into 
the early stages of the development process. Okune et al. (2018) underscore the need 
to address inequalities in research funding, infrastructure, and evaluation frameworks. 
Technological solutionism 
The concept of solutionism, particularly in the context of technology, has been 
widely explored in academic literature. Woodruff (2020) and Nachtwey and 

 
  
 
 
 
  
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
  
138 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Seidl (2020) both highlight the reflexive and often technological response to 
challenges, with the latter focusing on the role of tech companies in promoting 
solutionist ideas. Onyije and Francis (2012)  present alternative approaches to 
solutionism, proposing a direct relation between technology and social needs. 
Facchetti (2021) further critiques solutionist design in social design practices, 
advocating for the articulation of conflicts instead. Blythe et al. (2015) use design 
fiction and technological parables, respectively, to challenge solutionism. 
In the heritage context, a range of studies have explored the role of technology 
in heritage preservation, with a focus on both the potential and the challenges. 
Kumar et al. (2019) highlight the potential of additive manufacturing and digitali-
sation in repairing and preserving heritage sites and technical heritage. However, 
Germanà (2019) cautions against the overreliance on technology, emphasising the 
need to consider the nature, needs, and limitations of its use, as well as the impor -
tance of a process-oriented approach. Lupo et al. (2023) further stress the need for 
creativity and user engagement in virtual heritage projects. 
Within the theoretical framework of solutionism, I align with researchers in as-
serting that technological solutionism often assumes universal solutions, primarily 
grounded in Western technological frameworks, as a remedy for complex issues 
(Milan 2020; Milan and Treré 2019; Ndasauka 2024). This tendency, as exempli-
fied in the exercise of co-developing a mobile application for the Sart-Tilman and 
the colonial heritage exercise, may result in overlooking the specific historical, 
cultural, and social nuances of the colonial heritage of Tunis. Such an approach 
risks flattening the complexities of colonial heritage, reducing them to simplis-
tic narratives that fail to acknowledge the diverse yet local Tunisian experiences 
and perspectives. Moreover, it may perpetuate a one-size-fits-all model of heritage 
representation, which could undermine the authenticity and depth of the histori-
cal context, ultimately diminishing the capacity for meaningful dialogue around 
decolonisation and postcolonial identity in Tunisia. 
Furthermore, the inclination towards Western-centric digital preservation methods 
may lead to the prioritisation of certain approaches, potentially neglecting alternative 
means of preserving and documenting colonial history that hold greater cultural rel-
evance for Tunis, such as emphasising the importance of oral sources and memories. 
Consequently, the reliance on digital solutions may unintentionally contribute to a 
digital divide in the preservation of the colonial heritage of Tunis. A comparative lens, 
such as the well-preserved context of the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman, accentu-
ates the uneven access and participation in Tunis, favouring those with superior tech-
nological resources and infrastructure and thereby exacerbating existing disparities. 
Aligned with Western theories, the influence of technological solutionism, 
evoked earlier, in shaping research questions may underscore the transformative 
power of technology in understanding and interpreting colonial heritage. This de -
terministic view risks overshadowing the agency of individuals and communities 
in shaping their own narratives. Additionally, the field of technology studies, influ-
enced by Eurocentric perspectives, may frame research within Western contexts, 
potentially overlooking or underrepresenting non-Western experiences and contri-
butions to technological advancements. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges in the Development of Participatory Heritage Platforms 139 
Digital representations of Global South heritage and colonial heritage may in -
advertently perpetuate Western-centric narratives, biases, and perspectives, thereby 
impacting the authenticity and inclusivity of the historical record. These challenges 
underscore the imperative for a more nuanced and culturally sensitive approach to 
addressing the complexities of the colonial heritage of Tunis. 
Addressing the Western gaze in research on the Global South necessitates a 
commitment to reflexivity, critical awareness, and contextual sensitivity. How -
ever, in the case of the P@ 2.0 project, these principles were not considered. 
The project overlooked opportunities to emphasise the postcolonial context of the 
Tunisian heritage and its specificities. This absence highlights a significant gap in 
the approach, as decolonised methodologies and challenges to traditional power 
structures were not part of the framework of the project. Fostering partnerships 
that empower local communities is a crucial step towards a more equitable and 
inclusive approach to research in the Global South in general and Tunis in par -
ticular. Embracing decolonised methodologies and critically examining the tra -
ditional power structures inherent in research practices can foster more equitable 
and context-sensitive outcomes. 
Discussion: Decolonial computing as a path to decolonise technology 
to participate in heritage 
The development of the P@ 2.0 participatory platform faced significant obstacles 
due to both technological and cultural challenges. On the technological front, the 
most notable issue was the limitation of a one-size-fits-all approach. The design 
of the platform, based on the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman in Belgium, was 
primarily tailored for a Global North context. However, this approach proved inad-
equate when adapting it to the colonial heritage of Tunis, a Global South context. 
The nature of the heritage platform was not suited for diverse contexts, as the needs 
of the Tunisian heritage landscape required a more customised, participatory design 
rather than a generic framework. Furthermore, the objective of the heritage plat -
form shifted from promoting a heritagised site like Sart-Tilman to addressing the 
complex legacies of colonial heritage in Tunis, calling for a more nuanced platform 
that could engage with historical and social implications rather than just preserving 
artefacts. The experimental field – initially based in Sart-Tilman – highlighted the 
challenges of transferring this framework, built on the material aspect of heritage, 
to a different cultural and historical context, demonstrating the limitations of using 
a standardised materialistic approach across all settings. 
Simultaneously, cultural challenges arose, particularly the Western gaze embed-
ded in the development of the technology. The initial design of the platform reflected 
a cultural bias, influenced by Eurocentric perspectives, which overlooked the local 
context of Tunis. This misalignment created a power imbalance between the part-
ners from the Global North and the Global South, with the former maintaining more 
control over resources and decisions. The project’s reliance on technological so-
lutionism – believing that digital tools could offer a universal solution to heritage 
preservation – also overlooked local cultural practices and the specific needs of 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
140 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
the Tunisian community, potentially diminishing the authenticity of the heritage 
preservation efforts. Acknowledging these limitations and their tangible impact on 
heritage-related data management is crucial in the quest to design solutions that 
are not only more effective but also inherently attuned to the nuances of specific 
contexts. Drawing inspiration from the principles of decolonial computing (Irani 
et al. 2010; Philip, Irani, and Dourish 2012), I call on researchers to consider adopt-
ing frameworks that reorient participatory heritage platforms towards inclusivity, 
reflexivity, and cultural sensitivity. 
Before examining decolonial computing, it is important to draw the distinction 
between decolonial and postcolonial computing. The work of Ali (2016) makes this 
distinction particularly useful, as it helps clarify the differences between the two 
approaches. Ali’s analysis highlights how postcolonial computing focuses on the 
persistence of colonial legacies in technology but often overlooks broader struc-
tural power dynamics. In contrast, decolonial computing goes further by actively 
challenging these colonial structures, advocating for a more transformative and in-
clusive approach that prioritises marginalised communities and critically engages 
with the geopolitical and body-political positions of those involved. 
Decolonial computing is not a new domain or design space but an alternative 
sensibility in the design and analysis process. It raises a series of questions and 
concerns inspired by the conditions of postcoloniality, which are relevant to any 
design project (Irani et al. 2010). This approach advocates for a focus not merely 
on critiquing constructions of cultural difference but on exploring the productive 
possibilities of “difference” itself. The seams among differences are not merely 
sources of undesirable unevenness and aberration; they are also sites of creativity 
and possibility (Philip, Irani, and Dourish 2012). 
Decolonial computing prompts us to acknowledge and deconstruct the colonial 
biases and power dynamics deeply embedded within technology development. 
By embracing a decolonial approach, platforms can more effectively facilitate 
co-design, knowledge exchange, and community engagement. This results in a 
platform that is not only more culturally sensitive but also exceptionally attuned 
to the specific context, making it more inclusive and ultimately more effective. 
Additionally, I outline the benefits that a decolonial computing framework 
brings to the field of heritage across diverse settings. I emphasise the importance of 
bridging the gap between top-down, expert-driven approaches and grassroots com-
munity engagement. I align with Adamu (2023) in stressing the critical importance 
of addressing concerns related to questionable tactics in postcolonial discourse. 
Neglecting established dialogues within postcolonial studies, as demonstrated by 
the lack of commitment of P@ 2.0 to addressing power imbalances end coloniality 
in research, can create doubts regarding the commitment to challenging colonial -
ity. By focusing solely on designing a mobile application without integrating such 
critical commitments, the P@ 2.0 project risked appearing disconnected from the 
lived realities and postcolonial experiences, whether in the realm of technology 
or heritage. This disconnect undermines its credibility and underscores the neces -
sity of grounding technological interventions in robust postcolonial frameworks to 
ensure they meaningfully resonate with the affected communities. Moreover, the 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges in the Development of Participatory Heritage Platforms 141 
ongoing critique of solutionism and saviourism within the realm of HCI in Africa 
echoes the need for continuous self-reflection within the global HCI community. I 
join Adamu (2023) in calling for introspection, which is essential for promoting a 
more responsible and equitable approach to technology development in Africa and 
beyond. 
In the project P@ 2.0, applying decolonial computing principles would have 
fostered a more inclusive and context-sensitive approach to its development. By 
challenging Western-centric paradigms and emphasising the integration of local 
knowledge and perspectives, these principles could have ensured that the plat -
form authentically reflected Tunisian cultural and historical specificities. This 
would have involved engaging stakeholders as co-creators, embedding reflexiv -
ity and feedback loops throughout the process, and prioritising the representation 
of diverse narratives. Such an approach not only addresses power imbalances in 
heritage-related projects but also enhances the platform’s capacity to adapt to the 
evolving needs and aspirations of the local community. However, decolonial com-
puting does not come without risk. As Mbembe points out, when “post-” narratives 
prioritise ongoing emancipation, there is a risk of overlooking the power dynam-
ics within the postcolony, which involves the complex interactions of people and 
things shaping our perception of the world ( Adamu 2023). Accusations of using 
tricky tactics in postcolonial arguments are not without reason, as failing to engage 
with existing conversations in postcolonial studies can suggest a lack of honesty in 
efforts to oppose colonialism. If these concerns are not addressed, the link between 
postcolonial computing and the actual experiences of colonisation might seem dis-
tant and unfamiliar. 
Conclusion 
The development of P@ 2.0 provided a unique opportunity to explore participa -
tory platforms within diverse contexts, including non-heritagised historical urban 
landscapes and museums. This exploration revealed significant contrasts between 
the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman in Liège, Belgium, and the colonial heritage 
in Tunis, Tunisia. 
The contrasts between these two sites are evident in their use in design, objec -
tives, and contextual applications of heritage platforms. Sart-Tilman, as an Open Air 
Museum, provided a relatively neutral setting for P@ 1.0, where the platform’s top-
down approach was effective in gathering data to create digital content about exist-
ing heritage objects. The focus here was on the preservation of heritage, promoting 
art and leisure as heritage values. In this context, the platform functioned as a con-
sultative tool, offering information without requiring significant user engagement. 
However, the transition to the colonial heritage of Tunis for P@ 2.0 introduced 
more complex challenges. Unlike Sart-Tilman, Tunis’s colonial heritage is not yet 
recognised as heritage, which complicates its treatment as a neutral or stable cultural 
asset. P@ 2.0, designed as a participatory platform, aims to engage users in content 
creation and reinterpretation of this contested history, encouraging active participa-
tion to reflect on the legacies of colonialism critically. This approach proved less 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
142 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
effective in Sart-Tilman, where user contributions were not as necessary or feasible 
due to the more established heritage narrative. The shift to a participatory platform 
in Tunis, however, highlighted the need to engage users in redefining and reclaiming 
their heritage, addressing both the historical and emotional complexities involved. 
Furthermore, the technological limitations faced by P@ 1.0, initially designed for a 
Global North context, were more pronounced in Tunis, where cultural and histori-
cal differences impacted its relevance and user engagement. The contrast between 
these two sites emphasises the need for platforms that are adaptable to the specific 
cultural and historical dynamics of each heritage context rather than relying on a 
one-size-fits-all approach. 
In this regard, the notion of one-size-fits-all platforms can also be challenged 
by the recognition of significant contextual divides across different experimen -
tation fields. These divides, stemming from the diverse and complex nature of 
heritage contexts, serve as a compelling rationale against the adoption of generic, 
uniform platforms. Instead, they underscore the need for custom-made solutions 
that are sensitive to the unique characteristics, challenges, and opportunities pre -
sent within each specific context. One critical aspect of these contextual divides 
lies in the distinctiveness of experimentation fields, which encompass a broad 
spectrum of environments ranging from historical urban landscapes to museum 
settings. Each of these fields presents its own set of dynamics, intricacies, and pri-
orities, shaping the way in which participatory platforms are conceived, designed, 
and implemented. 
For example, the Open Air Museum of Sart-Tilman in Liège, Belgium repre-
sents a well-established institution with a heritage of modern open-air sculptures. 
Its structured organisational framework, comprehensive conservation policies, and 
dedicated team of professionals contribute to a highly curated and documented en-
vironment. In contrast, the historical urban landscape of Tunis embodies a complex 
tapestry of colonial legacies, postcolonial challenges, and socio-cultural dynamics. 
Here, the absence of dedicated institutions and the prevalence of urban transforma-
tions pose unique obstacles to heritage preservation and advocacy. 
The disparities between these two experimentation fields highlight the inad-
equacy of generic, one-size-fits-all platforms in effectively addressing the diverse 
needs and contexts of heritage preservation. Challenges such as cultural insensitiv-
ity, which often leads to a lack of appreciation for intangible aspects of heritage 
celebrated in museums, language barriers, and the disconnect between the devel-
opment language and the native languages spoken in the colonial heritage of Tu-
nis, become particularly pronounced when attempting to apply uniform solutions 
across disparate environments. 
By acknowledging the contextual divides inherent in experimentation fields, 
future research and design should advocate for a more nuanced and contextually 
sensitive approach to participatory platform development. This approach empha -
sises the importance of tailoring platform functionalities, engagement strategies, 
and community involvement initiatives to align with the specific requirements of 
each context. 

 
    
  
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges in the Development of Participatory Heritage Platforms 143 
Moreover, the recognition of the influence of the Global North over the Global 
South needs ongoing dialogue, collaboration, and knowledge exchange between 
researchers, practitioners, and local communities. By fostering a deeper under -
standing of the intricacies of each experimentation field, stakeholders can work 
towards co-creating solutions that resonate with the diverse cultural, historical, and 
social dimensions of heritage preservation.

9 Playing with history 
Engagement and mediations through 
Assassin’s Creed – an ethnographic 
perspective 
Kenza Gana and Renaud Garcia-Bardidia 
The nature and representation we have of video games have changed a lot recently. 
Initially conceptualised and viewed as children’s entertainment, they are now con-
sidered works of art, research objects, educational media, sports activities, or even 
full-fledged spectacles. Through their omnipresence in many people’s daily lives,  
video games have become more legitimate. They even feature in museum exhibits, 
both as a new form of heritage and as a medium to transmit knowledge and incite 
reflection on numerous aspects of history and heritage (Mochocki 2021). 
Many fields have explored the possibility of mediation through video games.  
They offer many opportunities as learning devices, most of the time taking the 
form of serious games. They can, however, participate in more recreational or 
indirect mediation by cultural institutions ( Vandromme 2021), most often to the 
benefit of a younger audience considered more responsive to these formats ( Meu-
nier, Bélanger, and Turcotte 2022). Similarly, video games interest researchers in 
marketing. These works particularly underline how they can generate consumer 
excitement, enjoyment, and engagement, therefore being an interesting way to 
playfully reach younger audiences ( Renault 2024). 
While most of these works of research revolve around mediation devices that 
were conceptualised as such, a few take an interest in how consumers can “poach” 
knowledge. These approaches stem from the work of Michel de Certeau (1994) , 
considering cultural consumption as a reworking of the cultural object (i.e., the  
text of a novel or in this case the script of a video game) around one’s own cultural 
references. This appropriation allows for a redefinition of the subject’s relation  
to the object, their desires, their imagination, and their cultural references within 
their usage of said object. Be it in education sciences ( Vari 2018), in sociology 
(Berry et al. 2022 ), or in history, these works explore profane learning in video 
games. 
From this perspective, historical video games represent a unique genre of the 
medium. The universes that they simulate plunge the players into meticulous recon-
structions of historical eras, offering them an immersive journey in the past, further 
asserting their importance when it comes to heritage. While the work relating to this 
remains nascent, researchers in tourism and heritage have already identified how  
important these video games are in acquiring a taste for history and the development 
of profane knowledge (Chapman 2019). They could, therefore, offer opportunities 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Playing with History 147 
for innovative cultural mediation (Zarandona et al. 2018) or even create video game-
induced tourism practices (Dubois and Gibbs 2018). 
This chapter proposes a close-up examination of the stakes of historical video 
games for heritage mediation, focusing on the case of the Assassin’s Creed fran -
chise. The latter is particular in its capacity for replicating different historical pe-
riods in an immersive manner. That exposition to a variety of historical events 
and figures makes players’ experience as educational as it is entertaining. One of 
the main selling points put forward by Ubisoft for these games is the meticulous 
work they put into historical reconstruction, a quality they further assert through its 
use in a museum context (Lavenir 2024). More precisely, we explore the various 
“poaching” uses of these historical video games in a professional context, most no-
tably by teachers, cultural mediators, and video game professionals. These experts 
make use of it as an educational tool, a means of popularisation, or an entertain -
ment medium. This demonstrates the versatility and richness of historical video 
games. By examining these practices, we show how video games can contribute to 
heritage consumption while mustering both individual and collective engagement 
in players. 
Foreseeable mediation through historical video games? 
The use of digital means in the heritage sector is already well established. Beyond 
the new possibilities for conservation, digitisation has increased informal learning, 
interpretation, and engagement while changing the dynamic between the different 
stakeholders. In that context, the actors of world heritage have taken an interest – for 
the last few decades – in digital media and their impact on accessibility and inclu-
sion (King, Stark, and Cooke 2016). The significant use of digitisation is a particu-
larly useful argument when trying to appeal to young audiences; less interested in 
traditional means of mediation. While the hypothesis that technologically socialised 
digital natives ( Prensky 2001) would require different – more playful – learning 
mediums is arguable, it remains no less true that this hypothesis is at the basis of 
many heritage practices. Therefore, a strong rise in digitisation of heritage sites can 
be observed since the 2010s. 
At first, it was serious games – video games dedicated to learning rather than 
to play – that garnered a lot of attention in the museum context (Paliokas and Sy-
laiou 2016) while yielding questionable results. Their implementation is like that 
of games in a teaching environment (Vari 2018). They potentially provide better 
affordance and could reinforce engagement even transforming skills or represen-
tations. Moreover, the use of digital devices could appeal to younger audiences. 
They seem – however – to face the same difficulty as in an academic context. Their 
reception hinged on a paradox: for players, these games tend to be less interesting 
than “real” video games; for non-players, they are a difficult medium to pick up 
which hinders their efficacy for learning. 
Conversely, video games without teaching goals that include a historical com -
ponent only raised the interest of heritage professionals and researchers alike quite 
recently. Historical video games now surpass novels, films, or even comic books 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
148 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
in terms of historical retelling, as is reminded by Éthier and Lefrançois (2021). In 
fact, historical video games are various: HistoriaGames lists around 2500 game 
titles.1 They take on many forms depending on the type of control given to play -
ers: role playing, strategy, adventure, historical simulators, educational games, 
etc. Most traditional video game genres are accounted for, only distinguishable 
by the historical context they simulate, in addition to video games that feature 
history for more than narrative or atmosphere elements. These games also dif -
fer as to whether they focus on a specific historical era, going from prehistory to 
contemporary times, or allow the player to navigate through the entire history of 
a civilisation, for example. 
In “Digital Games as History: How video games represent the past and offer 
access to history practice”, Chapman (2016) provides a more academic distinc -
tion between two major kinds of video game devices, classifying them based on 
whether the simulation is realistic or conceptual. The first type, named “realis -
tic simulation”, represents games where the creators try to reconstruct the past as 
faithfully and authentically as possible. The idea is to recreate historical environ-
ments through the scenery, the characters, their outfits, and the insertion of real-life 
historical events (Vincent 2021). Red Dead Redemption, Medal of Honor, and As-
sassin’s Creed belong in this category. On the other hand, “conceptual stimulation” 
historical video games do not seek a precise visual reconstruction of the past. While 
they do include historical events, their course can be altered by the player’s choices 
as is the case in Crusader Kings or the Civilization game franchise. Be that as it 
may, they still put players in situations where they will reflect on history based on 
the historical development models the game provides (Majewski 2021). 
Potential historical mediations through the first type of historical video games 
are easier to grasp. They would rest for the most part on their ability to replicate 
virtually recreational heritage and ancient locations. Research in education sci -
ences and history point out that games which – through virtual digital referential 
mimicry – give life to the past as history allow the player to experience and ex -
plore its sounds, images, and processes, and to make decisions on and in this past 
constructed as history (Chapman 2013). In this sense, the video game medium is a 
considerable means of immersion that captures the player in an entertaining virtual 
visit to historical sites. 
Individual engagement in historical re-enactment through the medium of his -
torical video games is therefore made possible by immersive mechanisms. This 
supposes the precise representation of the environment and time-accurate building 
to preserve architectural verisimilitude, but more broadly encompasses any aspect 
reflective of the period, from its morals and mentalities, to its everyday objects, etc. 
Video game immersion can make a player feel present inside the game and create 
an illusion of non-mediation between them and the game’s context. Faithful repre-
sentation of spaces and physical objects compared to reality is something players 
look for to make their experience more authentic and more captivating (Mochocki 
2021). Therefore, video games offer the opportunity to engage users in history or 
cultural heritage, despite the literature having seldom explored the role these cul -
tural experiences play compared to more traditional ones. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Playing with History 149 
Video games should, however, not be reduced solely to their in-game immer -
sive aspect. The medium does not escape from the convergence and transmedial -
ity described by Jenkins as early as 2006: it too is a decentralised construction of 
narrative worlds through multiple channels, generating a unified entertainment 
experience. Not only are its universes connected to numerous other fictional prac -
tices like cinema or literature, but they’re also strongly appropriated by their fan 
communities. Therefore, just as cultural heritage has become “polyvocal” and in -
dependent from experts (Agostino, Arnaboldi, and Lampis 2020; Witcomb 2007), 
developing this type of games as well as the discussions they generate online 
reinforce the opposition between academic and profane knowledge; between his -
tory and fiction. 
Video game engagement and learning 
Player engagement is at the centre of what is sought in using video games in a 
heritage or museum context, just as it is at the centre of economic models in the 
video game industry ( Kleer and Kunz 2021). Immersion – a psychological state 
of full integration in an interactive environment that provides stimuli and experi -
ences (Trépanier-Jobin and Couturier 2018) – is essential to understanding the gam-
ing experience. This immersion, oftentimes accompanied by a form of flow, stems 
from a profound engagement of the player, wherein they are fully absorbed by their 
activity – losing sense of corporeality and the passage of time ( Csikszentmihalyi 
1990). In certain games (particularly those with an open world), immersion is seen 
as a transposition of the player into another universe. Immersion and narrative trans-
position are often considered to play an essential role in transforming the player’s 
intentions towards the heritage locations and objects displayed through the gaming 
device (Bourgeon-Renault et al. 2016). 
However, observable learning in video games isn’t limited to their immersive 
narratives. Video games can generate other kinds of engagement, on an individual 
and collective level. Individually, playing requires the acquisition of skills useful 
to progressing in the game (such as in strategy and resource management games), 
that may be linked to the universe of the game – and therefore to knowledge outside 
mere gameplay. For example, performing on FIFA, a football simulator, implies 
handling a controller masterfully just as much as it does an understanding of the 
world of football to best compose teams, even developing financial savvy in man-
aging a team and its budget (Garcia-Bardidia, Trizzulla, and Maire 2023). These 
learnings allow and suppose information transfers between the realms of the game, 
sports, and academics. 
This variety in learning does not occur identically for every player. If we take 
the previous example, it will depend on the FIFA game mode the player chooses 
(solo mode or online competition, for example). It will also depend on the initial 
motivation the player had. As indicated more broadly by Pine and Gilmore (1998), 
a consumer experience, in this case that of gaming, can be attached to many reg -
isters. Education, escapism, aesthetics, or entertainment will manifest more sig-
nificantly depending on the player’s initial motivation. But these motivations can 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
150 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
change as the player appropriates the game: in this case, what was simple entertain-
ment can transform into a learning device. 
Gaming practices are strongly linked to the material and social contexts that feed 
into them. Considering that to “play” is not merely a matter of rules or device nor 
of distinction effects (Bourdieu 1979), he explores the emergence of stabilisation 
of different play styles: strolling, trolling, or inattentive play, for example. Each of 
these styles could give way to different appropriating of the historical dimension of 
a historical video game: strolling as a mode of exploration of the historical period 
or trolling as an attempt to generate controversy through historical deviations, for 
example. Moreover, if practices are influenced by the material and social contexts 
of the game, these practices will not only vary from person to person but also from 
group to group, depending on norms, culture, and social dynamics. Gaming experi-
ences can be affected by expectations of performance and success just as much as 
they can be by power and recognition mechanisms prevalent in communities. 
Beyond the individual learning observed in video games, we might therefore 
also find collective constructions of knowledge based on historical video games. 
Video games as objects bear a strong community component, which isn’t exclu -
sive to the gaming communities of Massively Multiplayer Online Role-Playing 
Games (MMORPGs). These communities are known for their significant infor -
mation production, using resources made available by various organisations ( Fine 
1989) – in the case of video games, mainly by the developer. Information, trick, 
and experience-sharing practices among players contribute doubtlessly to the con-
struction of knowledge about the historical period for each opus and more broadly 
of history in an ecosystem that encompasses Twitch (a streaming platform), Dis -
cord (a discussion server), YouTube, and other forums or blogs. 
What remains to be seen is the quality of the contributions of these games to 
fans from a historical point of view – and, therefore, the quality of the informa -
tion they in turn transmit to other players. Much like it is in other fields, this 
knowledge production puts into question both the discourse produced by history 
professionals (who may feel dispossessed) and that of internet knowledge creation 
defenders who oppose them (Éthier and Lefrançois 2021). In addition to that, first 
opposition comes that of more academically inclined individuals to an increasing 
control that cultural industries have on shaping the world. In the end, historical 
video games bring back – in a specific context and pertaining to history – the 
debate around the polyvocality of heritage ( Agostino et al. 2020; Witcomb 2007), 
around the consequences of the rise of amateur knowledge through the internet 
and – more broadly – around the tension between control and transparency in the 
media (Cardon 2019). 
Assassin’s Creed: A game suitable for historical learning? 
In this chapter, we focus on realistic simulation historical video games, choos -
ing the Assassin’s Creed franchise as our main example. This action-adventure 
game, developed by Ubisoft, invites players to delve into the memories of ances -
tors from different historical eras. The success of the franchise is demonstrated by 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Playing with History 151 
the total 200 million copies it sold. The latest opus, coming out March 2025, is set 
in 16th-century Japan at the end of the Sengoku period (also known as the War -
ring States period), a tumultuous era from a political, social, and military point of 
view. Throughout the last 16 years, the game has taken its protagonist, a barman 
descended from a long of assassins, to many countries, cultures, and periods, from 
the Third Crusade to the Renaissance, passing through the French Revolution, Im-
perial China, the Viking raids, Ancient Greece, and even Baghdad in the golden age 
of Islam in the 9th century. 
Players encounter a variety of historical moments and real figures (Gilbert 2016). 
Ubisoft gives great importance to the accuracy of the historical events depicted in 
the game, as well as the fine description of ways of life and civilisational aspects 
appearing in each opus. They collaborate with resident historians or academics – 
invited as history consultants on their teams, to enrich each game and improve its 
realism (Éthier 2018). Similarly, the permanent staff includes historians, whose 
academic background brings about a larger reflection of the field of history (Éthier 
and Lefrançois 2021). This effort by Ubisoft is even more significant as they have 
recently steered part of their activity towards partnerships with schools and mu -
seums. These projects come about as large-scale exhibits (such as the one in the 
Arab World Institute in Paris for the release of Assassin’s Creed Mirage – a virtual 
exploration of 9th century Baghdad and Madinat-al-Salam) as well as educational 
modules named Assassin’s Creed Discovery. 
Narrative historical video games retrace history through a combination of mis -
sions and cinematics. In this type of narration, the player takes control of a charac-
ter and makes decisions to progress through the game. As is the case for Assassin’s 
Creed, narrative historical video games can follow a linear or branching structure. 
A linear structure progresses without fail from a beginning to an end, the chain of 
events leading inevitably to the same conclusion. A branching structure allows the 
player to change the course of history, creating many possible paths and different 
game repercussions (Wolf 2001). Assassin’s Creed is particular in that it synchro-
nises the player’s actions to real historical events. The players cannot change the 
“course of history”, but – through their character – they can relive the past. 
While not all players purchase Assassin’s Creed games for their historical com-
ponent, choosing them solely on their distinct gameplay, they are all the same ex -
posed to representations of history and engage with them ( Chapman 2016). In the 
same way, this gameplay leads them to reflect on their role as agents of history. 
Research in education sciences has taken an interest in this video game as a learning 
device (Boutonnet 2018, 2022; Chapman 2016). Thanks to its powerful immersion 
and the authentic replication of the cities, buildings, and figures, these works of 
research find that players are placed in a historical context that is realistic and well 
documented (Boutonnet 2022). Historical video games like Assassin’s Creed are 
considered a device of profane historical learning on equal footing as any other 
fiction. 
Finally, the release of these games, as well as the manufacturing of history that 
the developer imbues it with, reflect the stake of historical video games as pos-
sible mediations as stated earlier in this chapter. First, the franchise intentionally 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
152 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
includes anachronisms, as the developers wished to maintain the recreational and 
entertaining aspect of the game (Éthier and Lefrançois 2021 ). Fact-checking is a 
widespread practice among players and can be observed on the game’s forums as 
well as numerous other online media like YouTube channels. Similarly, due to their 
non-conformity to academic norms – such as narration to provide an experience 
of the past (Éthier 2018), this profane learning may require mediation or support, 
to avoid too strong deviation from academic learning. Finally, Ubisoft’s editorial 
choices create controversy around the representations that some of the different 
games can vehiculate, the latest one pertaining to the choice of two main characters 
in Assassin’s Creed Shadows in feudal Japan – particularly that of Yasuke, a samu-
rai of African origin whose historical existence remains debatable. 
Considering all the above, we attempt through this chapter to understand 
how playing Assassin’s Creed can be mobilised to engage an audience in history 
through and beyond mere entertainment. More precisely, we will describe the 
practices of players who have used it professionally as a means of mediation dur -
ing classes or in other transmission contexts. We will compare these uses of this 
game to the practices and representations outside professional mediation as they 
appear in online discussions of Assassin’s Creed. We will make use of two stud -
ies with the larger scope of a PhD thesis. The first is a series of 11 semi-structured 
interviews with respondents with various sociodemographic profiles and usage of 
the game, whose particularity is a mix between their gaming practices and their 
professional context (see Table 9.1). The second is an ongoing ethnography that 
builds on participant observation of discussions between players on a Discord 
server around issues that combine gaming, history, and heritage. Based on these 
two data sets, we present emergent results on the different manners in which 
players engage in historical learning through Assassin’s Creed, be it individually 
or collectively.

creator/gamer
