development research engineer/gamer 

 
 
 
 
Playing with History 153 
The tension of “savage” mediations 
In order to explore and analyse player engagement, we have conducted a virtual 
ethnography (Hine 2020) on different Discord servers (seven channels named As-
sassin’s Creed Discussion wiki; Assassin’s Creed – Film and Series; video games, 
history; Assassin’s Creed – Books; Historical video game cinema – films – history; 
Assassin’s Creed Off-topic, The Real History; and video games, history, educa -
tion) either relating to different historical video games where contributors happen 
to discuss Assassin’s Creed or those dedicated specifically to the franchise, as well 
as the video game thread on a general forum. We also interviewed players who 
improvised mediations via Assassin’s Creed in their professional context based on 
their player experience. These two studies confirmed the benefit of this game as a 
historical learning resource. They complement the research on historical and herit-
age learning contexts through historical video games, beyond resources provided 
by developers – dedicated modules, use of immersive scenography in exhibits, and 
popularisation of historical research via the game. 
From immersion as engagement to community-wide historical mediation 
These two types of mediation – namely critique on digital discussion spaces and 
“savage” gamification in classes – rest upon common principles in terms of gam-
ing experience and engagement in the game. Therefore, the strolling – quest-less 
exploration in the game – tends to be a practice adopted by more experienced 
players. Contributors to the Discord servers we studied, as well as the teachers 
and mediators we interviewed, have reached levels of immersion and engagement 
that sensitise them to historical details in the game. They will often only retain the 
exploration component of their gaming experience. Strolling in the game is an op-
portunity to immerse in that specific historical period, to visit buildings; in sum to 
practice a form of heritage tourism that can overlap with other offline practices, as 
indicated by the following passage from a forum: 
[Seriously] who plays AC for the plot? […] I mostly enjoy virtual historical 
tourism while remaining aware of its limitations (the scales are off, they’re 
approximating, etc.). Still! There are beautiful things in all these games: the 
light through the stained glass of the Sainte Chapelle in Unity, the Topkapi 
Palace, the Duomo, going on an Odyssey through the Mediterranean from 
isle to isle of the Aegean Sea, the dream levels reminiscent of Egyptian Gods. 
And sometimes, it joins real life like when I’m leafing through a magazine 
and am surprised by a familiar image: “I’ve already been there, but when? 
How?” It’s impossible to recall and then I read the caption “Market of Jerusa-
lem”, which I visited every single way in Assassin’s Creed I. On the contrary, 
I remember my game actions when visiting the Castel Sant’Angelo in Rome, 
it was arranged exactly as it was in the game. 
(Excerpt from the video game thread of a general forum) 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
  
 
 
 
 
154 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
As in other historical video games, this passion for history is an element that is 
very present in the online discussions revolving around Assassin’s Creed. It is no 
doubt a trait more commonly found in contributors to the digital spaces we have 
studied than it is in the broader player base of the franchise. It is no less essential 
in evaluating the game’s quality, in the structuration of the community, and in the 
relationship that players have with Ubisoft’s work. While a portion of the Discord 
servers is dedicated to regular exchanges about the game, sharing shortcuts among 
players to progress in-game, others are specifically dedicated to history. We can 
observe various forms of knowledge production within them. Contributors discuss 
how events, characters, and environments are represented, often comparing game 
elements to historical facts. Some players will do additional research to fact-check 
the accuracy of the details presented in the game, sharing the books, articles, docu-
mentaries, explanatory YouTube videos, and other educational resources they used 
to confirm or debunk the game’s representations. Sources are important in their 
argumentation, as they provide further learning material for other participants. 
Beyond the diffusion of knowledge imported from sources other than the game, the 
community sets off or reinforces a disposition for self-learning and collaborative 
learning. It allows contributors to share their passion and gain recognition of their 
knowledge from their fellow forum members, whereas its legitimacy would be 
questioned elsewhere. The following verbatim illustrated this based on a recent 
criticism – voiced by a French politician – on the depiction of the French Revolution 
in the game which created this tension within a contributor: 
“Ah, the famous political litany ‘S not ou’fault, it’s video games’”. He should 
rather have taken a deeper interest in the Assassin’s Creed franchise, he would 
have noticed that we can learn lots of fascinating things about History and 
in far more interesting way that in some History classes (and that’s saying 
something, as I had my most memorable professors in these, and the classes 
were entertaining). He needs to stop thinking that nobody has a sharper mind 
than his in finding nuance. Quite frankly, [name of politician ], if only you 
knew the amount of time that I’ve spent scouring the Internet to complete my 
discoveries on Assassin’s Creed, you’d see the game differently. 
(Excerpt from the video game thread of a general forum) 
One significant form of these exchanges is the discussion of historical accuracy 
of the game’s elements. A typical example of this would be the interest in the archi-
tectural details and outfits: contributors can debate the exactness of reconstructions 
of emblematic buildings from a particular period or the verisimilitude of outfits 
worn by characters in long threads, as illustrated by the case of this uniform: 
It’s Cossack in this case. The garment does not share anything with any Rus-
sian uniforms of the “main” army but further resembles that of the Cossack 
Danube army that was created in 1828 (this remains to be proved as the level 
of expertise in Slavic history of the two illustrators is unknown). 
(Excerpt from Discord, Off-topic, The Real History channel) 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Playing with History 155 
As is the case for the Majewski (2021) description of Civilization, contributor 
players take on the role of critics. This role demonstrates both an active reception 
of the game and a willingness to rectify the gaps left by the community’s game 
developer, driving them to collaboratively construct knowledge registers in the 
form of wikis – as indicated by the following comment: 
In Mirage, Rostam is briefly mentioned with the equipment we pick up, and 
it is indicated this character was immortalized in the Books of Kings, which 
were written in the…10th century…I can’t find any other information on the 
Internet indicating Rostam was created before, do I mention it’s an anachro-
nism on the wiki? 
(Excerpt from Discord, Assassin’s Creed – Books channel) 
A particular aspect of these online exchanges is the great number of links they 
contain to other works of popular culture, which are also discussed in terms of ac-
curacy and authenticity, as shown by this comment about gladii and Roman legion 
organisation modes: “In movies, we often see spathae, which were longer swords 
than gladii but rather similar in general form…In HBO’s Rome the scaling of gladii 
is good, as are combat tactics that consisted in troop shifts to save up soldiers” (Ex-
cerpt from Discord, Assassin’s Creed – Films and Series channel). We encounter 
here both a demonstration of one’s expertise to the community and one’s ability to 
gauge historical accuracy across different contexts, as well as a suggested exten -
sion into other acclaimed works and therefore a common sense of taste for the 
“authentic” in historical media. 
Transference into savage recreation, between continuity and discontinuance 
The common – and desirable – trait presented by the sample we mobilise here is 
that respondents are Assassin’s Creed players who used the game in a professional 
context (mostly as part of their teaching or during heritage mediation sessions). 
Their trajectory is a recurring part of the construction of a taste for history that they 
put into place both recreationally and at work. Another – more emergent – trait is the 
form their gaming practice as well as their attachment to the Assassin’s Creed fran-
chise will take. They strongly resemble the forum contributors we described earlier: 
the community and its channels (YouTube, forums, and Discord), as well as stroll-
ing are frequently expressed as essential, because as Maud reminds: “I had a Dis -
cord community with Assassin’s Creed players where we chatted. But video games, 
they’re community-based anyway, which is a good thing” (Maud, cultural media -
tor). They too, like Rémi, have been sensitised to historical heritage via the game 
elements: “When I visited Versailles with my family for the first time, to realise that 
I already knew the war room, that I’d already seen the paintings because I’d played” 
(Rémi, 34 years old, cultural mediator). They have been socialised via Assassin’s 
Creed, most likely in addition to other instances of socialisation, to a practice of his-
tory closely resembling the one described earlier. Searching for additional informa-
tion to share is equally important; Hugo indicates the benefit of this act of sharing 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
156 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
with functionalities like Photo Mode: “Ever since there’s been this Photo Mode, 
the one you can enable in Odyssey and Valhalla. It’s good, you can share things 
with the community, passages of time” (Hugo, History professor). Furthermore, we 
find in these interviews a similar disposition for self-learning, cultural legitimacy of 
entertainment, and immersion in the game as motivations for the Assassin’s Creed 
gaming practice. 
These practices will contribute to shaping their use of Assassin’s Creed in a pro-
fessional context. First, this passion for video games – and in most cases for other 
works of pop culture – participates in the legitimation of Assassin’s Creed as me -
diation material, and more broadly of video games for professional use. The use of 
video games, as well as gamification on a larger scale, is clearly part of our respond-
ents’ tools to make classes or visits more enticing. As mentioned by this respondent: 
I teach philosophy to 17, 20-year-old kids, evidently one of the challenges 
is to generate interest, retain the students’ attention for a class that can be 
complex for someone who’s never studied it. So, sometimes I try to initiate 
reflection from a video game. As you can imagine, it was met with cheers 
and pleasure. 
(Philippe, 32 years old, Philosophy teacher) 
Breaking the codes of academic transmission, adapting to a neophyte audience are 
therefore part of the interviewed teachers and mediators’ preoccupations. How -
ever, in both cases, it is a matter of maintaining the integrity of their professional 
activity: a class must remain a class and a visit must remain a visit. Therefrom, we 
observe hybridisations that create tensions with their player experience. 
The first of these tensions has to do with the game’s status, between the virtual 
and the real. Unsurprisingly, the players/teachers use historical video games as 
educational material to illustrate their courses. As indicated by William: “It’s use-
ful, it’s concrete, it gives them a faster sense of reality than the fabricated effect 
on simple printed A4 pages, y’know” (William, 37 years old, History professor). A 
virtual medium, paradoxically considered more concrete, Assassin’s Creed allows 
an extended display of history and heritage. The second tension manifests here: 
whereas players (and therefore a portion of our respondents) could take on the role 
of a critic as a starting point for their appropriation of historical knowledge via 
the game, they do not take the game’s anachronisms into account. Accuracy, an 
essential of the community, is less important than the game’s ability to illustrate 
lessons and stimulate the students’ imaginations. This is what Hugo affirms while 
speaking about the material he uses for his history classes: 
Well, even though there is a thorough research effort prior, I think that games 
can be historically good despite errors because it has to stay entertaining 
first and foremost, it’s how the business works, content creators and video 
games rather than something that prioritises entertainment over learning 
completely, y’know. 
(Hugo, 29 years old, History teacher) 

 
 
 
Playing with History 157 
Paradoxically, this suspension of disbelief is more prevalent for teachers than it is 
for players. 
Beyond the historical material provided by the game, teachers and mediators use 
strolling and exploration practices – in this case as a form of virtual visit. Again, 
practicality goes hand in hand with the medium’s qualities, as indicated by Hugo: 
“Sometimes, I can’t organise school trips for visits, so I use Assassin’s Creed Unity 
for example. I make use of the game and very realistic depiction of Notre-Dame 
to make a virtual visit of it” (Hugo, 29 years old, History teacher). This is – of 
course – truer for visits made impossible by physical or temporal distance: “The 
game allows visitors to visit historical locations that no longer exist. I wouldn’t do 
it for Versailles that hasn’t moved one bit, but I do it for the Notre-Dame cathedral” 
(Eric, 46 years old, archaeologist). In the case of lost historical sites, historical 
video games can be a means of reconstitution, helping in the preservation and dif-
fusion of cultural heritage. However, this use implies deploying a dedicated media-
tion or a separate space within the scenography as elaborated by Rémi: 
For example, in the museum I used to work in, we had an Antiquity exhibit. 
We’d developed games taking place in Antiquity and wanted to pay homage 
to today’s games. We partnered with Ubisoft, who gave us a demo of Assas-
sin’s Creed Origins for Egypt to show the universe before starting the visit. 
(Rémi, 34, cultural mediator) 
Therein lies one last tension between the respondents’ practices within the players’ 
community and within a professional context. Whereas they may focus on con -
tribution and participation in the community, they mostly adopt a more top-down 
approach when mobilising the game in a class or during a visit. While oral partici-
pation and Q&As are encouraged, the notion of a flipped classroom is nowhere to 
be found. 
Conclusion 
Historical video games, such as Assassin’s Creed – through meticulous and immer-
sive reconstructions of historical periods – open a gateway into the past. They allow 
players to engage actively with history and cultural heritage (Chapman 2016). The 
two studies we have mobilised in this chapter reaffirm the benefits of this game as a 
historical learning resource. They, therefore, complement the research on historical 
and heritage learning contexts in historical video games, going beyond resources 
provided by the game developer – dedicated modules, immersive scenography for 
exhibits, and popularisation of historical research through the game. 
In this sense, historical video games can muster a profound engagement in play-
ers, pushing them to delve deeper into historical knowledge and develop a passion 
for history and heritage. It is especially true when players engage in communities 
of practice that rely on the participative interpretation of video games. This kind 
of engagement is common within our sample due the need to learn tricks to finish 
the game but also because of the immersive nature of historical video games. In 

 
  
 
 
 
158 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
our study, discussions on platforms like Discord reveal a distinct interest in history, 
with frequent exchanges about the accuracy of historical depictions in the games 
and additional research to fact-check these details. Through immersion, the prac-
tice of strolling complements the learning process – particularly the architectural 
component – that players put into place. More broadly, this engagement translates 
into a taste for heritage and into developing learning practices (sourced research 
and contributions to wikis) that provide contributors with pride and recognition 
while still being stonewalled by stereotypes about gaming. 
The use of historical video games can also be extended beyond recreation, par-
ticularly in professional contexts like teaching or cultural mediation. Teachers in -
clude these games in their coursework to illustrate historical concepts and motivate 
students, while cultural mediators use them to popularise historical knowledge and 
make historical site visits more attractive. In both cases, professional practices are 
rooted in a socialisation to history that integrates video games and contributes to a 
self-taught learning process through popular culture. 
However, improvised gamifications by players in professional contexts show 
that – despite being complimentary – these two modes of transmission can still 
come into tension, vis-à vis the quality of historical video games, the role of en -
tertainment, or the teacher/mediator position. If historical video games, especially 
realistic simulations like Assassin’s Creed, represent a promising avenue for in-
dividual and collective engagement in heritage mediation, they offer many paths 
forward in terms of research when it comes to these tensions, to keep producing 
innovative historical heritage exploration and transmission practices.

10 Digital media and safeguarding 
intangible cultural heritage of 
minority groups in China 
A case study of animated short films 
Orochen Tale of Nisan Shaman and 
Orochen Creation Story 
Jiyun Zhang 
Efforts to redefine “development” during the United Nations’  First Decade of De-
velopment (1960–1970) exposed growing tensions between narrowly economy -
centred approaches and the needs of newly independent nations. Simultaneously, 
international movements, such as decolonisation and the abolition of racial segre -
gation in major countries, underscored the necessity of broadening the concepts 
of “culture” and “heritage.” These shifts demonstrated an increasing demand for 
more inclusive and culturally sensitive approaches to development ( Labadi 2022, 
28–30). 
In response, UNESCO convened the first World Conference on Cultural Poli -
cies (MONDIACULT) in Mexico City in 1982, which redefined “culture” as  
encompassing modes of life, the fundamental rights of human beings, value sys -
tems, traditions, and beliefs (UNESCO 1982 , CLT/MD/1, 38). This expanded  
understanding was institutionalised by the UNESCO 2003 Convention for the  
Safeguarding of Intangible Cultural Heritage (hereafter, the “2003 Convention”),  
which moved beyond the widely criticised “Western-centric” and “monumental”  
approaches in favour of recognising the living heritage, or intangible cultural  
heritage (hereafter, “ICH”). These include oral traditions, performing arts, social  
practices, rituals, festive events, knowledge and practices concerning nature and  
the universe, and traditional craftsmanship, among others ( UNESCO 2022, CLT-
2022/WS/3, 5–6). 
The Convention linked safeguarding ICH to sustainable development and  
called for “the widest possible participation of communities, groups and, where  
appropriate, individuals” (hereafter “CGIs,” Jacobs 2017 , 2020; Blake 2019 ). It 
also emphasises “respect for cultural diversity and human creativity” while posi -
tioning ICH as “a driver and guarantee of sustainable development.” This focus  
became particularly pronounced following the Convention’s reorientation towards 
sustainable development between 2010 and 2016 ( Smeets 2024; UNESCO 2022, 
CLT-2022/WS/3, 5, 10, 80). To reinforce these narratives and construct participa-
tory mechanisms, it is essential to reach out and engage with the public, particu -
larly non-expert audiences and the youth. This requires presenting information in  
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-13 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
162 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
accessible and engaging formats and exploring divergent avenues for communica-
tion (Sediakina-Rivière et al. 2021 , VII). 
While the participatory ethos and the link between culture and sustainable 
development gained traction at the second UNESCO World Conference on Cul -
tural Policies in Mexico City (MONDIACULT 2022), significant gaps remain in 
understanding how digital media can mediate and transform heritage practices. 
The twenty-first-century media landscape accelerates synchronic exchanges and 
generates new cultural forms through technology-based mediation processes of 
heritage-making, yet the intricate interplay between media and ICH making re -
mains underexplored. With the rise of digitisation and the cultural industry, digi -
tal media, especially animation, has become ubiquitous in daily life and widely 
popular among audiences of all ages. Animation holds significant potential to 
play a structural and reconciliatory role in cultural communication ( Silvio 2010), 
including in heritage-making. 
This chapter explores the transformative role of digital media in heritage-
making, focusing on the animated short films Orochen Tale of Nisan Shaman and 
Orochen Creation Story, including their encoding and decoding systems, as well as 
their production and dissemination processes, as case studies. Drawing on critical 
heritage studies and media archaeology, I argue that cultural brokers serve as cru-
cial nodes in establishing and maintaining a dialogical and participatory heritage 
network. Collaborative projects based on digital media generate boundary objects, 
such as animation films, which not only promote cultural diversity but also enhance 
the quality and extent of participation in the sustainable development-orientated 
global project of safeguarding ICH. By unpacking the multimodal production pro-
cesses of these artefacts, this chapter highlights the intersections of environmental 
change, cultural-ecological transformation, and participatory practice. 
 Orochenheritage:Significance,challenges,andopportunities 
As one of the officially recognised 55 “ethnic” groups, the Orochen mainly live in 
the Greater and Lesser Khingan Mountains and forests in Heilongjiang and Inner 
Mongolia provinces in Northeast China, next to the Eastern Siberian Mountains 
and the Mongolian Plateau. Notwithstanding the constructed nature of “ethnicity” 
in identity politics, “Orochen” means reindeer herders, reflecting their ancestry 
among the Tungusic-speaking tribes that flourished in old-growth pine forests that 
harboured a vast variety and quantity of wildlife. Sharing cultural and linguistic 
ties with Indigenous Tungusic groups in Siberia—where the term “shaman” origi-
nates (Guo 2015)—the Orochen traditionally practised a nomadic lifestyle based 
on hunting, thus known as “China’s Last Hunter-Gatherers.” This lifestyle fostered 
an extensive body of ecological knowledge, skills, craftsmanship, rituals, and tra-
ditions, transmitted orally through their unwritten language, a Northern Tungusic 
dialect, and embodied in social practices. 
This method of passing down knowledge aligns with Taylor’s concept of 
“performance” and “repertoire,” distinct from the textual traditions of archives 
(Taylor 2003, 2016, 2020). These performative modes of memory transmission, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digital Media and Safeguarding ICH of Minority Groups in China 163 
which Smith (2006) refers to as “diminished” forms of knowing and understand -
ing the past, are precisely what the 2003 Convention seeks to safeguard. 
In expanding the definition of “heritage” beyond the material-centric framework 
of the 1972 World Heritage Convention, the 2003 Convention embraces living 
cultural expressions transmitted across generations—often marginalised or over -
looked by textual traditions and material relics. It emphasises safeguarding ICH 
as “the practices, representations, expressions, knowledge, skills,” and “the instru-
ments, objects, artefacts and cultural spaces associated therewith” that the CGIs 
recognise as part of their heritage. Furthermore, CGIs constantly recreated ICH 
“in response to their environment, their interaction with nature and their history, 
and provides them with a sense of identity and continuity, thus promoting respect 
for cultural diversity and human creativity” (UNESCO 2022, CLT-2022/WS/3, 5). 
The ICH of the Orochen is particularly significant for promoting cultural di -
versity, human creativity, and sustainable development. Their customary rules, ta-
boos, and shamanistic culture exemplify a harmonious relationship with animals, 
plants, and the natural environment (Naran 2015; Zhang 2025). Notably, the region 
of Northeast China served as a conservation area for over two centuries under the 
Qing dynasty (1636–1912), established by Tungusic-speaking tribes. However, the 
environmental decline in this region dates back to the nineteenth century, exacer -
bated by the abandonment of the Grand Canal infrastructure, which had transported 
strategic raw materials from Southern to Northern China. This abandonment led 
to the exploitation of ecologically crucial areas, including the former forest of the 
Northeast, resulting in environmental degradation and refugee crises ( Chen 2012, 
354–355; Marks 2017, 274). 
This exploitation intensified during the twentieth century with industrialisation, 
particularly under Japanese occupation and colonial rule in the 1930s. Post-1949, 
Northeast China became a national resource hub for energy and agriculture. The 
implementation of a series of “modernisation” policies in the later twentieth cen -
tury transformed the “Great Northern Wilderness” into the Northeast China Plain, 
drastically altering the landscape and ecology that had supported the Orochen’s 
nomadic lifestyle (Bai 2017; Marks 2017, 293). 
Hunting, once essential to Orochen identity and household subsistence, has 
been prohibited since 1996. As a vital carrier of their ICH, the Orochen language 
has become endangered. The 2003 Convention presents an opportunity to revitalise 
Orochen ICH, enhancing their sense of identity, participation, and continuity, fos-
tering dialogue and respect among different groups, and promoting the inseparabil-
ity of “culture” and “nature” in addressing environmental crises (Fei 2017; Fraser 
2020; Latour 1993; Wu 2005, 2015). However, quantitative research indicates that 
the Northeast region has the fewest state-level ICH elements among the seven geo-
graphic regions of China, with Heilongjiang ranking among the provinces with the 
least (Guo et al. 2022). 
In the twenty-first century, digital technology presents new opportunities for pro-
moting cultural diversity and formal and informal heritage-making. Among the Oro-
chen, smartphones have facilitated bottom-up cultural initiatives. By creating and 
sharing short videos on platforms like WeChat and TikTok, community members 

 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
164 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
transmit their language, songs, and stories, fostering local cultural awareness and 
confidence. As stated in the UNESCO 2005 Convention on the Protection and Pro-
motion of the Diversity of Cultural Expressions (hereafter “2005 Convention”), 
Cultural diversity is made manifest not only through the varied ways in 
which the cultural heritage of humanity is expressed, augmented and trans-
mitted through the variety of cultural expressions, but also through diverse 
modes of artistic creation, production, dissemination, distribution and enjoy-
ment, whatever the means and technologies used. 
(UNESCO 2023, CLT-2024/WS/1, 7) 
Cultural brokerage and boundary objects as key factors 
to extend participation 
Participation is a cornerstone of ICH safeguarding, as emphasised in the Ethical 
Principles for Safeguarding Intangible Cultural Heritage, which was endorsed by 
the intergovernmental Committee of the 2003 Convention in 2015 and integrated 
into the Operational Directive of the Convention in 2016, highlighting the “primary 
role” of the CGIs but also indicating the significant challenge in implementing “par-
ticipation” (UNESCO 2022, CLT-2022/WS/3; Jacobs 2020). It is also emphasised 
in the MONDIACULT 2022 Declaration as a crucial factor for strengthening cul -
tural policy and addressing main global challenges ( UNESCO 2022, MONDIA -
CULT-2022/CPD/6), thus is expected to be a key thematic area in the Global Report 
on Cultural Policies that UNESCO intends to produce in 2025 and 2029. The 2003 
Convention encourages “scientific, technical and artistic studies, as well as research 
methodologies,” to ensure effective safeguarding and recognises the crucial role of 
media in raising awareness by encouraging audiovisual productions that enhance 
the visibility of ICH (UNESCO 2022, CLT-2022/WS/3, 66). However, despite rec-
ognising that producing audiovisual artefacts with cultural and heritage sensitiv -
ity requires participatory methodology, it remains challenging to construct broadly 
participatory mechanisms because of the heterogeneity and fluidity of CGIs and the 
absence of a universally agreed-upon definition of “participation.” 
Drawing on actor-network theory, Harrison (2013) introduced the “shaman” 
from Indigenous ontology to conceptualise a dialogical mode of heritage manage-
ment. This notion reconsiders the interplay between human and non-human col-
lectives, emphasising heritage as a future-orientated practice linked to sustainable 
development. Harrison described Shaman as a diplomat and mediator negotiating 
across different worlds to maintain the unity of collectives. This notion was inte-
grated into the Operational Directive of the 2003 Convention through the concept 
of “cultural brokerage.” Cultural brokers, however, often operate within asymmet-
rical relationships, an inherent complexity acknowledged in scholarly discourse 
(Baron 2021; Jacobs and Zhang 2022). 
This section discusses the multiple layers of cultural brokerages and the multiple 
roles of main cultural brokers in the production and dissemination of animated short 
films as boundary objects, drawing from the collaborative project. While researching 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digital Media and Safeguarding ICH of Minority Groups in China 165 
shamanistic expressions of groups in Northern China as ICH, I observed that schol-
ars, research institutions, and local ICH CGIs had collected substantial amounts of 
data with significant scientific, artistic, and cultural value. However, these data, of-
ten removed from their original contexts, were stored fragmentedly and passively as 
archives, lacking interconnectedness and accessibility. From 2019 to 2022, I collab-
orated with the Non-governmental organisation (hereafter “NGO”) Orochen Foun-
dation to produce animated short films Orochen Tale of Nisan Shaman and Orochen 
Creation Story as an experiment to address this issue. 
As boundary objects, animation films offer a means to integrate these archival 
materials by situating them within a mediated context, forming new connections, 
and simulating and representing the cultural worlds from which the data were gath-
ered. Through this process, archives are reactivated, performing a new role in the 
dual tasks of promoting cultural diversity and safeguarding ICH. The production of 
these animations exemplifies the cultural brokers’ role as mediators and innovators. 
By working across disciplinary and cultural boundaries, cultural brokers enable 
the creation of participatory and dialogical platforms for ICH safeguarding. These 
platforms enhance participation by addressing the asymmetries inherent in cultural 
brokerage, fostering inclusivity, and ensuring that CGIs’ perspectives remain cen-
tral in the ongoing process of heritage-making. 
NGO and cultural translation 
Both Orochen Tale of Nisan Shaman and Orochen Creation Story reflect the “ani-
mistic” worldviews of Northeast China. The concepts and scripts stemmed from 
the field notes of Hing Chao, a cultural broker from Hong Kong who has navigated 
multiple roles with significant impact. Baron (2021) noted that while the multifac-
eted responsibilities of cultural brokers often present challenges such as role con-
flicts and ambiguities, which are often constraining, confusing, and stress-inducing 
but also creatively generative. Chao exemplifies this dynamic. He is a practitioner 
and leading advocate of Chinese martial studies in Hong Kong, as well as an au-
thor, researcher, educator, and curator who has facilitated extensive research pro-
jects in Northeast China over the past two decades. 
Chao’s engagement with the Orochen began in 2001 during his travels in Inner 
Mongolia. Captivated by their nomadic hunting traditions, he was struck by the de-
cline of their culture, which he described as “a pale shadow of a once vibrant and 
proud heritage.” His commitment deepened after meeting Shuxian Ge, an Orochen 
elder who lamented the loss of their language and way of life, expressing a wish for 
her grandchildren to learn about their heritage, even through a museum. This encoun-
ter galvanised Chao’s dedication to documenting and revitalising Orochen culture. 
He immersed himself in fieldwork, accompanying hunting expeditions, collect-
ing songs and oral histories, and adopting a community-centred approach. In 2004, 
using funds raised by a photography exhibition, he founded the Orochen Founda -
tion, an NGO focused on poverty alleviation, community empowerment, and as -
sisting with cultural documentation and revitalisation. In 2009, he co-founded the 
NGO Earthpulse, later renamed the Intangible Cultural Heritage Earthpulse Society 

 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
166 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
in 2015, to foster synergy among environmental protection, cultural safeguarding, 
and socioeconomic development. NGOs, as Lewis (2014) notes, are flexible actors 
capable of forming connections with the CGIs beyond the capacities of govern -
ment institutions. The Orochen Foundation’s initiatives included seminars, lectures, 
conferences, performances, festivals, and exhibitions across mainland China, Hong 
Kong, and overseas, such as the landmark “Hunters, Warriors, Spirits: Nomadic 
Art of North China” exhibition. Its partnership with the China National Museum 
of Ethnology launched the Orochen Cultural Heritage Preservation Project in 2005, 
documenting local musical traditions. Orochen Tale of Nisan Shaman recounts the 
story of Nisan, a female spiritual healer retrieving a child’s lost soul—a tale orally 
transmitted in Northeast China. This animated short film exemplifies these efforts, 
drawing from the Orochen Foundation’s 2007 publication, UNCOOKED. 
Chao described his early efforts as “learning on the job,” navigating challenges 
such as resource limitations, time constraints, and scepticism from outsiders, amid 
the rapid decline of traditions. Fundraising remained an ongoing struggle, and pub-
lic interest waned after the initial novelty of the Foundation’s work. Over time, he 
honed his ethnographic skills, often working closely with collaborators such as the 
Orochen artist Ying Bai. 
In 2011, he was invited to establish a Service-Learning course at Hong Kong 
Polytechnic University. Over five years, he led students on large-scale documen-
tation projects, culminating in facilitating a local cultural festival in the Orochen 
capital of Alihe in 2013, a photography exhibition at the Ewenki Banner Museum 
in 2016, and the publication of Mountains, Oh Mountains! An Oral History of the 
Orochen Hunters in 2017. 
In 2015, invited by the Government of Hong Kong, Chao launched the Hong 
Kong Cultural Festival with the support of the Intangible Cultural Heritage Earth -
pulse Society, extending platforms for international exchanges on ICH. As the 
heir of Wah Kwong Maritime Transport, a main corporate sponsor of the Orochen 
Foundation, Chao has served as the executive chairman of the private shipping 
company since 2019. 
For him, collaborating with the Orochen has been a transformative journey, fos-
tering a profound appreciation and enabling a new way of seeing the world shaped 
by Orochen values. His roles and efforts built connections across multiple social 
and cultural worlds, bridging Hong Kong, Northeast China, and global heritage net-
works, connecting heritage institutions, NGOs, cultural specialists, and local practi-
tioners. These initiatives fostered the safeguarding of Orochen ICH while enriching 
Hong Kong’s cultural landscape through resource redistribution, multimedia exhibi-
tions, and international collaboration. 
Animation filmmaking as academic research methodology and identity 
performance 
In this collaborative project, I took on dual roles: as an animation filmmaker and as 
a researcher exploring shamanistic expressions among groups in Northern China. 
Filmmaking became an integral part of my research methodology to acquire a PhD 

 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digital Media and Safeguarding ICH of Minority Groups in China 167 
in cultural heritage studies, serving not only to engage with academic networks 
but also to connect media archaeology with heritage studies. Additionally, it was 
a craftsmanship and “world-building” practice ( Silvio 2010) that performed and 
strengthened aspects of my own identity. 
Since 2011, I have been committed to creating animated short films to represent 
and interpret ICH. One notable example, The Song of the Maple Tree (2015), in-
terprets an epic verse of the Miao people in China and was well received among 
international audiences. However, the emotional resonance of ICH among CGIs 
prompted me to reflect further on heritage sensitivity. Despite involving the partici-
pation of local actors, including news media, artists specialising in “ethnic” music, 
the local safeguarding bureau, and a practitioner—the production risked tokenism. 
This was due to limited field exchange and the relatively passive participation of 
CGIs, who lacked agency in framing or providing feedback. 
My role as a research assistant for the UNESCO Chair on Critical Heritage Stud-
ies and Safeguarding the Intangible Cultural Heritage deepened my engagement with 
UNESCO Chairs and UNITWIN Networks on ICH (Jacobs et al. 2019). Gradually, 
I recognised that my earlier perspective was Han-centric to a certain extent, shaped 
by a romanticised view of “ethnicity.” Creating these two short films provided me 
an opportunity to address these issues as a media and art practitioner and cultural 
scientist. It allowed me to experiment with solutions to foster more meaningful par-
ticipation while observing the dynamics of collaborative projects firsthand. 
This approach proved to be a valuable participatory research methodology. 
The processes of production, dissemination, and reflection enabled sustained dia -
logues with other key stakeholders. These interactions illuminated how collabo -
rative projects are shaped by various influences, function as boundary objects to 
mediate relationships between stakeholders, and, in turn, impact the local CGIs 
and their heritage. Through these dialogues, the project demonstrated how partici-
patory media can contribute to reshaping both academic and cultural narratives. 
Local brokerage as creative strategy to overcome constraints 
The concepts for the films were developed in 2018 and originally intended for an 
exhibition on the shamanistic culture of Manchuria in Hong Kong between 2019 
and 2020. However, a series of political protests during this period rendered pub-
lic gatherings sensitive, leading to delays and cancellations of many exhibitions, 
including the planned showcase on shamanistic culture. This was further com-
pounded by the COVID-19 pandemic, which restricted mobility and made field-
work for voice recordings nearly impossible. These challenges necessitated remote 
collaboration among stakeholders, including the filmmaker, the Orochen Founda-
tion, the curatorial team, and particularly Orochen ICH practitioner Xiaoyun Guan, 
an ICH inheritor, author, and retired local governmental official. 
Born in Shibazhan Orochen Ethnic Township,
1 Tahe County of the far north 
of Heilongjiang in Northeast China, Guan is one of the first officially recognised 
provincial-level inheritors. With over two decades of experience promoting Oro-
chen cultural heritage, her work spans the identification, documentation, and 

 
 
  
 
 
 
 
168 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
transmission of ICH through lectures, training sessions, and community initiatives. 
Her career includes serving as deputy head of Shibazhan Orochen Ethnic Township 
Government in the 1980s, deputy director of Tahe County’s Cultural and Historical 
Office of the Political Consultative Conference, director of the Ethnic and Reli -
gious Affairs Bureau of Tahe County in the 1990s, and as deputy director of the 
United Front Work Department of Tahe County Party Committee in the 2000s until 
her retirement in 2013.
2 Notably, in the 1990s, she facilitated The Last Mountain 
God (1992), an ethnographic documentary directed by Zengtian Sun that chroni-
cled the declining Orochen traditions and the “primitive” life of Jinfu Meng in the 
forest, known as “the last shaman of the Orochen.” 
Although her government roles were not explicitly focused on ICH safeguard-
ing, her identity as an Orochen author and her passion for cultural traditions mo-
tivated her to align her work with ICH safeguarding. After attending the National 
Congress of China Folk Literature and Arts Association in 2006—shortly after the 
2003 Convention came into force—she began integrating ICH safeguarding into 
her activities, despite the notion of ICH being relatively new at the local level. 
Recognised as a provincial-level ICH bearer, Guan receives an annual subsidy 
of 5,000 RMB (approximately $700) from the provincial Department of Culture 
and Tourism, supporting her safeguarding efforts. In comparison, state-level ICH 
bearers receive 20,000 RMB annually (approximately $2,800). In turn, officially 
recognised bearers are obligated to report their safeguarding activities annually 
to pass assessments by the Department/Ministry of Culture and Tourism. Guan’s 
participation in scriptwriting, narration, voice recording, and dissemination of the 
films during 2021–2022 was integral to the projects. These contributions, which 
form part of her safeguarding responsibilities, fostered a participatory and dialogi-
cal approach to the animation filmmaking process, exemplifying the collaborative 
potential of local brokerage in overcoming constraints. 
Animated short films as boundary objects 
Boundary objects are inherently plastic—adaptable to the interpretations of differ-
ent actors while maintaining a shared identity across boundaries. They are crucial 
for fostering a common ground and shared context in collaborative research, bring-
ing together heterogeneous actors across diverse social worlds, and presenting 
adaptability to meet various constraints and challenges (Martínez 2021, 25–26). 
Animation as a boundary object holds immense potential to transform, reshape, 
expand, and transcend beyond the constraints of the physical world. This potential, 
described as “plasmaticness” by filmmaker and theorist Eisenstein (1988, 11), re-
fers to the capacity to subvert order, logic, stasis, and bias, while acknowledging 
concealed social forms and delivering the intangible, such as emotions, internalised 
feelings, and complex states of mind in tangible forms. 
Through its multimodal storytelling, animation embodies a performative mode of 
world-making. It enables knowledge integration mechanisms and stimulates multi-
sensory engagement (Leslie 2014; Piredda et al. 2013; Silvio 2010). Spanning mul-
tiple ICH domains—knowledge, craftsmanship, social practices, and performing 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
Digital Media and Safeguarding ICH of Minority Groups in China 169 
arts—the technologies and approaches of animation have been continually recre -
ated by practitioners in art, media, and heritage. As such, animation can serve as an 
invaluable tool for enhancing participation by informing and transmitting intangible 
knowledge, memory, and experience. 
 Performingthearchive:Process,participation,andprospects 
In the “postmodern” context, ethnographic and anthropological archives play a 
significant role in reviving Indigenous cultures (Dueck 2021, 408). Animation 
filmmaking, as a boundary object, offers a means to reconnect and reactivate frag-
mented field data and archives, assigning them new meanings and agencies. This 
section explores the processes and layers of heritage-making involved in construct-
ing and disseminating animated short films. 
Reconnecting and performing archives 
By integrating visual elements derived from anthropological and ethnographic ar -
tefacts, images, and records into the cinematographic design, animation filmmak -
ing becomes a powerful tool to reconnect fragmented archives and assign them 
new agencies. The research output of ethnologists Shuyun Guo and Yuguang Fu 
on the shamanistic cultures and arts of Manchuria (Fu 2009; Guo 2001, 2015) pro-
vided invaluable visual data, including sketches and photography of shamanistic 
icons, symbols, ritual crafts, and visual expressions collected during fieldwork. 
These materials informed the creation of the spiritual world that the Nisan shaman 
traverses while entering a trance to retrieve a lost soul. 
To present an overwhelming spiritual realm with these icons and figures, 
nineteenth-century European optical techniques, such as phenakistoscope and 
zoopraxiscope, were digitally recreated in motion graphic designs, performing 
“spirits” of the Northeast (Figure 10.1). Media archaeologists see these techniques 
as variations of animation that embody the “cinema of attraction,” which focuses 
on establishing visual contact, inciting curiosity, and providing spectacle-based 
Figure 10.1 Left: Digital recreation of phenakistoscope and zoopraxiscope, performing 
Orochen icons. © Jiyun Zhang. Right: Visual effect in the virtual exhibition 
Hunters, Warriors, Spirits: Nomadic Art of North China . © Indra and Harry 
Banga Gallery, City University of Hong Kong. 

 
 
 
 
 
  
170 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
pleasure to audiences, historically contributing to the dissemination of visual 
culture, technology, and science among the public ( Elsaesser 2016, 24; Gunning 
2006, 2014; Wynants 2019). 
Rooted in their forested environment, the Orochen craftsmanship uses organic 
materials such as birch bark and wood. Orochen hunters used to carve faces into 
trees to represent gods and conducted rituals to pray before hunting. Shamans’ rit-
ual performances are considered the earliest form of theatre in China (Chen 1936; 
Chen 2017). Thus, applying the graphic style of woodcarving prints and visual 
elements of traditional theatrical stage design were adopted as visual metaphors in 
storytelling (Figure 10.2). 
Figure 10.2 Orochen Tale of Nisan Shaman, graphic style and element of traditional theatri-
cal stage design. © Jiyun Zhang. 

 
 
  
Digital Media and Safeguarding ICH of Minority Groups in China 171 
Figure 10.3 Representation of Shaman in the Museum of Yitong Manchu Autonomous 
County. Photograph: Jiyun Zhang. 
The design of the shamanic garments drew inspiration from collections, exhibi-
tions, and presentations of the Museum of Yitong Manchu Autonomous County, 
the Shaman Culture Museum of Changchun University, and the Northeast Ethnic 
and Folklore Museum of Northeast Normal University (Figure 10.3). Keyframe 
motion designs of shamanic dance were informed by illustrations in the manuscript 
Manchu Genealogy Model Diagram, written in 1771 by Shaman Changqing of 
the Šušu Gioro Hala Clan. This manuscript, part of the collection at the Institute 
of Advanced Studies on Asia at the University of Tokyo, provided valuable visual 
references (Figures 10.4 and 10.5). 
The creative process benefitted from the insights and support of collaborators 
in Belgium, including Rob Breyne and Patrick Berx, animation tutors of KASK 
& Conservatorium in Gent
3; Edwin Carels, a film critic, curator, and media ar -
chaeologist; and Merle Erl Idris Ghyssaert, a musician and animation filmmaker. 
Their expertise contributed significantly to refining the project’s visual and narra-
tive strategies. 
Cultural translation and transculturation in soundscape 
The 2003 Convention emphasises the role of media in promoting knowledge of 
local languages and cultures as essential components of ICH (UNESCO 2022, 
CLT-2022/WS/3, 52). In this spirit, both films feature narration in the endangered 
Orochen language and integrate recorded Orochen singing performed by a local 
practitioner. 

 
   
   
172 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Figure 10.4 Illustrations of shamanic dance in the Manchu Genealogy Model Diagram 
by Changqing Šušu Gioro Hala, 1771. Open-source digital copy provided by 
Fudan University Library (lfglib.cn). 
Xiaoyun Guan played a central role in translating, editing, and recreating the 
scripts from Mandarin into the Orochen language. Her adaptations were grounded 
in the daily vocabulary and spoken habits of Orochen speakers. In addition to per-
forming and recording the narration, she supervised the synchronisation of the voice 
recordings with Chinese subtitles during the editing process. This collaborative 
Figure 10.5 Animation keyframes of shamanic dance in Orochen Tale of Nisan Shaman. 
© Jiyun Zhang. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digital Media and Safeguarding ICH of Minority Groups in China 173 
approach changed the relatively passive participation of CGIs as presenters and 
performers in similar projects, empowering them to frame and interpret their own 
culture using their mother tongue. 
The soft and melodic Orochen singing that opens and recurs throughout Oro-
chen Tale of Nisan Shaman is a recording of a soul-summoning song performed by 
Taofang Guan, an Orochen practitioner of birch bark arts and crafts. The recording, 
part of Hing Chao’s documentation of Orochen musical traditions, includes the 
performer’s explanation in Mandarin. She contextualised the song as one sung by 
mothers to their children as a prayer during illness. This interpretation aligned with 
the film’s concept, allowing the song to serve as a metaphorical trope in the sound-
scape, establishing the mood in the opening and echoing in the ending. 
Targeting a broad domestic and international audience, especially younger 
viewers, the films feature bilingual subtitles in Chinese and English. Orochen Cre-
ation Story incorporates Fandango 13 by the Eastman Percussion Ensemble, with 
the support of Michael Burritt of the Eastman School of Music at the University 
of Rochester, United States. The liveliness of the music seamlessly depicts spirits 
waking up to the sounds of the forest and celebrating the world’s creation. This fu-
sion of Orochen ICH with a contemporary percussion ensemble is built on a shared 
foundation: percussion music as a core cultural expression in both traditions. The 
encounter reimagines and transforms each cultural element, creating a novel ex-
pression. It exemplifies the broader process of transculturation—the dynamic and 
continuous patterns of cultural transformation (Taylor 1991). 
Exhibition and dissemination: Towards a Semantic Web of ICH 
These films were showcased to the public in Hong Kong from 23 July to 23 Oc-
tober 2022 as part of the exhibition “Hunters, Warriors, Spirits: Nomadic Art of 
North China” (Figure 10.6). This event—curated by Hing Chao, digital museolo-
gist Jeffrey Shaw (Professor of Media Art at City University Hong Kong, renowned 
for his pioneering work in new media art since the 1960s), and Sarah Kenderdine 
(Professor of Digital Museology at the École Polytechnique Fédérale de Lausanne 
and head of the Laboratory for Experimental Museology)—was a key feature of 
the Hong Kong Cultural Festival. The exhibition remains accessible online through 
a virtual platform, designed as a 360-degree interactive experience (Indra and 
Harry Banga Gallery 2022) (Figure 10.7). This digital exhibition overcomes the 
constraints of linear playback and passive viewing by enabling users to explore 
exhibition halls, watch films and videos, zoom in on artefacts from multiple angles, 
and access detailed information via clickable text and hyperlinks, simulating and 
enhancing the conventional museum experience. 
Media technological innovations can reshape sensory experiences and percep -
tions of cultural heritage ( Benjamin and Kang 2014 , 27; Hodkinson 2016, 5). 
Since 2018, media’s role in raising awareness of ICH, fostering mutual respect, 
and promoting safeguarding have been recognised as one of the core indicators 
within the Overall Results Framework for evaluating the implementation of the 
2003 Convention by States ( UNESCO 2022, CLT-2022/WS/3, 126). 

 
  
  
 
174 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Figure 10.6 On-site exhibition. Photo courtesy of Hong Kong Culture Festival. 
The dialogical and participatory working mechanisms established during the 
project continued to foster both formal and informal exchanges within the network 
even after the exhibition. This approach provided opportunities for further dissemi-
nation and reflection based on feedback from stakeholders. For instance, Xiaoyun 
Guan presented the films at the Fourth Arctic Research Forum Arctic Environment 
Figure 10.7 Screenshot of the online virtual tour Hunters, Warriors, Spirits: Nomadic Art of 
North China © Indra and Harry Banga Gallery, City University of Hong Kong. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Digital Media and Safeguarding ICH of Minority Groups in China 175 
and Humanities International Conference
4 in Heilongjiang in 2023, where she in-
vited me to co-author a report on the project. This collaboration offered valuable 
insights into some perspectives of CGIs, who viewed the films not only as innova-
tive tools for raising awareness but also as instruments for enhancing the status of 
Orochen cultural heritage within official heritage-making processes. Recently, Xi-
aoyun Guan submitted the films as part of the application materials for nominating 
Orochen Legends and Stories (鄂伦春乌印) on the prefecture-level ICH inventory 
through Tahe County. 
Integrating digital media and ICH as drivers of sustainable development: 
Reflections 
During an online meeting/interview with Xiaoyun Guan on 26 July 2024, we dis-
cussed challenges of safeguarding Orochen ICH and the potential of digital media 
as a transformative tool. Guan underscored the importance of embracing digital 
media to expand the reach of safeguarding activities. In light of the pivotal role 
oral traditions play within Orochen ICH, the multimodality of audiovisual media 
provides an optimal medium for recording, archiving, dissemination, and creative 
reinterpretation. Further, she highlighted that Orochen oral traditions are firmly 
embedded in embodied performances, providing rich material for contemporary 
artistic expressions. 
She proposed integrating ICH safeguarding with local development agendas by 
fostering the Orochen cultural industry. Suggested initiatives include promoting cul-
tural tourism, producing cultural goods such as films, television programmes, and 
theatrical performances, and establishing both an Orochen Ecological and Cultural 
Conservation Area and an ICH Learning Centre to empower and support CGIs. 
Since the opening of the National Highway 331 along China’s northern border, 
Shibazhan has experienced a notable increase in the number of tourists seeking to 
engage with “ethnic” tourism. This growing interest could further reinforce the role 
of Orochen ICH as a driver for sustainable development, aligning with the princi -
ples outlined in the 2003 Convention, which promotes integrating ICH into devel-
opment policies to improve living standards, foster local economies, and enhance 
social cohesion while raising awareness and fostering mutual respect ( UNESCO 
2022, CLT-2022/WS/3, 53, 64). One noteworthy example of such integration is Ten-
cent’s 2018 mobile game Nishan Shaman, developed by six graduate students. The 
game exemplifies the creative potential of the cultural industry to engage younger 
audiences and activate ICH. 
Though the popularisation of ICH discourse sometimes led to (over-) commer -
cialisation and decontextualisation (Bortolotto 2021), before this, numerous tra-
ditional cultural forms that are significant to local identities had been labelled as 
“backwardness” and “superstition,” concealed, and marginalised within the domi-
nant “modern” discourse, as had the relevant CGIs. ICH safeguarding discourse 
has mitigated the stigma, strengthened CGIs, and facilitated greater opportunities. 
To enhance participation, Guan proposes capacity building on audiovisual me-
dia, cultivating talents within “ethnic” groups, creating the Orochen-initiated nar -
ratives, and strengthening and enhancing local cultural identities and confidence. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
176 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Though the Orochen language was introduced to the local Township Central School 
curriculum, it was ultimately merged with another school due to the number of en-
rolled students in primary school and junior school falling below 30. This reflects 
the demographic change in the northern frontier regions. Some suggested that the 
prohibition on commercial logging of natural forests in Heilongjiang since 2014 
for environmental protection may have contributed to this demographic change 
(Central Government Portal 2014; Wang 2021). Yet, Guan does not view this pol-
icy as pertinent to Shibazhan, instead attributing it to natural conditions like the 
harsh weather and a broader socioeconomic dynamic. 
Our discussion was briefly interrupted by a phone call from her daughter, in-
forming Guan that her grandson is admitted to the Tianjin University of Science 
and Technology. Consequently, in September, he will be relocating to Tianjin, a 
metropolis next to Beijing. This moment underscored the persistent tensions be-
tween local attachment and the aspirations tied to opportunities in urban centres. 
It drew attention to the imbalanced distribution of educational, medical, and eco-
nomic resources as a main factor driving emigration and demographic decline in 
marginalised border regions and fundamentally alienating these communities from 
their heritage. 
This underscores the necessity for enhanced collaboration between local CGIs 
and other regional, national, and international stakeholders. ICH safeguarding is “of 
general interest to humanity and should therefore be undertaken through coopera -
tion among bilateral, subregional, regional and international parties; nevertheless,” 
the CGIs “should never be alienated from” their own ICH ( UNESCO 2022, CLT-
2022/WS/3, 112). 
The exhibition brought together international curators with expertise in digi-
tal museology, and artists and scholars external to the Orochen communities. The 
expertise, academic backgrounds, resources, methodologies, experience, and net-
works are significant for disseminating Orochen culture, assuming the significant 
role of cultural brokers towards constructing a Semantic Web of Orochen ICH, 
linking and integrating relevant data with improved accessibility. The exhibition is 
open to the public free in Hong Kong and is anticipated to be introduced to muse-
ums in Europe. 
Conclusion 
This chapter emphasises the significance of cultural brokers as human actors and 
boundary objects as non-human actors in establishing and maintaining a network 
for safeguarding and promoting Orochen ICH, cultural diversity, and sustainable 
development. It has demonstrated the crucial role of this network in cultivating 
participation. Digital media is pivotal for facilitating the documentation of “per -
formances” as embodied ICH, and it extends CGIs’ participation by enabling non-
formal and bottom-up safeguarding measures beyond physical constraints. 
However, for safeguarding ICH of marginalised CGIs such as the Orochen, be-
yond archiving, it is crucial to recreate ICH as living heritage and as boundary 
objects through collaborative projects. As technology-mediated boundary objects, 

 
 
  
  
  
  
 
  
Digital Media and Safeguarding ICH of Minority Groups in China 177 
animated short films, including their production and dissemination processes, re-
new ICH and exert a transformative effect on stakeholders, while also influencing 
the status of ICH elements and local CGIs. They facilitate connections between 
memory and innovation, bridging the 2003 Convention with the 2005 Convention. 
Given the various levels of cultural brokerage in collaborative projects to safe-
guard ICH and promote cultural diversity, and the multiplicity of identities among 
cultural brokers, it is not always straightforward to delineate the boundaries be-
tween CGIs and authority. None of the collaborators of this project fits rigorously 
into these categories. Moreover, the transformative potential of boundary objects 
can reshape “outsiders” into part of the CGIs, given their active involvement in 
research, communication, management, and recreation of ICH. To encourage “wid-
est participation,” the concept of CGIs and participation should be open and fluid. 
Meantime, the “heritage community” in the Faro Convention can be employed as 
a provisional measure until a more comprehensive toolkit for a participatory ap-
proach is available. 
Conversely, this fluidity also presents some challenges, particularly for minority 
cultures. For example, Xiaoyun Guan shared that she has often been approached 
by researchers from universities across China, as well as international scholars. 
While there has been considerable interest in Orochen culture, there is uncertainty 
about the sustainability of their involvement and commitment. Furthermore, many 
approached the CGIs and requested and collected “data” for ICH safeguarding-
related purposes, leaving the CGIs in the dark regarding the ultimate use of these 
“data.” This concern exemplifies the multifaceted challenges inherent in effecting 
positive and sustainable changes in the safeguarding of ICH for minority groups, 
particularly when the ecological systems that nurtured their cultural expressions 
have undergone a profound transformation.
