Section III 
Managing heritage 
participation 



11 Opportunities and challenges of 
participatory heritage governance 
Developing the Seminaarinmäki 
Campus as a European Heritage 
Label site in Finland 
Tuuli Lähdesmäki 
Over the past decades, participation has become a buzzword that has found its 
way into heritage governance, encompassing a variety of objectives, principles, 
and practices rather than a structured procedure ( Colomer and Pastor Pérez 2024 ; 
Cortés-Vázquez, Jiménez-Esquinas, and Sánchez-Carretero 2017 ). Such govern -
ance commonly seeks to challenge the top-down, expert-centric model of manag -
ing heritage sites and heritage narratives, and to bridge the gap between heritage 
experts and non-experts or the public. It encourages and guides heritage sector 
actors to actively involve citizens, communities, and stakeholders in the processes 
of heritage assessment, conservation, meaning-making, communication, decision-
making, and/or management (Colomer and Pastor Pérez 2024, 82; Lähdesmäki and 
Mäkinen 2019, 45; SoPHIA 2020, 28). A key to such activities is the sharing of re-
sponsibilities among the participants involved (György et al. 2019, 15). In practice, 
such processes are expected to build on diverse collaborative working methods and 
co-creative designs for interaction. 
While participatory heritage governance is well accepted in theory and as a 
principle, its implementation faces a number of challenges. First, participatory her-
itage governance, as such governance in any field, is challenged by the ambiguous 
concept of participation (Colomer and Pastor Pérez 2024, 81; see Lähdesmäki and 
Turunen in this volume). Second, such governance is challenged by the vague no-
tion of community, which remains a contested concept in heritage studies (György 
et al. 2019 , 13; Waterton and Smith 2010 , 8). All communities are more or less 
imagined constructions that do not necessarily involve an internal consensus of 
their narratives and activities ( Waterton and Smith 2010 , 8). In addition to (and 
also because of) ambiguous concepts, scholars have identified various challenges 
to the implementation of participatory heritage governance. An expert report com-
missioned by the European Commission (EC 2018, 27), based on a wide range of 
empirical data, notes that “participatory governance of cultural heritage seems to 
have had difficulties progressing from a rhetorical level to a practical one”. Several 
case studies have come to the same conclusion, pointing to “the lack of a true par-
ticipatory turn in cultural heritage management” (Colomer and Pastor Pérez 2024, 
81) and recognising “gaps in the citizen–governance relationship and places where 
the citizen seems to be missing” (Li 2017, 236). Thus, participation can be limited 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-15 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
184 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
to tokenistic, symbolic, or authorised participation, where processes are opaque 
and social participatory agency is only cosmetic (Colomer and Pastor Pérez 2024; 
Pastor Pérez and Colomer 2024). 
Even if heritage institutions try to provide access to a wider public, engage 
citizens, involve stakeholders, and be more responsive to their needs, participa -
tory governance can still remain a top-down process (György et al. 2019, 15). In 
a bottom-up process, responsibilities are truly shared and communities have the 
power and opportunity to more autonomously define their own heritage, take ini-
tiatives, make decisions, and engage in and create activities independent of, but 
in collaboration with, existing heritage institutions and authorities ( György et al. 
2019; Roued-Cunliffe and Copeland 2017; Sani et al. 2015; Sonkoly and Vahtikari 
2018). Such bottom-up processes do not obviate the need for heritage authorities 
and administrative bodies: collaboration requires both. As György et al. (2019, 15) 
note: “It is clear that wide range of actors are needed in every stage of the process, 
i.e. public authorities and bodies, private actors, civil society organisations, NGOs, 
the volunteering sector and other interested people”. 
In this chapter, I explore the opportunities and challenges of participatory herit-
age governance when it is put into practice and its goals and implementation are 
managed at the grassroots level. I ask what participation in participatory herit-
age governance ultimately means; how participatory heritage processes could and 
should be managed; and what such governance and management require to be 
successful. To respond to these questions, I focus on specific case 
The chapter’s empirical case study focuses on the Seminaarinmäki Campus in 
Finland. This tangible site, with its intangible heritage of equality in education, was 
awarded the European Heritage Label (EHL) in 2022. This case study allows for 
the exploration of participatory heritage governance in action due to its institutional 
framework: The EHL is the European Commission’s flagship heritage action, fol-
lowing the European Union’s (EU) explicit objectives to promote a participatory 
approach to cultural heritage (CofEU 2014; EC 2019). Since 2011, the Commis-
sion has awarded the EHL to 67 heritage sites, based on the sites’ applications and 
the recommendations of expert panels at national and European level. The main 
focus of the EHL is on the social sphere of cultural heritage (Čeginskas et al. 2021), 
as stated in the decision of the EHL action (EP&C 2011, 2): 
The main focus of the action should be on the promotion of and access to 
the sites as well as on the quality of the information and activities offered, 
as opposed to the preservation of the sites, which should be guaranteed by 
existing protection regimes. 
In this chapter, I extend my previous research on the EHL (e.g. Lähdesmäki 2016; 
Lähdesmäki et al. 2020) by turning my analytical gaze to the grassroots level and 
examining how participatory microecology works at the case EHL site. Without a 
detailed picture of the dynamics and management of participation at the grassroots 
level, our understanding of the participatory governance of the EHL action remains 
deficient. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Opportunities and Challenges of Participatory Heritage Governance 185 
Participatory heritage governance in the EU 
In recent decades, scholars have commonly characterised EU policy-making as 
multilevel governance (e.g. Bache and Flinders 2004 ; DeBardeleben and Hurrel-
mann 2007; Hooghe and Marks 2001 ; Piattoni 2009a). It refers to the increasing 
complexity of governance in a globalised world in which “supranational, national, 
regional and local governments are enmeshed in territorially overarching policy net-
works” (Marks 1993, 402–403). In addition to this vertical interaction, multilevel 
governance also operates horizontally, linking different levels of government and 
increasing the interdependence between their governmental and non-governmental 
actors (Bache and Flinders 2004, 3). Multilevel governance has been interpreted as 
having contradictory effects on democracy: It encourages non-state actors to partici-
pate in governance processes, thereby increasing democracy, but at the same time it 
undermines democracy by complicating governance through the involvement and 
equalisation of different types of actors with different mandates in governance pro-
cesses, thus creating a complex mix of governing actors (Piattoni 2009b, 164). 
EU policy-making combines the principles of multilevel governance with the ob-
jectives of participatory governance (Jakubowski 2024; Lähdesmäki et al. 2020). Both 
are in line with the EU’s core mission of promoting democracy in Europe. Although 
the participatory approach was already explicitly articulated in EU policy discourses 
in the early 1990s (Saurugger 2010), and the current EU treaty in force, the Treaty of 
Lisbon, emphasises both representative and participatory democracy, scholars have 
noted that despite the EU’s self-imposed commitments to citizen participation, “the 
Union’s institutions appear to be slow to fully embrace it” (Bekemans 2018, 51). 
In the cultural sector, however, the EU has been more agile in implementing par-
ticipatory processes. Over the past decade, the EU has launched several heritage 
policies and initiatives that emphasise participation and the role of communities and 
stakeholders in heritage processes. These policies and initiatives reflect, with some de-
lay, the participatory ethos of other international heritage conventions and agreements 
(Jakubowski 2024), such as the UNESCO Convention for the Safeguarding of the 
Intangible Cultural Heritage, adopted in 2003, and the Council of Europe Framework 
Convention on the Value of Cultural Heritage for Society, adopted in 2005. 
The EU’s first explicit heritage policy, the European Commission’s Communi -
cation Towards an Integrated Approach to Cultural Heritage for Europe, adopted in 
2014, demonstrates the close link between multi-level and participatory governance 
(Lähdesmäki et al. 2020). The aim of the communication is to “strengthen policy co-
operation at different levels” (EC 2014, 3), “support new models of heritage govern-
ance” (EC 2014, 3), and “continue developing more participative interpretation and 
governance models that are better suited to contemporary Europe, through greater 
involvement of the private sector and civil society” (EC 2014, 7). In the same year, the 
Council of the European Union adopted its Conclusions on Participatory Governance 
of Cultural Heritage (CofEU 2014, 2), inviting EU Member States to 
develop multilevel and multi-stakeholder governance frameworks which 
recognise cultural heritage as a shared resource by strengthening the links 

 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
  
 
  
 
 
   
 
 
 
 
186 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
between the local, regional, national and European levels of governance of 
cultural heritage, with due respect to the principle of subsidiarity, so that 
benefits for people are envisaged at all levels. 
Furthermore, the conclusions encouraged Member States to “promote the in -
volvement of relevant stakeholders by ensuring that their participation is possible 
at all stages of the decision-making process” (CofEU 2014, 2). These stakeholders 
include “public authorities and bodies, private actors, civil society organisations, 
NGOs, the volunteering sector and interested people” (CofEU 2014, 2). 
In 2015, EU Ministers of Culture agreed to set up a working group of heritage 
experts from 26 EU Member States and Norway to identify innovative approaches 
to multi-level governance of heritage, involving the public sector, private stake -
holders, and civil society, and to enhance cooperation between different levels of 
heritage governance ( EC 2018, 12). The group published a comprehensive hand-
book with recommendations for policy makers and heritage institutions (EC 2018, 
13). Building on this work and the legacy of the EU’s celebration of the European 
Year of Cultural Heritage, the Commission published the European Framework 
for Action on Cultural Heritage in 2019. This framework is based on the participa-
tory governance of heritage and emphasises participation in all its five pillars ( EC 
2019). In the pillar “participation and access for all”, the EHL is identified as a 
tool for engaging the wider public ( EC 2019, 17). 
My previous research on the EHL has shown how the governance of the ac -
tion involves and networks administrative actors and authorities at the EU, Eu-
ropean, national, and local levels. However, despite the increased networking, 
the regulation of the action is top-down, with the European selection panel, EU 
officials, and the EHL Bureau – an organisation that won the Commission’s bid 
to coordinate the action in 2023 – exercising the highest regulatory and admin -
istrative power. The previous research also showed how local heritage managers 
could find the multi-level governance of the EHL confusing if the governance 
structures and the roles and responsibilities involved were unclear or vague ( Läh-
desmäki et al. 2020 , 36). Despite the emphasis on participation and involvement 
of citizens, communities, and stakeholders, the participatory governance of the 
EHL action does not seem to reach “below” the EHL site managers. The EHL 
action does not provide further guidance or tools for managing participation at 
the site. Similarly, the Commission’s monitoring of the EHL sites addresses par -
ticipation only in the narrow sense of access to the site and the promotion of 
multilingualism ( EHL Monitoring Report 2024 ). 
The Seminaarinmäki Campus case study: Data and methods 
The Seminaarinmäki Campus combines tangible, intangible, and living heritage 
in a constantly evolving area used daily by hundreds of students, teachers, and 
researchers. It was the first EHL site in Finland, proposed by the University of Jy-
väskylä and the Director of the University Museum as site manager. In its selection 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Opportunities and Challenges of Participatory Heritage Governance 187 
report (EC 2021, 29), the European panel described the main cultural heritage sig-
nificance of the site as follows: 
The Seminaarinmäki Campus – Equality in Education represents the Finnish 
education system and the high-quality teachers’ training promoting equal -
ity of all people from its very beginning. Based on the tradition of the first 
Finnish-speaking teachers’ college created in 1863 as Jyväskylä Teacher 
Seminary in the city of Jyväskylä in central Finland, the school transforms 
into the multidisciplinary University of Jyväskylä (JYU) in the twentieth 
century. The architecture embodies the values and pedagogical innovations 
developed at the site. 
The architecture of the site encompasses several historical layers, including 
the former Teachers’ Seminary buildings of the 1880s and 1900s and the modern 
university buildings of the 1950s and 1970s, many of which were designed by the 
key figure of the international modernist architectural movement, Alvar Aalto. 
The EHL action requires the winning sites to implement a project focusing on 
raising awareness of the site’s European significance, developing educational activi-
ties to communicate this, increasing access to the site, raising the site’s profile, and 
networking with other EHL sites ( EP&C 2011, 4). The project at the Seminaarin -
mäki Campus, Science for All, was planned to create a science centre in which 
“the multilayered European history of Seminaarinmäki and the active science world 
of today interact and act as places for encounters, learning and research for the 
operators and visitors” and which “promotes thought-provoking dialogue between 
research, education and society, and organises diverse physical and virtual activities 
for a variety of audiences” (JYU 2021, 3). 
My analysis of the implementation of participatory governance at the Semi -
naarinmäki Campus is based on three data sets: the observation of the prepara -
tion of the EHL proposal on an online platform, an autoethnographic diary of the 
development of the Campus as an EHL site, and 16 interviews with eight focus 
groups. The last two data sets were collected over two years from the Commis -
sion’s decision to award the Campus in spring 2022 to spring 2024. The autoeth-
nographic diary contains 39 short texts recording my observations and thoughts 
on events, activities, practices, and discussions related to the Campus as an EHL 
site. The focus group interviews involved a total of 26 interviewees, who were 
divided into eight groups, each consisting of three to four people with a similar 
professional background or expertise and/or working in the same field. Each group 
was interviewed twice, with approximately one year between the interviews. In 
a few groups, some respondents changed during the study. The site manager was 
included in three groups. 
The focus groups were formed based on my own view of the key stakeholders of 
the campus as an EHL site and a snowball method, asking each group for their sug-
gestions for the next interviewees. The most common suggestions were organised 
into groups that broadly represented the university community at Seminaarinmäki 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
188 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Campus and the city of Jyväskylä. Most of the interviewees worked in professional 
and/or managerial positions in their units or organisations or in various commit -
tees and thus had the ability, competence, and power to influence the development 
of the campus as an EHL site. The focus groups were (1) the Finnish delegation 
that received the award at the EHL ceremony in Brussels, including the site man -
ager, the chair of the university’s Cultural Committee, and the representative of 
the National Heritage Agency in Finland; (2) the staff of the university museum; 
(3) the experts working on the Science for All project; (4) the representatives of the 
university’s Campus Development Committee, which is responsible for develop -
ing the university’s campuses according to the university’s strategy, strengthening 
their attractiveness, and promoting community on them; (5) the representatives of 
the Faculty of Education and the University School; (6) the representatives of the 
city’s urban planners and developers; (7) the representatives of the city’s muse -
ums and third sector cultural centres; and (8) the representatives of the university’s 
communication and the city’s communication and tourism units. All interviews 
focused on the development of the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an EHL site, the 
role of the interviewees in this development, and the opportunities, challenges, and 
key actors in this development. In addition to the transcriptions, the data includes 
my interpretative notes made after each interview. 
The data collection was inevitably influenced by my position as a researcher 
affiliated with the University of Jyväskylä and as a member of the academic com-
munity working on the campus on a daily basis. While such a position provides me 
with a unique insider’s view of the research topic, it also requires special ethical 
considerations and reflexivity. In the study, the autoethnographic diary acted as a 
platform to critically reflect on my observations and interpretations. 
The interview data was examined using qualitative thematic analysis, which 
began by coding the instances where participation and its governance were ad-
dressed. The coded instances were grouped into four main themes – communi -
ties and stakeholders; participation practices; responsibilities in participation; and 
resources and means of coordinating participation – and reflected on through the 
interpretive notes and the autoethnographic diary. Based on the thematic analysis, 
the opportunities and challenges of participatory governance were identified. 
Participating in the cultural heritage of the Seminaarinmäki 
Campus 
A participatory approach to the development of the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an 
EHL site was initiated in the application phase when the University Museum, as 
the coordinator of the application process, invited several stakeholders representing 
eight university units or managerial groups, the city’s urban development project, 
and a third sector cultural centre located on the campus to participate in the prepara-
tion of the EHL proposal. According to their expertise and interests, the stakeholders 
registered on an online platform in thematic discussion groups to brainstorm the con-
tent of the proposal according to its six main thematic sections: European Value, 
Youth, Awareness, Access, Networking, and Sustainable Development. Due to the 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Opportunities and Challenges of Participatory Heritage Governance 189 
COVID-19 pandemic and lockdown starting in March 2020, the work of the brain-
storming groups was mainly carried out online. Based on the collaborative work in 
the groups, the University Museum staff prepared and finalised the proposal, guided 
by the comments of several experts involved in the preparation process and the mem-
bers of the Finnish EHL Steering Committee, coordinated by the National Heritage 
Agency. Although the participatory preparation process was bottom-up, it was still 
part of top-down governance, as the EHL Steering Committee expected applicants to 
follow such a process in order to be selected as an EHL candidate in the national pre-
selection. After the Seminaarinmäki Campus was awarded the EHL, the process was 
introduced as a good example of participatory proposal preparation in the National 
Heritage Agency’s webinars for future Finnish applicants. 
The EHL proposal requires applicants to describe, among other things, the op -
erational capacity and organisational structure of the site. However, the proposal 
does not require applicants to plan how cultural heritage processes will be gov -
erned locally. This may have an impact on the implementation of the participatory 
approach at the EHL sites, as the Seminaarinmäki Campus case study shows. 
The EHL award has generated enormous enthusiasm at the Seminaarinmäki 
Campus. The first focus group interviews revealed eagerness, pride, and high ex-
pectations about how the label could be used on the campus and in the city. All 
focus groups identified various opportunities that the label has created for their 
organisation or work. The interviewee from the National Heritage Agency was 
pleased, even relieved, that the stakeholders involved in preparing the proposal 
were self-directed and eager to take the initiative from the bottom-up. The inter -
view data show that the first year of data collection can be described as an EHL 
honeymoon, characterised by the focus groups’ optimistic visions for the future. In 
the second year, the honeymoon moved into daily life, bringing with it the realism 
of the label: opportunities do not materialise automatically but require work – and 
participation in it. 
Communities and stakeholders 
The thematic analysis revealed different conceptions of the community whose 
cultural heritage the Seminaarinmäki Campus is. These notions reflect the posi -
tions of the interviewees and show how the focus groups tried to identify the Sem-
inaarinmäki Campus as their heritage. The focus groups outside the university 
generally emphasised the residents of the city as the community that shares the 
heritage of the site, which is located close to the city centre and shapes the history 
and cultural environment of the city. Focus groups within the university tended 
to emphasise academics and students as the key community of the site. Although 
the EHL was awarded to the Seminaarinmäki Campus as a place that promotes 
equality in education and is fundamental to the development of the Finnish school 
system, children, young people, or the university school were rarely seen as the 
key heritage community of the site. Children and young people were the target 
group of the Science for All project, but their agency was limited to participation 
in its activities. 

 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
190 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Neither the EHL application nor the selection report identifies a community 
whose heritage the Seminaarinmäki Campus represents. Instead of communities, 
the ethos of the action emphasises social practice: communication about, access 
to, and activities on the site. This practice was also frequently mentioned in the 
focus group interviews. All interviewees recognised that the heritage of the Semi -
naarinmäki Campus is related to various stakeholders whose participation in the 
development of the campus as an EHL site has been encouraged. In addition to this 
wealth of identified stakeholders, the interviewees agreed on the key actors whose 
involvement in the development was considered crucial: the university management 
and the city management (Figure 11.1). The data also show how the non-university 
interviewees identified key actors more broadly from city departments and cultural 
organisations, while the university interviewees identified key actors more narrowly 
within their own organisation. 
The data show that the academic, cultural, and managerial actors inter -
viewed knew each other quite well but did not engage in joint activities to 
develop the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an EHL site. In addition, the university 
groups and the groups representing the city and its organisations formed their 
own networks with surprisingly few intersections despite their close physical 
proximity. 
Figure 11.1 The focus groups and the key actors and stakeholders of the Seminaarinmäki 
Campus, identified by the groups. University groups and actors are shown in 
grey, non-university groups and actors in white, and mixed in grey and white. 
Source: Tuuli Lähdesmäki. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Opportunities and Challenges of Participatory Heritage Governance 191 
Participation practices 
The EHL award did not bring any new resources to the University Museum but 
required it to undertake various communication and education activities. These 
activities included the Science for All project, which brought together experts 
to carry out various outreach tasks. Between 2022 and 2023, 8,000 children and 
young people participated in various types of workshops and events organised 
on the campus (EHL Monitoring Report 2024). Among the interviewees, the ex-
perts working on the project had the clearest idea of their participation in the 
development of the campus as an EHL site. This observation can be interpreted 
as follows: If a certain type of stakeholder participation is regulated by policy 
and management, it will take place and is recognised by these stakeholders as 
participation. 
Another actor who explicitly identified their action as participation in the de -
velopment of the EHL site was the interviewee from the third sector cultural cen -
tre, which had planned, for example, dramatised campus tours and applied for 
external funding to implement its activities. The driving force behind their partici-
pation was the potential of the label to provide new work opportunities for artists 
who do not have a steady income. The third sector actor had ambitious plans for 
developing cooperation between the cultural actors on the campus and in the city – 
and perceived their position in such planning as odd since they were the only actor 
without stable funding. 
The interviews with the representatives of the university’s Campus Develop-
ment Committee and Culture Committee revealed that the EHL had been dis -
cussed in various university working groups and steering committees whose 
interests were related to cultural heritage. In particular, the chairperson of the 
Cultural Committee described various networks in whose meetings the im -
portance of developing the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an EHL site had been 
stressed. The discussions in the meetings did not necessarily lead to concrete 
activities. Such discussions can be interpreted as “participation in meeting min -
utes”, where the topic is brought up and kept in the management discussions 
without further decisions on practice. 
When asked about their involvement in the development of the Seminaarin -
mäki Campus as an EHL site, all focus groups discussed their day-to-day work 
and current issues in their units or organisations. For example, the Campus 
Development Committee wanted to talk about the university’s strategic work 
and branding; the representatives of the city’s urban planners and developers 
presented plans for revitalising the city centre; and the representatives of the 
city’s museums envisioned broader cooperation within the museum sector and 
discussed how the Alvar Aalto Foundation’s proposal to include Alvar Aalto’s 
architecture on the UNESCO World Heritage List might affect their work. In 
the interpretive interview notes, my first impression of these discussions was 
often that the interviewees were not really talking about participation in heritage 
or the development of the EHL site. For example, after the interview with the 

 
 
192 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
representatives of the Faculty of Education and the University School, I wrote in 
my notes (12 May 2023): 
This group remained the least focused on the EHL, and it was obviously dif-
ficult for them to think about the label itself and what it meant. The discus-
sion generally continued to focus on the different activities they have in their 
units, what kind of research on education is being done or taught, or how 
equality issues are currently being promoted in school. Similarly, their future 
visions and challenges emphasised the visions and challenges of their units 
in general, not really the challenges of developing the EHL, although I tried 
to emphasise this. 
Later in my autoethnographic diary, I began to reflect more critically on my 
interpretations of the interviews and to consider how my position as a heritage 
researcher influenced my expectations of stakeholder participation: What does 
participation in heritage mean in general and specifically in the case of Semi-
naarinmäki Campus and its intangible heritage of equality in education? Since the 
stakeholders were expected to participate in the development from the bottom-up 
and their participation was not coordinated by anyone, each stakeholder had their 
own idea of heritage participation and saw their current everyday work as such. 
Responsibilities in participation 
The focus group interviews revealed the stakeholders’ desire for clearer and more 
structured governance of the participation in and development of the Seminaarin -
mäki Campus as an EHL site. This wish was also shared by the staff of the Uni -
versity Museum, who wanted the University management to be explicitly involved 
in the development of the site, and by the representative of the National Heritage 
Agency, who expected the European Commission to provide clearer guidance for 
the EHL action. For the Commission, however, the EHL is based on the bottom-
up, self-initiated implementation of the action by the awarded sites ( Lähdesmäki 
et al. 2020). The museum staff expected similar self-initiated participation from the 
Seminaarinmäki Campus stakeholders and were dissatisfied with the passivity of 
some stakeholders. 
The discussions about the key stakeholders revealed that the interviewees had a 
common understanding of the responsibilities for the development of the campus 
as an EHL site. When asked to identify the stakeholders who should be involved 
in such development, all focus groups identified various actors, but with the ex-
ception of the site manager, they rarely identified themselves. This illustrates the 
challenge of self-initiated development: Stakeholders expected someone else to 
take the initiative and were unwilling to act without top-down coordination. During 
the second year of data collection, some of the interviewees outside the university 
even expressed frustration at the lack of coordination. They expected someone to 
invite them to participate in joint activities where the development of the site could 
be planned together. The desire for coordination does not necessarily indicate an 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Opportunities and Challenges of Participatory Heritage Governance 193 
inability to participate on one’s own initiative, but rather shows respect for the ac-
tors whose task the coordination is expected to be – they were not run over. 
As Figure 11.1 shows, the interviewees frequently noted that the highest au -
thorities, “the university” and “the city”, should take more responsibility for 
development. Many of the focus groups saw their interests and efforts in develop -
ment as challenging without explicit support from the leaders of the university 
and the city. This highlights the impact of managerial hierarchy on participation. 
For example, the representatives of the Faculty of Education and the University 
School noted the following about the development of the EHL in Seminaarinmäki 
(Focus Group 5, 6 May 2024): 
Interviewee 1: Well, I think that – because this [the university] is a hierarchi-
cal system anyway – if the highest authority starts to promote it, then things 
will inevitably happen. 
Interviewee 2: Someone has to lead it if we want it to move forward. (…) It 
is clear that it will not move forward if no one is actively shepherding it. If 
it’s just somebody’s job among a million other jobs, how well can anybody 
shepherd it? The head of the university should in fact clearly provide re -
sources for someone to take it on and see to it that it moves forward. 
One of the focus groups, the Campus Development Committee, included the 
managerial leaders of the university. Their view of responsibilities differed from 
that of the other groups in that they emphasised the academic community as re -
sponsible for the development of the campus as an EHL site and welcomed their 
self-orientation in the development process. They expected everyone in the aca -
demic community to participate in the implementation of the EHL action but iden-
tified the site manager as responsible for coordinating the implementation. 
Resources and means to coordinate participation 
Widening access, engaging citizens, audience and community work, educational 
activities, and participatory development of heritage sites require both human and 
economic resources and means of coordinating such participation. The EHL award 
does not include any new funding for the sites, so it is expected that all their activi-
ties will be carried out with existing resources, unless the sites are able to attract 
new funding independently from different sources. 
There was little discussion of resources in the focus groups during the first year. 
The only interviewees who raised the issue and emphasised its crucial role were the 
representatives of the city museums and the third sector cultural centre. As experts 
in building exhibitions, preserving heritage, communicating its significance, and 
working with audiences and communities (all on limited budgets), they highlighted 
the contradiction between the ambitious visions for the development of the site 
and the lack of resources to carry out such work. 
The EHL award brought a number of new roles and responsibilities to the Uni-
versity Museum, including the Museum Director, who acted as a site manager and 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
194 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
intermediary between the various stakeholders. The tasks and duties were shared 
among the museum staff, with no one being the sole “official coordinator”. After 
the “EHL honeymoon”, the site manager and the representative of the National 
Heritage Agency were concerned about the workload and the implementation of 
the required tasks, noting that these issues were “stressful” or even “terrifying” 
(Focus Group 1, 11 August 2023). During the second year, the staff spoke more 
openly about the need and lack of resources required to implement the EHL action. 
During the second year, many other focus groups also noted the need for re -
sources to realise the opportunities that the EHL could bring to the Seminaarinmäki 
Campus. Closely related to human and economic resources, respondents mentioned, 
for example, the lack of time, the workload of current tasks, and the lack of com -
munication as obstacles to their participation in the development of the site. The 
only focus group that did not see the need for additional resources was the Campus 
Development Committee. 
Perhaps due to the lack of resources, the focus groups discussed less about the 
means to generate and support participation in the development of the Seminaarin-
mäki Campus. Soon after receiving the award, the Finnish delegation and the staff 
of the University Museum considered good ways to bring stakeholders together 
and to launch the EHL action on the Seminaarinmäki Campus. These interviewees 
suggested setting up a working group or steering group to brainstorm and plan the 
next steps. As one of the interviewees noted (Focus Group 2, 2 June 2022): 
But in a way that would require some sort of managerial organisation. I don’t 
suggest building a stiff bureaucracy, but setting up a kind of steering group to 
think about what we can get out of this, including research and teaching. We 
need to think about this with the different stakeholders. 
However, such kick-off meetings did not take place, and networking continued 
in a less coordinated way through smaller meetings. This practice seemed to cause 
dissatisfaction in most of the focus groups. In particular, the groups outside the 
university expressed a desire for joint planning meetings, working groups with rep-
resentatives of key stakeholders, better communication, and structured coordina -
tion. At the same time, the University Museum staff and the Science for All project 
representatives wished for more self-initiated, active participation by stakeholders, 
including the university community. This stalemate in participation in the develop-
ment of the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an EHL site continued throughout the data 
collection period. The similar concerns and wishes expressed in the first interview 
of the focus group were usually repeated in the second one. 
Participatory heritage governance: Opportunities and challenges 
The Science for All project brought local citizens, especially children and young 
people, to the campus and enabled them to engage with research as workshop 
participants and co-researchers in citizen science projects planned and coordi -
nated by scholars, teachers, and students at the University of Jyväskylä. The idea 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Opportunities and Challenges of Participatory Heritage Governance 195 
of equality of education was nurtured on the campus from the bottom-up through 
the daily work of several scholars and teachers – whether or not it was identified 
as work to promote the intangible and living heritage of the campus. The third 
sector cultural centre enlivened several events on the campus through guided 
tours and performances dramatising the history of the campus. Since participa-
tion in the development of the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an EHL site was not 
managed top-down, each stakeholder practised such development based on their 
own interests and ongoing activities. 
The focus group interviews suggest that stakeholders saw a lot of potential in 
the EHL. The groups envisioned how the label could strengthen the identity of the 
university and its academic community, attract new students and scholars, bring 
tourists to the campus, enliven the city for local residents and visitors, brand the 
city and the university, and increase their national and international prominence. 
This potential was seen to be based on the cooperation and joint work of various 
actors and stakeholders within and outside the university. After the EHL honey-
moon, these opportunities gradually turned into challenges, as the development of 
the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an EHL site lacked explicit common goals, jointly 
agreed modes of operation, and participatory governance of the development pro -
cess based on participatory methods. There are several toolkits and guidelines 
for participatory methods in general (e.g. Sloccum 2003; VSO 2004) as well as 
tailored for heritage practitioners ( György et al. 2019), including the European 
Commission’s (2018) report on the Participatory Governance of Cultural Heritage. 
However, the Commission has not provided EHL site managers with toolkits or 
guidelines to help them manage participation at the grassroots level. 
The study of the Seminaarinmäki Campus shows that participation in the herit-
age sector requires participatory governance based on planned coordination, fa -
cilitation, and participatory methods. If heritage governance lacks these elements, 
the concept of participation becomes vague. As indicated above, such governance 
is also desired by the public/stakeholders for various reasons (for a similar result, 
see Pitarch-Garrido, Alba-Pagan, and Requena-Jiménez 2025). First, coordination 
and facilitation can provide a clear framework for participation and thus facilitate 
it, especially if the public or stakeholders do not consider themselves competent 
enough in heritage matters or do not want to take the initiative or responsibility in 
heritage processes. Second, top-down coordination can facilitate participation in a 
context where the public and stakeholders have adapted to a particular governance 
culture, respecting each actor’s turf and avoiding stepping on anyone’s toes. 
One of the key challenges in developing the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an 
EHL site is based on the action’s commitment to promoting intangible heritage at 
a tangible heritage site – in the case of Seminaarinmäki, promoting the heritage of 
equality of education at the campus consisting of protected built heritage, including 
the iconic Alvar Aalto buildings. As the heritage on the campus is diverse, differ -
ent stakeholders had different views on what heritage the EHL action should or 
should not promote. Some stakeholders were also hesitant in their views because 
they did not know what the focus of the EHL action was on the campus. Such 
hesitation influenced their views on participation in the care and promotion of the 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
196 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Seminaarinmäki Campus as an EHL site: Top-down facilitation of participation 
would have specified what is the heritage that stakeholders are expected to care for 
and promote. 
The main challenge for participation and participatory heritage governance on 
the Seminaarinmäki Campus was the lack of resources. After the “EHL honey -
moon”, the lack of resources was recognised by most of the focus groups. Re-
search, toolkits, and guidelines on participatory heritage governance highlight 
insufficient funding – and the lack of other resources that depend on it, such as 
human resources with appropriate qualifications and competencies and the abil -
ity to devote time to facilitating participation – as practical obstacles and barriers 
to participatory heritage governance (EC 2018; Li 2017; see also Osmani 2008 
for participatory governance in general). Indeed, participatory heritage governance 
requires adequate and wisely allocated resources, not only for heritage managers 
and facilitators of participation but also for participants and communities and their 
activities in heritage care and promotion. Participatory governance needs to be 
recognised as a task that requires time, commitment, and specific skills. Further -
more, the European Commission’s report (EC 2018, 40) identifies process-related 
obstacles and barriers in participatory heritage governance that resonate with the 
findings of my research. The report emphasises the importance of “creating trans-
parency for all parts of the project and explaining clearly: (i) what the actual goal 
is; (ii) why the project was initiated; and (iii) who has the decision-making power 
at each step in the process”. Such a shared understanding of the common goal was 
lacking among the Seminaarinmäki Campus stakeholders. 
Conclusions 
Over the past decade, the EU has sought to promote a participatory approach to 
cultural heritage through its heritage policies and actions, such as the EHL. De -
spite the participatory emphasis in EU policy discourses, managers and heritage 
professionals working at EHL sites are not provided with further guidance or tools 
to implement such an approach in practice. This study suggests that guidelines are 
urgently needed – otherwise participatory heritage governance may remain token-
istic, failing to move from a rhetorical to a practical level. 
The study of the Seminaarinmäki Campus as an EHL site revealed that vari-
ous stakeholders beyond heritage professionals are interested in participating in 
the care and promotion of cultural heritage that they consider meaningful. The 
focus group interviews provided a broad understanding of heritage participation: 
Many of the interviewees understood their daily work at the campus or in the 
city’s institutions as such participation. Such an understanding expands or blurs 
previous notions of heritage participation discussed in the research literature (see 
Lähdesmäki and Turunen 2025). The data highlight that heritage participation can 
take different forms, which are difficult to fit into existing models or categori -
sations of participation. This is particularly the case for intangible heritage, as 
the case of Seminaarinmäki and its focus on equality of educational shows. Such 
an understanding of heritage participation also expands and blurs the notion of 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
Opportunities and Challenges of Participatory Heritage Governance 197 
community, making it difficult to define its boundaries. This study thus highlights 
the rhetorical function of the notion of community in heritage discourses, noting 
that it may have little resonance outside its discursive sphere. In addition, the 
study highlights various challenges to heritage participation caused by internal -
ised managerial hierarchy in the involved organisations, different conceptions of 
heritage to be promoted, and lack of coordination and resources for participatory 
heritage management. 
The study highlights tensions that may be inherent in participatory heritage 
governance: Heritage managers want to create space for bottom-up participation 
without “too much” top-down management, while stakeholders may expect more 
top-down management from heritage managers and therefore be reluctant to take 
action on their own initiative. This underlines the key aspect of participatory 
heritage governance: It requires competent bodies to govern through planned 
coordination, facilitation, and participatory methods and adequate resources in 
terms of staff and funding. 
The European Commission emphasises participatory governance in its heritage 
policies and initiatives. In the EHL action, the Commission and the EHL Bureau 
provide networks and facilitate events for EHL site managers and encourage col -
laboration between EHL sites. The results of this study indicate that the EHL action 
should be developed to better support the implementation of participatory heritage 
governance at the grassroots level, in order to strengthen stakeholder and commu-
nity participation in cultural heritage at EHL sites.
