DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-16 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
12 Engaging communities of practice 
in cultural heritage 
A multi-site study of private partners 
within French cultural ecosystems 
Renaud Garcia-Bardidia, Pénélope Girard, 
and Mathieu Viau-Courville 
Private partners – designers, artists, freelance consultants, makers, contractors, and 
creative businesses, including start-ups, think tanks, and architectural firms – play 
a crucial role in the cultural sector by providing services and support. Their in -
creasing presence in museums, working closely with museum staff on a range of 
projects, including exhibition development, education programmes, and the design 
of user-centred digital resources and tools, underscores the need to understand how 
these professional cooperations shape today’s museum landscape and its ties to the 
for-profit sector. 
Public-private partnerships (PPPs) have increased exponentially in recent 
years, particularly in countries with higher proportions of freelancers and inde -
pendent start-ups in their culture and heritage ( EIF 2021; Marinier 2023). A trend 
has also arisen in corporate partners being more engaged in content or programme 
co-development (Rius-Ulldemolins 2015). Despite the growing role of the private 
sector in cultural heritage and the increasing calls for museums to become driving 
forces behind dynamic and responsible cultural ecosystems, a significant research  
gap exists in assessing public-private relationships. 
This chapter aims to contribute to understanding how cultural organisations 
operate with private partners, seen as a critical factor in defining their ecosystem  
(Jung 2022). We present the preliminary findings of a multi-site research project  
with the Jardin de l’Arquebuse in Dijon and the Museum-Aquarium in Nancy 
(both in France) that examined how private partners contribute to the develop -
ment of cultural organisations. In particular, we highlight some of the findings  
that reveal how museums in this study have the potential to be active agents in 
shaping communities of practice, enabling both new forms of visitor engagement 
and the establishment of healthy partnerships within local cultural ecosystems. 
In the following sections, we maintain that museums in this study proved mean -
ingful places that contribute to (i) facilitating the production of creative chains, 
(ii) stabilising local professional practices, and (iii) acting as stepping stones for 
local creators and industries. 
After reviewing definitions and divergent understandings of public-private re -
lationships in culture and heritage, we present the project methodology, which in -
cluded fieldwork and qualitative interviews, and discuss our findings. Our research 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
   
 
 
 
 
Engaging Communities of Practice in Cultural Heritage 201 
revealed that while private partners are often at the heart of day-to-day operations, 
their contributions also raise new challenges that shake up the traditional museum 
management model. This is especially true when private partners are called to ex-
periment with new practices that challenge established contractualisation practices 
in the public sector. This limits the ability of partners to collaborate fully within 
each ecosystem. Nevertheless, the research also revealed the human affinities that 
help sustain and facilitate public-private relations, especially when service provid-
ers are smaller, less established in the marketplace, and more familiar with the cul-
tural and heritage sectors. In such cases, the result of a successful partnership is the 
creation of singular, innovative cultural venues. The skills acquired on both sides 
then contribute to the local stabilisation of professional practices and the resilience 
of cultural ecosystems. 
Public-private partnerships in cultural ecosystems 
A growing wealth of scholarly literature on public-private relations in culture and 
heritage encompasses various arguments about their potential benefits and pit -
falls. While there is no uniform definition of a PPP, its basic concept describes 
a contractual relationship between the public and private sectors and resources 
concluded to provide public services or services of general interest ( European 
Commission 2003 ; Witters, Marom, and Steinert 2012 ). The term has gained 
prominence in recent years, and several variations of its definition have been pro -
posed, depending on context and institution. Governments and leading organisa-
tions have established PPP laws and dedicated PPP units to sustain a successful 
pipeline of PPP projects over time, emphasising what is mutually beneficial for 
the private and public entities involved, particularly regarding accountability and 
shared risks. For instance, the OECD defines PPPs as “long-term agreements be -
tween the government and a private partner whereby the private partner delivers 
and funds public services using a capital asset, sharing the associated risks. PPPs 
may deliver public services both with regards to infrastructure assets (such as 
bridges, roads) and social assets (such as hospitals, utilities, prisons)” ( OECD 
2024). In the USA, the National Council for Public-Private Partnerships describes 
PPPs as a “contractual agreement between a public sector agency and a for-profit 
private sector concern, whereby resources and risks are shared for the purpose 
of delivering a public service or development of public infrastructure” ( Li and 
Akyntoye 2003, 3). In all definitions, the goal or set of goals remains somewhat 
similar: PPPs aim to leverage the private sector’s innovation and efficiency to 
enhance the delivery of quality, affordability, and public service accountability. 
In our study, we defined a PPP following the latter USA definition, emphasising 
the links between a public organisation – a museum or cultural cluster – and a 
for-profit private partner, whether an individual contractor or a start-up providing 
services to the cultural sector. 
Debates are still ongoing, and the subject of the public sector working in tan -
dem with the for-profit world is not readily accepted. While some will embrace 
a new era of creativity, innovation, and inter-sector cross-fertilisation, others 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
202 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
consider PPPs more problematic. The debates are not new. The advent of New 
Public Management theory ( Michael and Kaye 1997 ) in the cultural sector in 
the 1980s raised several concerns among heritage and cultural professional and 
academic circles ( Tobelem 1997). Particular concern was raised over neoliberal 
models in culture, which promote successful economic development through mar-
ket liberalisation and freedom for both the private sector and individuals but can 
also lead to a loss of power and a greater chance of externalising core museum 
functions ( Janes 2007). McLean (1997)  highlighted several potential challenges 
for museums should expert-driven organisations give in to consumer/user-driven 
models, which she saw as potentially catastrophic for museums, as they could see 
their activities wholly paralysed. Tlili (2016) saw a betrayal of the hegemony that 
museums have held in the high arts, where economic values are perceived as out -
weighing intrinsic cultural values. There have also been concerns about museums 
becoming part of a 
generalised arena of cultural and creative industries that are as much focused 
on entertainment as they are on their collections; a growing fear that contents 
and buildings can be utilised for multiple purposes that extend beyond the 
idea of “core” and “intrinsic” functions. 
(Gray 2015, 6) 
Others were more receptive to the idea and the potential benefits of relying on 
marketing methods and practices to enhance visitor- and community-driven ini -
tiatives. Mottner and Ford (2005) argued that healthy internal competition could 
increase museum performance. Norris and Tisdale (2014) maintained that muse -
ums gain greater political clout when positioned within the creative industries. Of 
course, it has become increasingly common for museums to devise complex mar -
keting strategies and profit from partnering with industry over the years. Prokůpeka, 
Betzelrb, and Lootsc (2022, 6–7) list recent cases of museums engaging with private 
partners to capitalise on their collections and earn an income through licensing fees. 
Their listed examples include the Swiss brand Swatch, which created watch designs 
based on artworks from the Amsterdam Rijksmuseum collection; the Louvre and 
the Van Gogh Museum, which collaborated with fashion brands to create clothing 
designs inspired by their collections to appeal to younger audiences; and the Rijks-
museum which decided to digitise artefacts and exhibits for free, even for commer-
cial use, in hopes their open access initiative would attract new donors and sponsors. 
Indeed, art museums have developed particularly effective strategies over the years 
to generate new revenue streams through sponsorships, donations, and commercial 
activities, such as venue hire. 
Whether for or against private partners in cultural heritage, they are now ubiq -
uitous, their expertise recognised through professional networks and associations, 
and their contributions are often fundamental to the heritage sector – albeit the sub-
ject remains an unspoken reality. Consider, for instance, built heritage, where cul-
tural heritage properties often contradict received ideas that only the public sector 

 
  
 
 
 
Engaging Communities of Practice in Cultural Heritage 203 
can safeguard and maintain cultural heritage. This point was recently argued by 
Abdoul (2021, 57), who also maintains that 
Although heritage assets are conceptualised as public or merit goods in many 
cases, the private sector not only plays an important role in financial support, 
in-kind donations, and volunteers but also a significant part of heritage prop-
erties remains in private hands. 
However, a few clarifications are necessary. First, public-private relations 
should not be conflated with privatisation. Understanding this distinction is crucial 
for navigating the complexities of the heritage and museum sector, particularly in 
terms of accountability and ownership. In a PPP, the public sector retains primary 
responsibility for delivering and ensuring the quality of the public service, whereas 
in privatisation, that responsibility shifts to the private sector (although the public 
sector may still regulate pricing). Ownership does not change hands with PPPs, and 
the public sector remains accountable (United Nations Economic Commission for 
Europe (UNECE) 2008, 4). To secure more projects from the creative industries, 
museums are developing new policies and “how-to” guidance documents to fa-
cilitate successful collaboration between museums and private creative industries, 
while ensuring the highest ethical standards regarding accountability and owner -
ship. These guidance sheets are designed to simplify collaboration between muse-
ums and creators or creative businesses, from initial idea development and partner 
search to relationship building and navigating practical and contractual challenges. 
Second, any hostility towards private partners in heritage often results from con-
flicting allegiances, particularly in France. A frequent comment among museum 
circles, especially academic ones, is that the ultimate goals of private partners are 
profit-driven and, thus, incompatible with those of nonprofits and the ideal of pub-
lic service. Private partners’ interests are not always exclusively profit-driven; they 
can be geared towards other dynamics, such as innovation or sustainability, which 
can be beneficial in establishing creative museum dynamics and responsible cul-
tural ecosystems. Moreover, in countries where the numbers of freelance creators 
and creative start-ups are rising sharply, while their contractual practices may be 
profit-driven to survive as independent creators and businesses, their primary inter-
est often lies in developing and specialising in particular creative sub-fields – e.g. 
science-society relations, cultural mediation, education, research, and so on.
1 In-
dependent creative businesses and freelancers also continuously develop new hard 
and soft skills and knowledge, accumulating considerable business acumen, which 
would be almost naive to disregard solely because of their private, for-profit status. 
Third, most museum sectors in Europe still heavily depend on and fight for 
government support to remain faithful to their responsibilities of adequately fund-
ing culture, ensuring it does not succumb to external pressures. In France, this is 
crucial to maintaining the cultural sector’s scientific and intellectual rigour and 
independence. Therefore, one also needs to distinguish clearly between arguments 
against private partners in culture and arguments about the role of government in 
supporting culture, the latter having little to do with PPPs. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
204 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Reconciling the influence of the marketplace ethos with conventional museum 
interests, such as public education or collection development, remains a vividly de-
bated topic in the heritage field (Gray 2014; Griffin 2008; Janes 2007). Attempts to 
reposition museum leadership towards cultural management models to boost the 
institution’s openness and attractiveness to increase visitor attendance and generate 
new revenue streams have been met with significant resistance, with documented 
clashes between senior curators and cultural managers in France, New Zealand, and 
other countries have led to cases being brought to court to revert such decisions 
(Viau-Courville 2021, 333). Nevertheless, although grounded in managerial rheto-
ric (Zan 2006), new museum organisational management structures and organic/ 
distributed curatorial models can also help institutions fulfil value-driven missions 
towards human rights, social justice and museum activism by leveraging commu -
nity engagement and participation and giving a voice to underrepresented communi-
ties (Janes and Sandell 2019; see also Viau-Courville in this volume). 
Public-private cooperations in cultural heritage can form highly effective and 
creative teams when adequately managed and framed in clear ethical guidelines. 
Significantly, we maintain that they can form highly effective trans-institutional and 
inter-professional communities of practice based on mutual trust and recognition 
of each partner’s know-how and skills – generating new forms of engagement and 
joint creative endeavours (Herne 2006). For this, PPPs depend mainly on the power 
structure within which organisational relationships are formed, and understanding 
the interplay between public organisations and private partners can yield relevant 
insight into how museums deal with a calculated risk by entrusting something new 
or unknown (Luhman 2017). This implies a working relationship between private 
and public partners as a form of an enacted power relationship, which can be asym-
metric and symmetric (Köster 2003). Asymmetric power relationships are prevalent 
in the business and industry sectors, with suppliers heavily reliant on manufacturers. 
Conversely, symmetric relationships are those in which power is equally distributed, 
as in partnerships. The latter implies creating communities of practice in which crea-
tive power is equated with sharing expertise rather than retaining it. Accordingly, in 
our study, we understood communities of practice following Wegner et al.’s defini-
tion: “Groups of people who share a concern, a set of problems, or a passion about 
a topic, and who deepen their knowledge and expertise in this area by interacting on 
an ongoing basis” (Wenger, McDermott, and Snyder 2002, 15). 
Underpinned by the PPP model and partnership-building dynamics in culture, the 
DYNECO-ICC project sought to map the collaboration practices between cultural 
organisations and their private partners, understand how they form dynamic com -
munities of practice within their respective cultural and creative ecosystems, and 
produce a qualitative assessment of each partner’s perception of the creative process. 
Through a creative ecosystem, we understood a circular feedback system grounded 
in ecological perspectives where “individuals, human societies, and nature are inter-
connected and interdependent through communicative networks” (Jung 2011, 321). 
Accordingly, we sought to explore how our study’s institutions and their staff were 
embedded in a more extensive network of private creators and institutional partners, 
interacting, co-creating, sharing knowledge, and influencing one another. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Engaging Communities of Practice in Cultural Heritage 205 
Methodology 
We relied on a case study methodology and qualitative research design (Dumez 
2016). Two sites in France were selected: the Nancy Museum-Aquarium and the 
Jardin de l’Arquebuse in Dijon. Both are major multi-site institutions. The for -
mer features a natural history museum, botanical gardens, and a tropical aquarium, 
while the latter comprises a planetarium, a natural history museum, and a botanical 
garden. The sites were selected based on a set of predefined criteria: (i) they were 
part of ongoing research and monitoring activities already carried out by OCIM
2 
and CREGO; (ii) they were known for their community engagement practices on 
different levels within their ecosystems; (iii) they maintained long-standing part -
nerships with private partners; (iv) they applied methods and practices to co-create 
projects with private partners, be they formal or informal practices. We analysed 
each site separately before conducting a comparative analysis and producing a vis-
ual mapping of each site’s public-private cooperation networks. 
Semi-structured interviews (n=10) were conducted at each site between Febru-
ary and September 2023 – five in Dijon with museum leadership and private ser -
vice providers, five in Nancy with management and municipal partners. The first 
set of interviews with each institution’s leadership allowed us to assess workflows 
and internal working practices. The second set of interviews was conducted with 
two professional groups: internal museum staff and external private partners. Rely-
ing on sociological approaches to cultural organisations, the interviews primarily 
focused on project development with external partners – including project over -
view and development, key milestones, tools, communication processes within 
the partner ecosystem, tensions, and evaluation. The discussions aimed to gather 
insights about each partner’s perception of the established partnership and who is 
involved in and contributes to each step of the creative process. Particular attention 
was also afforded to comparing perceptions about who and what may be part of the 
creative ecosystem. To address this, questions included: Who are external service 
providers? What are creative industries, and who is part of them? What is a cultural 
ecosystem, and does this concept apply to your institution? Qualitative analyses 
based on 14 hours of interview transcripts were assessed to allow theory to emerge 
from data (Glaser and Strauss 2017). 
Findings 
Private partners within museum ecosystems 
In her research examining the links between art museums and the social actors 
they aim to serve, Yuga Jung approached museums and cultural organisations as 
open, complex systems that are social and multilayered ecosystems, intercon -
nected to their external environments – an individual or institution is part of a 
broader network or social system, which is part of a larger group or community, 
itself part of a country and, ultimately, the world ( Jung 2017; 2022). Thinking 
of an ecosystem in the museum sector involves applying management studies 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
206 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
and systems thinking to arts administration and education. This enables a better 
understanding of an institution’s governance strategy or business model in estab -
lished stakeholder relations. 
In our research, the perceived role and place of private partners in the museum 
ecosystem primarily depended on the position of the interviewed participants, with 
views from management and senior administrative positions differing from those in 
project development and execution. When examining how projects are assembled 
and who is or is not involved in the process, participants in museum leadership roles 
tended to view partners from the creative industries as more silent service provid -
ers than as contributing partners. However, they also acknowledged that these part-
ners play an essential role in day-to-day operations and institutional programming. 
In contrast, participants in project development roles – such as education officers, 
exhibition developers, event planners, and curators – viewed private partners as key 
contributors, thereby enhancing the institution’s visibility and outreach. 
The participants reacted strongly to the notion of the ecosystem, initially lean -
ing more towards describing the internal institutional dynamics. For example, one 
museum director noted, “Our ecosystem is our museum team, all colleagues with 
whom we have worked at one time or another and with whom we would be happy 
to work again.”
3 Another museum director added: “The private actors, service pro-
viders, and so on … we would not necessarily place them within our ecosystem, but 
the term “ecosystem” itself does fit to describe our scientific and cultural partners.” 
“Service providers,” they continued, “are a tool or a means to an end, available to 
help us reach our institutional objectives, e.g., valorisation, promotion, and edu -
cation.” When working with the private sector, participants in management roles 
also recognised that each partner benefits from the collaboration – meaning that 
for private partners, collaborations can lead to new revenue streams and increased 
visibility for presenting or testing new designs, tech products, or proof of concepts. 
In contrast, staff in project development roles tended to consider private col -
laborators in the creative industries as equal partners and integral to the museum 
and cultural ecosystem. As one exhibition developer explained, 
Broadly speaking, I would include those private professionals who bring 
their expertise and skills – those who can provide me with valuable support 
and insight to bring the project to a successful completion. While I provide 
our external craftsmen with a detailed contract that includes pre-established 
specifications, I also enjoy exchanging ideas with them. They will tell me, 
“You proposed this, but I suggest something else.” 
Exhibition developers tended to value the contributions of their private creative 
industry partners highly. As another put it, 
The creative industries bring in complementary competencies. We must ac -
cept and understand that our institution does not possess all the required 
knowledge and skills – museums seldom have a designer or scenographer on 
staff. Therefore, working hand in hand with private partners is an opportunity. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
Engaging Communities of Practice in Cultural Heritage 207 
Most participants described their experience working with creators and service 
providers as a co-creation process, sharing knowledge and ideas “organically” and 
relying on them to “polish all corners of the final project.” Private partners were 
viewed as helpful in addressing the challenge of utilising (and developing) digital 
tools in ways that align with the institution’s pedagogical mission. According to 
participants, such practices are also indicators that the creative industries and free-
lance creators align with the vision and mission of a public service. 
Reconciling production needs and constraints 
In our study, the partnerships and work dynamics between museums and their 
for-profit partners do not necessarily align with PPPs as defined within traditional 
political or legal frameworks – they do not involve major long-term investments 
leading to privatisation or service delegation. Instead, museums collaborate with 
a broad and diverse network of small to medium-sized private entities that are in -
volved in the museums’ day-to-day operations. As described by an events manager: 
As far as our service providers are concerned, they can be anyone, from a 
university researcher in the field of geology to a local hairdresser who par -
ticipates in [one of our community outreach projects] by offering haircuts to 
everyone. This is what I love about my work: there is a diversity of fields and 
areas we can reach out to – for exhibitions and other content-driven projects, 
we can reach out to start-ups specialising in video games or VR, whereas for 
our outreach and community projects we can work with hairdressers, danc -
ers, magicians, rock musicians, whether self-employed or members of local 
artistic organisations. 
By engaging with their external partners and collaborators, museums tap into a 
wealth of expertise and resources that are fundamental to propelling them forward 
in their mission. These work relationships also highlight established work dynam-
ics, specifically those that may shift from asymmetric to symmetric relationships 
depending on the project type. Museums’ reliance on public partners is, fundamen-
tally, an indication that they lack the necessary human resources or financial means 
to meet the growing demand for high-quality projects while maintaining rigorous 
standards for each initiative. This leads to museums’ relationships with private part-
ners almost always starting as asymmetric – and only shifting to symmetric col -
laborations once projects are underway and when the right human affinities are in 
alignment. The asymmetric reliance on external service providers is often exacer -
bated by museums’ increasing competition for funding and audiences, driven by 
the rising demand for digital and immersive experiences, which require specialised 
skills and substantial budgets for design and implementation that museums typically 
do not possess. As one director emphasised, 
[our increased dependency on] and decisions to outsource content produc -
tion stem from our expanding visions and missions, in a context where our 

 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
208 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
budgets and human resources remain stable. We need scientific competencies 
and naturalists, but we also need to engage our public and local stakeholders, 
meaning we need [cultural] mediators, people who can create exhibitions, se-
cure sustainable partnerships, manage events, all this with an understanding 
that we have an institutional commitment to knowledge sharing and develop-
ment, cultural action. 
Outsourcing is thus much about being careful not to lose control over produc -
tion design, museological content, or ethical standards regarding ownership as it 
is about managing sustainable PPPs and ensuring museum accountability. As de -
scribed by a director: 
Another solution [and a common practice] is to outsource an exhibition de -
signer who will be tasked with finding and creating innovative designs. We 
establish a clear contract, and it will be up to the designer to reach out to 
relevant subcontractors where appropriate to fulfil their mission of creating 
new, innovative exhibition experiences. As far as we are concerned, insofar 
as we maintain control over our content creation and direction for exhibi -
tions, we are happy to outsource directly. 
Participants also raised concerns about the risk of incorporating too many digital 
elements and interactive content, which could compromise the museum’s control 
over the content and lead to dependency, or “gadgetisation,” on digital and immer-
sive technologies. A compromised control over museological projects may nega -
tively impact approval from territorial authorities,
4 including local governance and 
state or municipal funding. Likewise, private partners operating in the cultural and 
creative industries are struggling with the simultaneous pressure to be innovative 
and creative while also being effective and responsive to change. It can prove chal-
lenging for the museum or its constituents to fully appreciate and evaluate the value 
of the service rendered and the time it took to experiment with and develop a new 
exhibition design or installation. This is because creativity and innovation may lead 
to incorporating too many visually complex elements or offering rich sensory ex -
periences but also compromise the users’ understanding of the museum’s scientific 
contributions or mission. 
Participants also emphasised the risk of audiences missing out on content if 
digital products are perceived as being too superficial. To prevent such situations 
from occurring, museum teams develop upfront sets of ethical guidelines and re -
quirements that align with their mission and values. Beyond ensuring that private 
partners meet deadlines and maintain the highest quality and price standards, com-
munity participation, a low carbon footprint, and encouraging the development of 
the local economy were all factors identified by our study participants highlighted 
as being strongly prioritised when signing agreements with private partners. These 
key contributing factors, participants further emphasised, underscore the role of 
private partners as integral components of the local museum ecosystem. 

 Engaging Communities of Practice in Cultural Heritage 209 
Creating meaningful museum partnerships in the public market 
In our study, respondents’ answers during interviews revealed similarities in the 
types of private partners they typically worked with, including their activities, 
size, number of employees, and place of business. An analysis of the relationships 
between museum teams and their partners also helped describe how asymmetric 
forms of power are addressed, as well as the importance of human relations in 
building projects within the public marketplace context. 
The decisions of the two museums studied here to work with private partners 
were indeed strongly influenced by public procurement regulations. In France, 
public procurement offers under €10,000 generally require receiving price quota -
tions from at least three different service providers, whereas projects under €40,000 
require a public tender process through a dedicated online platform. Participation 
involves submitting numerous project documents and adhering to several criteria, 
including accessibility and environmental sustainability. Tender applications must 
usually be submitted between two weeks and two months. 
While regulations may enable greater transparency in tender processes and help 
prevent abuses in contract allocation or pricing, they also tend to increase the ad -
ministrative burden on private service providers. This often discourages creators 
and businesses, who consider the effort spent to be equivalent to unpaid working 
time and not worthwhile, given the statistically low chance of success. Participants 
in our study also noted that local creators and businesses often contest the crite -
ria and results of public procurement decisions. They also agreed that public pro-
curement procedures limit creativity and the possibility of establishing long-term 
collaborations and learning from each other’s experiences (through a series of pro-
jects rather than just one). Another challenge is that pre-existing public contractual 
agreements leave little room for experimentation and unexpected, yet welcome, 
creative outcomes. Some participants in management and senior administrative po-
sitions reported using newer types of public procurement procedures, which allow 
for greater flexibility and the possibility of changing providers more frequently, 
thereby encouraging a wider range of local creators and businesses to participate. 
Submission times are also longer than those of traditional tenders, and museums 
and other cultural organisations can now work directly with potential providers to 
successfully prepare their applications. 
Nevertheless, asymmetry remained noticeable in terms of power and access to 
relevant information for establishing meaningful and long-term partnerships with 
the private sector, as private creators and businesses struggle to respond to public 
tenders within the allocated timeframes and adapt to ever-changing formats and 
requirements. Respondents provided details about their working relationship that 
moved beyond the linear service provider/museum framework and towards a more 
co-creative and interdependent relationship. For instance, one museum described 
working with a local graphics designer following a tender call to rebrand the mu-
seum’s visual identity. Over time, the designer became an integral part of the mu -
seum’s brand and communications strategy. In the words of the director, it was the 

 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
210 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
quality of their interaction and co-creative process that positively impacted the 
professional relation: 
We really exchanged ideas. It was not just the graphic designer proposing 
something and then you saying yes or no—that wasn’t it. When we worked 
on the project, we called each other; I would take a break, and we would 
talk for half an hour, saying, “So, what do you think?” “Yeah, wait, I’m not 
sure; how do we do this?” Which animal do we choose?’ There was a real 
discussion about the substance. […] He was invested in the project. For the 
designer, working with us was beneficial, as it provided them with a great 
deal of visibility in the city, and they fully acknowledged this. And for us, 
it was helpful because they gave the museum a pretty cool image. 
As we hypothesised at the outset of this research, respondents in project develop-
ment roles reported working increasingly in and striving towards a more symmetri-
cal, co-creative manner. They also tended to favour working with younger creators 
and businesses, which were perceived as more receptive to bringing in new ideas and 
dynamic working relationships – given that education and exhibition staff in muse-
ums also tended to comprise younger professionals. As noted by one event manager: 
We enjoy working with young people and are rarely disappointed. They are 
also less well-known and more affordable in the field. We generally find many 
positives, although at times they require more supervision or follow-up. 
The human affinities that make or break good relations between museum teams 
and their private partners can be further highlighted by examining what our study 
participants described as failed or successful partnerships. In one instance, one of 
the museums in our study recently encountered difficulties in implementing an 
educational project with a private service provider who initially appeared to be 
a good fit – the partner was a start-up with young professionals who came rec-
ommended by a local elected official. However, despite numerous meetings, their 
proposed designs and educational model for serious gaming, which were intended 
to integrate a forthcoming exhibition, did not meet expectations in terms of quality 
standards, production delays or alignment with public procurement procedures. 
However, beyond these technical issues, according to participants, what negatively 
impacted the partnership was a lack of awareness of social norms in the public 
sector and expectations surrounding public contracts. As illustrated by a director: 
One must understand that a public contract is not about becoming friends 
(…) mutual trust must be established first, especially when we are talking 
about a €60,000 operation that affects my entire budget cycle. 
Participants described the profiles of their long-term partners – those with whom 
they have worked successfully on two or more museum projects – as being more 
typically affiliated with universities or public institutions or having a history of 
collaborating with a territorial authority. As a result, they tend to be familiar with 

 
 
Engaging Communities of Practice in Cultural Heritage 211 
the operational frameworks of public organisations, and cooperation with them, 
according to our study participants, is based on a deeper mutual understanding. The 
proximity of sociological profiles and professional trajectories thus facilitates the 
personal affinities that solidify these partnerships, particularly in highly regulated 
and administratively burdensome museum sectors such as in France. 
In contrast, participants were adamant about continuing to work with a broad 
range of private partners. They acknowledged the struggles of young freelancers 
and start-ups to meet the requirements for participating in museums’ public pro -
curement offers, which are increasingly complicated by administrative rules within 
public organisations. They were also mindful of freelance designers, who are “often 
underpaid or underappreciated for their work.” For instance, designers must deliver 
increasingly faster, and participants described their concerns over “Uberisation”
5 
in the cultural sector. Working with smaller and local partners often allows us [the 
museum] to contribute to fair compensation for the services provided. Similarly, 
working with less experienced actors should allow for the “right to experimenta -
tion and mistakes,” a reality and convivial spirit that should be maintained and 
encouraged by museums. As one manager noted: 
When partners are beginners, this can sometimes prove to be somewhat cha-
otic, but we allow for such possibility. You have the right to make mistakes, 
and that is a rare and valuable opportunity to have when one is being paid 
for a service. People tell us that they feel very welcome here and that they 
are comfortable. I take pride in that because the social aspect matters to me. 
Navigating the fluid boundaries of communities of practice 
A successful partnership will lead to long-term collaborations between the museum 
and its private partners. This is especially true when private partners demonstrate a 
deep understanding of the museum’s mission and work culture and exhibit strong 
skills in creative adaptability and flexibility – particularly when faced with tight 
production deadlines. This often leads private creators and businesses to become 
regular collaborators, working together from one exhibition to the next. As one 
director noted: 
Another example of a great encounter: the makeup artists for the opera whom 
we met at our shared event—they work freelance. You talk with them for an 
hour that day, and you get along well; deep down, you know that one day you 
will work with them again for another event. 
Working again with a service provider that has become a regular partner re -
quires stepping outside the public procurement framework, which is generally pos-
sible through an artistic work clause, as long as the service cost is not too high. 
This, in turn, encourages regular experimentation. 
Regarding comments made by private respondents in our study, they were 
aware of the ecosystem of museums and cultural organisations. Respondents from 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
212 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
start-up creative businesses considered themselves part of that ecosystem, although 
they had not initially targeted museums or the cultural sector as part of their busi-
ness model. For private for-profit creators, these partnerships sometimes drive – or 
validate – their development model and area of expertise, as noted by a service 
provider who has focused on scientific and technological culture: 
What we have been focusing on for the past year is finding potential cli -
ents to sustain the business – clients who will commission educational 
programmes and content that are not only interesting for them but also 
for everyone, including students in schools, teachers, and the general pub -
lic. That is why science museums are particularly relevant to us – we’ve 
worked with Lab 71, and working with the Cité des Sciences in La Villette 
is also foreseeable in the future, and now the Natural History Museum in 
Dijon [Jardin de l’Arquebuse]. We share the same goal: to help the public 
discover new things and enable people to understand the world better. For 
[one of our clients], we designed a project on living cells, with some mod -
ules now accessible through the national education system. This is why it 
is relevant to be part of this [scientific] museum ecosystem – it also leads 
to other projects. In Dijon, we met the IT department while we were in 
the start-up accelerator. Since we already had experience with museums 
and educational content, they were immediately very interested – first for 
schools and then for a museum exhibition, with a co-creative approach to 
the partnership. 
During these co-creative projects, skills and practices are developed and 
applied. This is especially true in one of the two museums analysed, where 
service providers are often beginners. Public-private relationships are often 
operationalised by museum staff, who consider local creators and businesses 
part of their broader audience and engagement mission. Therefore, working 
with them is yet another way of demonstrating impact. As a communications 
officer noted 
With artists who are more established and accustomed to working with 
museums, they typically have a communication kit. For smaller, lesser-
established creators, things are more limited … perhaps a selfie here and 
there or a few lines to present their work … Those whom we come to 
know through increased visibility and online presence tend to secure more 
contracts. Therefore, we offer media training for younger creators. It is part 
of our mission. 
As further emphasised by a director, “To be able to explain to my policy repre-
sentatives and territorial authorities our impact, not only on our visitors but also on 
our other partners, i.e., to help them grow and evolve, to play a role in all this, and 
to encourage those who have just started, as a kind of steppingstone.” 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Engaging Communities of Practice in Cultural Heritage 213 
In our study, a tangible shift was observed in the attitudes of those in leadership 
positions regarding the role of private partners. While never spontaneously cited 
as primary partners, respondents began to unpack the increasingly complex and 
ever-present role of private creators in the museum through our conversations – 
how they enable the institution to address new issues or reach new audiences by 
co-designing new digital tools or bringing in complementary skills. It was as if 
private partners were partly in and partly out of the boundary of a museum ecosys-
tem, as a kind of comfortable ambivalence that seemed to work for everyone in -
volved. There was a kind of interdependence between the museum and its private 
partner in that both could influence and affect each other over time. For instance, 
when discussing their relationship with a local creator who designed a series of 
exhibition posters that became an essential part of the institution’s branding and 
public image, a museum director noted: 
We allowed him to change the way we would have done things and in turn, 
that also allowed him to grow [his visibility and status as a creator], which 
also over time brought in more ideas and possibilities. 
Conclusion 
An increasing number of studies have been published on the role of museums in 
fostering creativity and innovation as ethically driven and future-minded institu -
tions that promote active citizenship, participation, and engagement. The Network 
of European Museum Organizations (NEMO), the Institute for Museum and Li-
brary Services (USA), the American Alliance of Museums, and the Organisation 
for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) have all been developing 
policies and programmes aimed at bridging the gap between museums, heritage, 
and sustainability in culture. In line with such approaches, there has also been 
a growing interest in the role of cultural and creative activities in the economy 
(Mickov and Doyle 2018) and how museums and other cultural organisations pro-
mote creativity by effectively engaging creative communities and, in doing so, 
contribute to debates on creativity’s social and economic value (Montuori and 
Donnelly 2016; Norris and Tisdale 2014). This places creativity as a driver for 
economic growth in a new creative economy, marked by innovation, particularly 
“game-changing” and “disruptive” innovation (Montuori and Donnelly 2016, 745). 
The case studies discussed in this chapter showed that museums and cultural 
organisations can facilitate the creation of creative networks by bringing together 
local makers and creative businesses, encouraging them to develop innovative so-
lutions. There seems to be a logical consequence of the growing awareness that 
museums not only need to reach out to and rely on external competencies but also 
on their role as driving forces behind creative ecosystems. This is illustrated by 
roles in museums, such as exhibition developers and curators, which are progres -
sively shifting to facilitators, enabling highly efficient communities of practice. 
One exhibition developer noted, “I see my role as being an orchestra conductor 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
  
  
214 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
who brings together different competencies to achieve a common goal – creating 
an exhibition or part of an exhibition.” A museum director added, 
It is an ecosystem that we have built gradually over the years, and we must be 
mindful of the importance of maintaining it, constantly reminding ourselves 
that having an established list of contacts is never enough – the list must 
continue to grow. 
While respondents in museum leadership positions initially described asymmetric 
power relationships with their private partners, with the museum relying on an agreed-
upon service, our study revealed that museum staff are engaged in more symmetric 
relationships, where creative power is equally distributed through shared and mutually 
reinforcing skill sets. More significantly, museums and their staff remain within the 
boundaries of an ethical PPP, retaining ownership and accountability for the quality of 
the public service rendered. These results may depend on the two cases studied here. 
Mediations in Natural History Museums may for instance need more scientific part-
nerships with start-ups and being middle-size institutions located in middle-size cities 
may strongly affect power issues. Nevertheless, the context of these two institutions 
helps in return to observe more finely how homology between partners enables emerg-
ing communities of practice that strengthen the local cultural ecosystem. 
While there has been a growing body of literature on the positive impact of mu-
seums working with other sectors, including leisure (Tobelem 2018), digital culture 
(Bousson and Evrard 2013), and social work (Silverman 2010), less has been pub-
lished on the impact and benefits (or indeed challenges) of museums collaborating 
with the creative sectors (Mairesse and Rochelanet 2015). Our study aimed to bridge 
the literature gap by comprehensively reviewing museums working with the private 
for-profit sector. Despite the limited sample size, preliminary results from our re -
search revealed the human affinities that facilitate public-private relations, especially 
when service providers are smaller, less established in the marketplace, and more 
familiar with the museums and heritage sector. In such cases, the result of a success-
ful partnership creates a win-win situation – with singular, innovative cultural venues 
with growing service providers. The skills acquired on both sides contribute to the 
local stabilisation of professional practices and the resilience of cultural ecosystems.

321–42. 
Wenger, Etienne, Richard McDermott, and William Snyder. 2002. Cultivating Communities 
of Practice: A Guide to Managing Knowledge. Boston: Harvard Business Review Press. 
Witters, Louis, Revital Marom, and, Kurt Steinert. 2012. “The Role of Public-Private Partner-
ships in Driving Innovation.” In The Global Innovation Index, edited by Soumitra Dutta, 
81–87. Geneva, Switzerland: World Intellectual Property Organization, 2012
