13 Reframing knowledge in cultural 
heritage 
Promoting openness and public 
engagement in museums through 
organic management 
Mathieu Viau-Courville 
Against a landscape of rapidly-evolving societal changes, recent years have seen 
museums reconfigure and reshape their narratives, creativity, and overall organi -
sational design. This has been in response to a growing moral demand to stimulate 
dialogue on pressing societal issues, use different modes of remembrance, and pro-
mote different models for citizen engagement and participation. The 21st-century 
museum must now operate in new definitions of heritage, cooperation networks 
and industry links, and, most significantly, the need to foster participation from 
various communities. This requires thinking about the ability of museum and herit-
age work to explore transversal and agile learning models that can quickly adapt to 
and champion social impact to meet the needs of all communities. 
Cultural heritage practitioners and thinkers must also now think about, experi -
ence, and value 21st-century museums as vital resources to resonate the concerns 
of people’s lives; how museum and heritage work can be an opportunity to confront 
the physical, technological, and virtual spaces and challenges to engage the past, 
present and future. This can be done in at least three ways. The first concerns the 
value of strong, collectively built museum narratives as tools for social inclusion 
and reconciliation. They provide opportunities for including groups with divergent 
perspectives and reaffirm museums’ role in contemporary society. By moving away 
from linear narratives, museums can work towards open-ended narratives that of -
fer multiple interpretations based on community members’ stories, memories, and 
lived experiences. It is a way to reconfigure public memory to include a variety of 
histories rather than dictating truths. The second calls for new forms of organisa -
tional structures and different interpretations of the role of museum professionals 
operating in these new structures to best embrace organisational models that can 
shift power relations in exhibition-making and place work with the community at 
the forefront. The third is a call to value the museum as a work in progress and an 
arena for community members to have their voices heard. 
This chapter will focus on the latter arguments about the need for new museum 
organisational models to facilitate participation in heritage. As the author has 
done elsewhere (Viau-Courville 2021), it will develop the argument that museums 
must continue to focus their organisational energy on building high-functioning 
multidisciplinary teams that prioritise creativity and efficiency by investing in  
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-17 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
218 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
staff development, creating a better management system of curatorial authority, 
and leveraging community participation strategies. Given how museums in the 
21st century have become more abundant and varied than ever before, what should 
be looked after today is a pluralist perspective of what museums can do – here 
looking beyond their traditional core functions as guardians of cultural heritage, 
whose mandate is acquisition, conservation, research, and presentation of herit -
age, and think of museums as ethically committed and community responsive, 
multifaceted, inclusive, and creative institutions. This inclusive perspective reso -
nates with several arguments put forward by museum thinkers and practitioners 
over the years, including Stephen Weil’s (1999) well-known invitation to think of 
museums not being about something but instead being for something, and recent 
critical approaches to heritage that value how museums “increasingly recognise 
and address with concern the deep societal inequalities and asymmetries of power 
and wealth – across the globe as well as nationally, regionally and locally” ( San-
dahl 2019, vi). 
The chapter will draw on ethnographic case studies of organisations leading the 
way towards “responsibilising museum practice” through contemporary and cause-
driven uses of heritage. Using in-person interviews and participant observation, 
along with approaches in systems thinking and sociological models to organisa -
tional theory, it will present a sample of museums across Latin America and Eu -
rope that have deconstructed traditional curatorship in efforts to become ethically 
committed, community-responsive, and creative institutions. It will focus on what 
the author has called elsewhere “managerial curatorship,” a museum practice and 
model that sees professional project managers taking on curatorial tasks, such as 
exhibition-making, to enhance the institution’s openness and publicness ( Viau-
Courville 2017, 2021). The chapter will show how managerial curatorship encour-
ages an organic and team-based network model to operate and manage museum and 
heritage projects and share ideas internally and externally with communities rather 
than focusing on compartmentalised and mechanical systems of curatorial author -
ity. This provides good opportunities for implementing socially engaged heritage 
practices and renegotiated power arrangements and shows how cultural heritage 
organisations can fit into a broader group of stakeholders and ensure continued col-
laboration and empowerment. The chapter will end with a discussion about how 
managerial curatorship also signals the importance of building creative skills in col-
laborative work – i.e. the capacity and transferable skill of creativity that directly 
impacts individual and collective well-being. 
Organic management in heritage 
Managerial curatorship started developing in the late 1980s, particularly in French-
speaking Canada and parts of Europe, before being progressively adopted in other 
cultural and heritage organisations across Latin America and, more recently, in 
the UK and US. In most cases, it originated from an interest in creating a stronger 
relationship between museums, local communities, and heritage by envisioning an 
alternative model to the traditional top-down museum organised by disciplinary 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Reframing Knowledge in Cultural Heritage 219 
departments, each headed by scholar-curators, and work towards what would to-
day be referred to as a form of curatorial or museum activism that explicitly aligns 
“with broader efforts to bring about environmental, social and political change” 
(Janes and Sandell 2019, 137). 
The managerial model has facilitated an exploration into how knowledge and 
expertise are deconstructed as the domain of museum curators and reframed as the 
work of community participation and experts as critical to relevant and fair repre-
sentation in exhibitions and stories (Anderson 2023, 256). This sees managerial 
curatorship as a way for museums to share decision-making about exhibitions with 
members of groups connected to the cultures and objects considered for display – 
that is, a co-curated exhibition process that values an “insider’s perspective” along 
with the view and knowledge of other kinds of experts (Quinn 2020, 78–79). Such 
a form of a distributed model of curatorial work, as it is argued here, enables both 
greater visitor engagement and an overall understanding of participatory work in 
its ability to be reproduced as a particular skill set among different stakeholders 
and communities. 
The term managerial curatorship is framed within social sciences and arts man-
agement and is partly based on Luca Zan’s expression “managerial rhetoric” in 
arts and museums. Zan maintains that the expression should not be mistaken for 
privatisation or the trivial commercial exploration of cultural heritage, as if in -
dicating some degeneration or misuse of a body of knowledge and techniques. 
Instead, it points to an interest in developing appropriate, more flexible forms of 
management: 
If the heroic vision of management sees the manager as a solitary decision-
maker and the very decision as a force similar to that of an isolated thinker, 
almost as a scientist in the culminating moment of discovery, the observable 
truth is different, with managerial work characterized by fragmentation, lim-
ited periods of solitude, and continuous interruptions, brief contacts, ques-
tions, and exchanges of words and ideas. 
(Zan 2006, 1) 
As a model promoting the deconstruction and redistribution of knowledge and 
expertise in the museum structure, managerial curatorship is more specific than 
other known forms of shared or collaborative curating – i.e. co-curatorship. Along-
side their management responsibilities, exhibition managers often lead content de-
velopment. They take part in each project’s financial and production planning and 
have the final say on content, thematic outline, and scenario. While they are neither 
the curator nor an expert on the exhibition’s topic (unlike guest curators, chosen 
for their expertise or interest in a topic), they manage the project in a role similar to 
that of an orchestra conductor – a recurring expression in the field (see also Garcia-
Bardidia et al., this volume). Moreover, managerial curatorship involves the ex-
plicit intent of hiring generalist profiles in the museum – instead of curatorial or 
academic ones – that may bring the necessary soft skills needed to create new links 
between the museum and communities. Hence, there is a need for a specific term 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
220 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
for this practice, which signals an intent for newer forms of collective and creative 
work and a challenge to traditional forms of governance in cultural heritage – a 
departure from the old hierarchies of power in heritage with the lone curator figure 
at the top of the authority and decision-making process.
1 
Examples of early managerial curatorship include the arrival of generalist ex-
hibition managers in some French-Canadian museums at the end of the 1980s. 
They were hired because of their versatility in project management tasks, including 
exhibition content development (Arpin 1998, 41). Trained in different fields such 
as journalism or communications, they were tasked with synthesising and organis-
ing existing knowledge and content from various disciplinary fields and external 
sources (e.g. university researchers) or content reports drafted by internal general-
ist documentalists. The model promoted teamwork and interdisciplinarity through 
multiple viewpoints towards an exhibition built on perspectives from a team of 
generalists (Šola 1994). Contrary to museums following Anglo-Saxon traditions 
at the time, the model departed from hiring subject area specialist curators as core 
components of their staffing structure. This was, and perhaps remains, the more 
radical characteristic of managerial curatorship. 
Managerial curatorship also aligns with a group of museum thinkers in sys -
tems thinking and sociological models to organisational theory, such as Yuha 
Jung, Ann Rowson Love, Robert Janes, and Richard Sandell, to mention but a 
few, who consider that “museums will be better off when they operate as open, 
dynamic, and learning systems as a whole as opposed to closed, stagnant, and 
status quo systems that are compartmentalised and hierarchical” ( Jung and Love 
2017, 3); that soft and organic management and organisational models are critical 
to be effective internally and socially relevant in the external environment ( Jung 
and Love 2017, 4). 
Systems thinking is a relevant model for the museums and heritage sector 
because it can increase power sharing and champion traditional museum or -
ganisational structures and hierarchies of power. This follows arguments such as 
those by Robert Janes (2010) and Richard Sandell (2007) on the relevance of a 
greater interconnection between museum departments, teams, and communities, 
promoting a culture of inclusivity and leveraging diverse perspectives. Like -
wise, it responds to the call for museums to promote a bottom-up perspective 
that enables local communities and stakeholders to engage in and contribute to 
cultural initiatives as cultural producers instead of consumers, with a particular 
focus on “social improvement, issues of oppression and gender issues” ( Jung 
and Love 2017, 8) 
As Burns (2007, 179) argued, applying different variants of systems theory 
“does not mean that consensus, individual agency, linearity, representativeness 
and so on are no longer relevant or important, only that they should be seen as one 
part of a greater whole.” From an organisational perspective, Burns maintains this 
concerns a shift in emphasis from “consensus to parallel development” ( Burns 
2007). Drawing from the author’s fieldwork in museums across Latin America 
and Europe, the following sections present case studies in managerial curatorship 
in São Paulo, Brazil, and Leeds, UK. They were selected based on their use of and 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Reframing Knowledge in Cultural Heritage 221 
experimentation with managerial approaches as advocacy work for the profit of 
social improvement, social justice, and inclusion. They are also helpful in high -
lighting the renewed trend in the museum sector towards hiring generalist profiles 
that bring complementary and necessary new skill sets that enable institutions to 
better engage their communities. 
Managerial approaches for advocacy and social justice 
Managerial curatorship has been most prevalent since the 1990s, particularly 
among newly created museums and cultural organisations and around an interna -
tional network of heritage professionals and thinkers closely tied to ICOM and its 
different conventions (e.g. 1972 Santiago Roundtable or the Quebec and Cordoba 
declarations). In Brazil, as in other countries in Latin America, many heritage pro-
fessionals have pursued 1980s radical reassessments of the purpose of museums 
in society, particularly the idea that cultural heritage can be used to service com -
munity development and promote positive social change. The Museum of Sexual 
Diversity and the Museum of the Person, located in São Paulo, explore and repre-
sent the stories and memories of marginalised, oppressed, and silenced people. The 
two activist museums have explored forms of managerial thinking in their curato-
rial approach to share or give full curatorial authority and agency to local social 
groups and communities by developing a project-driven organisational culture that 
places participation and memory work at the forefront. Teams in these museums 
see their work as contributing to creating spaces for advocacy and curatorial activ-
ism through shared and distributed work instead of traditional research or object-
based curatorship. 
The museum of sexual diversity 
The Museum of Sexual Diversity was created in 2012 as a space for dialogue and 
actions for social justice, acceptance, and empathy in a largely conservative coun-
try whose military dictatorship long denied recognition and fundamental civil 
rights for LGBTQI+ communities. According to one of its founders and first di -
rector, Franco Reinaudo, the museum is a statement for inclusivity, human rights, 
and celebrating diversity. The museum is strategically located inside the República 
metro station and under the República square, a symbolic area and hub for LG -
BTQI+ social life but also for violence and hate crimes fuelled by homophobia. 
The organisation is a symbol of resistance to censorship and discrimination, hav -
ing been shut down twice in recent years but always managing to reopen. Located 
in a transit metro corridor with high numbers of daily visitors, the museum was 
designed as an open and safe space where LGBTQI + communities can “mediate 
their identities and ensure they are no longer invisible and marginalised” (Franco 
Reinaudo, personal communication). The goal has been to promote a conversa -
tion between the museum and the broader population, never using aggression but 
humour, playfulness, and museum educational initiatives that enable it to reach 
visitors on a personal level. For example, during the exhibition Caio Mon Amour 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
222 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
(2016), which celebrated the life of Brazilian writer Caio Fernando Abreu, poems 
by Abreu were placed outside the museum for the metro users to take with them. 
Over time, these kinds of iterative museum actions enabled the development of a 
broader audience and following among the population of Sao Paulo. 
The museum’s projects are fully co-curated, promoting a collaborative and 
shared process between the museum and its stakeholders – that is, to be “managed 
by members of this community – artists, activists, writers, among others – that 
would participate in all aspects of its operations, from the management of its col -
lections to the conception and planning of its exhibitions” (Reinaudo 2020, 20). 
From an organisational perspective, this entails a team of staff hired from local 
stakeholders and many volunteers. The museum’s collaborative spaces welcome 
volunteer – and, when possible, paid – contributions from local artists, activists, 
and members of LGBTQ+ groups to produce artistic happenings, talks, and social 
gatherings. Contributors, whether paid or volunteer, see the museum and its ex-
hibitions as “disruptive initiatives” that address difficult and taboo subjects such 
as discrimination, violence and trauma – and therefore see value in contributing 
to and being part of the museum’s development, which they consider an agent for 
counter-memories and counter-narratives. The museum’s projects are co-curated 
by ethical choice and a means to better respond to and address the demands and 
claims of its communities (which are in constant flux). 
On average, the museum team has been composed of six to seven people. 
This includes the director, three to four educators, a collections officer, and a 
production officer. The museum’s organisational structure has been one of advo -
cacy by design. Most staff did not have a museum background or experience in 
heritage-based projects. Instead, they were hired based on diversity criteria and 
the importance of having a museum team representing the broader population, 
including but not limited to LGBTQI + communities. Team members were of -
ten hired from underprivileged areas of São Paulo – the peripheral areas around 
other municipal centres – and as existing members of the LGBTQ + communi -
ties. They were then trained to develop and facilitate co-produced curatorial pro -
jects. Over the years, the team worked with hundreds of artists, designers, and 
activists to “talk about sensitive matters like sexual identities, gender expression, 
and other issues that are deeply misunderstood by the general public, and which 
fuel violence against LGBTQI + people” (Reinaudo 2020, 23). By gathering and 
synthesising stories, memories, research, and objects from different sources to 
produce exhibitions on themes ranging from the city’s Drag Queen performances 
to fashion, poetry, or São Paulo nightlife in the 1970s. 
According to the museum team, maintaining an open and ongoing dialogue and 
collaboration with the community is the organisation’s lifeblood and primary pur-
pose. The goal is for the community to contribute to the museum’s programmes, 
content, and memory work with a museum practice and museological thinking 
that supports – and constantly adapts to – such endeavours, not the other way 
around (Reinaudo, personal communication ). This means that as an institution 
fully dedicated to representing and responding to the interests of its community, 
it must be prepared to accept that its programming and development strategy will 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
Reframing Knowledge in Cultural Heritage 223 
almost always lead not only to unforeseen outcomes (always seen as a positive 
thing) but also require museological practices that depart from traditional curato -
rial methods or approaches. From an institutional point of view, this also resonates 
with working 
towards a pluralistic model of diversity management – an approach that 
acknowledges and accepts differences and values them within the organisa -
tion. Within the diversity management approach, the individual is not re -
quired to adapt to the organisation, rather the employer “will be open to the 
possibility of changing organizational culture and systems.” 
(Sandell 2007, 214) 
Over the years, projects have often pushed the limits of what museums can 
normally do – invite LGBTQI + choir groups to practices outside museum hours, 
get in trouble with metro management as crowds gather to watch and listen to 
the museum’s happenings or public events, or allow LGBTQI + associations to 
organise meetings and serve food in the exhibition areas. As such, Museu da Di -
versidade Sexual is an example of how museological work must constantly adapt 
to fit community needs. 
Museum of the person 
The Museu da Pessoa (Museum of the Person) is a virtual museum dedicated to 
making Brazilian stories accessible, transferable, shareable, and inheritable for 
future generations. Since its creation in 1991, the museum has implemented more 
than 300 different projects, produced an 18,000-life story archive and 72,000 
digitised personal images that together tell the history of Brazil in the 20th and 
21st centuries. The museum team has also worked with over 90 major Brazilian 
unions and companies to tell their stories through the lens of the memories of the 
Brazilians they employed and their families and designed multiple experiences 
with communities and public schools in more than 230 cities in Brazil and around 
1600 organisations (schools, social organisations, and communities). 
Museu da Pessoa has developed its methodological approach to memory 
work – social memory technology – that enables communities and local groups to 
share, document, and safeguard their memory collectively and autonomously. As 
described by Worcman and Garde-Hansen (2023, 137): 
Social memory technology shares the basic premise of social technology, 
which is that through communal participation and use of knowledge, low-
cost technologies can be created that will have greater social impact and 
enormous potential for reapplication. It starts with the understanding that 
the right to memory is the right to create, preserve, disseminate, and legiti-
mize memories and is fundamentally concerned with the cultural rights of 
groups to be recognized inside a nation for their connections (past, present, 
and future) to persons outside their nation. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
224 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
The museum has privileged a managerial curatorship model in which project 
managers act in a role analogous to that of a cinema director who leads a team of 
documentalists, audiovisual technicians, and fieldwork administrators – preferring 
generalists over scholar-curators or subject area specialists. The model is designed 
to facilitate the implementation of the four underlying principles of social memory 
technology (Worcman and Garde-Hansen 2023, 136): 
Principle 1. The idea that any individual can and should be considered a key actor 
in a global and a local (mediated) heritage through the narration of his or her 
life story; 
Principle 2. The right of every group to produce their own memory; 
Principle 3. The practice and outcomes of communities and individuals produc -
ing their memories, life stories and heritage, should be open, transparent, and 
inclusive; 
Principle 4. The potential for change – social, cultural, and theoretical – should be 
a key outcome of (re)constructing and (re)performing the past in the present as 
personal memory. 
Projects generally have a pre-content phase that covers preliminary exploratory 
research related to fieldwork preparation. This phase helps the team get acquainted 
with its communities’ sociocultural and demographic characteristics. It is essen-
tial to apply the museum’s methodology and prepare for the second phase, which 
covers field interviews and training local communities. The project manager then 
collects and synthesises all interviews on behalf of the community or communities. 
A pre-concept document outlines the main themes/topics that have emerged from 
fieldwork. This document then serves as the basis for the third phase, during which 
the project manager sits with the museum director, the head of museology, and 
the head of marketing. All four will then discuss and agree on the content of the 
summary document. For the final phase, the museum selects and hires an external 
designer who draws the exhibition’s spatial outline and scenario. In keeping with 
co-curatorial and transparency principles, during the project’s entire lifespan, from 
fieldwork to the final exhibition concept, all participating members of the com-
munities are kept informed and upraised on the project’s advancement, curatorial 
direction, and hiring decisions for production and design. Activist organisations 
like Museu da Pessoa, through their decades of fieldwork among Brazil’s local 
populations – including work with Indigenous, marginalised or vulnerable groups – 
have developed a rapport with its stakeholders, enabling trust to be enacted and 
facilitating the creative and collaborative process. 
Since 2021, Patrimônios has been working to preserve Indigenous knowl -
edge and traditional cultural expressions by designing a more respectful and 
ethical methodological approach that enables collaborative work with, for, and 
by Indigenous peoples. The project has brought together and trained groups of 
young people along the riverbanks of Rio Negro with the support of local as -
sociations – including the Federação das Organizações Indígenas do Rio Negro 
(FOIRN) – to record and value the stories and memories of the Elders – seen 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Reframing Knowledge in Cultural Heritage 225 
as holders of Indigenous knowledge and traditional cultural heritage. Using ap -
proaches to decolonial storytelling methodologies (e.g. Archibald, Lee-Morgan, 
and De Santolo 2019 ; Tuhiwai Smith 2022), more than 400 hours of training and 
sharing/co-developing methodologies workshops have been organised, and 61 
in-person interviews have been conducted, 34 of which were in native languages 
and led by local community members (70 hours of recordings) whom the mu -
seum has trained in carrying interviews on their own terms (Lucas Lara, personal 
communication). 
Patrimônios
2 came in response to meeting the needs and demands of the 
Amazon communities for greater recognition of traditional knowledge and for 
them to share their stories on their terms. The process allows for horizontal and 
shared authorship so that counter-narratives can be heard by representing com -
munity voices in the museum ( Onciul 2015, 72). It also promotes awareness in 
the community of how social memory is constructed and the tools made available 
through cultural and heritage institutions that make it possible to safeguard these 
memories (Worcman and Garde-Hansen 2016 , 2023). 
Thackray Museum of medicine 
Contrary to activist museum traditions in Latin America, museums following 
Anglo-Saxon traditions in Europe and North America commonly hire exhibition 
developers, or managers, for their team-building qualities and ability to run or as -
sist the production process (Gray 2014, 116–17; Lord, Lord, and Martin 2012, 
100–101). Curators play a pivotal role as subject matter experts in production while 
taking on a more collaborative approach than in previous decades (McCall and 
Gray 2014). Indeed, a developer may occasionally be required to mediate between 
curators and designers. 
Nevertheless, managerial approaches progressively integrate organisational 
thinking and bring new forms of curatorial work into the Anglo-Saxon model, 
seeking generalist staff profiles as Brazilian and Canadian museum counterparts. 
In the UK, the Thackray Museum of Medicine is one example. A learning source 
about past healthcare practices and medical heritage, the museum wants to be a 
transformative element in Leeds by bringing together people from different back -
grounds to think about healthcare practices and medicine, past and present, and 
how everyone can play a role in shaping a healthy future. The museum is located 
inside St James’s University Hospital, where it maintains several strategic partner-
ships with its various research centres and care units. It is also located next to the 
Harehills area of Leeds, which ranks among the top ten per cent of the UK’s most 
deprived areas for employment, education, and housing. This presents a particular 
challenge for the museum, which seeks participation from members of Leeds’s 
underprivileged communities. 
In the early 2020s, the museum reorganised its two core sectors – collections 
and programming – and introduced new permanent producer roles focusing on 
developing links with the local communities. This came in response to the need 
to be more open and expert at partnering with local communities and bringing 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
226 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
new perspectives and contributions to the museum’s exhibition content, public 
programmes and collecting initiatives. Producers, also called “community co-
producers,” are hired because of their knowledge of the city and capacity to gen -
erate projects that lead to meaningful participatory projects that will ultimately 
impact museum exhibition programmes and collecting practices. Their role is to 
champion social impact to meet the needs of all people. As of 2024, the program -
ming sector is headed by a lead producer instead of a curator who manages the 
team and public programmes and promotes spaces and actions for participation 
in the museum. The team also comprises a community co-producer who devel -
ops projects with/by/for local citizens and a specialist curator with a doctorate in 
medical history and heritage who carries a research-focused role. 
According to the museum’s leadership, the key to hiring new producer profiles 
has been removing prior museum experience and higher education credentials as 
a job requirement, enabling a more inclusive hiring process (Jamie Taylor, per -
sonal communication). This fundamental aspect of the hiring process should not be 
confused with the 1960s and 1970s affirmative or positive action policies, which 
have been associated “with the lowering of standards and perceived as an attack on 
meritocratic principles of selection” (Sandell 2007, 214). On the contrary, by hav-
ing more generalist profiles, producers bring in new and essential complementary 
soft skills, such as agility, leadership, and management skills, but fundamentally, 
human relations and the ability to build rapport, communicate, and listen. Before 
joining the Thackray, producers worked in different sectors, such as theatre and so-
cial community work. They had extensive experience in advocacy and community 
projects dealing with LGBTQ+ issues, contentious stories, and trauma. Moreover, 
although not initially thought of as a goal by the Thackray Museum, another ad-
vantage that arose by removing traditional museum credentials in the hiring pro -
cess has been an opportunity to increase diversity in the workplace. Some recent 
producer hires came from what are still underrepresented social or ethnic groups in 
the UK museum workforce. 
Accordingly, Thackray’s newly formed staffing structure has facilitated the de-
velopment of several collaborations with local associations and stakeholders in 
recent years to address contemporary topics such as health and LGBTQI+, sex, 
and social identity. These include work with the Black Health Initiative, a commu-
nity engagement organisation working towards equal access to education, health, 
and social care in Leeds, and local influencers on body positivity. The recent par -
ticipatory exhibition, Private Parts (2023), was created as a “comfortable space to 
talk about uncomfortable things” and explore how our identity shapes health and 
healthcare. Producers gathered various co-curators and storytellers who were part 
of the exhibition. Among the participants and local contributors to the exhibition 
was Shaun, who highlighted the need for accessible, easy-to-read information to 
make it easier for people with learning disabilities to understand who they are 
and to access the healthcare they are entitled to.
3 Sarah explained the myths and 
misunderstandings surrounding sex and disability and shared her journey towards 
achieving body positivity and launching #fatdisabledworthy. Andi, a member of 
Skyline, which provides support services for anyone living with or affected by HIV 

 
 
 
Reframing Knowledge in Cultural Heritage 227 
in Leeds, discussed the stigma that comes with HIV/AIDS and observed that it is 
still very much alive in our society today. Lynne, a urogynaecologist, explained 
the conditions she encounters in her practice and the available treatments. She also 
discussed the current focus on physiotherapy over surgery where possible. As an 
actor, activist and DJ who identifies as intersex trans non-binary, Ki shared his 
experience of being intersex and the importance of community. Herfa shared her 
painful journey with fibroids and the challenges she has faced along the way. She 
also explained how she channels her feelings about her condition through her art. 
Pauline reflected on the joy and sadness of her life as a transwoman. 
The producers’ role has enabled building strong community relations and co-
curatorship opportunities by reaching out to local citizens and creating an active 
programme of informal and free cultural events at the museum, from dress-making 
workshops to food gatherings, dancing, and local amateur historians talking with 
Elders. Since producer and not curator roles lead these projects and the museum’s 
public programming initiatives, this directly impacts exhibition content and col-
lection making – as this has been the institutional goal. The community actively 
enhances the museum’s collections by contributing to collaborative collecting pro-
jects, with the museum now focusing on safeguarding stories as well as objects, 
similarly to what has elsewhere been called contemporary curating (Battesti 2012). 
This likewise resonates with the principles of what Sandell (2007, 217) has coined 
as “effective diversity management” in which whatever their position in an organi-
sation, an individual’s personal experiences and cultural background may influence 
the internal agenda and, in doing so, encourage a greater diversity in its service 
delivery, thereby enhancing its ability to engage with diverse audiences.” 
Setting creative outcomes for social change 
Organisational change comes in response to environmental changes and how 
decision-makers interpret these changes. The projects discussed in this chapter 
applied strategies in managerial thinking to challenge the status quo. They im-
plemented staffing and organisational structures to leverage heritage and mem -
ory work for cultural democracy and democratisation. Moreover, they designed 
open-ended narratives for members of the community that provide opportunities 
to “explore forms of co-ownership through dialogic and transactive methods. This 
requires systems of governance which ensure that community museums exist not 
only for communities but of communities” (Rassool 2012, 15). In all cases, muse-
ums have been accumulating and redistributing knowledge and know-how related 
to and generating from creative and collective agency. 
Museu da Pessoa has developed its methodology – social memory technology – 
designed to promote specific creative competencies among staff and community 
members in the co-production of experiences through memory work. The Thack-
ray Museum’s redevelopment has been transformational, not just for the museum 
itself but also for the local area and its communities. Both the Museum of Sexual 
Diversity and the Thackray took proactive measures to engage underprivileged or 
marginalised communities. More significantly, all museums found ways to create 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
228 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
meaningful connections and promote social cohesion by explicitly seeking new 
generalist roles on their staff. 
Indeed, the case studies in this chapter point to a return to the generalist figure 
in the museum, looking to implement a model similar to the one some muse -
ums developed at the end of the 1980s by bringing non-curatorial profiles whose 
complementary soft skills – project management and human relations – helped 
to bridge the gap between the museum and their communities. Moreover, it 
helped these museums fulfil their political or social agenda – advocacy work for 
social justice, diversity, and social agency and memory. They have also placed 
community – not research – as a primary institutional objective. Research sup -
ported such initiatives, to be sure, but archival or object-based curatorship was not 
necessarily the starting point. All three sites also proved conducive to designing 
spaces that engender and facilitate public debate and conversation while having 
museum teams whose efficiency and institutional targets will depend on their ca -
pacity to leverage heritage work to reach their creativity goals. In so doing, the 
teams have shown competence in setting creative outcomes by designing projects 
that can yield “relevant and effective novelty” ( Anheier and Raj Isar 2010, 376). 
Creativity concerns the ability or power to create, bring into existence, invest in a 
new form, produce through imaginative skill, and make or bring something new into 
existence (Encyclopedia Britannica 2025). It concerns a quality no longer limited to 
exceptional individuals working in scientific or artistic activities. Instead, it appears 
in any human activity across different communities ( Jiménez López 2011 , 201). 
Thus, creativity extends beyond the strict remits of visual and artistic expression do-
mains and the “creative communities” founded by artists – whose networked culture 
is informed by artistic practice – to cut across several areas ( Cambell 2010, 189). 
It is in this sense that managerial thinking has brought on critical competencies of 
working creatively with others and implementing innovations in mobilisation, plan-
ning, construction, and organisation of stories, together with the best ways to make 
them shareable and inheritable (through different types of public programming and 
media, including exhibitions but also in situ participatory heritage projects). For 
community members, this resonates with Arjun Appadurai’s approach to creativity 
as an inevitable skill towards empowerment by aspiration to improve the condition 
of economically underprivileged social groups or having previously marginalised 
stories being brought forward ( Appadurai 2013, 179–95). For staff, this also pro -
vides common ground among museum teams to work collectively towards reaching 
pre-defined creativity outcomes and desired audience impact – measured in transfer-
able or social-emotional skills. 
As cultural heritage institutions that bridge people with their environment, sets 
of knowledge and ideas, the projects in this chapter also give further meaning to 
Norris and Tisdale’s (2014) arguments that museums are uniquely positioned to 
help members of the public nurture their creativity. In recent years, museums, as 
in culture more broadly, have prioritised the exploration of curiosity, motivation, 
and self-identity as central elements in the distinctive forms of learning models 
(Caban et al. 2003; Falk 2006, 2024; see also Montuori and Donnelly 2016). These 
organisations are naturally prepared to engage 21st-century skills
4 by designing 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Reframing Knowledge in Cultural Heritage 229 
favourable contexts to promote working and thinking creatively and innovatively 
as part of the newly established critical skills for future life. Museum staff cul-
tivate creative thinking skills by designing participatory and social experiences, 
centring around visitor idea generation and often involving hands-on manipulation 
and creation. 
More than a contemporary buzzword, creativity allows us to examine all aspects 
of museum work, including social impact, internal organisations, learning and edu-
cation, and curatorship. These can counter social polarisation in favour of more 
open, inclusive societies. Museums can be vital in nurturing collective creativity 
by issuing creative challenges, designing spaces and tolls for creative projects, and 
encouraging conversations among and with their communities (Norris and Tisdale 
2014, 200). Museums in this chapter have shown ways to affirm, legitimise, and 
validate new conventions of creativity. 
Conclusion 
Like the systems thinking paradigm, to paraphrase Jung and Love (2017, 9), the 
managerial and advocacy ethos in the museums and heritage sectors can be said to 
encourage organic and team-based network models to operate and manage exhibi-
tion projects – or the museum as a whole – as well as the sharing of ideas internally 
and externally with communities rather than focusing on compartmentalised and 
mechanical systems. This invites a rationale and framework for an ethical approach 
to museum thinking, management, and decision-making, as it creates the condi-
tions for a fresh approach for museums to “become reality-based” and acknowl-
edge “the profusion of different “realities” that define our humanness” to “become 
more involved in the broader world” (Janes 2013, 392–93). This chapter discussed 
managerial curatorship, a team-based museum practice that signals museum think-
ing that departs from only thinking about accessibility and awareness-raising to 
full-on engagement, shared authority, and collaborative curatorship. It also signals 
an important shift in museum staff profiles from specialists to generalists. 
Analysing museum work through the lens of managerial curatorship offers valu-
able insights by moving beyond a normative or purely academic understanding of 
curatorship and the aspirations of a specific group – namely curatorial circles. This 
opens up new approaches for evaluating how museum work can quickly respond to 
the changing needs and interests of audiences and communities and affect societal 
change by applying what I believe is the inevitable necessity for more flexible, 
efficient, and multifaceted museum teams able to adapt and deliver ethically com-
mitted and community quickly responsive exhibitions (and other initiatives). 
Since “museums can only be as good as their analysis of society and their aware-
ness of people’s lives” (O’Neil in Cameron 2020, 354), there is the predicament of 
turning them into favourable sites for multiple narratives to thrive about the past, 
present, and future. For this, many arguments have been put forward in favour of 
shifting a museum’s mode of address from monologic to dialogic, providing more 
space for polyvocal and shared authority – for museums to give up part of their au-
thorial voice to allow the public or specific communities to speak for themselves and 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
230 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
be heard in a public space (Mason, Whitehead, and Graham 2013, 164). This signals 
a major conceptual shift we are experiencing as societies: from traditional museums 
and views of culture and heritage to being more socially involved and driven to val-
uing change and diversity – a new vision for museums in a rapidly changing world. 
Notes 
1 The evolution of professional roles and knowledge management extends beyond mu -

cation, research, and financial strategies. Traditional tenure-track roles increasingly 
collaborate with non-academic staff, such as research facilitators, who support grant 
acquisition and project development. Since the 1990s, North American and European 
universities have embraced entrepreneurial approaches to reduce reliance on gov -
ernment funding and secure diverse external grants. Embedded within departments, 
research facilitators bring specialised skills in grant writing, strategic planning, and 
network-building, reflecting broader efforts to align academic research with societal 
challenges and foster science-society dialogue.
