14 Civic engagement in cultural heritage 
The Transylvanian Saxon case over 
the last century 
Daniela Stanciu-Păscărița and Sorin Radu 
Every year the Transylvanian Saxons meet in a town in Romania or Germany for a 
gathering called Sachsentreffen, where they present their folk costumes and tradi -
tions and organise balls and bazaars of products specific to their culture. In 2024, 
this gathering was organised in Sibiu under the title “Heimat ohne Grenzen” – 
home without borders. The name under which it was decided to hold the interna -
tional Saxon meeting is eloquent for one of the objectives of this study, as it meets 
some of the characteristics of the identity construction of this community whose 
home is transnational and multidimensional. The community regrouped as a result 
of a process of migration to localities in Germany and Austria, which became their 
new home. The intangible heritage has been transferred to the homeland ( Vater-
land) to which they emigrated, being displayed at such events charged with a sym-
bolic identity value, but the tangible heritage is in Transylvania, in the Heimat to 
which they feel spiritually linked. 
The urbanity of Transylvania and the architectural elements, building facades, 
urban furniture that define the urban space developed organically due to Austro-
Hungarian imperial policies, but also under the aegis of fashion and taste inspired 
by Central and Western Europe. The Habsburg Empire was a diverse collection 
of territories with distinct traditions, languages, and religions. While rural areas 
were often homogeneous, cities, particularly on the empire’s edges (e.g., Banat, 
Transylvania, Upper Hungary, and Bucovina), were more ethnically and culturally 
mixed, as was Vienna at the centre. Following the 1867 constitutional reforms, 
increased mobility, industrialisation, and better education led to migration, making 
cities more diverse. The “struggle for the city” in the years after 1867 involved 
various groups, including state authorities, municipalities, and ethnic and religious 
communities (Horel 2023, 1). 
The Southern part of Transylvania which was legally referred to as the 
Königsboden/fundus regius until the end of the 19th century, was inhabited and 
organised by the Transylvanian Saxons, a group of Germans who settled in this 
territory in the medieval period ( Klein 1971 ). The appearance, aesthetics, and 
architecture of the Transylvanian towns on the former land of the king today con-
tain elements of tangible heritage of particular cultural and historical value, rang -
ing from public or private buildings to public parks and gardens or monuments of 
artistic or memorial value. As expressed most forcefully by the United Nations 
DOI: 10.4324/9781003569695-18 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
234 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization (UNESCO) Convention for the 
Safeguarding of the Intangible Cultural Heritage, intangible cultural heritage, 
and by extension all cultural heritage, is “constantly recreated by communities 
and groups in response to their environment, their interaction with nature and 
their history, and provides them with a sense of identity and continuity” (UNE -
SCO 2003). An important aspect of the authorship of this tangible urban heritage 
is represented by the migration of Transylvanian Saxons towards Germany and 
Austria throughout the 20th century, which was taken over, reorganised and man-
aged by Romanians, understanding these phenomena in the key to understanding 
Germanness without Germans (Cercel 2019 , 5). 
The aim of the present study is to capture how the authorised heritage discourse 
evolves and transforms under different political contexts of the same urban milieu. 
The analysis of an association which ought to involve in the creation, preservation, 
and valorisation of the urban heritage is of importance, from a multidimensional 
perspective, creating liaisons between the centre and the periphery of the Austro-
Hungarian Monarchy. For this reason, the case study of the Association for Embel-
lishment of the City of Sibiu (see AIOS 2024) matches perfectly one of the sets 
of heritage practices, tied to the visitation of sites and institutions within tourism 
and leisure activities (Smith 2006, 12), being an example of involvement of social 
actors in the cultural heritage management. The comparative approach between 
imperial realities and the functions of the association in post-communist Romania 
is relevant in order to grasp the way in which other actors, of different ethnic -
ity decided to rebrand and adjust the goals and practices of the (founding) asso -
ciation. This transnational perspective, understood in the key of Philo-Germanism 
without Germans (Cercel 2019, 5) is representing new forms of understanding 
of the urban heritage under different state structures. The turn from the imperial 
authorised heritage discourse to the post-imperial one which occurs as the boarders 
change and the political situation shifts from a multinational empire to a national 
state depicts changes of perspective in the means of valorising the heritage. The 
post-communist authorised heritage discourse is reliant on the knowledge claims of 
technical and aesthetic experts, and institutionalised in state cultural agencies and 
amenity societies (Smith 2006, 12). In this regard, the AIOS ought to preserve and 
valorise the urban heritage of Sibiu under the new political and cultural realities 
of post-communist Romania when the discourse on minorities and multiculturality 
changed again. 
Methodological approach on “heritage discourse” and the 
Transylvanian case study 
The discourse on “heritage” naturalises the practice of preserving certain cultural 
elements for future generations, promoting Western elite values as universally ap-
plicable. It legitimises specific practices that shape popular and expert views of 
“heritage,” while marginalising alternative perspectives. The “authorised heritage 
discourse” obscures the social and cultural roles of “heritage,” framing it as a prac-
tice that constructs and regulates values. This dominant discourse, influenced by 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Civic Engagement in Cultural Heritage 235 
technical and aesthetic experts, is institutionalised in state agencies and heritage 
organisations. It prioritises monumentality, historical depth, expert judgment, and 
nation-building, reinforcing a self-referential system (Smith 2006, 11). 
The expansion and modernisation of the (multicultural) city in the Austro-
Hungarian Monarchy reshaped its urban landscapes. Transnational and dynastic 
names were given to public spaces, such as hotels, cafés, schools, and railway sta-
tions, while Austrian memorials reinforced loyalty to the monarchy. New streets 
were created to reflect national identity, commemorating Hungarian heroes, kings, 
and ministers. Cultural institutions were built along these streets, symbolising na -
tional discourse. Cities can be seen as “imagined communities,” where individu -
als engage mainly with familiar neighbourhoods ( Horel 2023, 5). Conceived as 
a palimpsest (Horel 2023, 5), the city is continuously reimagined through social 
practices, constantly rewritten but also retaining older narratives. It is an evolving 
assemblage of images that shape its heritage, even as some groups disappear, a 
common occurrence in East Central Europe. In this regard, the analysis of an as -
sociation implicated in creating and valorising the urban landscape is of interest. 
In this specific case, the cultural heritage was built and preserved by the Transyl-
vanian Saxons, who represented the demographic majority, but who after the 1990s, 
due to a massive migration phenomenon, unique in Romania’s history, turned into 
an abandoned community (Anghel and Oltean 2019 ). Their role has been taken 
over by other actors, with a majority Romanian component, interested in the pres-
ervation and maintenance of open spaces for leisure. The AIOS was (re)founded 
in 2001 (AIOS 2024) with the aim of bringing together Sibiu’s young elite, such 
as businessmen, researchers, professors, lawyers, politicians, journalists, etc. They 
all came together with the declared aim of supporting local administrations and of-
fering their help for the development of the Sibiu community and its projects. The 
study shows how the old and the new Association for the Beautification of Sibiu 
delves into cultural heritage to promote community involvement and advocate for 
the historical perspectives and importance of the city’s cultural heritage. 
The research aims to explore in depth two fundamental aspects of cultural poli-
cies oriented towards the preservation and promotion of tangible heritage specific 
to Transylvania. The first line of research focuses on the close connection between 
the authorised heritage imperial discourse of the 19th century with the functions 
and efforts of AIOS in order to valorise this heritage, recognising it not only as an 
integral part of local history, but also as an essential part of the cultural heritage of 
the Transylvanian Saxons. This perspective emphasises the importance of herit -
age as a link between the past and the present, as well as its role in strengthening 
cultural identity. The second line of research deals with the complex emotional 
and cultural situation of Transylvanian Germans who emigrated, leaving behind 
a tangible heritage to which they remain deeply attached. Although they have 
succeeded in establishing cultural associations in their new homelands, such as 
Germany and Austria, these organisations face the limitations imposed by national 
borders and therefore cannot interact with Transylvanian heritage in the way 
they would like to. The study aims to highlight the challenges and aspirations of 
these communities in preserving the connection to their cultural heritage despite 

 
 
 
236 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
distance and geopolitical changes, relying on the way the discourse regarding her-
itage and its paternity evolved. 
The relevance of the research is thus built around the importance of the value of 
tangible built heritage, perceived as a cultural heritage of an abandoned community, 
taken over and managed by a new community. We present the case study of Sibiu, 
a city defined by its Saxon character, being in the past the centre of power of the 
seven fortresses (Siebenbürgen), the seat of the Saxon University, of the governor 
of Transylvania, and the seat of multiple political, cultural and economic bodies of 
the Saxon organisational structure (Roth 2006, 150–158; Teutsch 1928, 128–9). 
After 1990 and the massive wave of Saxon emigration to the West, Sibiu became 
the example par excellence of Germanness without Germans (Cercel 2019, 15). 
In order to capture the functions of the tangible heritage in the Transylvanian 
urban space in the last century, we followed archival documents that reveal the 
actions of the AIOS and its perspectives on the tangible built heritage during the 
Belle Époque. The qualitative analysis of the festive writing (Festschrift) published 
in 1929 generates new information about the involvement of the Transylvanian 
Saxons in the management and enhancement of the beautification of the urban 
landscape. In order to capture the work of the AIOS, founded in the 2000s with the 
aim of getting involved in the social and civic life of Sibiu, we interviewed some of 
its members. These are relevant in the context of capturing the arguments and so-
cial involvement of some actors who understand themselves to be in charge of the 
education for the tangible heritage built by the Transylvanian Saxons but cared for 
by Romanians, a phenomenon understood in the key of a Philo-Germanism with-
out Germans (Cercel 2019, 5). The red line of involvement in the beautification of 
the city is thus pursued through a comparative approach of two associations – one 
founded more than 100 years ago and the other (re)founded after the 2000s – with 
the aim of generating different perspectives and dynamics in different political, 
national and social contexts in order to develop a participatory approach of cultural 
heritage, the study being at the confluence of cultural and urban history. 
Migration and cultural heritage 
During the 20th century, the population dynamics of the German minorities in Ro-
mania reflected significant changes, as evidenced by the statistical data collected 
at different periods. In 1930, German minorities constituted an important segment 
of Romania’s population, accounting for 4.1% (745,000 people). This proportion 
fell to 2.2% (about 391,000 members) by the 1956 census, marking a considerable 
reduction in the number of German communities. An even steeper decline was 
recorded by 2011, when Romania’s National Institute of Statistics reported only 
36,000 people identifying themselves as belonging to these communities, consti -
tuting 0.18% of the country’s total population. Analysing these figures, it can be 
seen that the proportion of members of German minorities declined significantly in 
two distinct stages: a nearly halving in the first 25 years after 1930, followed by a 
further reduction of 355,000 between 1956 and the beginning of the second decade 
of the 21st century. This development indicates that the population of the German 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Civic Engagement in Cultural Heritage 237 
communities in Romania has contracted dramatically in about 90 years. Compared 
to the 1930 figures, the proportion of this minority, once the second largest in Ro-
mania, fell to just 4% in 2011, a decrease of 96% from the original figure. This 
downward trend reflects socio-political changes and population migration over the 
past century (Beer 2019, 23). 
Although in the collective mind the Transylvanian Saxons boast a historical pres-
ence in the western and central regions of today’s Romania since the 12th century, 
the Germans in Romania emigrated en masse in the second half of the 20th century 
to their imagined homeland, the Federal Republic of Germany, which positioned 
itself as a kin-state and intervened on their behalf throughout the 20th and 21st cen-
turies. After the Second World War, German communities were present in Romania, 
West Germany, the German Democratic Republic, and Austria. Maintaining their 
distinct identity as Transylvanian Saxons, they evolved throughout the 20th century, 
incorporating the identity of Germans in Romania. Their shared experiences after 
1918, especially in the post-war and communist era, contributed to the formation of 
a common identity, which did not necessarily conflict with their sense of belonging 
to a specific ethnic group (Koranyi 2019, 75). 
The tangible cultural heritage in the geographical region of the Transylvanian 
Saxons, who a century ago had their home/Heimat here, is characterised by a dou-
ble heritage: one of the Transylvanian Saxons, who built and exploited it until their 
emigration, and the other of the Romanians, the “new Germans,” who took over 
and continued to exploit these spaces. This hypothesis outlines two perspectives 
of heritage analysis and valorisation: the first focuses on the distinctive heritage of 
southern Transylvania, represented by fortified medieval churches, baroque pal-
aces and castles, as well as buildings in the Secession and Jugendstil styles, parks, 
and public gardens that together make up a unique architectural ensemble, giving 
the region a central European character in the South-East European context (Hagen 
2021, 32–41). The second perspective explores the motivation of the study, which 
is to examine this heritage from the perspective of the emotional bond of the Tran-
sylvanian Saxons, who, although they left the region calling it Heimat, continue to 
keep it alive in their hearts and contribute to the preservation of the heritage from 
their new homeland, the Vaterland. 
Mathias Beer identifies and systematises the work of the main actors in Roma-
nia and Germany who aim to valorise and manage cultural heritage. Associations 
of emigrants to the Federal Republic of Germany (FRG), made up of people from 
the same villages or cities, take responsibility for their compatriots and the herit-
age left in the areas inhabited by Transylvanian Saxons, Swabians from the Banat, 
the Sathmar Swabians and ones from the mountainous Banat in Romania. These 
organisations support museums in their home regions, take care of the preservation 
of churches and cemeteries, manage the history of their native communities, and 
continue to promote German social structures in Germany, thus being vital custo -
dians of cultural memory. Their role as intermediaries between local communities 
in Romania and Germany is often underappreciated. In Germany, the equivalents 
of the regional organisations of the Democratic Forum of Germans in Romania are 
the Associations of Transylvanian Saxons, Banat Swabians, Sathmar Swabians, 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
238 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
and Bukovina Germans, which are dedicated to the preservation of cultural herit -
age through publications, archives, and museums, exemplified by the Transylva-
nian Museum in Gundelsheim ( https://siebenbuergen-institut.de/) and the Central 
Museum of the Danube Swabians in Ulm (https://www.dzm-museum.de/en/). The 
Evangelical Church CA and the Catholic Church in Romania, together with their 
counterparts in Germany, play a crucial role in the preservation of the cultural 
heritage of the German minorities, as religious life has been fundamental in the 
formation and development of these communities (Beer 2019, 43–44). 
With few or no community members and the maintenance of historic build -
ings requiring considerable financial resources, the upkeep of these buildings is of 
paramount importance. Responsibility for cultural heritage management is shared 
between the Romanian and German state. Key documents relating to the history 
and culture of the German minorities are preserved in Romania’s national archives 
and museums. This includes the funding of cultural activities at local and regional 
level as well as the support of institutions representing the German minorities in 
Romania and in the Federal Republic of Germany (FRG). Research centres such 
as the Institute for Social Sciences and Humanities in Sibiu (financed by the Ro -
manian state), the Siebenbürgen Institute in Gundelsheim, the Institute of Danube 
Swabians History and Regional Studies in Tübingen and the Institute for the Cul -
ture and History of the Germans of South-East Europe in Munich are supported 
by the Federal Government of Germany. These institutions play a crucial role in 
preserving the cultural heritage of the German communities and incorporating it 
into the collective memory of Romania and Germany. Efforts are carried out on 
several levels, with varying degrees of involvement and different emphases, but 
increasing in the context of transnational cooperation ( Beer 2019, 40–44). 
Transylvanian associationism and its function 
in heritage preservation 
Harvey (2001, 323) identifies a specific “strand” of heritage, which is more accu -
rately understood as a distinct discourse that emerged in late 19th-century Europe 
and evolved into a dominant “universalising” discourse in the 21st century. This dis-
course obscures the power relations underlying its formation, thereby concealing the 
cultural and social functions of “heritage.” While there is general interest in the past, 
the heritage discourse shapes particular cultural and social practices with significant 
consequences in late modernity. Despite claims linking heritage to postmodern com-
modification, its origins are rooted in 19th-century nationalism and liberal moder -
nity. Although alternative discourses exist, the dominant one remains closely tied to 
stewardship of the material past (Smith 2006, 17). The 19th century necessitated the 
development of “new devices to ensure or express social cohesion and identity and 
to structure social relations” (Hobsbawm 1983, 263). Monuments came to symbolise 
the new Modern Europe, serving as representations of national identity and European 
achievement. This drive aligned with the liberal education movement, which recog-
nised a moral responsibility to educate the public on civic duties and foster social 
stability by promoting national community and responsibility (Smith 2006, 18). 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Civic Engagement in Cultural Heritage 239 
During the long 19th century, Transylvania witnessed an associative efferves -
cence, a phenomenon that had a significant impact on the social and cultural struc-
ture of the region. The associations, following the example of those throughout 
the Habsburg Monarchy and later in Austro-Hungary, reflected the diversity and 
dynamism of Transylvanian society. According to Borbála Zsuzsanna Török, asso-
ciations and social clubs served as public places where societal norms and formal 
regulations were observed, including discussing and voting on common problems 
and formulating a financial plan. Defined as “any voluntary and structured group 
of people formed independently of the state for collective purposes other than 
financial gain,” these spaces favoured the convergence of wealthy and educated 
people of both noble and common origin. This mix gave rise to a new upper class, 
composed predominantly of academics, high-ranking bureaucrats, and clergy – 
professions more intimately linked to government operations. This trend was par -
ticularly widespread among Transylvanian Saxon communities ( Török 2016, 85). 
In addition to the economic and scientific associations founded at the end of the 
V ormärz period, the large number of other associations that emerged in Saxon 
towns and municipalities is characteristic, be it gymnastics, beautification, mu -
sic and women’s associations, voluntary fire brigades or associations that served 
ecclesiastical or tourist purposes. They all represented an element of coming to -
gether and cohesion and created niches that largely escaped state control ( Gottas 
2006, 1211). These entities have been crucial in promoting civil involvement and 
have contributed to the development of an active public sphere, essential for cul -
tural and social progress. 
Heritage and urbanity: The Association for the Embellishment 
of the City of Sibiu 
The AIOS played an important role in reshaping the urban landscape and helped 
to create a vibrant public sphere in a smaller city on the edge of the Empire. Its 
activities were closely linked to national developments and the nation-state build -
ing process, reflecting the aspirations and values of its inhabitants. At the same 
time, the founding of the association and the work carried out by its members 
were circumscribed by a civic spirit specific to the period and linked to the qual -
ity of citizens involved in the community. The study provides a detailed insight 
into how this association influenced civic life and shaped the cultural identity 
of Sibiu, emphasising the importance of associationism in shaping the Transyl -
vanian public space and facilitating intercultural dialogue in an era of profound 
transformations. 
In the 19th century, the Habsburg Empire underwent significant transfor -
mations in town planning and architecture, reflecting the social and economic 
changes of the time ( Schorske 1981, 91). The demolition of medieval fortifi -
cations and defensive towers was a crucial step in the modernisation of urban 
space, allowing cities to expand and adapt to the needs of a changing society. 
This period saw a transition from traditional architectural styles to eclectic and 
Art Nouveau, which brought a new breath of fresh air to the urban picture. In 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
240 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Sibiu, for example, the extension of Ocnei Street and the construction of the new 
bridges were part of this modernisation process, improving connectivity and ac -
cessibility in the city. The opening of new traffic arteries and the construction of 
public buildings such as the Philharmonic and the Synagogue helped to integrate 
the city into the cultural and social trends of the monarchy, marking an era of 
prosperity and architectural innovation ( Avram and Bucur 1999; Sigerus 1997; 
Bozdoc 2021; Gündisch 2018).
Generically known as the Belle Époque, the period from the end of the 19th 
century to the end of the Great War was characterised by diversity, innovation, 
and a growing interest in public space. Urban actors increasingly sought after so -
cialising in urban public space, frequenting cafes, restaurants, and reading clubs, 
and strolling on promenades and in parks (Jackson 2019, 6–10). Urban space was 
opening up to a wider public and green spaces were becoming increasingly popular 
(Lukacs 1990). 
Furthermore, the parks serve as contested symbolic spaces, reflecting the col -
lective historical memory of various national groups. Additionally, they function 
as sites of individual memory, becoming integral to the personal recollections 
of city residents from both national groups ( Witoszynska 2020, 138). Markian 
Prokopovych argues that the “municipal concern with the maintenance of old 
private parks and the establishment of new green spaces was continuous through -
out Habsburg Lemberg’s history.” His research explores the dual role of public 
green spaces in Lemberg, viewing them both as symbols of Habsburg authority 
and as recreational areas, utilised by various social groups for different purposes. 
He notes that, during the 19th century, municipal authorities primarily focused 
on transforming the urban landscape into classical and imperial spaces of sym -
bolic representation. However, by the end of the 19th and early 20th centuries, 
the situation shifted significantly, driven by the growing independence of local 
municipal authorities. As the city navigated its complex position – “bound to the 
Empire administratively and to the nation locally” – it sought a compromise that 
balanced loyalty to the imperial centre with the rising local national aspirations 
(Prokopovych 2006). 
The AIOS is an example of civic involvement in the urban and cultural de -
velopment of a city. Founded in 1879 by local elites, AIOS has had a significant 
impact on Sibiu’s urban appearance and infrastructure, contributing to the crea -
tion of a vibrant public space and improving the quality of life of its inhabitants, 
as well as to the urban and aesthetic development of the city. Through projects 
such as the landscaping of the Under the Alder Park and the railway station area, 
AIOS has demonstrated how coordinated citizen action can transform a city. With 
a vision to improve green spaces and urban infrastructure, the association has con-
tributed to creating a harmonious urban landscape and enriching Sibiu’s cultural 
heritage. The initiative to abandon the construction of a bazaar in favour of green 
spaces reflects the association’s priorities for a sustainable urban environment and 
improving the quality of life of its inhabitants. The efforts of founding members 
and association leaders such as Gustav Lindner and Friedrich Jickeli have been 
instrumental in realising these ambitious projects. Today, the AIOS continues to 

 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Civic Engagement in Cultural Heritage 241 
be an important pillar in the community, promoting urban development projects 
and heritage protection. 
Promoting heritage 100 years ago 
In the associative and civic history of Sibiu, the years 1879 and 1880 remain 
the benchmark in that five associations were founded: Hermania Men’s Choir 
(Männerchor Hermania), the Hunters’ Association from Sibiu (Hermannstädter 
Jagdverein), the Kommis Association (Kommisverein), the Transylvanian Car -
pathian Association (Siebenbürgischer Karpathenverein), and the AIOS (Verein 
zur Verschönerung der Stadt Hermannstadt), which in a population of about 
20.000 inhabitants represented an important associative proportion, emphasis -
ing the strong civic character and the desire for social involvement of the peo -
ple of Sibiu. The taxonomic diversity of the newly founded associations at the 
end of the 19th century can be seen from the names of the associations, which 
covered a wide range of leisure activities such as hunting, music, or mountain 
walks. Another argument that reinforces the idea of civic involvement of the 
Transylvanian Saxons from Sibiu is represented by the 64 associations existing 
in the city in 1904 ( HBGV 1904). 
The declared purpose of the association at the time of its foundation is the 
beautification of the city and its immediate surroundings, namely the crea -
tion and maintenance of gardens in the urban space belonging to the munici-
pality […]. The aim will be realised by associating male and female people, 
from and outside Sibiu, who have a sense of beauty and a sincere love for 
our city. 
(Buchholzer 1929, 6) 
The concept of Verschönerung des Stadbildes – beautification of the city – was fash-
ionable in the Austro-Hungarian Dual Monarchy, both the concept and the practice 
radiating from Vienna and recalibrating the interests of urban actors for beauty in 
the cities of the monarchy. Linked to this concept was the creation of parks and 
green spaces for walking and strolling, with the aim of enabling a stronger pen -
etration of social actors into the urban space. Although the statute of the associa -
tion mentioned the presence of both female and male, with the aim of promoting 
inclusion, in reality, in the early years of the association’s activity, the membership 
lists included exclusively men. Another aim of the association was to preserve the 
memory of those who considered it essential to be civically and socially involved 
in Sibiu’s urban life, the statute noting that “the names of the founders and their 
dedicated contributions will be duly preserved in the memory of Sibiu for eternity” 
(Buchholzer 1929, 6). 
The first president of the AIOS, Gustav Lindner was a professor and director of 
the Academy of Law in Sibiu, later in Cluj-Napoca, and during his time in Sibiu 
he was one of the most effervescent and civically involved urban actors in the city, 
being among the founders of two associations: Association of V olunteer Firemen 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
242 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
(1872), Association for the Embellishment of the City of Sibiu (1879), or among 
the leaders of other such socially involved organisations: the Transylvanian Car -
pathian Association (Siebenbürgischen Karpathenverein, between 1901 and 1909). 
His civic engagement is described in archival documents as: 
known and highly regarded for his rare zeal and excellent aptitude for the 
often difficult tasks of association life. A warm love of nature, a deep under-
standing of its beauties, and a relentless and noble thirst for action enabled 
Lindner also to play a leading part in this field of association activities, and 
under his leadership the committee of the newly organized association estab-
lished, in an amazingly varied and intense activity, the first ideas and plans 
for the beautification of our city. 
(Buchholzer 1929, 8) 
An important actor in the development of the association was Dr. Friedrich 
Jickeli, doctor at the Franz Josef Citizen’s Hospital and later the city physician 
(Stadtphysikus), who from 1879 to 1896 served as president of the association. 
He gained wide and lasting recognition for his home town due to his charitable 
involvement and his long service on the board of the association. The Associa -
tion gave him not only satisfaction for his aesthetic taste, but also the opportunity 
to contribute to the health of the city. Archival documents eulogistically review 
him, noting that for 17 consecutive years he guided the association, making per -
sonal sacrifices. In a span of 15 years, the association managed to completely 
transform and create, virtually from nothing, many of the facilities that today are 
unthinkable to be missing from the city’s public spaces. For example, on the Sol -
disch site, once a swampy garbage dump near the Bretterpromenade, a modern 
city park was developed with the help of Adolf von Stock’s aesthetic plan and the 
pioneering efforts provided by the k. u.  k. Military Command. Thus was born 
the city park with shady trees and perfect lawns. However, a turning point for the 
association was in 1881, when it took over responsibility for the upkeep of the 
town promenades (Buchholzer 1929 , 9–10). 
Carl Dörschlag, a drawing teacher at the Protestant School in Sibiu and a well-
known painter, was the president of the association from 1896 to 1914. The as -
sociation he led took care of several public spaces at the end of the 19th century, 
including the old promenade, the green areas on the Soldisch, the spaces around 
the theater, the railway station square and the Park Under the Alder, which they 
landscaped, the promenade on the Schulerschanze and the Elisabeta Park. The as -
sociation boasted an annual membership of between 400 and 500 people. It was 
constantly supported by people and institutions who contributed to its charitable 
and noble actions. The first and most important supporter was the city administra -
tion, which provided substantial resources from the very beginning, through the 
city hall and the city council, recognising the association’s efforts for the common 
good. Other supporters have included Hermannstädter allgemeine Sparkassa, var -
ious financial institutions, Albina, Bodenkreditanstalt, Hermania, and numerous 
private donors (Buchholzer 1929 , 13–16). 

 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
Civic Engagement in Cultural Heritage 243 
Emil Sigerus, director of the Transylvania Insurance Bank, was an active mem-
ber of the association’s board since 1896, serving as a director from 1914 to 1921. 
This was made possible by his passion for nature and botanical science in particu-
lar. His tenure was marked by the difficulties of the Great War and the post-war 
period, which were the most challenging times for the Association since its founda-
tion (Buchholzer 1929, 22). At the annual general meeting on 23 June 1921, tribute 
was paid to Emil Sigerus for his dedication and meticulous work as a committee 
member, especially during the tumultuous times of the war. After the war, the post 
of director went to General Leopold Hofbauer, who was succeeded three years later 
by Fritz Reissenberger, a teacher at the girls’ school. New monuments dedicated to 
Transylvanian cultural and religious personalities George Barițiu and Georg Daniel 
Teutsch were inaugurated during this period, as well as new alleys, passages, and 
streets were opened and laid out. The old promenade was much closer to the city 
centre, thus becoming more popular and frequented by visitors. Inevitably, public, 
and especially cultural, activities suffered a decline in intensity during the conflict 
and post-conflict years, going through various crises. Nonetheless, a new park was 
developed in the Lower Town to provide this area with a friendly community cen-
tre and recreation space. Although it has been a difficult period, the association 
has enjoyed the understanding, appreciation and support of a growing number of 
citizens, as evidenced by the increase in membership to a record high of around 800 
members (Buchholzer 1929, 25–27). 
These initiatives not only enriched the architectural heritage of Sibiu, but also 
contributed to the creation of a vibrant and accessible public space, reflecting the 
commitment of the then authorities to urban development and quality of life for 
citizens. After 1948, the AIOS ceased to exist, as it was communism that caused a 
sudden halt in civic involvement through the association. However, after the fall of 
communism in Romania and the rediscovery of the values and phenomena that pre-
dated communism in Romania, urban actors began to rethink their place in society. 
Promoting heritage nowadays 
The Association was re-founded in 2010, aiming to continue the tradition of bring-
ing together active members of the community to support the development of Sibiu. 
With an agenda focused on research, education, heritage protection, urban involve-
ment, and community development, AIOS has initiated and supported various pro-
jects, ranging from city-related studies and the organisation of archaeological sites, 
to educational programs and campaigns to beautify green spaces. These initiatives 
reflect AIOS’ commitment to the promotion of a sustainable development model 
and the inclusion of citizens in decision-making processes affecting the urban en -
vironment. In this context, the presented study can provide a valuable insight into 
the role of civic associations in shaping the public sphere and shaping the urban 
landscape, emphasising the importance of community participation in the develop-
ment process of cities. 
The socio-professional status of the founding members of AIOS is complex, 
including cultural figures, historians, archaeologists, museographers, businessmen, 

 
 
 
244 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
politicians, lawyers, and academics. The projects implemented by AIOS focus in 
particular on the fields of heritage education, with the aim of conveying informa -
tion to urban actors in Sibiu in order to awaken their interest in getting to know 
and valorising the tangible heritage of the city and the region (AIOS 2024). AIOS’ 
members consider that civic involvement involves the active participation of citi -
zens in the life of the local community and in the decision-making processes that 
influence the development and well-being of the city. It involves a range of ac -
tivities and initiatives designed to help improve quality of life and promote com -
munity values. Answering the question regarding the goals and activities of AIOS 
in the city of Sibiu, some members believe that these are education and public 
awareness, research and documentation projects, promotion of cultural tourism, 
partnerships and collaborations, and last but not least cultural and artistic events. 
Moreover, the top five keywords that best describe the Transylvanian cultural herit-
age are history, architecture, culture, diversity, and tradition. These five concepts 
identify some key elements of the understanding of cultural heritage under the 
umbrella of (post-communist) Romania and its metamorphosis into a state embed-
ded in democracy and cultural European values. The current activities of AIOS are 
primarily focused on heritage education for the general public. These initiatives are 
largely directed towards the civic population of Sibiu, with the objective of foster-
ing a greater appreciation for local history and cultural heritage. While legal regu-
lations regarding the preservation of green spaces and monuments have become 
more stringent than they were a century ago, AIOS is less involved in the physical 
maintenance of such sites. Instead, the organisation emphasises the importance of 
educating citizens about heritage preservation, particularly in light of the commu -
nist era, which largely neglected the valorisation of built heritage. 
Conclusions 
In conclusion, this study has explored the evolution of the authorised heritage dis-
course within the context of Transylvanian Saxon heritage, particularly focusing 
on the city of Sibiu. The research has highlighted the dynamic interplay between 
tangible and intangible cultural heritage, demonstrating how these elements were 
initially preserved and valorised by the Transylvanian Saxon community, and later 
taken over by new social actors following significant migration after 1990. The 
analysis of the AIOS, both in its historical and contemporary forms, illustrates how 
civic engagement and cultural practices have shifted within the broader political 
and social contexts, transitioning from a predominantly Saxon-driven preservation 
effort to one led by local Romanian actors. Through a comparative approach, the 
study has emphasised how heritage, especially urban landscapes, can act as a pow-
erful symbol of identity, continuity, and community involvement across borders. 
The challenges faced by Transylvanian Saxons in maintaining their connection to 
their homeland, despite geographic and political separations, reflect a broader phe-
nomenon of cultural continuity in the face of dislocation. Moreover, the findings 
show that, despite these challenges, the tangible heritage of Sibiu, deeply embed -
ded in its imperial Saxon past, continues to serve as a cultural bridge, linking the 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
Civic Engagement in Cultural Heritage 245 
city’s diverse historical influences to present-day efforts of preservation and com -
munity engagement. 
The case study of Sibiu and its evolving heritage practices provides a profound 
understanding of how cultural heritage is not only preserved but also reimagined 
under different political regimes. It emphasises the necessity of a transnational ap-
proach to cultural heritage management, recognising the shifting roles of different 
communities and the need for inclusive, participatory practices that acknowledge 
and celebrate the diversity of historical narratives. Ultimately, the research under -
scores the importance of civic engagement in the preservation of urban heritage, as 
well as the potential of cultural heritage to transcend national boundaries, acting as 
a unifying force that shapes both local and transnational identities. 
The examination of heritage and associationism in late 19th-century Europe, 
particularly within the context of Transylvanian society, reveals the profound in -
tersection of cultural, social, and political forces. Harvey’s (2001) critique of her-
itage as a universalising discourse provides a lens through which we can analyse 
how the past is mobilised to shape national identities, power structures, and civic 
cohesion. The heritage discourse that emerged during the rise of 19th-century 
nationalism – linked to ideas of territoriality, ethnicity, and the pastoral care of 
the past – remains influential today. It actively shapes social practices and politi -
cal agendas, while simultaneously obscuring the power dynamics that underpin 
these discourses. In the case of Transylvanian Saxon communities, civic engage -
ment manifested itself through a proliferation of associations that helped shape 
the public sphere and foster a sense of community. These organisations, such as 
the AIOS, were not only instrumental in transforming urban landscapes, creating 
green spaces, and promoting public welfare, but also in the construction of cultural 
identities tied to local and national aspirations. The urban spaces they shaped – 
through parks, promenades, and public buildings – were reflective of a broader 
Habsburg legacy, merging imperial representations with local needs for modernity 
and national identity. 
Moreover, the role of these civic associations in Sibiu underlines a key paradox: 
while these spaces were deeply tied to national and ethnic discourse, they also 
embodied a form of identity that was marked by the Germanic presence without 
the direct presence of Germans. AIOS, for instance, was composed of a diverse 
group of local elites, including academics, professionals, and public figures from 
various social strata, who, despite their complex socio-professional backgrounds, 
worked collectively towards urban beautification and civic engagement. This dem-
onstrated the intersection of local identities with broader European trends in herit-
age preservation, national identity formation, and the creation of a participatory 
public sphere. 
The evolution of these civic associations, particularly the AIOS, also speaks to 
a larger process of continuity and change, as evidenced by the association’s rebirth 
after the fall of communism. Today, AIOS remains a symbol of the ongoing inter-
play between heritage, public space, and community participation, contributing 
to both the preservation of cultural heritage and the promotion of civic respon -
sibility. As such, AIOS offers a valuable example of how heritage discourse and 

 
  
 
 
 
 
246 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
associationism are deeply embedded in the creation and transformation of urban 
landscapes, revealing the complex historical legacies that continue to shape mod-
ern public spheres.
