15 Revisiting the darkness 
Participatory approaches for 
engaging with difficult and “dark” 
heritage places 
Suzie Thomas 
In this chapter, I discuss working with participatory approaches  at places that can 
be associated with so-called “dark heritage”. I do this by reflecting on my own 
experiences in teaching and research as well as referring to the literature on this 
subject and the chapter takes a wide global context. My own research has, so far, 
mostly been carried out at different sites primarily in Finland, the United Kingdom, 
and Belgium, although increasingly, I am thinking about, collaborating with and 
exploring issues and case studies further afield, including in the Republic of the 
Marshall Islands and the USA. 
The concept of “dark heritage” and how it can be understood has been ex -
plored elsewhere (e.g. Fuggle 2020 ; Scarlett et al. 2023 ; Thomas 2022 ). For  
that reason I do not repeat those debates in detail here, although it is important  
nonetheless to outline what this term might mean in the context of participatory  
research. I find “dark” heritage, like the concept of “heritage” more broadly, to  
be one of those terms that is more or less widely understood in some way or  
other by most, but which does not often get a clear definition assigned to it. For  
some time, heritage specialists and other scholars have been aware of and debat -
ing the more problematic sides of heritage – the “heritage that hurts” ( Sather-
Wagstaff 2016 ). We often nowadays refer to this as “dark heritage”, (usually)  
places (rather than intangible heritage or portable objects), openly associated  
with suffering, loss, and atrocity. It can also refer to places with hidden pasts  
that have been revealed and revisited through a refocussing on such aspects as  
colonialism, societal oppression, or other injustices. These heritages have some -
times taken on state-sanctioned, international recognition as heritage, even as  
UNESCO World Heritage sites.  
A more relevant set of questions than the definition of dark heritage itself for this 
chapter, is, rather, how does the “darkness” of a heritage place affect the ways in  
which participation with that heritage can occur? Can regular approaches for herit-
age participation be followed, or are special considerations needed that are different 
to approaches that would work at more positive, unproblematic, or “light” heritage 
places? How do the shared and experienced traumas at dark heritage places affect  
the needs and wishes of potential participants? We should certainly ask what the  
goals are of developing participatory heritage at “dark” heritage sites. Such places, 
connected to turbulent pasts, are somehow transformed through tourism and through 
This chapter has been made available under a CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 International license.-

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Revisiting the Darkness 249 
heritage management interventions into places where people nonetheless choose to 
spend weekends, holidays; essentially their spare time. 
(Dark) heritage is powerful 
For me, my interest in heritage studies in particular grew out of first studying 
archaeology for my Bachelor’s degree in Archaeology and Prehistory at the Uni-
versity of Sheffield. During that time it struck me that I found that the more in-
teresting aspect of archaeology was not the landscapes and artefacts uncovered, 
categorised, and interpreted, but rather what reactions they got. Why did people 
get so interested, even emotional sometimes, about objects and places so associ-
ated with the past? Why do some people report picking up on uncanny or otherwise 
unusual atmospheres, even ghosts, at certain places? What is the hold that the past 
has over us humans? 
Whether it is traditional practices passed down through generations, the past as 
justification for contemporary antagonism and aggression, or the sense of wonder 
one has when stepping into a museum or visiting an ancient place, it is hard to 
deny that heritage – the past as experienced in the here and now – has a certain 
power. Heritage has been identified as a possible means for generating wellbeing 
(e.g. Burnell and Woodhouse 2022), for being a “social glue” (Viljoen and Henama 
2017, 8), or for leading to sustainable regeneration of whole neighbourhoods or 
even regions (e.g. Pendlebury and Porfyriou 2017). But it can also generate seri-
ous disagreements and can itself become a pawn in political and social conflicts, 
as different groups try to manipulate the past to support their own causes. In many 
ways this can be exacerbated by the presence of violence, injustice, suffering, and 
hate in the past. 
Difficult (per Macdonald 2015) or dark heritage is increasingly a phenomenon 
in itself. We know already that many members of the public still perceive heritage 
as positive: places and practices of which to feel proud, useful to attract visitors 
and tourism revenue, and fun places to spend free time and holidays. Visiting her-
itage places is indeed strongly associated with leisure and enjoyment, even when 
educational elements are involved. This has proven true even for sites associated 
with pain and suffering – Alcatraz is consistently one of the top five highest-rated 
tourist attractions in San Francisco, arguably because it is a famous thing that one 
“should” visit when in the city, rather than any abiding enthusiasm for the history 
and heritage of penitentiary incarceration on the part of so many visitors; a highly 
commercialised experience (Strange and Kempa 2003, 388). 
Participation 
The so-called participatory turn is no longer a novel phenomenon in museum and 
heritage work, and has been discussed, researched, and implemented across the 
globe for decades already. Finally in 2022, after protracted debate, even the Interna-
tional Council of Museums’ (ICOM) definition was updated to include that muse-
ums “operate and communicate ethically, professionally and with the participation 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
250 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
of communities, offering varied experiences for education, enjoyment, reflection 
and knowledge sharing”. 
The 2008 ICOMOS (International Council on Monuments and Sites) Charter 
for the Interpretation and Presentation of Cultural Heritage Sites includes in its 
Principle 6 that 
The Interpretation and Presentation of cultural heritage sites must be the re -
sult of meaningful collaboration between heritage professionals, host and 
associated communities, and other stakeholders. 
(ICOMOS 2008) 
But what does this actually mean in practice? Certainly the definition from 
ICOM (2022), along with policy developments such as the Faro Framework Con-
vention and the participatory practices on which the UNESCO 2003 Intangible 
Cultural Heritage Convention is based and in many parts of the world implemented, 
imply that participation is becoming (or already is) mainstream for heritage and its 
official custodians as well as for its publics. 
In her incredibly influential 2010 publication The Participatory Museum, for -
mer museum director Nina Simon tackled this topic head-on; providing acces -
sible and applicable concepts related to facilitating and allowing participation in 
museums. In this open-access book filled with illustrative case studies and exam -
ples, she critically explored the ways in which museum institutions try to open 
themselves up to a wider participation, both on site and digitally ( Simon 2010). 
One core challenge of this wider participation, naturally, is the ability to “let go” 
and to relinquish control to the public. Letting go is difficult, however, especially 
with potentially divisive or controversial, dark heritage. This is heritage which 
may go in unpredictable directions, and which possibly may be appropriated or 
used to support ideals or agendas that are problematic to the state or to society 
more broadly. 
Good intentions of participatory projects using even non-dark heritage as a 
platform for social and economic, ideally sustainable, development, can also 
encounter both successes and failures in their implementations; and it may be 
that participatory heritage projects can simultaneously succeed and fail. In her 
2023 book Rethinking Heritage for Sustainable Development , critical heritage 
scholar Sophia Labadi assesses several participatory, but nonetheless top-down, 
internationally funded projects in Sub-Saharan Africa. She notes also of her own 
analysis that: 
I recognise that activities cannot have exclusively “good” or “bad” impacts. 
Instead, each situation is recognised as having possible positive dimensions as 
well as negative ones, with heritage project participants having various – even 
opposing – motivations, opinions and behaviours. Addressing such complex-
ity on the ground highlights the multiplicity of possible narratives on heritage 
for development. 
(Labadi 2022, 14) 

 
 
  
Revisiting the Darkness 251 
This is important to remember when evaluating participatory projects, and 
points also to decisions that we need to make about how and what to evaluate. 
What does, or should, success look like? 
At the University of Antwerp, in our two-year Heritage Masters programme – 
which launched in September 2022 – all students have to take at least one course 
on participatory methods in heritage work. As a departmental team, my colleagues 
and I feel that this is important for students to become aware of, even if they do 
not consider it the main aspect of heritage that interests them. Many, if not most, of 
the students will become future heritage professionals and will have to engage with 
different publics in their work. 
The students need to be aware of the possibilities that open up through consider-
ing how to encourage greater engagement with different audiences. This includes 
thinking about how to democratise and decolonise the past, and particularly how 
to reach previously excluded or less involved sections of society. It also includes 
a focus on failure as an opportunity to learn, and there are plenty of moments to 
disabuse the students of any ideas that participatory heritage is a simple or easy 
topic. Significantly, for several course excursions, we have visited a unique and 
excellent case study of natural and cultural heritage site in West Flanders – Kamp 
Vloethemveld (Figure 15.1). 
Figure 15.1 University of Antwerp Heritage Studies excursion in 2022 to Kamp Vloethem-
veld, Zedelgem. 
Source: Photo: Suzie Thomas. 

 
 
 
 
252 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Vloethemveld is a former prisoner-of-war (PoW) site which was run by the Brit-
ish in the Second World War and held not only German PoWs but also many from 
the three Baltic states of Latvia, Lithuania, and Estonia, as well as individuals of 
other nationalities that fought alongside Germans. Former inmates from all these 
nations are known to have partaken in activities connected to the Holocaust and 
other wartime atrocities (Aerts et al. 2022). With an arguably dark heritage among 
its many other heritages (the site is also a nature reserve in the present time and has 
cultural remnants relating back as far as the Roman times), Kamp Vloethemveld 
exhibits numerous controversies and competing values for its stakeholders and 
managers. As far as teaching is concerned, it is an excellent case study for illustrat-
ing the difficulties in making decisions for managing, interpreting, and conserving 
heritage where there are so many opposing, often emotional, viewpoints. The site 
covers many domains of heritage and as such presents a useful fit for the diversity 
of interests and specialisms that our students can choose to develop. Class dis-
cussions following visits to this place indicate that the students have mostly been 
sensitive to these multiple meanings, and we debate together the range of likely 
stakeholders (both within and outwith West Flanders itself), and identify possible 
points of confluence and discord. This site has also inspired one (award-winning) 
MA dissertation already (Kindt 2023). 
Peace-washing 
It would seem that many sites associated with “dark” pasts experience some kind of 
lightening from outside. Following Philip Stone’s influential spectrum of dark tour-
ism, Stone (2006) suggests that activities such as commercialisation can lighten a 
site of dark tourism, as can time, as a place becomes less potent the further back 
in history its associated “dark” event is. I would add to this that connecting such 
places to messages of peace and hope may be an attempt to cleanse their past, or 
at least try to ensure for them a lighter and more positive future. I have considered 
this as a form of “peace-washing”, similar to “greenwashing” for otherwise envi-
ronmentally damaging actions (Miller 2017), or “culture washing”, used to refer to 
toxic workplaces that convey a false impression of a healthy and happy organisa-
tional culture (Schmidt 2020). 
In introducing the term peace-washing, I mean to express that I find it ironic 
that, for example, the two UNESCO World Heritage Sites associated with the most 
extreme violence of all – nuclear war – namely Hiroshima Peace Memorial (Gen-
baku Dome) in Japan and the Bikini Atoll Nuclear Test Site in the Republic of the 
Marshall Islands (Figure 15.2), are both linked with peace in their inscription texts. 
The implied suggestion here is that we as a species are somehow able to learn from 
our mistakes (a difficult claim to make in the current political climate). Or perhaps 
it is as if these places can somehow be cured of their non-peaceful pasts through 
a contemporary association with peace. These sites are not necessarily associated 
with fun and leisure in the same way as some other dark tourist sites, but the edu-
cational focus of them is made somewhat paradoxical by the peace-washing that 
takes place with both. In the case of Bikini Atoll (and the other test sites of the 

 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
Revisiting the Darkness 253 
Figure 15.2 Bikini Atoll Nuclear Test Site, 1.10.2005. 
Source: Photo: Ron Van Oers, copyright UNESCO, Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike 3.0 IGO. 
Marshall Islands), this occurred even at the time of the testing, with the Indigenous 
Marshallese communities to be displaced told by the US Government that their 
sacrifice would contribute to world peace. The Indigenous Bikinians were evacu-
ated from their Atoll, with the promise that their sacrifice was “for the good of 
mankind” (Niedenthal 2013, xxxviii). Furthermore the UNESCO World Heritage 
inscription of 2010 notes how the violent past of Bikini and its significance in the 
beginning of the “nuclear age”, is at odds with its “paradoxical image of peace and 
of earthly paradise” (UNESCO n.d.). Plans not yet realised for the site’s manage -
ment and interpretation include the provision of a Peace Museum. 
According to some historians and international relations scholars such as Rhys 
Crilley (2023), we have recently entered the very dangerous Third Nuclear Age 
(beginning, apparently, in August 2019 with the withdrawal of the USA from the 
Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty). This is noticeable in the media, with 
discussions of nuclear war and threats from politicians becoming more and more 
commonplace. However, in such times when the rhetoric, even in everyday news, 
steps perilously close to normalising the possibility of nuclear war, can hopes for 
peace continue to be symbolised through these places? 
This question is relevant also for the motivation of visitors to (dark) heritage 
places. Do visitors seek lessons on peace, or do they seek something else? As 
shown by the field of dark tourism studies as a subsection of interest within tour -
ism studies, as well as the still-growing interest in so-called difficult or “dark” 
heritage within the broader field of heritage studies, there is awareness both within 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
254 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
and outwith academic discourse that there is some special draw that such sites 
hold – for researchers as well as for the wider public. This might not always be 
perceived as an overtly negative energy in itself, and dark tourism scholars have 
long documented that alongside the commonly assumed fascination with the ma -
cabre, other motivations can be a driver for visiting dark tourism places. As James 
Kennell and Raymond Powell wrote in 2021, “[O]ther motivations for visiting 
dark sites, such as a desire for novelty, nostalgia, curiosity, entertainment and pil -
grimage are the same as motivations for visiting [other] heritage sites” ( Kennell 
and Powell 2021, 368). 
Remembrance is often also an important driver for visitors. It is also a driver for 
state actors who make the decisions on what places to memorialise and how. In a 
third-year Bachelor’s course on Dark Heritage that I teach at the University of Ant-
werp, for the past couple of years we have taken an excursion to Kazerne Dossin in 
the nearby city of Mechelen. Kazerne Dossin, consisting of a memorial, a museum, 
and a research centre for human rights, is subsidised by the Flemish Government 
and the City of Mechelen, and is a part of the European Holocaust Research Infra-
structure. The memorial is on the location of the Dossin Barracks, and to cite the 
Kazerne Dossin website: “25,490 Jews and 353 Roma were deported from the Dos-
sin barracks on 28 train transports to Auschwitz-Birkenau between 1942 and 1944” 
(Kazerne Dossin, n.d.). The Jews were targeted by the Nazis during their occupa-
tion in Belgium (and there is much discussion in the museum about the complicity 
of Belgian authorities and many civilians), while the Belgian government itself 
took the decision also to persecute Roma people. 
During the annual class visit, we observe the techniques applied within the per-
manent exhibition in particular to tell the story of what happened, and what con-
tinues to happen as the world faces seemingly endless instances of human rights 
abuse. A core message in both the text and the stories told by the tour guides is that 
the victims, having been dehumanised by their oppressors, must be rehumanised. 
A wall spanning four floors (Figure 15.3) depicts the 25,843 victims – with pho-
tographs where these have been found by the museum’s researchers or donated by 
relatives, otherwise, there are silhouettes, and this number – 25,483 – is repeated 
regularly throughout the museum and by the many tour guides. At the same time, 
great care is taken not to apply numbers to the wall itself – these human beings 
have been numbered in the past, and will not be again. Care is taken also to use 
the names of individuals as their stories are told, through anecdotes shared by the 
guides, through recorded testimonies of survivors playing throughout the museum, 
and through the searchable database. 
Challenging terminologies 
Humanity, specifically re- rather than de-humanising, is also important in a project 
that I have been involved with in the USA, a digital heritage project that is opening 
up access to records, former belongings, and other data on two internment camps 
in Idaho where, in the Second World War, Japanese and Japanese-American people 
were kept (as elsewhere in the USA) as detainees in the aftermath of the attack 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
Revisiting the Darkness 255 
Figure 15.3 University of Antwerp students in Conservation/Restoration visiting Kazerne 
Dossin in 2023. 
Source: Photo: Suzie Thomas. 
on Pearl Harbor, as part of an alarmist and fundamentally racist reaction by the 
American Government. 
Efforts to redress that past have included artistic engagements, awareness-raising 
by prominent Japanese-Americans such as George Takei (Takei, Eisinger, and Scott 
2020), himself a survivor of several of these camps, and increased historical and 
archaeological research. Spurred out of archaeological research at these Idaho sites 
of Kooskia and Minidoka, the Internment Archaeology Digital Archive will eventu-
ally make available a fully accessible database and website of these two internment 
camps, as well as downloadable curricula on both sites (Michigan State University 
2023), with the possibility of expanding in the future. 
The researchers at Michigan State University on this project – which is funded by 
the US National Parks Service’s Japanese American Confinement Sites Program – 
have taken great effort to consult not only with heritage specialists and practitioners 
but also with community members, especially those who are survivors or descend-
ants of survivors of Kooskia and Minidoka. An important feature of the site is that 
among the searchable terms (which we have discussed in project meetings), a term 
which appears in the original government records for many of the internees is not 
to be included. That term is alien, and it is excluded at the request of descendants, 
recognising the power of that word to dehumanise people all over again. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
256 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Other terms around dark heritage can also raise questions, not least the term 
dark heritage itself. Lapland’s Dark Heritage, a research project with which I was 
involved, along with other scholars from the Universities of Oulu and Helsinki in 
Finland, aimed to understand the material (and immaterial) legacy of the Second 
World War and its remains in Finnish Lapland. I and my colleagues encountered 
challenges on more than one occasion about the name of the project. 
Especially the local residents that we interviewed in cities such as Rovaniemi, 
sitting roughly on the Arctic Circle, questioned why this heritage could be thought 
of as “dark”. To many of our informants, it was local, and thus “their” heritage, and 
this was what made it interesting to them, rather than any connections with wartime, 
PoWs, fighting, forced labour, the Nazis, or other traditionally negative tropes. 
Since the conclusion of the project, I have been thinking more and more about 
whether “dark heritage” is the right term to use. It is certainly becoming more vis-
ible in academic discourses ( Thomas 2022), and even the University of Antwerp 
Bachelor level course is called “Dark Heritage”. However, I am also mindful of 
the power of terms and labels: is the concept of “dark” too problematic to discuss 
in this way? There has been limited discussion of whether dark heritage as a term 
has unfortunate racial connotations, although such as David Goldberg (1993) have 
discussed the uses of “black” and “white”, as metaphors for good and evil, as 
having their origins in racist thinking, when discussing the origins of racism in 
the West. I expect the problematization of the term will develop as the term itself 
continues to be used in scholarship, even if we are not quite there yet. Are other 
terms such as dissonant, ambivalent, or negative, as scholars such as Tunbridge 
and Ashworth (1996) and Meskell (2002) have used, more appropriate and less 
loaded? As a field within heritage studies, we do not seem to have reached a con -
sensus on this. 
Participatory research in dark heritage: Burden on survivors, 
descendants, and stewards 
Another important issue to flag at this stage is the possible burden that academic 
and visitor interest in dark heritage can have on survivors and descendants, as well 
as the current stewards of dark heritage places. While it may feel favourable to 
include participants to research, this may also be a lot of ask of some people, espe-
cially if it relates to past traumas or assumes that participation will be made on a 
voluntary basis without any compensation for time or knowledge. 
In the Autumn of 2023 at the University of Antwerp we were lucky to have a 
special guest lecture from Verne Harris, Archivist of the papers of Nelson Mandela 
in South Africa. Harris was in Belgium for a week in order to give a keynote at a 
conference in Oostende (Harris 2024). In an open and fascinating discussion with 
our Master’s students, Harris highlighted the importance of participatory commu -
nity projects for healing and moving forward, following traumatic chapters in his-
tory. These projects may often be small in scale but according to Harris, have a far 
more lasting impact than the musealisation of sites of atrocity or the building of 
grand new memorial structures. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Revisiting the Darkness 257 
He argued that these large structures, on the one hand, speak to a human need 
to create and to build something as a means of dealing with (and perhaps to try to 
bring closure to) a difficult past, but on the other hand, they may be of less help 
to survivors, as they tend to become tourist attractions for outsiders rather than 
providing what victims need. He commented too, that in some cases when talk-
ing to survivors of the atrocities of Apartheid, rather than following the route of 
formal memorialisation at places connected to that past, some have told him that 
they would prefer that a place is completely demolished, altered, or repurposed. 
They simply never want to have to see it again. Being confronted with a place that 
holds such painful memories may not be conducive to healing, and healing itself is 
ultimately a very personal process (Harris 2023; also Harris 2014). 
As museology scholar Elizabeth Crooke has commented in the case of the Trou-
bles in Northern Ireland,
1 perhaps (researchers or the wider public) are not entitled 
to every single story and memory. She observes that “we need to respect the people 
and allow some personal memories not to become public history. We may not have 
the right to all knowledge” (Crooke 2001, 136). Some things maybe need to stay 
silent and people should also be allowed to forget or at least not to revisit. 
Dark heritage participation 
Complex and varying issues need to be kept in mind then, when planning or at 
least hoping to develop participatory projects around dark heritage sites. As with all 
participatory heritage, we can critically examine how that participation develops, 
taking in mind the extent to which the process is top-down or bottom-up. 
It should be fair to assume that there is not one set participatory model that will 
fit all collaborations and engagements. Some communities will have the energy, 
experience and (perhaps needed) expertise to really set their own agenda and bring 
ideas to a heritage project, or even create one themselves with or without the help 
of the professionals Others may really need a lot of information, didactic in deliv-
ery, to bring them to the table. In the context of more difficult heritage too, it may 
be that a group or community would rather not work with that heritage, or may pre-
fer that others deal with this for them rather than having to be involved themselves. 
Stewardship can also be a burden, and it may not be fair to expect communities, 
groups, or individuals in some cases to take this on. 
Particularly survivors that have been vocal or activist in the past may become 
expected to act as spokespeople for their communities and for the experiences – 
both positive and negative – believed to have been endured by those communi -
ties. This is particularly the case with Indigenous communities, who in many cases 
already have a difficult history with academia itself, having historically been the 
subject of study of anthropologists, medical researchers, and others. 
The Republic of the Marshall Islands’ National Nuclear Commission (NNC) 
starkly acknowledges this burden. Ariana Tibon-Kilma, a Commissioner at the 
NNC, recently gave a lecture in the Hanford Nuclear Waste Scholar Series, where 
she elaborated on the damage caused to Marshallese people by the US nuclear 
weapons testing programme (Tibon-Kilma 2023). This programme was carried out 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
258 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
on their islands and atolls between 1946 and 1958. During this time many inhabit-
ants were displaced from their ancestral homelands to make way for the bomb, 
most infamously on Bikini Atoll where Castle Bravo as well as some twenty-two 
other devices were detonated. Yet the damage extended well beyond this initial 
(and subsequent) displacement, causing also serious health problems from radioac-
tive fallout as well as losses of different kinds of tangible and intangible heritage 
(Brown 2013). 
As Tibon-Kilma described in her talk, for years afterwards those who had been 
exposed to radiation were continually inspected by American medics. The Mar -
shallese trusted that these inspections were medical treatment intended to help 
them regain health, but only learned later that this was instead medical experimen-
tation and monitoring of the effects of the radiation on them without any effort to 
tend to their illnesses (Tibon-Kilma 2023). 
Oft-cited comments from the USA’s Atomic Energy Commission in 1956 re -
garding the opportunity in the Marshall Islands to observe and study the effects 
of radiation exposure on the people of Rongelap Atoll indicate abundantly the 
dehumanisation of the local Indigenous population: 
Now data of this type has never been available. While it is true that these 
people do not live, I would say, the way Westerners do, civilised people, it is 
nevertheless also true that these people are more like us than mice”. 
(Atomic Energy Commission 1956, in Barker 2013, 46) 
This legacy of being the subject of scientific study remains raw among the Mar-
shallese, and among the many recommendations and guidelines they issue to pro -
spective researchers looking at aspects of the nuclear legacy (including heritage 
legacies) in the Marshall Islands, the Republic of the Marshall Islands NNC remind 
the following: 
Supporting research is a burden. Knowledge production – through research, 
teaching (e.g. study abroad), and media representations – is not benign, and 
often serves the interests and priorities of those seeking the knowledge rather 
than the Marshallese people. Even well-intentioned research or knowledge 
production is invasive, and creates hardships for communities. 
(NNC 2021) 
This highlights effectively and strongly how ethnographic researchers – even 
those with the best intentions – need to take care to be truly participatory, and to 
know when to realign goals, when to change direction, and ultimately also when to 
back off. 
As a means of addressing these burdens, in some parts of the world it is now 
common practice and even expected to offer financial compensation to Indigenous 
informants for their time and effort. This is true in Australia, where it has been 
standard practice for some years now to pay fees to Aboriginal informants in return 
for their time and knowledge (e.g. Kildea et al. 2009, 146), and to budget this into 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Revisiting the Darkness 259 
project proposals. Even better of course is to try to work on the terms of local, and 
especially Indigenous communities, who are the knowledge holders. 
Up until now I have worked with so-called dark heritage in primarily European 
settings. One of the most rewarding projects for many reasons – the sheer amount of 
research output we were able to produce, the connections with colleagues, and most 
importantly the friendships with voluntary participants – was the aforementioned 
Lapland’s Dark Heritage project. This was a project that ran for four years, funded 
by the Academy of Finland. The social connections that were possible to be made 
were helped in no small part by the deliberate planning into the project – relatively 
unusual for archaeological research in Finland at that time – of public participation 
opportunities. 
This meant that we arranged in all years of the project (apart from the first sum-
mer, which was for scoping possible sites and talking to local people), public ar -
chaeology excavations where it was possible for people to join in. This still had to 
consider safety issues such as ensuring (under the Finnish system – the rules vary 
from country to country) that participants had arranged their own insurance, and 
limiting numbers so that the volunteer to staff ratio meant that supervision could 
be done safely. 
In the first year that a public excavation was organised, the online registration 
for volunteers was full within six hours of its announcement, and featured a range 
of participants both from further afield (the south and middle of Finland) as well as 
local residents from Inari and Ivalo. 
One aspect that caused a little concern at first, especially in the first season when 
we excavated so close to both the Siida Sámi museum and the road, on a site that had 
been a German military hospital during the war (Figure 15.4), was that our presence 
and the publicity that it generated might make the site vulnerable to souvenir hunt-
ers. I remember one evening discussing with colleagues what we would do if in the 
morning we returned to find the site dug over or damaged in some way. Fortunately 
this did not happen – despite stories and also evidence elsewhere of metal detector-
ists and others targeting Second World War sites in Lapland for saleable items, but 
it highlighted that while raising awareness about these places we may also make 
them vulnerable. 
On social media we made careful decisions about what imagery we would share 
of the excavations and discoveries. A Masters student from European Ethnology 
in Helsinki was employed to manage the majority of the social media interactions 
through platforms such as Instagram, Twitter, and Facebook (Hekkurainen 2018). 
This included avoiding posting images of material remains we found, such as frag-
ments of pottery, that bore Nazi symbolism. We had less control over the decisions 
of the media, however, and we noticed in several news reports that these symbols 
had been used in their photographs, presumably so as to be more eye-catching. On 
only one occasion in the final year of fieldwork we posted an image of tree graf-
fiti in the shape of a swastika – carved into a tree next to the Kaamanen German 
airfield. This was shared as part of a larger collection of images from the site, and 
somewhat nervously, although in the end there were no comments on the picture at 
all, adverse or otherwise. 

 
 
  
260 Engaging Communities in Cultural Heritage 
Figure 15.4 Excavations at the former German military hospital in Inari, Finnish Lapland, 
in 2016. 
Source: Photo: Wesa Perttola. 
Lapland’s Dark Heritage touched on heritage concerned with conflict, and as 
such had the potential to be harmful, both emotionally and physically. We en-
countered older people, especially local elder Sámi and Finns, who had their own 
memories of the period, especially of evacuation (as children) and of returning to 
discover their homes and landscapes looking very different, often with the build -
ings all burned down. One elderly man in Inari described digging a hole in the 
ground to sleep in as a young boy before they were able to rebuild a home. 
A Sámi elder reminisced that the rapid evacuation to Sweden had not been the 
first time that she had travelled abroad in her life. To my shock, she explained that 
some years earlier, she had been in the Baltic countries as part of a living exhibit 
in the style of the infamous Worlds Fair human zoos. In another also very moving 
case, my colleague was taken into the forest around the village of Vuotso to find the 
location of a home-made memorial for a young boy who had been a later victim of 
the war, when in 1958, an unexploded ordnance had taken his life, and injured sev-
eral others (Koskinen-Koivisto and Thomas 2018). The place, a source of pain for 
the surviving brothers who had been forbidden from going to the site as children, 
was semi-forgotten but as a result of my colleague asking about it and being taken 
to see it, it was rediscovered also by the brothers. 

 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
   
 
 
 
Revisiting the Darkness 261 
 Finalreflections 
I continue to try to make sense of participatory dark heritage-making as a process or 
set of processes, or non-processes. Among the very human chaos that many of us en-
counter within heritage, dark or otherwise, I also see several key points that I think 
are worth keeping in mind for participatory action – whether the goal is research, 
interpretation, reconciliation, commemoration, or anything else for that matter. 
(Re-)Humanization – at many dark heritage places, some kind of othering, whether 
through pogroms, imperial and colonial oppression, or other exploitations and 
destructions, has taken place, resulting in the dehumanisation of the victims. It is 
essential to remember that our participants – our colleagues in fact – as well as 
those no longer with us, must have their humanity acknowledged and respected. 
Groups are not homogeneous, so room has to be made for individual voices and 
viewpoints. 
Burden – the work of dark heritage-making and dark heritage research may be a 
no longer tolerable burden to some, particularly those who are often called upon 
for research and to speak for their communities, and for those who are tired of 
being studied. Alternative ways can be found, even if this means walking away 
or waiting. 
Time – they say that it is a healer, and in some cases, the growing temporal distance 
between dark heritage pasts and the present can help to make it easier to reassess 
and confront difficult pasts. At the same time, newer generations may come with 
a very different set of questions and expectations than their predecessors. Past 
silences may be resented, or new identities may be sought. 
Responsibility – for a heritage scholar engaging with dark heritage, an expectation 
may also become evident, because some may see your work as an opportunity to 
raise awareness of what happened, that your research will somehow contribute 
to significant (societal) changes (which may or may not be the case). As with all 
participatory heritage projects, expectations may need to be managed, and care-
ful and gentle exit strategies may be needed for when a project comes to its end. 
Finally, Forgetting can also be wished for – not everyone wants to engage with or 
remember dark heritage, and that is fine. 
Note 
1 The Troubles is a term used to describe the conflicts in Northern Ireland between 
Protestant Unionists and Catholic Republicans that were at their most violent from the 
late 1960s until the Good Friday Agreement of 1998.
